# Stitch: design

Status: direction agreed in discussion, 2026-10-11. Milestone 1 and most of milestones 2 and 3 are implemented, milestone 4 is built, and milestone 5 has started with tools written in Otter (§2.17); see §5.

Stitch is an agent orchestrator, and the successor of edge talk (`~/devel/edge-talk`). A Rust node does the work and runs on every machine, the iPhone included. Native apps, a CLI and a TUI are its clients. Chats, with attachments, stay first-class. Around them, Stitch supervises agents that work for hours or days. Otter (`~/devel/otter`), the language that succeeds Shards 1.x, and its runtime, is the engine for agents and tools, not the application core.

edge talk is not ported but rewritten. The Otter repository (Shards 2.0, formerly shards2) has [`docs/host-requirements.md`](../../otter/docs/host-requirements.md) (commit `0006a1a`), which lists what a production host needs that Otter lacks, with proposed decisions H1–H14. That list came from a read-only survey of edge talk, which it calls "a large production 1.x host". Its §7 sets Otter's priorities for this host (calling it "an agent-orchestrator host"): the P0 rename, Tracks A to D, and what is deferred. This document records two things:

- what the survey found that is specific to edge talk (§1);
- Stitch's direction and how it changes those requirements (§2, §3).

## 1. What the survey found

Counts are from 2026-10-10, measured on `ae6949c7`.

- **Scripts:** 312 `.shs` files, about 32,000 lines (about 26,000 outside `Tests/` and `Extra/`). 180 are entry scripts, built to `.sho`, and Swift starts 171 of them by name.
- **Shards used:** 401 distinct, 14,730 call sites. 53 exist in Otter by name, covering 13% of names and 54% of calls. 338 are missing.
- **`check`** (`shards2 check` at the time, now `otter check`): 1 of 312 files passes, both as written and after a mechanical rewrite of labels and operators.
- **Swift shards:** 169, about 23,500 lines:
  - 68 SwiftUI and 37 RealityKit;
  - 16 WebView and 11 sign-in;
  - the rest system integrations.

  26 files suspend inside activation with a blocking poll loop. The app binds 176 `ext/*` externals and polls them every tick.
- **Modules:** about 40 1.x modules are linked. Used in production:
  - sqlite with cr-sqlite, FTS5 and sqlite-vec;
  - http, including streaming and a server;
  - llm through llama.cpp, mainly for embeddings;
  - fs, json, imaging, events, hashes, ssh, local shell, fileops, WebSocket, markdown, pdf, spreadsheet;
  - candle (UMAP only);
  - the engram shards.

**Lesson: edge talk did too much in Shards.** Application core was written in a scripting language:

- **UI as shards:** SwiftUI and RealityKit trees built by data flow.
- **Per-provider duplication:** title, summary and gist generation in about 8 provider variants each.
- **Database logic everywhere:** 695 `DB.Query` call sites across the scripts.
- **Implicit plumbing:** 486 `Do` calls reaching into caller scope, 150 globals, and 209 `Await` blocks moving script code onto worker threads.
- **Frame bindings:** Swift polling pointers bound into script frames.

That is hard to refactor and type, and it pushed requirements onto the runtime that a better split never needs.

**What is actually used most:**
- embeds (web UI, mini-apps);
- local scripting for agents and text tools;
- SSH and local shell;
- engram memory, which runs automatically.

## 2. Direction

### 2.1 Nodes and clients

- **One Rust node** holds the user's data, runs agents and serves clients. It runs as a daemon on macOS and Linux, behind the CLI and TUI, and in-process on iOS.
- **Every node is a full node, the iPhone included.** It holds the data it needs, works offline, and can start, steer, approve and run work. Local-first is a requirement.
- **Clients** (the iOS app in SwiftUI, a desktop app, CLI, TUI) are views and controllers of a node, local or remote. They are not script runtimes: no UI built from shards, and no Swift shards.
- **One binary** (decided 2026-10-11) on macOS and Linux: `stitch` is the node, the CLI and the TUI. `stitch node` runs the node in the foreground (under launchd or systemd, the daemon). Every other command finds the node of its data directory (a lock file and a Unix socket there) and talks to it as a client; when none runs, it starts one in the background, so a run outlives the terminal that started it. One node per data directory: a second process is always a client, so the store and the index never have two owners. Reads (listing, status) may also open the store and index directly; SQLite and exclusive file creation make that safe. On iOS the app links the same node library and calls the same API in-process. A node of another version is never talked to past `Hello`, whose place and shape in the protocol are fixed (decided 2026-10-12): it says what runs, and a node that a newer client greets stops at once when idle, or once idle, so the client (or the next one) starts its own binary; an older client changes nothing. Within a protocol version, a **build** (the commits of Stitch, Otter and engram, `stitch --version`, `stitch node status`) says which code runs; the node watches the binary it was started from and restarts into another build installed there once idle, and `stitch node restart` restarts it, the successor started by the node's own process (or its supervisor), never by the asking client, so not inside a thread's sandbox (decided 2026-10-28, [self-improve.md](self-improve.md) §2.1). Busy means a chat or loop at work, a command running in a shell, or a tool call run for another node (asked, or queued and taken, §2.14); a detached thread waiting on an approval or on `wait` is not, since it resumes when the node starts again. A new build's restart holds for commands in shells at most `STITCH_DRAIN_SECS` (30 minutes), then cuts them (logged; each thread whose shells were cut is told once the node is back, §2.2, decided 2026-11-08, issue nqxw), threads at work, chats and calls for other nodes still holding it; `stitch node status` says what it waits on and how long shells still hold it (decided 2026-10-31, issue ubbm: a dev server left in a thread's shell kept gamer on its old build for hours, the canary never starting). The node finds a newer build installed by itself, whatever process was told: its watch compares the build installed with its own from its first looks, and takes its binary's path without Linux's ` (deleted)`; a new build's restart still waiting after an hour is told to the user once (which build, what keeps the node busy, `stitch node restart --now`), never cut for threads at work, and `node status`/`node builds` say since when it waits; a client finding no node of a service-run data directory waits for the service's (30 s) before it starts one of its own (decided 2026-11-03, after gamer ran its old build for 9 hours: the service's node had waited behind a node a client started outside it, and missed the install; self-improve.md §3, 2).
- **The TUI reads the store off its event loop, and only when something changed** (decided 2026-10-22, after typing lagged with a hiccup every two seconds on a long thread, and every idle TUI took ~5% CPU, 23% for a debug build). Every two seconds the event loop itself re-read the thread list, the attention view, every report and the open thread's activity, each read first walking every record root to index what was new (three walks a tick); measured on a copy of gamer's data (326 threads, a 1,687-entry thread open, release build), a tick took 90 ms (roots walk 8 ms each, the thread list 29, the attention view 37: one query per question of 465, reports 13, the open segment 8), and the keys typed meanwhile waited; a key and its redraw take 60 µs. Now a poller thread, with its own connection to the index, does the timer's reads and sends what it read to the loop (`UiEvent::Polled`; an answer for a view since left is dropped and asked again), and reads only when something changed: the index's SQLite `data_version` (the node commits all it writes and syncs), the open thread's open segment's time (a turn's steps, not indexed till sealed), or the view asked about (scope, thread, what was shown, whether it follows); a check is 60 µs. It walks the roots for files nothing indexed every 30 s instead of on each read, and `Index::refresh` writes nothing when it finds nothing new (it always upserted the root, so every reader's `data_version` moved). The attention view's open questions are one query (37 ms to 0.8 ms). After: an idle TUI on that thread uses 0.07% CPU (4.45% before), a tick that reads (something changed) takes 26 ms on the poller, never on the input path. `bench_tui` (ignored; `STITCH_BENCH_DATA`, `STITCH_BENCH_THREAD`) times these against a copy of a data directory. Later: the node's own notifications (a feed of what changed) in place of polling.
- **The local client protocol** is internal: the same binary on both ends, postcard frames over the socket, a version check on connect. Remote clients (driving one node from another's CLI) come later, as another iroh protocol with the node trust model.

### 2.2 Rust core, Otter for agents and tools

To decide where something goes, ask two questions:

- **Do I want to change this while it runs, or let a model write it?** Then it goes in **Otter**:
  - agent definitions and loop policy;
  - tools and text tools;
  - memory policy (when to observe, what to inject);
  - embed and mini-app logic.
- **Must it be correct, fast and stable across versions?** Then it goes in **Rust**:
  - the store and its format, and sync;
  - the node protocol;
  - provider adapters and streaming, written by hand over HTTP (reqwest) and server-sent events, no provider SDKs or agent frameworks. Anthropic Messages, OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions (llama.cpp, Ollama, OpenRouter and others), Responses (OpenAI/Codex and xAI), and Gemini's native API (§2.8, Gemini); built in besides: Kimi on the Anthropic adapter, DeepSeek and OpenRouter on Chat Completions (§2.8);
  - provider credentials, in the OS keychain (a file readable by the owner only where there is none, such as a headless Linux box), never in records, so they never sync. Keychain entries belong to one data directory (decided 2026-10-12, after a test run overwrote the user's key): a second node on a machine, or a test, never reads or overwrites another's. On iOS and visionOS, one data directory per app, they are the data protection keychain's (milestone 4). A key is looked up in the data directory's file, then the environment, then the keychain, so a node that has none there never asks the keychain;
  - the run scheduler and permissions;
  - SSH and local shell sessions;
  - the embed web server;
  - the engram engine.

Engram follows this split: the crate is Rust, and its policy, Rust for now (§2.9), moves to Otter. Otter stays because it is what makes the app more than another agent harness:

- **Checked code:** LLM-written tools are composed and type-checked before they run.
- **Permissions from effects:** a tool's signature declares io, time, random and suspension, and grants follow per effect and per node. The first step is built (§2.17): a tool with no effects runs without asking.
- **Hot reload:** tools and agents change without restarting the node.
- **Clean concurrency:** cancellation and suspension come out clean in agent loops.

Agent loops are written in Otter over Rust primitives (a model stream, a tool call, spawning a worker).

### 2.3 Orchestration, not chat

The way the work is actually done:
- bounce between agents;
- spawn agents to do work;
- let them run, possibly for days;
- come back, check, and often do nothing.

That is supervising a team, not chatting. The `.agent-handoffs` workflow in the Otter repository is the working prototype: immutable records (reviews, resolutions, verifications), status derived from references, and agents that publish, address and verify each other's work.

**Stitch is the root** (decided 2026-10-11). The user talks to Stitch, and Stitch's own agent does the work or detaches threads to do it (below), watches them, answers them where it can, and brings the user what needs them. Its chat is the entry point; the CLI and TUI are ways to talk to it.

- **The root agent** is the chat loop (model, tool calls, results, until done) whose main tool is an **interactive shell** (decided 2026-10-11): sessions that keep their directory, environment and running programs, where programs can ask and be answered and full-screen programs are read from a terminal screen (`stitch-shell`). Git, tests, Claude Code, Codex and everything else installed come with it. Orchestration is `stitch` commands run in that shell (detach a thread, list and open threads, sync), the same commands the user runs. Asking the user is the root's reply. Tool calls and results are transcript events, so everything it does is a record.
- **Shells run under rules:** in a sandbox where the OS gives one, where only what reaches beyond it asks (below); otherwise read-only and allowlisted commands run, and anything else pauses the turn for the user's approval, a `question` record on the attention view, asked inline too in the CLI or TUI that runs the chat (below). Timeouts and output limits bound every command. Shell sessions are Rust (§2.2); the rules are configuration now and Otter policy later.
- **Shells are spawned, never forked** (decided 2026-10-12): the node is multithreaded and links system frameworks, and `fork` runs their atfork child handlers before any of Stitch's code. On macOS, Network.framework's crashed every child (SIGSEGV) once a node had run a while, so no shell started until a restart. `posix_spawn` runs nothing of the parent's in the child: the shell becomes a session leader with its terminal as stdio and is executed, or the spawn fails. The terminal becomes the controlling one by being opened as the new session's leader: on Linux by the spawn itself; macOS opens before `setsid`, so there `/bin/sh` opens it again and executes the shell in its place. On macOS the node spawns `stitch-shell-helper`, installed next to `stitch`: a small single-threaded process without the node's frameworks that forks the shell the classic way (`setsid`, `TIOCSCTTY`, exec) and reports its pid, then how it ended. The helper was added for bash's jobs now and then failing `setpgid` (EPERM, `bash: child setpgid (…): Operation not permitted` in the output), taken then for an effect of the trampoline; it is not, and the helper does not avoid it (found 2026-10-12): bash calls `setpgid` for a new job in both the shell and the job, and on macOS the two racing now and then fail the job's call. It shows through the helper too, and in plain C with `fork` and `setpgid` alone (24 in 16,000 racing, none with the parent's call first); the parent's call gave the job its group in every case looked at. Worth reporting to Apple; nothing in Stitch hides bash's message. The helper is the one `STITCH_SHELL_HELPER` names (a build of one's own, a test's), else the one next to `stitch`. Node and helper shake hands (decided 2026-10-12): the node passes the protocol it speaks, the helper says its own first and starts nothing for another, and a helper of another version is an error that says how to install a matching one, not a reason to go around it; the helper started without a protocol (by a node from before the handshake, still running after an upgrade) behaves as that node expects. Only when the helper is not there or cannot be spawned (said in the node's log) does a shell start through the trampoline: what the helper reports failed (a shell it could not execute, a terminal it could not take) is the error, and a missing directory is said as such before anything is spawned. A shell that dies before its first prompt is reported with its program, pid, signal and whatever it printed. Allocating its terminal is tried again, up to eight times with a pause from 1 ms doubling, when macOS fails `posix_openpt` with -6 (XNU's internal `EREDRIVEOPEN`, two opens racing for one PTY minor, not `ENXIO`; the next try succeeds); any other failure, or that one past the bound, is said with the call that failed and its raw errno.
- **The web tools** (decided 2026-10-11, as edge talk has them): `browse_web` reads a URL (a page as Markdown of its main content with its JavaScript run; a PDF as text by page, with page images where text is missing or pictures are large; images as images; spreadsheets and CSV as Markdown tables; other files saved with their path), `web_eval` runs the model's JavaScript in a loaded page, `search_web` searches (Brave or Perplexity, with a stored key). All node primitives in Rust, in attached and detached threads alike, independent of the shell's approvals:
  - **One binary:** Chromium is driven over the DevTools protocol (`chromiumoxide`); PDFs are read with `pdf-extract` and rendered with `hayro`, spreadsheets with `calamine`, HTML turned into Markdown with `htmd`. The browser is the only outside piece: an installed Chrome, Chromium, Edge or Brave, else Chrome for Testing's headless shell fetched by `stitch browser install` (never by a tool call). A first version on Node.js and Playwright was replaced for this.
  - **Fast and isolated:** the browser starts with the first call, is shared by the calls after it and stops when idle; each call gets a fresh browser context (no profile, cookies or storage, nothing shared between calls), with downloads denied and dialogs dismissed. A page is read once it has settled (a second after load and half a second without DOM changes, at most five) or when `wait_for` shows. The main document's response tells a page from a file, so a file is never fetched twice except when its URL hid what it was.
  - **Passing for a browser** (decided 2026-10-28, after Wowhead's CloudFront refused every call with a 403): the headless Chrome is started as one with a window would look to a site. Its user agent is the same build's without `HeadlessChrome` (read from the browser on its first launch, which is then started again under it; kept per executable); no `--enable-automation` (chromiumoxide's defaults are passed without it, so `navigator.webdriver` is false) and `--disable-blink-features=AutomationControlled`; a 1920×1080 screen (`--screen-info`; headless Chrome's is 800×600) around a 1280×987 window holding the 1280×900 page. The rest of the request (headers, TLS and HTTP/2 fingerprints) was measured to be a windowed Chrome's already. This gets through request-level bot rules (CloudFront, PerimeterX, Akamai: Wowhead, Zillow, Ticketmaster). It does not get through checks that run JavaScript in the page (DataDome, Cloudflare's challenge, Reddit's): measured, those pass a windowed Chrome (under Xvfb) and not a headless one with the same requests, and running windowed was declined (a display server to depend on, windows that land on the user's desktop under Wayland). Instead `browse_web` **says when it hit a wall** (a challenge's title, or a 403 or 429 with next to no content): `[Blocked: this is …]`, pointing to the user's own Chrome (§2.18). A page that **moves on by itself** while it settles (a check that passed, a script's redirect) is followed and read where it ends, with that page's address and status (before, the call failed with "Inspected target navigated or closed", Stack Overflow's every time). Lightpanda was considered and left: a non-Chrome engine and network stack is easier to tell from a browser, not harder, and it renders no screenshots.
  - **On the phone, in the app's WebKit** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user, who found the phone's agent handing every page to another machine): iOS and visionOS start no Chrome, so the app lends the node a browser of its own (`web::hosted`, the `PageHost` trait; `stitch-ffi`'s `WebPages`, `set_web_pages` before `Stitch.open`; `WebPages.swift`), and the phone offers `browse_web` and `web_eval` with the desktop's names, inputs and outputs. **The host is kept dumb, the node keeps the reading:** the app only opens a page, runs a script in it (`callAsyncJavaScript`, page world), tells where it is, screenshots it and closes it; settling, `wait_for`, the main content as Markdown (`htmd`, now on every target), bot walls, the model's code with its console and the file fallback are the node's, the same scripts Chrome runs (`web/read.rs`, shared by `page.rs`), so a page reads the same on both. Each page is a `WKWebView` with a non-persistent data store (nothing of the user's, nothing shared), desktop content mode (Safari's own Mac user agent: edge talk's lesson was that a faked Chrome agent trips bot checks), 1280×900 like the desktop's page (a viewport meta `width=device-width` said at document start, before the page's own, from a content world the page does not see: iOS lays out a page that says none 980 wide, which gave `innerWidth` 980), iOS 26's scroll-edge blur hidden (it smeared a screenshot's top), dialogs dismissed and new windows refused (an `alert()` left unanswered held the model's code until its timeout), media only on a tap, no window opening, no other app opened, downloads cancelled; it sits behind the app's own views in its window, so it renders and its timers run as a shown page's (out of a window WebKit throttles it) but is never seen or touched. A main document that is not a page (a PDF, an image, a download) is cancelled and fetched by the node, as on the desktop. A page that committed and stays ready for five seconds without finishing its load is read as it is (edge talk's lesson: a subresource that never ends). A script across a navigation is reported as one and run again in the next document; the model's code is inlined in the call (CSP-proof), its console captured by the node's wrapper (`[log] …`, `warn` as `[warning]` as Chrome says it), its errors with the line of its own code. Screenshots are one pixel a CSS pixel, as the desktop's; an element's is clipped to the page's first screen once scrolled into view (Chrome's can be taller). **Differences, by the platform:** pages render only while the app runs (iOS suspends WebKit in the background, so a turn in a background refresh may find them timing out); WebKit is not Chrome, so a site may serve it otherwise; the bot walls are the same. The user's own browser (§2.18, `browser_*`) stays desktop only: it is the user's Chrome, through its extension. `NSAllowsArbitraryLoadsInWebContent` lets pages over `http://` load as a desktop's do. **Tested:** `web/hosted_tests.rs` runs the desktop's live fixtures and assertions (`live_tests::reads_like_a_browser`) through the hosted path with a host made of Chrome's pages, plus a scripted host for what the node makes of each answer (navigations, timeouts, a selector that never shows, line numbers, files); `StitchUITests.test9lBrowsesTheWebHere` has the mock model call both tools on the simulator against fixtures it serves (a page its script renders with a screenshot, code with its console and the 1280×900 viewport, an alert that must not block, a PDF served without its name saying so).
  - **Bounded:** 30 seconds a call by default (at most 120), 50 MiB a download, 32,000 characters inline with the whole text saved for the shell, PDFs in ranges of at most 20 pages and 10 rendered pages a call, spreadsheet previews of 20 sheets, 200 rows and 50 columns. Downloads are kept a week in `<data>/downloads/`.
  - **Images** the tools return go into the blob store and the transcript as a tool output after the tool results, so providers see them and they replay and sync like attachments. Page content is untrusted: the prompt says so, and the tools never act on it.
- **Calendar, reminders and places** (decided 2026-11-10, with the user, for parity with edge talk's `check_events`, `overdue_reminders`, `add_reminder`, `complete_reminder`, `delete_reminder`, `add_calendar_event`, `delete_calendar_event` and `find_nearby`, "especially when used from iOS"): the device lends the node its EventKit and MapKit as the app lends WebKit (`personal::Host`, set before the node opens; `stitch-ffi`'s `PersonalData`, `set_personal_data`; `PersonalData.swift`), and the node offers, in two groups toggled like the others: **`calendar`**: `calendar_events` (a range, default today and the 7 days after; calendars by name or id; a text query; with the calendars' names, sources, the default and the read-only ones), `calendar_add_event` (title, start, end, all-day, location, notes, URL, calendar, alarms in minutes before), `calendar_delete_event` (`span` this or future, for a repeating event), `reminders` (incomplete, overdue or completed, in a due or completion range, lists by name or id, a query; with the lists), `reminder_add` (title, due, notes, list, priority), `reminder_complete` (done, or not done again), `reminder_delete`; **`maps`**: `find_nearby` (Apple Maps' local search around the device's location, or around a place named in `near`, a radius, nearest first with address, phone, website, category, distance and coordinates). **The radius holds** (decided 2026-11-10, after “pharmacy” in the user's suburb answered only the city centre's, 6.6–7 km away, under the default 4 km: a search's region is only a hint to MapKit): the device searches with the region required (`regionPriority = .required`), and for a query naming a category (`pharmacy`, `farmacia`, `ATM`, `supermarket`…, English and Italian: `personal::category`) filtered to it (`pointOfInterestFilter`) and joined by an `MKLocalPointsOfInterestRequest` of that category (its radius at most `maxRadius`, 2 km); the device returns all it found, and the node keeps the places within the radius (its distance, or measured from the coordinates), once each, nearest first, cut to `limit`, counting those found beyond; when none is within, it searches wider in steps (decided 2026-11-10, after “Apple Store” within 300 m found nothing within 900 m either, the one wider search then: rare places need more): at most three more searches (`personal::wider`; four `find_nearby` calls in all, each the device's two or three MapKit requests), each radius at least three times the last and the last 50 km (300 m: 1.7, 9.1, 50 km; 4 km: 12, 36, 50 km), stopping at the first that has something within its radius; the answer says those places are BEYOND the requested radius, at which radius they were found and the radii that found nothing (“nothing for “Apple Store” within the requested 300 m, nor within 1.7 km or 9.1 km. Widened to 50 km: this place is BEYOND the requested 300 m radius”), or every radius tried when none did. **Calendars and lists with one name** (decided 2026-11-10, after two “Work (iCloud)” calendars, which `calendar` could not tell apart): the device names them by `calendarIdentifier` (`calendar_ids`, `calendar_id`; a `calendars` operation lists them), and the node resolves what the model gives: a name no other shares, the id, its short id (its first 8 characters, more where needed) or the line as listed; listings show the short id beside the names that repeat (`Work (iCloud, id 1A2B3C4D)`, and in each event's or reminder's brackets), and so does every other answer naming one (decided 2026-11-10, after adds and deletes said a bare `[Work]`): what an add, a tick or a delete answers and the delete's question to the user (the node reads the calendars, or the lists, for it; when it cannot, the bare name), and a shared name is refused with every candidate and its id, never picked silently; a read-only calendar is refused before adding. A reminder's completion (`completed_at`) is when it was ticked (`completionDate`, set to now as it is ticked; checked by the UI test against its due date). **The host is kept dumb, the node keeps the tools:** schemas, checking, the asking and the text the model reads are Rust (`personal.rs`); the app runs one operation, JSON in and out. **Times** are what the model writes: a day (`2026-11-12`), a wall-clock time where the device is (`2026-11-12T15:00`) or an instant with an offset; the node checks them (jiff) and hands the device `{date}`, `{local}` or `{epoch}`, so the device's own calendar and time zone place them (the node keeps no time zone of the phone's); answers come back with the device's offset, all-day ones as days, the last inclusive. **Identifiers are EventKit's**, given with every item listed so the agent acts on what it saw: a reminder's `calendarItemIdentifier`, an event's `eventIdentifier`, with `#<start, epoch seconds>` for an occurrence of a repeating one (they share the identifier). **What asks:** reads run; adding an event or a reminder and ticking one run (easily undone, as edge talk's did), refused to a read-only thread; **deletes ask the user**, naming what goes (the node reads the item first: “delete the event “Dentist” 2026-11-12 15:00–16:00 (+01:00), and every later occurrence”), yolo allows them, a read-only thread is refused. App threads (§2.22) get none of them (their guard lists what they run). **Permissions** are the system's, asked lazily the first time a tool needs them: full access to events and to reminders (iOS 17's `requestFullAccessTo…`, `NSCalendarsFullAccessUsageDescription`, `NSRemindersFullAccessUsageDescription`; a write-only grant is asked to be raised), the location while in use for `find_nearby` without `near` (`CLServiceSession` and `CLLocationUpdate`, `NSLocationWhenInUseUsageDescription`); a denial is the tool's error, saying where in Settings to turn it on (and, for the location, to give `near`). The location is read only with the app in front; a turn in a background refresh is told so. **Where:** iOS and visionOS. A node with no host offers none of them (desktops, Linux; `/tools` shows the groups unavailable there), so a desktop thread hands such work to the phone (`spawn`). **macOS, deferred:** the desktop node is a bare binary under launchd (`~/.cargo/bin/stitch`), not an app, and macOS's privacy grants (TCC) for calendars, reminders and location are kept per signed code: an ad-hoc signed binary replaced by each update would lose them (or never be asked, with no `Info.plist`). The path is a small helper app in a bundle signed with the team's identity (its grants survive updates), started by the node and answering the same operations (the `Host` protocol over its stdin and stdout, or XPC), reusing `PersonalData.swift` (EventKit, MapKit and CoreLocation are the same APIs there). **Tested:** `personal.rs` (times, every tool defined and grouped, calls checked and normalized, the radius kept, nearest first, the wider searches in steps up to 50 km, bounded, labelled beyond it, categories named, calendars of one name told apart in listings, confirmations and the delete question, and refused when ambiguous, the asking with a scripted device: a delete declined, allowed, with nobody to ask, under yolo; adds without asking; a read-only thread refused every change; the device's refusal as the tool's error; the answers as the model reads them; no host, no tools); `StitchUITests.test9oCalendarAndRemindersHere` has the mock model add an event and list it at its local time, add a reminder, tick it and list it done, on the simulator's EventKit (permissions given by `simctl privacy`).
- **Seeing and asking** (decided 2026-10-12, from edge talk): `view_file` shows local files through the same readers as `browse_web`'s downloads, and lists a directory (decided 2026-10-14: sorted, directories ending in `/`, sizes, symlinks marked and never followed, at most 1000 entries and 30,000 characters with the rest counted (up to 100,000), its own entries unless `depth` asks for up to 5 levels), since a node with no shell (iOS) has no other way to look around; every image sent to a model carries a short ID (`img:` and the start of its blob hash) and `zoom_image` crops a region from the stored original. **Image size** (decided 2026-10-18, after one screenshot over 3000 px made every request of a thread fail, for good, with Anthropic's "exceed max allowed size for many-image requests: 3000 pixels": above 20 image and document blocks in a request, every image must fit a stricter limit, 2000 px in its documentation): no image reaches a model over 2000 px on its long side (`images::MAX_SIDE`, under every provider's limits: Anthropic's 8000 and many-image limit, OpenAI's, which scales larger ones itself), in three layers. (1) Where it enters: a tool's image (`view_file`, `zoom_image`, page screenshots, MCP results, remote calls: all go through the loop's `ToolOutcome.images`) is stored scaled down, aspect kept, its original stored beside it (referenced by the tool result, so it syncs) for `zoom_image`, and the result says so (`3000×1000 scaled to 2000×667 (×0.67)`, the original's ID); a crop is at most 2000 px. The user's attachments stay as sent (the app shows them). (2) When sent: every image of the history, whatever provider, is fitted to 2000 px and a few MB (cached per blob), its ID line saying how it was scaled, so older records with larger images (and attachments) work as they are. (3) A 400 naming a many-image limit (`… many-image requests: N pixels`) has every image of the request fitted to N and is sent again, once per step. Not handled: the 32 MB request limit, for a thread with very many images. Asking another model is spawning a thread on it, without tools (§2.7; `ask_model` was the first form, a blocking call). `browse_web` can return a screenshot of the page or of one element. Edge talk's other tools are covered by the shell, superseded (tasks, the reasoning tool), written in Otter (its Google Flights tool, Stitch's built-in `google-flights` skill since 2026-10-16, §2.17; its `shards_search`, `shards_help`, `shards_check` and `run_shards`, rewritten over Otter's Rust API as `otter_search`, `otter_describe`, `otter_check` and `otter_run`, Stitch's `otter` skill since 2026-10-17, §2.17), browser control (§2.18, decided 2026-10-17), rebuilt in Rust (its `generate_image` and `edit_image`, one `generate_image` since 2026-10-18, §2.8, Images), or later work: search across past conversations, desktop control. Its calendar, reminders and `find_nearby` are the phone's (Calendar, reminders and places, above, since 2026-11-10).
- **Its context** is in its system prompt (its instructions and the node's facts, fixed for the thread) and in what it can look up: the threads running, what waits on the user. So "how is the sync fix going?" needs no names.
- **The time** (decided and built 2026-10-31, with the user, who noticed nothing told the model the date or the time: a thread continued days later did not know it, and "yesterday", schedules and `wake_at` times were guesses). Two parts, both kept off the prompt cache's way: **the system prompt says when the thread started** (`# The time`, `prompt::time_section`: "This thread started on Saturday 2026-10-31 at 07:45 CET (Europe/Rome)", the node's zone), fixed with the prompt as the rest of it is; and **a message after a pause starts with a note of the time** (`chat::time_note`: `[Sat 2026-10-31 14:02 CET: 5 hours since the previous message]`), when it comes an hour or more after the message before it, or on another local day (so "today" stays right across midnight). The note goes on messages that come from outside (the user's, reports, notes, wake-ups, messages woven in, Stitch's wrap-up), never on the model's replies or the tools' images; a compacted thread's summary does not count as a message for the pause. **Derived, not written:** the note is made from the messages' own times (`at`, in milliseconds as stored) each time the history is built, and the live turn makes it from the same time it writes, so a step and the next read alike and the cache holds; the message's text stays the user's (clients do not show the note), and threads from before get their notes too. *Why not a clock in the prompt:* the prompt is the cache's prefix (§2.8), so a time there breaks it at every step; the instructions' context (`# Where this turn runs`) is said again whole when it changes, so a date there would repeat all of it each day. *Why not a stamp on every message:* noise, and models take it too seriously (greetings by the hour, comments on the gap); the prompt says the notes are for reference, to mention the time only when it matters. Within an hour, the shell's `date`. *Open:* the zone is the node's; a user's zone (a phone's thread running on gamer while the user travels) would be a setting.
- **Instruction files and skills** (decided 2026-10-11): a thread's prompt also holds the `AGENTS.md` files that apply where it works: the home workspace's (the user's own, on that node: a record root syncs records and segments, not files beside them), then the user's own instructions as records (below), then its place's from the top of the checkout or folder down to where it started (`CLAUDE.md` where a directory has no `AGENTS.md`, with its `@file` imports inside the checkout). Skills are `skills/<name>/SKILL.md` in the home workspace: the prompt lists their names and descriptions, and the agent reads one with its shell when a task fits it. User-level files and skills of other agents (`~/.claude`, `~/.codex`) are not read, since they name tools Stitch lacks. The prompt stays as the thread started (§2.4); when the files change, the next message carries them as a `context` part. A place has skills of its own too, `<place>/.stitch/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`, listed only for its threads, and a thread can turn skills off and on (§2.14).
- **Your own instructions** (decided 2026-10-16, with the user): for every thread, and for a place (a project, a folder), written from any node, the phone included (Settings, a chat's menu; `stitch instructions [--global] [--edit|--set <file>]`), and told to a thread after the home workspace's `AGENTS.md` and before the place's own files: what a user wants of agents in a project without committing it to the repository, which others share. They are `instructions` records (`place` = the place's key, the text as the body; an edit is a new record and the latest counts, so two edits at once keep the later), in the root where the place's threads live (a project's own records; the home workspace for the global ones and a folder's), so every node holding the place has them.
- **It is woken** when a thread it detached finishes or needs something: that thread posts to the one that started it, which answers it or tells the user.
- **Its policy is editable:** the prompt is `agents/root.md` in the home workspace (a default is compiled in), and the rules for what it runs alone and what it asks are the node's configuration (`[shell]`: `approval`, `allow`). The loop and its tools are Rust node primitives now; when Otter's native loop is ready (§2.8, milestone 5), the loop policy moves to Otter and the tools stay primitives that its scripts call.
- **Long work is detached:** the root does short things itself, and detaches a thread for work that takes long or should outlive the conversation, which then has its own transcript, budget and shell sessions.

**Detached threads and schedules** (decided 2026-10-11; they replace the earlier tasks, runs and worker contract):
- **Every agent is a Stitch thread.** A detached thread is the same agent loop, running on the node with no client attached: started with a goal by the user (`stitch chat --detach "…"`, `/spawn` in the TUI), by another thread (the `spawn` tool, §2.7), or by a schedule. It keeps going after the terminal closes.
- **Other coding agents are not a kind of worker** (§2.8): Stitch's own threads do the work; handing it to Claude Code or Codex is deprecated.
- **Shell environment and approvals** (decided 2026-10-12): the shells a chat starts have the environment of the client that chats (its `PATH`, SSH agent, credential helpers), not the long-running node's, which may have been started from elsewhere; detached threads have the node's. `stitch yolo on`, `/yolo`, runs every command without asking (`[shell] approval = "allow"`, saved) until it is turned off. *Revised 2026-11-14:* the modes are `ask | auto | yolo` (`stitch mode`, `/ask`, `/auto`, `/yolo`): `auto` (`approval = "auto"`) runs every command as yolo does, except what the hazard guard stops or cannot judge (§2.28).
- **What runs without asking** (decided 2026-10-13, with the approvals above: agents reach for `cd x && ls 2>/dev/null | head`, which the word-prefix check asked about, and the default `stitch` and `git branch`/`git remote` prefixes let an agent run `stitch yolo on`, answer its own questions, or delete a branch unasked). A command line is parsed as the shell would split it, minding quotes, into commands at `;`, `&&`, `||`, pipes and newlines; it runs when each is allowed and the line writes and runs nothing else: redirection only from files and to `/dev/null` or a descriptor, no substitution, subshell, group or here-document (substitutions, subshells, groups and loops whose commands are all allowed run since 2026-11-08: read-only threads, "The allowlist widened"). A command is allowed by its words' prefix (`VAR=value` in front taken off, `git -C <dir>` read as `git`), never in its writing forms (`find -exec`, `sort -o`, `git branch -d`, `rg --pre`, `--output`, `stitch thread tools … +x`); `cd`, `pushd`, `popd` only move. The defaults are read-only tools and read-only `git` and `stitch` subcommands (setup §4). The model is told the rules, as they stand, in `shell_run`'s description.
- **Always** (decided 2026-10-13): an approval a prefix can cover offers `always` beside yes and no; the question records what it grants (each command the rules did not allow, as its program, or program and subcommand for those that have them) and the thread's place, so an answer from any node grants on the node that asked: `[shell.places."<place>"] allow`, saved in its `config.toml`, read at each check, so it frees approvals waiting on the same thing at once. An MCP call offers it too (decided 2026-10-13): it grants the tool, unprefixed, of its server (`grant_server` on the question) in the thread's place, `[mcp.places."<place>".allow] <server> = ["<tool>"]`, kept and freeing waiting approvals the same way; that holds for a call another node runs for the thread, on that node. An Otter tool's call offers it as well (decided 2026-10-16, §2.17): it grants the tool by name (`grant_otter = true` on the question; `grant_shards` in one asked before the rename, which still works) in the place, `[otter.places."<place>"] allow = ["<tool>"]`. Grants stay per node, like the rest of the rules.
- **The sandbox** (decided 2026-10-15, with the user, after counting the approvals of the node's first days: 184 asked, 164 allowed, and the 20 declined were stale questions of cancelled threads, not harm caught; nushell was weighed and left: models write bash, and the danger is in the programs a shell runs, not in its syntax): the default approval mode is `sandbox` where shells can be sandboxed. A thread's sessions run confined by the OS, writing only in its place (a checkout with its git directory and every worktree of it, `<checkout>-<name>` beside it included, or its folder), temporary directories (and `just`'s `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/just`, not the rest of the runtime directory), the toolchains and build caches agents use (`~/.cargo`, `~/.rustup`, the target directory, `~/.cache`, Xcode's), the records of the node's data directory (not its configuration, keys or sign-ins), and `[shell] writable`; they read everywhere, reach the network, and signal only processes of their own sandbox. Inside, no command asks: a mistake can only spoil what git or the folder's history keeps. What reaches beyond the sandbox asks, as a parser reads the line (leniently where `commands` cannot vouch for it, so a push inside `$(…)`, `bash -c` or a script's here-document asks too; wrappers such as `time`, `env`, `timeout` and `xargs` taken off): pushing and publishing (`git push`, `gh` but its reading subcommands, `cargo`/`npm` `publish`), remote shells and uploads (`ssh`, `scp`, `rsync host:`, `curl -d`/`-X POST`), throwing away uncommitted work (`git reset --hard`, `git checkout -- …`, `git clean -f`, `git restore` of the worktree, `git stash drop`, `git branch -D`) or a whole tree (`rm -r` of `.`, `*`, home, a `.git`, or a directory of the place), and what has a program outside the sandbox act for it (`sudo`, `open`, `osascript`, `tmux` but its listings, `docker`/`podman` but theirs, `launchctl`, `systemctl`, `defaults write`, `security`, the acting `stitch` subcommands: `answer`, `yolo`, `key`, `auth`, `shell`, …). An `always` answer grants such a prefix (`git push`) in the place; the read-only defaults never do. `write_file` and `edit_file` write without asking wherever the sandbox would. A command that must write elsewhere runs with `outside: true`, in an unsandboxed session of its own (default `outside`), under the rules above; read-only threads keep theirs in either. **Not routed around** (decided 2026-10-16, issue #3, after a thread refused a write in `~/devel/otter` cloned it into `/tmp/otter-host`, built and committed there, and another thread took its Cargo dependencies from that clone): temporary directories being the one place that always writes made them the way around a refusal. Now `git clone` asks unless its destination is written inside the place (absolute or `~`, a `<checkout>-<name>` beside it included: a relative one or none may be wherever the session went), and `git worktree add` asks unless its path is inside the place, beside it, or relative with no way up; a sandboxed command whose output shows a write refused is told that it was outside the place, and to run it with `outside: true` or report it blocked, never to copy the project elsewhere; and the prompt and the rules the model reads say the same. Copying (`cp -r`) is left to that instruction: a parser cannot tell a project's copy from any other. Where shells cannot be sandboxed (iOS, a Linux kernel without Landlock, no shell helper), `sandbox` asks as `ask` does, said once in the log; `stitch yolo off` returns to the default. **A session follows the mode** (decided 2026-10-17, after yolo left a thread's `main` session confined: the loop, rebuilt without a sandbox, no longer offered `outside`, and nothing asked): a session started in the sandbox that a loop without one (yolo, `ask`) runs a command in starts again outside it, and one started outside it under yolo starts again in it once the sandbox is back, unless it was started `outside` on purpose; the result says so ("restarted outside it: fresh directory and environment"). One with a program running in it is not restarted under it: the command is refused, saying to stop that program or use another session.
  - **macOS:** the shell is executed by `sandbox-exec` with a Seatbelt profile: everything allowed but writing outside those directories (`/dev`'s terminals, null and random devices aside) and signalling outside the sandbox; worktrees made later match `^<checkout>-[^/]+`. macOS applies no sandbox inside another, and Swift's tools make their own (SwiftPM for package manifests and plugins, the compiler for macros), so a sandboxed session has an `xcodebuild` and a `swift` first on its PATH that turn theirs off (`-IDEPackageSupportDisableManifestSandbox`, `-IDEPackageSupportDisablePluginExecutionSandbox`, `--disable-sandbox`), and `XCODE_XCCONFIG_FILE` gives the compiler `-disable-sandbox`; the app then builds from scratch inside.
  - **Linux:** the shell helper restricts itself with Landlock (write rights of ABI 6, never ABI 9's `ResolveUnix`, so the node's socket stays reachable) and executes the shell in its place; signals are confined from Linux 6.12 (ABI 6), and on older kernels `kill`, `pkill` and `killall` ask. Worktrees there when the shell starts are listed; one made later is writable from the next session.
- **Read-only threads** (decided 2026-10-13): a thread with `approval = "read-only"` (`spawn`'s `read_only`, `--read-only`) is refused at once what the rules do not allow, commands and MCP calls alike, yolo or not, and never asks; what it spawns is read-only too, whatever it asks for. Survey work then never blocks on the user, and a thread cannot loosen what spawned it.
  - **Enforced by the OS, not only by the rules** (decided 2026-10-17, after a read-only thread on mbp ran `git worktree add ../stitch-global-dir` and then `claude -p --permission-mode acceptEdits`, which edited that worktree: both were on the place's `allow` list, and its sandbox was a writable thread's). A read-only thread's shells run in a sandbox that writes only temporary directories (`std::env::temp_dir()` and its macOS parent, `/tmp`, `/var/tmp`; `sandbox::sandbox(…, read_only)`): not its place, git directories or `<checkout>-*` siblings, `[shell] writable`, toolchains and package caches (`~/.cargo`, `target/`: `cargo check` and `cargo test` do not run there; a survey reports, builds are a writable thread's), `Library/Developer`, `Library/Caches` or the data directory. Reads go everywhere, as before. Its rules honour only the built-in read-only commands (`DEFAULT_ALLOW`), with their writing forms guarded, plus `[shell] read_only_allow` (what the user vouches for as reading only): `[shell] allow`, `[shell.places.*] allow` and "always" grants do not apply. Agents and writing commands are refused whatever a list says (`refused_read_only`): `claude` but with `--permission-mode plan`, `codex` and the like, `git commit|push|checkout|reset|worktree add|…`, `just|cargo|npm … install|publish`, `rm`, `cp`, `touch` and the like. `write_file`/`edit_file` are refused (`Editing`), Otter runs go ahead only effect-free, MCP calls only as their server's `allow` says, as before. **Where there is no sandbox** (`ask` mode, Linux without Landlock), the rules are the only guard: a command they let run (a built-in read-only one, or one of `read_only_allow`) is trusted to read only.
  - **In its sandbox, a denylist** (decided 2026-11-08, with the user, after counting beast's read-only refusals: of 304 distinct ones, most were shell syntax or harmless tools: `sed -n a,bp` 76, `for` loops 37, `awk 'NR>=…'` 22, `$(…)` 18, `xargs`/subshells/`export`/`P=x; $P` about 15, and reading tools not on the list (`journalctl`, `pgrep`, `nvidia-smi`, `free`, `lsblk`, `ip -brief`, `ss`, `getent`, `strings`, `unzip -l`, `pdftotext -`, `cargo --version`, `gh pr view`, `gh auth status`, …) about 60; the real side effects among them (`git fetch`, `git checkout -b`, `just worktree`, `ssh`, `sudo`, `stitch yolo`) were rightly refused). "Read-only" means no changes on disk, which the read-only sandbox enforces, and none of the known acting channels, not that no code runs. So a read-only thread's command in a session started in its read-only sandbox (`Rules::allows_read_only_sandboxed`, through `allows_sandboxed`; `approve` no longer turns a read-only thread's sandbox off, and `shell_send` counts a session as such only when it was started read-only) runs unless a command in it, read with its loops, substitutions, subshells and redirections (`parse` with `SANDBOXED`; a line it cannot read, a here-document, leniently, where any run of its words that acts counts), is refused, never asked: what the writable sandbox's `beyond` asks for (pushing and publishing, `ssh`/`scp`/`rsync host:`, `curl -d`/`-X POST`, `gh` but its reading subcommands, `sudo`, `tmux`/`docker`/`podman`/`systemctl`/`launchctl` but their listings, acting `stitch` subcommands, discarding work, clones and worktrees outside the place); `refused_read_only` (agents, git's changing subcommands, install/publish, the file tools that write, and the tools' writing forms); git but its reading subcommands (`GIT_READS`: `git fetch` is refused, and so is an alias, which could push; `git -c` too); `stitch` but the read-only list's subcommands; what acts through a daemon, the desktop, a device, a cloud account or mail, or runs what it is given (`acts_elsewhere`: `docker` but its listings, `xdg-open`, `dbus-send`, `nmcli`, `xcrun simctl|devicectl`, `kubectl`, `aws`, `wrangler`, `rclone`, `sendmail`, `parallel`, `strace`, `alias`, `just|make worktree|deploy|release|…`, `curl --unix-socket`, `gh repo clone`, …); a command whose name is computed (`$P`, `$(…)`, a word with spaces through `env -S`), `eval` and `source`; a shell reading its commands from its input (`… | bash`, `bash -s`; `bash -c` is read for what it runs); and what `xargs` runs unless the read-only list allows it. The project's own programs and interpreters run (`./target/release/x`, `python3 -c`, `node --test`, `npx vitest`; `cargo build` too, failing to write `target/`). **Residual, accepted:** a program that runs can use the network and local sockets with the user's credentials (a script could push, call an API, or drive a local server such as a viewer's websocket), which a parser cannot see; closing that needs the fixers' credential isolation (docs/self-improve.md §5), not more rules. A read-only session that is not sandboxed (`outside`, an SSH host, a node without a sandbox, yolo) keeps the allowlist; `describe()` tells the model the rules of each, the sandbox's and then the stricter ones. Checked against the 304 refusals: 280 now run in the sandbox and 240 unsandboxed; those still refused there are `git fetch`, `git checkout -b`, `just worktree`, `ssh`, `sudo`, `stitch yolo|model|browser`, a program named through `$P`/`$t`, and `sed` with no command.
  - **The allowlist widened** (decided 2026-11-08, the same count; it holds for every unsandboxed session, read-only or not): `allows` reads a line with `parse(…, COMPOUND)`, so `for`/`while`/`if` blocks, `$(…)`, backticks, `<(…)`, `$((…))`, subshells, `{ …; }` groups and here-strings run when every command in them is allowed (writing redirections, `>(…)` and here-documents still do not); keywords (`do`, `then`, `!`, …) are taken off a command everywhere, which also closes a gap of the writable sandbox, where `for x in a; do git push; done` ran unasked (`do` was read as the program); `xargs`, `timeout`, `env`, `time` and the other wrappers run an allowed command (`nohup` not: it writes `nohup.out`); `read`, `export`, `set` and the like run. Added to `DEFAULT_ALLOW`, with their writing forms guarded in `writes`: `sed` (printing forms only: `sed_prints` parses the scripts and refuses `-i`, `-f`, `w`/`W`/`e`/`r` and `s///w`/`e`), `awk`/`gawk` (`awk_prints`: no `-f`/`-i`/`-l`, no `system`, `getline`, `@load`, nor a `>` or `|` at a `print`'s top level), `journalctl`, `pgrep`, `nvidia-smi`, `free`, `lsblk`, `ip` (show forms), `ss` (not `-K`), `getent`, `strings`, `unzip -l|-t|-p|-Z`, `pdftotext … -`, `systemctl` listings, `--version` of the toolchains, `docker`/`podman` listings, `gh` reading subcommands (not `--web`, `--show-token`), and more reading `git` subcommands (`for-each-ref`, `ls-tree`, `cat-file`, `merge-base`, `ls-remote`, `reflog`, `config --get`, …). `view_file` takes `start_line` and `lines` (numbered lines of a text file, streamed, within its 32,000 characters, saying where to go on), so reading part of a file needs no `sed`. *Open:* a program named through a variable set on the same line (`P=x; $P`) stays refused: what a variable holds depends on what ran before in a persistent session, which a line's parser cannot know.
  - **The user switches it, agents cannot** (decided 2026-10-17). The mode is a `set` record (docs/records.md §6) on the thread, `field = "approval"`, `value = "read-only" | "default"`, authored by the user (`Author::user`; one an agent's run or a node wrote is ignored: `Index::current_user_value`), and it syncs like any record. The effective mode (`meta::read_only`, through `Shared::read_only`, which the tools, `spawn`'s inheritance, `threads`, `stitch.live` and the app read) is the latest such switch, else the thread record's `approval` as created. No tool switches it, and `spawn`/`send` cannot; `stitch thread <id> --read-only|--writable` is a writing form of `stitch thread` (so a thread's shell asks for it, and a read-only thread is refused it). The user switches it with `stitch thread <id> --read-only|--writable` (`Request::ReadOnly`, which prints the mode), `/read-only on|off` in the TUI (its status line says `read-only`), or the app's Read-only toggle in a chat's menu (`set_read_only`; a badge under the title, a lock in the list). An agent in a read-only thread can only suggest it in its reply, as its rules tell it.
  - **When it takes effect.** Writable takes effect at the next turn (tools are built per turn in `shell_tools`). Read-only takes effect at the next tool call of a running turn: the rules, edits, MCP and Otter calls read the thread's mode live (`Granted::read_only`, through `Live`), sessions are started in the read-only sandbox from then on, and the thread's sessions on the switching node close at once (what runs in them ends: an agent started there stops writing); on another node, a sync closes the writable sessions of threads now read-only (`close_switched`), and a session started writable is never used by a read-only thread (it is closed and started again). Rebuilding the running turn's rules was chosen over cancelling the turn: the turn goes on, reading only. A session started read-only is started again writable at the next turn once idle. The model is told at its next turn, with its instructions (the same "instructions changed" note): "This thread is now writable (switched by the user)" or "now read-only", and `shell_run`'s description of the rules is the mode's.
  - **Inheritance.** What a thread spawns after it was switched to writable may be writable; children already spawned keep their own mode, each switched on its own. Switching to read-only does not reach the children (open: whether it should). `spawn`'s result says when the child is read-only because its parent is ("read-only (inherited from this thread …)"), which the agent of 2026-10-17 learned only from the user's screenshot. A thread assigned to another node starts with the mode its assignment says.
  - **Known limit:** a writable thread's sandbox writes the data directory's record repositories (`home`, `projects`), so a command there could forge a user-authored `set`; the same holds for every record the user writes, and needs signed authorship to close.
- **Open: a model's judgement on approvals.** A classifier over EmbeddingGemma 2 embeddings is not a gate: commands that differ in their effect (`rm -rf ./target`, `rm -rf ~`; `git branch -a`, `git branch -D main`) sit close, and a threshold fails silently on exactly those. Uses that may come: proposing an `always` grant from the user's own repeated answers in a place (counted by prefix, embeddings to group variants); flagging a question as likely destructive on the attention list, never approving; and, for auto-approval, a small model judging a command with its thread's context under a written policy, after the parser, as Claude Code's auto mode does.
- **Steering a running loop** (decided 2026-10-12): messages the user sends while a chat's loop runs go to the node on the chat's own connection and are woven in after the current step's tool results, before the next model call, as user messages written in the step's place; the client keeps each until the node says it was woven, sends what was not taken as the next message, and can take one back before it is. Interrupting stops the model mid-reply (what it wrote is not kept) or the tools mid-call (the command a call entered gets Ctrl+C, in whichever session the call ran it, as it does when the loop is aborted, and again while that same command runs, up to three times with two seconds each for its prompt: one that lands while bash forks a list's next command is taken by bash; a command entered before or after the call's is not touched, and one still running after that is said in the node's log; that call and the ones after it get "interrupted by the user" as results, so the transcript stays whole), and the loop ends with `interrupted`. Detached threads are steered with `send` and stopped with `cancel` (§2.7): a plain `send` waits for the running turn to end, `send` with `interject` is woven into it as typing is (§2.7). A chat sent to a thread on which another turn runs (one a job's note or a report woke, another client's) waits for it on the thread's lock, and the node says so at once (`Waiting`, decided 2026-10-13, after a message sat on "sending" with nothing shown): the TUI then shows that it waits, follows the running turn as it is written, and queues what is typed meanwhile, as while its own turn runs. Interrupting while it waits ends the chat with its message unsent (`Unsent`, decided 2026-10-15, after Esc there did nothing until the other turn ended): the turn that runs goes on, and the message, its attachments and what was queued come back to the input. **So does the client leaving while it waits** (decided 2026-10-21, after a TUI quit while it waited had its message sent when the other turn ended, to no one): its connection closing ends the waiting chat unsent; a turn that already began still goes on to its end when its client leaves. The TUI opening another thread while its message waits keeps that connection (it said the turn goes on, and closing it took the message back): what it had queued is taken back and said not sent. **While it waits, the message goes into the turn that runs** (decided 2026-10-21, after four messages queued behind a turn a job's note woke were never seen by it, the status line saying they would be woven in): when a loop runs on the thread here, the waiting chat relays its message to that loop's steer, and what its client queues meanwhile (`Steer::relay`), woven in after its next tool results as that loop's own client's typing is, written as the user's; each one taken is said to the client (`Woven`, the message itself as ID 0), which leaves it out of its queue (the turn it follows shows it). One whose message was taken ends when that turn does, with no turn of its own (`ChatDone` with stop `woven`, no usage): what that turn did not take was never said woven, and the client sends it after. One whose message that turn ended without taking sends it as its own turn, as before, with the queued ones it had back. Interrupting while waiting takes back what was not taken; when the message was taken, it interrupts the turn it went into, and the chat ends with it. A loop that starts on the thread after the chat began waiting is relayed to as well. While a turn is pending the TUI shows a spinner line with what it does (sending, a tool call and its command, thinking, writing, waiting) and for how long.
- **A step's calls are seen while they run** (decided 2026-11-01, with the user, after a report's turn spent 13 minutes in one `shell_run` (git push, fly deploy, a release build) and the TUI following the thread showed only that a turn was running: no command, nothing moving, so it looked stuck). A step is still written whole, once its calls returned (the segment format is unchanged); what clients follow meanwhile is the step the loop keeps for replay (`pending-step:<thread>`, §2.6), which now also says the call that runs and since when (`running`, written as each call starts and dropped however it ends: its result, an error, the loop cancelled) and the process that runs it (`pid`), so a step a stopped or crashed node left behind is not shown. `chat::step_in_flight` reads it: its calls (name, whole input, `call_summary`'s line, the ones done with their outcome), the one running and since when. `Request::InFlight { thread }` answers it (`Reply::Lines`, the step as JSON, or none), from this node's loops, else asked of the node that runs the thread (`Question::InFlight`; asked again after 8 s when it said none, a minute when not reached, at once while a call runs there). The TUI asks it every two seconds for the open thread when no turn of its own runs on it (its own turn shows its calls from its stream, as before) and shows, in the live area above the input, the call's input whole (a command on its lines, eight at most) and under it a spinner, `running shell_run in `deploy` on devbox-1 · 3m12s` (`· call 2 of 3` in a step of several), the same line its own turn's spinner shows; a message sent meanwhile waits for that turn with that call shown above its waiting line. When the step is written the transcript has it and the line goes. **The app** (`Stitch::in_flight`, `ChatModel.inFlight`, read every two seconds while no turn of its own runs on the chat) shows it under the transcript as a turn's own steps show theirs: the command whole (twelve lines at most, selectable), then a spinner and the same line, ticking (`InFlightCall`; fixture `inflight`). **The dashboard** (version 6): `stitch.live.threads` rows carry `in_flight` (name, headline, summary, since) for a call a loop runs here, a call starting moves the threads' mark, and a working thread's row shows the command and a spinner with its time, ticking, in place of its last words; threads running on another node show theirs once their node's dashboard is read (open). *Not yet:* the model's own streaming (thinking, writing) of a turn a client did not start, which stays live only for the client that started it and for followers through the live feed (`Question::Follow`).
- **The node's log** (decided 2026-10-27, after a report held 53 minutes and a start unconfirmed for six hours could not be placed in `node.log`, whose lines had no time): `stitch node` with its output not a terminal (launchd's, systemd's, `connect_or_start`'s file) sends stdout and stderr through a pipe to a thread that writes each line to that file with its local time first (`2026-10-27 10:13:05.123 sync: …`, `logstamp.rs`); a panic is written there directly too, with its time, so one that ends the process is not lost in the pipe. A terminal's output (`stitch node` run by hand) is left as it is. Lines another process appends (a second `stitch node` refusing to start) carry no time.
- **Background work tells its thread** (decided 2026-10-12): a shell command started with `background` (or watched with `shell_watch`, for its end or a line matching a pattern) is a job; when it ends or matches, the node tells the thread, with the last lines it printed. Started in a session of its own (none named: `bgN`), its session closes once it ended and the thread was told (or waited for it with `shell_wait`), unless something else runs or watches there: a thread that runs many background commands keeps no shell for each. Every loop running on a thread takes such notes, as it takes the user's messages: woven in after the current step's tool results. A thread with no loop running is woken with the note, and a note the loop did not take before it ended wakes the thread after it. Reports from spawned threads (§2.7) arrive the same way, except that those coming while the thread is idle are gathered a few seconds and wake it once, together, and those of work spawned or sent with `wake: false` are kept for the user and wake nothing (§2.7). `shell_wait` blocks on the same conditions when the model has nothing else to do. Jobs are live state on the node that runs them, not records: `stitch jobs` and the TUI's jobs list show them with the detached threads of every node (those are records), stop a command with Ctrl+C (closing its session when stopped again) and cancel a thread. Cancelling a thread ends its jobs, then writes its report, then stops every loop on it (one woken again included) and closes its sessions, without telling it; the loops are unlisted as `cancel` returns, not when their aborted tasks are next taken up, so a `threads` right after says it ended, not working (issue uc98: under load CI saw `working` there). Sessions and jobs do not outlive the node process, but the threads they cut are told (decided 2026-11-08, issue nqxw: gamer's restart into a new build cut a chat's four background jobs after the drain, and the thread learned it only from a log that stopped growing; `jobs` on the new node said none ran or ended): a node stopping or restarting for any reason, before it closes its shells, writes for each thread whose shells run something (its jobs, by ID, command and session, and the commands its sessions run with no job) a note to `<data>/cut-shells.json`, and the next start, once detached threads resume, tells each thread its note as a job's end is told (woven, or waking it) and removes the file, so it is told once: "[Stitch: the node restarted (why) at <time> and cut what ran in your shells: job 3 `cmd` in session "pt"; …; the sessions are gone: start again what you still need.]". A crash writes nothing. A stopping node starts no loop: what a loop it stops leaves untaken wakes nothing (the thread resumes at the next start, and its reports are delivered again then).
- **A detached thread's work ends with its turn** (decided 2026-10-12, after a thread stopped at its step limit kept editing and committing: its own jobs woke it, with a fresh budget and no way to cancel it). When it reports, its jobs end and its sessions close with what runs in them (`[detached] keep_shells = true` keeps the sessions), and its prompt tells it to wait for what it needs before its last message. A thread that reported is woken again by a report (the reply to a spawn or send of its own: it may have ended its turn counting on it) or by what is sent to it; once cancelled, only by what is sent to it. Notes it did not take (a job that fired during its last step, typing aside) wake nothing then. A loop woken again is listed like the first, so `cancel` stops it.
- **Jobs watch more than commands** (decided 2026-10-12): a job is any condition the node checks for a thread at no model cost, firing once into the same thread (woven, or woken): a session's command ending or printing a matching line; its screen showing a new match for a pattern (as below), no longer showing one (that it showed since the last input or during the watch: gone from a screen that never showed it is no news; shown and gone are told row by row, the echo of what was sent aside as for a new match, so a command naming the pattern neither shows it nor keeps it from going), and/or not changing for N seconds, all at once (`shell_watch`). Quiet ignores a line's spinner glyphs and ticking numbers only on a line a spinner animates (a spinner glyph its first non-blank character, or an ASCII frame `| / - \` and a space starting it unindented: a `·` between words or an indented `- ` list item is no spinner's), and keeps a percentage or a count of a total (`45%`, `3/10`) even there, so a spinner's elapsed time or token count is quiet and progress is not (revised 2026-10-13: a bullet or a `·` anywhere made a download's percentage quiet); a file changing, having a matching line appended, and/or not changing for N seconds (`watch_file`, polled every half second, reading only what was added, all of it however much; a file replaced (another inode: rotated, recreated), shrunk, or gone and back is read again from its start, one not there yet counts from when it appears; of a line not ended yet at most 1 MiB is kept, its end, and a match in it counts). `shell_wait` blocks on the same session conditions, and `shell_run`/`shell_send` return early when the screen shows a new match for a pattern or goes quiet the same way. A new match (decided 2026-10-12, for `shell_watch` and `shell_wait` too) is a screen row the pattern matches, anywhere in it, that is neither among the rows it matched before the last input (the screen as it was is taken before writing; count by count) nor the echo of what was sent: a run of rows (blank and decoration-only rows aside) whose letters and digits, joined, are a stretch of one of the last few texts sent (kept across the bare Enter that `submit` sends, each with where it was written), so the echo is told however the terminal wraps it or a program (an agent's input box) re-renders, indents or frames it. The echo is looked for where the text was written (revised 2026-10-13): from the cursor's line at the time, followed up as the screen scrolls (the lines above it found again), a run that is the whole text or long (spanning rows, unless it is the first), and the runs right after it (a program showing the text again); the first row that is not ends it, and what comes after is output, news whatever it repeats of the text (`./build: Error in step 2` after `./build`, a printed stretch of the command, lines `2` and `3` after `for i in 1 2 3`). Above that line (an input box and history redrawn there), or anywhere when the line cannot be found again (scrolled away, the screen redrawn), a run is echo when it spans rows, is the whole text, or is long, so an answer that is a word of the message (`STEP1-DONE` on its own) is news. The line typed at the prompt is echo, prompt and all, and only that line: dropping every row that held the command hid output naming it. Matching the echo by its first line only let a long message, wrapped, fire a watch on its own marker. These are the in-thread half of the event triggers planned for schedules (above): a trigger on a schedule starts a new detached thread; a job continues the thread that set it, on the node where it watches; both should share one condition engine when schedules are built.
- **The model lists, stops and reads its jobs** (decided 2026-10-17, with the user, after an orchestrating thread drove two threads and four Claude Code sessions for a day keeping jobs 1–8 in its head, with no way to stop a command but Ctrl+C typed into its session; built from the proposal that was `docs/jobs-spec.md`). Tools of the `shell` group, so not on a phone, which has no jobs:
  - **`jobs {all?}`**: the thread's jobs running (id, `shell` for a job on a command, else `watch`, its session and command or file, its condition with its time limit, when it started, the last 5 lines of its command's output), the commands its sessions run with no job on them, then the jobs that ended in the last hour, newest first, with how (exit status, `stopped`, `timed out`, `cancelled (unwatch)`, `had by shell_wait`). `all` lists every thread's on the node; listing asks nothing. Ended jobs are live state like the rest: the node keeps 200 at most, their output 16 MB in all (the oldest output goes first), and none of it survives a restart.
  - **`job_stop {job | session, signal?, grace_secs?, all?}`** stops for real. A job on a command (a background command, a `shell_watch` on one, also one that already fired on a line or timed out while its command runs on) stops that command: Ctrl+C (sent again up to three times within the grace), then, still running after the grace (default 5 seconds, at most 60), SIGTERM to the terminal's foreground process group, the command's job (bash gives each job its own; a build's compilers and test binaries are in it), then after the grace again SIGKILL. Each signal goes to every group the command runs in meanwhile, since an interactive bash runs a list's commands (`a; b`) as jobs of their own and goes on to the next when one is killed by anything but SIGINT; SIGKILL is given two seconds. Once the command ended, what is left in those groups gets SIGTERM, then SIGKILL a second later: Ctrl+C ends `just`, cargo and the test binary, and a child that ignores SIGINT stays behind, orphaned in the group (verified with a `just` recipe running `cargo test` whose test spawned such a child, and a script whose child, grandchild and itself ignore SIGINT, SIGTERM and SIGHUP: every process ended, `tests/root.rs`). A command the shell itself runs (a loop of builtins) that outlives all of it has its session closed. What leaves the group (`setsid`, `setpgid`, a daemon, a launchd service such as Xcode's build service) is not reached: no process-tree walk. `signal` starts at `term` or `kill` instead. The notice goes with it; the result says each step, how it ended and its last 20 lines. A watch on anything else is cancelled. `session` stops what runs in a session of the thread's with no job. Another thread's job takes `all`, and the user's approval (yolo allows it, a read-only thread is refused). The CLI's `stitch jobs stop` keeps its Ctrl+C, then closing the session.
  - **`job_output {job, lines?, from?}`**: a shell job's output, running or ended: read from its session while that command is still the session's last, else what was kept when it ended (up to 1 MB, its end). The last 200 lines by default, at most 2000 and 32,000 characters a call, with which lines of how many and where to go on. A session's log keeps a command's first 2,000 lines and its last 20,000.
  - **`max_secs`** on `shell_run` with `background`, `shell_watch` and `watch_file` (at most a week): when it passes first, the job fires as timed out with what was printed so far; with `stop_on_timeout` (a job on a command) the command is stopped as `job_stop` does, the note saying how.
  - **Notes** name the job, carry 40 lines at most, and when they are cut say `job_output` has the rest. `unwatch` says it cancels the notice only, and points to `job_stop`.
- **Sessions close with their command's group:** closing a session (`shell_close`, a thread's end or cancellation) sends SIGHUP to the shell, which passes it to its jobs, then SIGKILL to the shell's group and to the command's group, so a command that ignores SIGHUP no longer outlives its session.
- **A shell that does not start says why** (decided 2026-10-12): why its output ended (the end of it, its terminal closing, or which read error), how the shell ended (exit status or signal, or that it had not), and what it printed; the node's log has it too. A session never signals a shell it already reaped: its pid, and its group's, may be another process's by then.
- **A long command is sourced, not typed** (decided 2026-10-12): the default bash runs without line editing, so its terminal is in canonical mode, which holds at most 1024 bytes of input (macOS kept 1019 bytes of a longer line and dropped the rest, its Enter included, so the command never ran). A command over 768 bytes is written to a file of its own in the session's own directory (owner only; written under the session's input lock, so two calls never share one) and run with `. '<file>'`, in the same shell, as typed would; the file is removed once its prompt comes back; the jobs list and notes name it by its own first line.
- **A session's name is no promise it is the same shell** (decided 2026-10-12): sessions end when the node restarts or their shell exits. Only `shell_run` starts one, and its result says when it did ("New session …: a fresh shell"); `shell_send` and `shell_view` refuse a session that is not there or has ended, so text meant for a program never lands in a new shell that took its name.
- **Typing into a program, and people typing too** (decided 2026-10-12): `shell_send` gives a program that asked for bracketed paste (and holds the terminal: a mode a crashed program left on is not the shell's) its text as a paste, as a terminal does (sent bare, Claude Code lost the start of a long text), and `submit` presses Enter on its own once the screen settles (inside a paste it submits nothing). `stitch shell attach <thread>/<session>` puts a session in the user's terminal, live, until its session ends (said so), the node goes away (an error; a stopping node closes its connections) or Ctrl+] detaches; what they type is sent as theirs and the agent is told: woven into its loop after a pause in the typing, else at its next call on the session, which sends nothing on top of it: checked as the call's input is written, under the lock the person's typing takes too, so typing that came while the call waited (on an approval, say) is minded as well.
