Input is written whole by a task of its own (a call cut short leaves no paste half open) and at most for 5 seconds, a person's typing too: a program that takes no more input leaves the rest unsent and the session free for the next input, a Ctrl+C. A command is typed only at the prompt, told under that same lock, and the shell is marked busy with it before the lock is let go: of two `shell_run` at once on a session, one runs and the other is told a program is running. Typing never wakes a thread: a note a loop ends without taking goes back to the session, for the agent's next call there; it is taken and woven in one step, so a call in between sees it either still there or told. Only the session tools take it.
- **Every approval is a question record** (decided 2026-10-13, after a spawned read-only thread sat fifteen minutes on its first `ls`, unseen, with its parent in `wait` on it, and approvals of threads gone by lingered on the attention list): attached or detached, a command that needs approval becomes a question in the attention view, answered from any client, the phone included; the chat's own client is asked inline as well, and the first answer wins (answered elsewhere, the client is told and drops its prompt). The pending call is a record, so a restarted node still asks, and continues once answered (§2.4).
  - **Nothing declines for want of an answer:** a question waits until answered or until yolo is turned on (then it runs, answered by the node). A client that leaves answers nothing (attached chats used to be declined after 15 minutes or when the client went): the turn goes on, its question on the attention list. So opening another thread in the TUI while a turn runs lets go of the turn instead of sending the next message into it, and what was typed for it and not yet taken is printed, not lost.
  - **Withdrawn when nothing asks:** a question still open when the loop that asked stops (interrupted, cancelled, the thread reported without it; a call for another node whose caller is gone) is answered by its node, declined and marked `withdrawn = true`, which a loop asking again never takes up as a decision. A stopping node leaves its detached threads' questions open, since they resume and take them up; a resumed thread that asks something else withdraws what its earlier run asked. A node withdraws the questions it wrote that nothing on it asks or will ask (no loop of their thread listed there) once its threads have resumed at start, and every minute after.
  - **Seen:** the TUI's status line shows `⚑ N` while N questions wait, and a new one is told once in the conversation; `stitch attention` and the TUI list each with its thread, the node that asked and how long ago. A new question is synced to every peer at once. A `wait` on a thread blocked on an approval returns, once per question, saying so (§2.7), so the waiting agent tells the user rather than wait on what only they can move.
- **Reporting back:** when a detached thread's turn ends (finished, given up, over budget), it writes a report to the thread that started it or last messaged it (a `reports-to` edge), which reaches that thread's agent (§2.7); what needs the user goes to the attention view. A TUI shows a report only where it belongs: one to a thread arrives in that thread (the open one has it in its transcript); one to nobody, in the view of the place its thread belongs to; never another thread's in this one. When a chat ends, what the open thread gained after the chat's last step (a report's turn woken as it ended) is printed, not taken as shown.
- **Budgets** are per detached thread: tokens, money, time, spent over all its turns (decided 2026-10-12): a turn woken by a report or a message gets what is left, and with nothing left it does not run, its report saying so. Exceeded, it stops and reports. Steps are a turn's own: a turn takes at most `max_steps` model calls, set per spawn (`spawn`'s `max_steps`, `--max-steps`), else by the node (`[detached] max_steps`), else 50; reaching it, the thread is told so and takes one last step without tools to say where the work stands (a tool called anyway ends it there), then stops and reports.
- **Steps in a chat the user is in** (decided 2026-10-20, with the user, after browser and coding chats ended silently mid-work at the 50 steps every turn had, the last step a tool call and nothing said): a turn takes `[chat] max_steps` model calls (default 200), then asks the user for as many again, an approval ("Continue for 200 more steps? This turn has taken 200 and is not done", `session = "steps"`) on their clients and pushed to their phones, asked whatever yolo says. Granted, it goes on; declined, or stopped while asking, it wraps up as a detached thread does (one last step without tools, told to say where it stopped and that "go on" continues), and the chat ends with a note saying it stopped at the step limit. This holds wherever the thread runs: a chat typed on one node into a thread another node runs (passed on as an assignment with `chat = true`) takes the running node's `[chat] max_steps` (not the 50 of a sent turn or the spawn budget's steps; the thread's budget otherwise stays as the writer left it, lifted by the user's writing, §2.7; not for a room turn, which does not ask locally either) and asks the same way, the question synced to the user's clients (fixed 2026-11-14, issue 2026-11-14T085125Z-gamer-63f7).
- **Schedules** (decided 2026-10-19 with the user; v1 built (`crates/stitch-node/src/schedules.rs`, `cron.rs`), triggers in v2) wake detached threads on their own: at times, and later on events. Stitch then works without anyone starting it.
  - **The record.** A `schedule` record (docs/records.md §4) holds a `name`, the `goal` (the message each firing brings), when (`at`, one time, or `every`: a five-field cron expression (names of months and days, `*/n`, ranges, lists; day of month or of week, as cron, when both are restricted), a shorthand (`@hourly`, `@daily`, `@weekly`, `@monthly`, `@yearly`), or an interval from the last firing (`@every 30m`), read in `tz` (IANA) or else the pinned node's local zone; a time skipped by daylight saving fires after the gap, one repeated fires once), the `place` (as a chat names it: `global`, `project:<repo>`, `folder:<folder>`; the record lives in that place's repository, with a `folder` field as a thread's, so its threads are placed as it is), `model`, `max_steps`, `read_only`, a `budget` per firing (`usd`, `tokens`, `minutes`) and a `daily_cap` in money over all its firings, an optional `check` (or `check_file`, below), `thread = "fresh"` (below), `catch_up = false`, `wake = true`, and `reports-to` (a thread; its reports are kept for the user, `wake = false`, unless `wake = true`). Changes are `set` records on it (§6): `state` (`active`, `paused`, `ended`; deleting is ending), and its other fields (a field set to an empty string is gone); the latest counts as for any `set`. v1 writes only `state` from its surfaces; editing the other fields (`stitch schedule set`) is to come.
  - **Pinned to a node.** A schedule names its `node`, the one that fires it; by default the node that creates it, but never a phone (iOS cannot wake on its own): one made there names a desktop (`[schedules] default_node`, else it asks). It fires only there: an offline node fires nothing elsewhere, and catches up when it is back (below). Moving a schedule is a `set` of `node`.
  - **Threads are resumed.** By default (`thread = "continue"`) the first firing starts a detached thread (`scheduled-by` the schedule) and every later firing continues it, as a `send` does, so it remembers what earlier runs did and found; `thread = "fresh"` starts a new one each time. The thread to continue is the latest one `scheduled-by` it (as it goes on after a compaction, `fork::current`); the thread itself has no budget (each firing brings its own), so the reports and messages that wake it between firings run as the user's chat would. The firing's message, from `schedule <id>`, names the schedule, how it fires and the firing time (`[Schedule <id> "<name>" (every `0 9 * * *` (Europe/Rome)): the firing of 2026-10-20 09:00 CEST.]`), then the goal, then `What its check found:` and the check's output. A thread that another node runs by then (the user continued it there) is passed the message there as a `send` would (an assignment), without the firing's own budget: a known limit.
  - **A check before the model** (the part that keeps a frequent schedule cheap): `check` is Otter code (inline, or a `.otr` file of the place) run at each firing on the pinned node, with no model call: its files confined to the directory of its thread (else the place's, else `<data>/workdirs/<schedule>/`), read-only for a read-only schedule, the network open, for at most `[schedules] check_secs` (default 120). It is top-level code, and what that returns counts: nothing (`none`, `false`, an empty string, sequence or table): the thread is not woken, and the skip is counted in the node's state, not written as a record. It returns something: that value, as text, is added to the firing's message ("CI on main failed: …"). A check that fails is a firing that failed (below). An inline check that does not parse or compile is refused when it is set (`schedule` create, `wake_at`), with Otter's diagnostics (decided 2026-10-29: a wake-up whose check began `let r = …` failed at every firing and paused itself). Its effects (network, files, the clock) run without asking: the schedule was the approval.
  - **Firing.** The pinned node keeps a timer for its active schedules (`schedules::run`): it reads them from the index, fires what is due, and sleeps until the next time (30 seconds at most; woken at once when a schedule is made or changed here, or records arrive by sync). When each last fired is the node's own state, not records: `<data>/schedules.json` (with counts of firings, quiet checks, skips and failures in a row), a new schedule counting from its creation. A firing whose time passed while the node was down runs once at its start, not once per time missed; with `catch_up = false`, one more than two minutes late is skipped (counted). A deadline (`until`) that passed meanwhile is that one firing, as the deadline (decided 2026-10-29: a self-wake that had polled before going down fired its next missed poll instead, found nothing, and its deadline was then behind it, so it never fired). A firing is marked `pending` in `schedules.json` until it is done: one the node stopped in the middle of (its check, waiting for its thread) fires again when the node is back. A firing due while the thread's last turn still runs is skipped (counted), so runs do not pile up. Each firing's turn has the schedule's per-firing budget (`detached::turn_with`), whatever the thread spent before; a firing does not run once `daily_cap` is spent (what its threads spent since midnight in its zone), and its money budget is what is left of the cap at most. The firing history is its threads' reports: `stitch schedule show` lists the turns of the threads `scheduled-by` it (the last ten) with their outcome and cost, and the node's counts; `stitch schedule next` the next times. (Not a `schedule` reference on each turn and report, as first written: the threads are the schedule's own, so their reports are its firings; a message the user sends such a thread shows there too.)
  - **Auto-pause.** After three firings in a row that end `failed` or `over-budget` (the report of the turn a firing started), or whose check fails, the node pauses a recurring schedule (never a one-shot or a self-wake: a pause would also stop its deadline) (a `set` of `state = paused` it authors), says so in its log and pushes to the user's phones ("Schedule paused"). Resuming (by the user) resets the count and does not run what was missed while paused. An entry in the attention view (an `AttentionItem` for it) is deferred to the views' work.
  - **Self-wake** (§2.4: a thread mostly asleep at no cost): a thread can schedule its own continuation (`wake_at`), a one-shot schedule (`self_wake = true`, its `thread` reference the thread) pinned to the node it runs on: at a time (`at`, `minutes`), or a check polled `every` few minutes (default 5) until it returns something, with a deadline (`until`; default the time given, else 24 hours) at which it is continued anyway, told so (and with what the check said, or that it failed). A check that fails (an error, not "nothing") continues the thread at once with the error (decided 2026-10-29: it was counted as a schedule's failing firing, and three of them paused the wake-up, its deadline with it, silently). A self-wake always catches up, whatever `catch_up` says. It ends after it fires (`state = ended`). A wake-up due while the turn that set it still runs waits for that turn to end (six hours at most) rather than being skipped. Its turn runs within the thread's own budget and reports where the thread's turns do, waking whom they wake. This replaces ending a turn just to be woken by nothing; the detached prompt says so.
  - **Who may make one.** The user (`stitch schedule add`; the TUI and the app later) makes schedules without asking (`Author::user`). An agent's `schedule` tool asks the user (a question, as a command's approval: "schedule "<name>" <when> on <node> in <place>: <goal> (budget)"; yolo runs it, and the tool's description and its result say so, so an agent does not tell the user they were asked), since a schedule spends while nobody watches; the record is then the node's (`Author::node`), its reports going to the thread that made it unless it says otherwise. `stitch schedule add|pause|resume|run|rm` from a thread's shell asks too (an acting `stitch` subcommand), so an agent cannot make a user-authored schedule around the tool; `list`, `show` and `next` run without asking. A thread's own self-wake does not ask (it continues within its own budget). A read-only thread cannot make schedules (the asker refuses it, as other acting tools), only its own self-wake, which is read-only too. Pausing, resuming, running now and deleting a schedule do not ask.
  - **Surfaces.** The `schedule` tool (create, list, show, next, pause, resume, run_now, delete) and `wake_at`, both in the `weave` tool group; `stitch schedule add|list|show|next|pause|resume|run|rm` (`add --on <node>` pins it elsewhere: `--node` is the CLI's own option; `Request::Schedule`, the tool's input as JSON, answered as the user); `[schedules] default_node` and `check_secs`. Deferred: the schedules in the Running view of the TUI and the app (next time, last outcome, cost today), and their attention entries.
- **Notices** (decided 2026-10-19 with the user; built the same day, `notices.rs`): a report is read when the user next looks, so an agent with news they would want now (what a scheduled run found, long work done or stuck, what they asked to be told of) sends a notice: the `notify` tool (`title`, `body`; in the `weave` group), and `stitch notify <body> [--title] [--thread]` for scripts, checks and builds (the thread whose shell runs it by default, `STITCH_THREAD`; a read-only default prefix, since it only tells the user). It is pushed to every phone as a question is (§5, milestone 4: sealed, the relay's placeholder outside), naming its thread, the thread's title as its subtitle; opening it opens that chat, after syncing with the node that pushed it (15 s at most). Not a question: no collapse ID, no badge, no buttons; grouped on the phone by thread. It does not ask (it spends nothing and tells only the user), works in read-only threads, and is capped at 10 per thread an hour (the error says when the next can go), so a loop gone wrong cannot buzz the phone all night. A phone's own threads cannot push to it: their notices wait in a queue its app takes every three seconds (`Stitch.takeNotices`) and shows as local notifications. Nothing is written as a record: the tool call in the transcript is its history. The detached prompt and the `schedule` tool tell agents of it (a goal saying "notify me when …"). **On the desktop too** (decided 2026-10-30, with the user; `toasts.rs`): whatever a node pushes to the phones (`node::push`: a notice, a question, loose ends, a schedule paused) it also shows as a notification on its own machine's desktop, through `notify-rust`: D-Bus `org.freedesktop.Notifications` on Linux (zbus, already linked by `keyring`; a user service has the session bus), the Notification Center on macOS (`NSUserNotificationCenter`, posted as the Stitch app when `/Applications/Stitch.app` is installed, else as Terminal, so its notifications must be allowed in System Settings; set explicitly, since the crate's default looks the app up with an AppleScript that hangs), a toast on Windows (untested). Only that node's desktop: a thread's notice shows where the thread runs, not on every desktop (fanning out to peers is later, with clicking one to open its chat). On by default for the user's node (data in `~/.stitch`), off for any other data directory so tests and throwaway nodes show nothing; `[notifications] desktop = true|false` overrides. A question's notification has no timeout and is closed when the question is answered or withdrawn (Linux; the handle is kept by question ID). The freedesktop body is markup on most servers, so it is escaped; the thread's title goes above the body where there are no subtitles. Showing never holds up a push (5 s at most), and a machine with no session bus or notification server shows nothing, logging the first failure. Later: the TUI's own notice, and quiet hours (with v2's digest).
- **Calls** (decided 2026-11-25, with the user; `rings.rs`): for what is urgent and cannot wait for the user to look, an agent rings their phone with a real incoming call: the `call_user` tool (`reason`, a few words, the caller line "Stitch: <reason>", 80 characters at most; in the `weave` group beside `notify`). The phone registers a second push token for calls beside its notification token (a VoIP token: `Request::RegisterVoip`, kept as `voip` in the same `device` record, each registration keeping the other's token; a phone that allows no notifications may register for calls alone). The node sends each phone that has one a VoIP push (`apns-push-type: voip`, topic `<app>.voip`, priority 10, kept by APNs 30 seconds at most), its whole payload sealed as a notification's content is (§5, milestone 4): the call's ID (a UUID), the thread, its repository, the node, the caller line, the thread's title and when it was made; nothing in the clear. Through the relay it is `POST /voip`. The phone reports it as an incoming call at once (the platform requires it of every VoIP push), opens it, and refuses a stale one. **The tool waits** (60 s at most) for the phone's word, which the phone's own node passes on to the node that rang (`Request::RingOutcome` there → `Question::Ring`, tried three times, each asked again on a fresh connection when the kept one brings no answer within 6 s, as a phone woken by the push keeps connections its peers dropped while it was suspended; the first word wins, a second phone's later one is dropped): *answered*, the user is on a voice call with the thread (the client's voice call mode, §2.26), and the tool tells the model that what it says next is read aloud. **The phone says *answered* the moment the user answers, before it syncs or sets anything up** (decided 2026-11-26, after a call answered on a cold phone whose word came only after a sync, the thread's, the call's audio and its first words, past the tool's 60 s: the tool said missed, the agent never spoke); it then starts the call and syncs the thread's repository alone from the node that rang (`Request::SyncRepo`, the repository from the push), all at once, and sends the user's first words, "(I picked up your call.)", into the waiting turn, whose reply streams to the call live. So the reply that is read aloud answers those words, **the tool waits on for them** (30 s at most) once answered; past that it tells the model they are on their way and to say nothing to the user until they come (woven in later, or a turn of their own). **A late answer** (the tool gave up) is taken without error and logged; the first words still reach the thread, as a turn of its own, whose reply the call reads aloud; *declined*, it says so; *missed* (the phone gives up after 30 s and says so, or never says), a notice goes instead (`Missed call from Stitch`), so it stays on the phone. A phone that cannot ring (an app from before calls, a relay that answers 404, APNs refusing) gets a notice instead, and the tool says so. **Limits, per node, whatever thread asks:** one call every 5 minutes, none for 30 minutes after a decline (in memory: a restart forgets them), the error saying when the next can go and to use `notify`; a phone's own node cannot ring itself. Nothing is written as a record: the tool call in the transcript is the call's history. Tested: `tests/calls.rs` (two nodes: the phone registers, the Mac's agent rings through a mock relay, the sealed call opens with the phone's key, the phone answers, syncs the thread's repository and sends its first words, which the waiting tool weaves in, the reply streamed to the phone; a second call at once is refused; an answer after the tool gave up still reaches the agent as a turn of its own), `rings::tests` (the limits, the first word winning), `apns::tests` (registration, the payload); `apns::tests::ring_a_real_phone` rings a real phone with an APNs key, given its tokens in the environment. Later: quiet hours; ending the ringing on the user's other phones once one answered.
  - **Event triggers (built 2026-11-14, docs/events.md):** the first v2 trigger. A schedule's `on` (a topic pattern) fires it when an outside program publishes a matching event to its node (`Request::Event`, the stable `events/1` protocol, `stitch-client`, `stitch event`); its `check` is the filter, run on each event with the payload as input (compiled as any value); `debounce`, `max_per_hour`, `daily_cap` and `until` limit it; events coalesce into one wake. A thread's subscription (`subscribe` tool) is such a schedule continuing that thread within its own budget, as a self-wake is; asks with a deadline are answered with `event_reply`, by the threads they were delivered to only; a read-only thread only when the ask came through a subscription made with `answers` (decided 2026-11-16 for meeting threads: recorded on the ask as it is delivered; set by the user or with their approval, never by a read-only thread; fixers stay refused). A schedule's gate serializes its timed firings and event wakes, its hourly wakes and `daily_cap` money reserved under it; the ingress is bounded by quotas (answered `overloaded`) (review fixes, 2026-11-14, docs/events.md "As built"). local-68.
  - **Capability profiles (built 2026-11-16, docs/events.md "Capability profiles"):** a schedule's `profile` (set at creation only: `subscribe`'s and `schedule`'s `profile`, `--tools/--shards/--effects/--trusted/--untrusted` on `stitch event subscribe` and `stitch schedule add`) is the ceiling of every turn it starts: tools allow-listed (`event_reply` always), Otter shards checked when code compiles (`otter_run`, `otter_check`, skills' tools, `Tool.Call`'d ones). Untrusted (the default) taints: the node writes it on the thread (a user `set` of `profile`) before the payload reaches the model, holding a running turn from its next call and every later turn, fork and compaction until the user lifts it (`stitch event untaint`); its shards are allowed by effect class, its payloads fenced as data, its thread out of memory, its reports waking nobody. Enforced by the node, never the prompt.
  - **v2, triggers on the same record and the same check:** a file or folder changing (the `watch_file` engine), git (a new commit on a branch, a PR or issue opened or commented on, polling `gh`), a CI run ending, another thread's report or outcome, a webhook endpoint on the node, the machine idle. A trigger is a condition the node checks at no model cost; when it holds, the schedule fires as above. Jobs (`shell_watch`, `watch_file`) and triggers then share one condition engine. Also v2: a heartbeat agent (opt-in) that wakes each morning, reads the attention view, finds stuck threads and writes the "since you last looked" digest; compaction of a resumed thread after its context grows past a threshold; quiet hours for pushes, reports gathered into a morning digest.

**Where threads belong** (decided 2026-10-11, revised the same day):
- **A thread has a place:** a project, a folder on one node, or none (global).
- **A checkout git cannot read** (decided 2026-10-14, with the user): a desktop reads a checkout's first commit and `origin` with the `git` command; where it cannot be run, a directory with `.git` is taken for a folder until git is installed (then it becomes its project, its threads with it). That is said: once in the node's log per checkout, by a CLI command run there, and in a turn's context, so the agent can offer to install git. Folder sync never takes it for a folder's files: it looks for `.git` itself.
- **Every git checkout is a project,** known by the first commit of its history, found the first time Stitch runs in it: nothing to set up, nothing written into the checkout. Every clone, on any node and at any path, is the same project. A fork shares the first commit and so the project; `stitch project split` in a fork's checkout gives it a project of its own, told apart by its `origin` remote, so later clones of the fork find that one. Projects are listed in the home workspace (`project` records); a project's threads live in its own record store in the node's data directory (§2.5).
- **A repository re-created from a new first commit keeps its project only when the user rebinds it** (decided 2026-11-22, with the user, after a repository re-created upstream from one new commit, at the same origin, with the checkout reset to it, failed every thread of its project before its turn: the noted checkout was forgotten as holding another project, and the search refused the directory). Identity is never merged by path or by a reused origin silently: a re-created repository is a new project (a chat there lists it) until the user runs **`stitch project rebind`** in its checkout (`--project <name|id>` when several projects have that origin), which has the project of the origin follow it. It refuses a checkout with no origin, an origin no other listed (non-split) project with another first commit has, a backward step (below), and a choice among several unnamed. Until then, a turn of the old project that meets such a checkout (a noted one is forgotten as before; the search of `checkout::ensure`, an existing clone destination or a clone) fails saying so: `<path> holds a repository re-created from a new first commit at the project's origin; if it is the same project, run \`stitch project rebind\` there`. An agent running the command asks the user (it is on no allow list). Rebinding writes a `project` record with `repo` (the project), `reroot` (the new first commit) and `remote`, and no `root`, so builds before it skip it (no format change). `projects()` gives each project the roots it was re-rooted to (`reroots`, a `reroot` of a project not listed counting for nothing), and a checkout of one of them is that project's (after a split fork's match, the earliest such project), so new chats there join the old project with its threads, instructions and records. Re-rooting only goes forward: a root that is another project's listed before this one is no re-creation of it (an old checkout, not yet reset, seen by a thread of a project listed later for the new root), and a cycle written anyway (two nodes at once) is ignored by `project_of`, each root its own project's again. A checkout of a project's own first commit and origin is still that project's (`place::holds`), so a project already listed for the new root keeps finding it. A repository of another origin, or none, where a project was is forgotten or refused as before. Tested with `a_project_follows_its_repository_recreated_only_once_rebound`, `place::tests::a_project_follows_its_repository_rerooted`, `place::tests::rerooting_only_goes_forward` and `place::tests::a_noted_checkout_recreated_at_its_origin_is_kept_only_once_rebound`.
- **Outside git,** a thread lives in the home workspace with a `folder` field: the node and the folder, home-relative (`~/notes`). Nothing is set up: a folder becomes a place with its first thread. The same `~` path is the same folder on every node, automatically (paths another node wrote as `/Users/<name>/…` or `/home/<name>/…` count as its `~`). `stitch folder link <node:path>` (or `/link`) joins folders with different paths (a relative path is in that node's home directory, as with `scp`: `mbp:notes` is `mbp:~/notes`; a bare path is on this node, from the current directory). A link is checked first (decided 2026-10-20, after a link to a folder that did not exist renamed the real one everywhere): when both sides are already one folder it says so and writes nothing; this side must be a directory, and the other a folder Stitch knows: a directory on this node, or a location a thread's `folder`, a `folder` record (location, link, unlink, sync) or a folder sync snapshot shows on its node; anything else is refused as maybe a typo, or a folder no thread has run in yet, unless `--force` (`/link --force <node:path>`). `stitch folder unlink` (or `/unlink`) keeps one to its node. Both are `folder` records in the home workspace. A folder with threads that later becomes a git checkout is folded into the project, and its threads are listed there.
- **In the home directory,** or when asked (`--global`, `/new global`), a thread is global.
- **The place decides where shells start:** where the user is, when that is inside the thread's place; else the project's checkout or the folder on this node (each node remembers where it has seen each project checked out); else where the user is.
- **A project with no checkout here is found, cloned with the user's yes, or refused; never skipped** (decided 2026-11-18, with the user, after a new chat of paragon the phone put on beast started in beast's home directory: beast had paragon checked out but had never run in it, so `checkouts.toml` did not name it, the turn's context left the place out, and the agent answered about the stitch checkout it found instead). Before a project thread's turn on a node that runs programs (`checkout::ensure`; a phone keeps its own clones, §2.16, and a node whose shell is off reads only the place's records, §2.14), when this node knows no checkout of it: first an **unseen checkout** is looked for, a directory named as the project or its origin, then any checkout directly in the parents of the checkouts this node knows (the most used first), then `~/devel`, `~/dev`, `~/src`, `~/code`, `~/projects`, `~/repos`, `~/git`, `~/Developer`, `~/work` and `~`, matched by its first commit (and a split fork's origin); one found is noted, as a chat run in it would. None: the user is asked, as an approval of the thread (session `clone`), to **clone it** from the origin the home workspace lists (`git clone <url> <dest>`, HTTPS with the node's GitHub sign-in in the environment of that command only, not written into the clone, or SSH when this node's checkouts use it), into the most used parent of the known checkouts, else `~/devel`; asked whatever the approval mode, yolo too, as a phone's first clone is. Only an origin of the form `host/path` (plain characters, no user, port, query or option-like part) or a local path is cloned; the command asked is shell-quoted, nothing is made before the yes, one question and clone at a time per project (another project's turns do not wait on it), and a clone not done in 30 minutes, or whose turn was cancelled, is killed with what git started and its partial directory removed. Declined, failed, no origin, a destination that exists, or no thread written yet to ask for (a new local chat, a detached start): the turn fails before its model call with why, naming the project and the node (`The turn did not start: project paragon has no checkout on beast…`), as a failed call is shown in the transcript and the app. And a project turn's context always names its place: its directory when there is one, else that it has no checkout here and not to take another repository for it. **A noted checkout is trusted only while it is still the project** (decided 2026-11-19, a review finding of that change): `checkouts.toml` named a directory for good while it existed, so one that now held another repository, or no `.git`, was taken for the project. `Checkouts::of` reads a noted checkout with git as the search matches one (its first commit, and a split fork's origin) the first time a node process uses it, and again only when its `.git` changed (its device, inode and birth time: a stat per turn, no git); one that holds another project, lies inside another checkout, or has no `.git` any more is dropped from `checkouts.toml` (the node log says why), and the next one noted, else the search and the clone above, takes over. A phone's own clones (§2.16), and checkouts on a node where git cannot run, are trusted as before. Tested with `a_project_with_no_checkout_here_is_found_or_cloned_never_skipped` (an impostor where the project was noted, the project found where it went), `a_new_chat_of_a_project_on_a_node_without_its_checkout_fails_saying_so` and `place::tests::a_remembered_checkout_no_longer_its_project_is_forgotten`.
- **A thread with no place has a working folder of its own** (decided 2026-10-17, with the user, after a phone's global thread wrote `apps/<name>/` into the app's container, which cannot be written): when its client gives no directory (the phone, a detached thread, one continued by a report or message), it is `<data>/workdirs/<origin>/` in the node's data directory, made when first needed, `<origin>` the first thread its `continues` edges lead back to, so a fork or a compaction keeps its folder. Relative paths (shells, `write_file`, `view_file`, Otter tools' files) start there, it is written without asking as a place is, and the turn is told its path. A client in a directory (the TUI) keeps it, as before; only the home directory as a fallback is gone (on a phone it is the app's container; a node with no shells and nothing known uses `<data>/workdirs/_`). Never the home workspace, which holds records. Promoting such a folder into a place (a new folder, or merged into an existing folder or project) is open (§4). **It syncs between nodes as a folder place does** (decided 2026-11-01, with the user, after an app a phone's global thread wrote in its working folder was nowhere once the thread was continued on the Mac): it is a synced folder (§2.16) whose identity is `workdir:<origin>`, so its store derives from the thread and every node finds it with nothing to set up, and forks and compactions share it. Stitch made it, in its own data directory, for its own thread, so it syncs unasked (no ask-first guard, no never-synced rule for the data directory); the limits and ignore rules (`.stitch/ignore` in it) hold as for any folder, and `stitch folder sync --stop` in it still stops it. It is lazy: a node makes the directory and merges the others' changes only when a turn on the thread runs there (or `stitch folder sync` is run in it), with passes before and after each turn and after every sync, as for a folder place; phones the same, in their data directory. An existing one-node working folder starts syncing from what is there at its next turn. A client in a directory (the TUI) keeps it as the start, and the turn's context names the working folder's path on this node when it holds files, so the agent finds what was written there elsewhere.
- **Lists and reopening follow the place:** the TUI, `stitch threads` and the app show the place's threads and global ones, every other on request, the most recent first whatever place each belongs to (decided 2026-10-13: grouped by place, a thread started on the phone a minute ago, global, came after every older thread of the project; `last` still takes the place's own first). A client with no directory there (the phone) names a new thread's place instead (`Request::Chat::place`: `global`, `project:<repo>`, `folder:<folder>`; a folder outside the home directory keeps its node). The TUI reopens the thread last opened in that place (kept per node, not synced), else the place's latest; a place with no thread of its own starts on a new one (decided 2026-10-23: falling back to the last global thread had a project's review typed into an old global test thread, which stayed global, its title the test's). The thread picker's title says what it shows and what Tab switches to (`showing: here and global · Tab → here only`).
- **Going up to the parent in the TUI** (decided 2026-10-27, with the user: nothing said a thread opened was a child, and the way back was the picker). A thread's parent is the thread that spawned it, else the one it reports to (`weave::row`'s parent), followed to that thread's newest compaction; a fork is not a child. When the open thread has one, the status line shows `<title> ↑ <parent> (^P)`. `/parent` or Ctrl+P opens the parent; on a thread with none, the status line says so. Ctrl+P, not Ctrl+U as the user first suggested: Ctrl+U already clears the input, as in readline. Going down (the same day): `/children` lists the threads whose parent is the open one (those spawned by or reporting to any compaction of it, a compacted child once, as its newest), newest first, with their state, start and place when not this one, in a picker that opens the one picked; the status line counts them, `↓ N (/children)`, the count read again as the poller reads the thread list. Both are on the status line only: a line in the scrollback on opening (tried first, the same day) read as a chat message (the user). No key for going down yet (open). The open thread's title, place, parent and children are read from the index as it opens and at each poll (`Family`), not from the thread list: the list holds only the scope's threads, so a thread of another place (a child spawned in another checkout, one opened from a report) showed as "new thread" with no ↑, and Ctrl+P did nothing there (the user, the same day).
- **Every node holds every repository:** the home workspace and every project's records, so a project is there before it is cloned, and every node is a backup of the others. A folder that is only another node's is a label there.

**Concepts:**
- **Thread:** a conversation with an agent, attached (a client is talking to it) or detached (running on its own toward a goal, with a budget). Its transcript holds everything it did.
- **Schedule:** when to start a detached thread, and with what goal.
- **Record:** an immutable report, finding, decision, question, answer or handoff.
- **Artifact:** files, commits, embeds.
- **Edges:** spawned-by, reports-to, scheduled-by, handed-off-to, verifies, depends-on.

**Views,** from most often opened to least:
1. **Attention:** only what is blocked on the user (decisions, approvals, questions, failed verifications). Empty on a good day.
2. **Since you last looked:** a digest per thread of what moved, finished or is waiting, built from the reports threads write. The user reads reports, not transcripts.
3. **Running:** the detached threads as a tree (who started whom), with state, node and budget used, and the schedules.
4. **Graph:** the edges above, rendered visually in 2D or 3D, to explore how chats, tasks, agents and their results connect and how the work grew over time. The record format keeps every edge explicit, typed and timed for it ([records.md](records.md) §10); how it is drawn is open (§4).
5. **Transcript:** one thread's conversation, tool calls included, where the user can step in.

Chat stays first-class next to these views (§2.11): starting a conversation, asking, attaching things and reading answers is still the most direct way to work. A chat is a thread like any other in the graph; the views above are what grows around it when work fans out.

Long-running work needs a budget per thread (tokens, money, time), wake triggers (a schedule, a message, a file change, a CI result, another thread's report), and permission gates per effect and per node.

### 2.4 Threads are event-sourced

A thread's durable state lives in the store, not in a process. A detached thread:
1. wakes on a trigger;
2. loads its context from the store;
3. runs some steps;
4. appends records;
5. sleeps.

Consequences:
- a crash loses nothing;
- any capable node can continue a thread, unless its shell sessions pin it (§2.7);
- a thread that lasts days is mostly asleep at no cost.

For Otter scripts, instances stay short-lived per step, and Otter never needs to serialize engine state across machines, which its plan keeps out of scope.

**A thread forked across nodes has one history** (decided 2026-10-13, from a thread used on two machines, `…xeqy`). A thread is a chain of segments, each naming the one before (`prev`); a node continuing a thread writes after the head it knows. Used on two nodes, it forked whenever one continued from a copy that was behind: on 2026-10-13 the Linux node, missing the Mac's evening of segments, was woken by late reports at 04:53 and continued from the previous day's 13:38 step; the history was then the *latest head*, so a minute later the Mac's own chat jumped to the Linux node's short branch (135 messages instead of 344), and the user, typing into the Mac from Linux, talked to an agent with no memory of the evening, while a turn on the long branch (the user's message in another window, at 04:52) had run a docs pass that the short one never saw. Now:
- **The main line:** (since 2026-10-22, a user's rewind decides first: §2.15, "Rewinding a thread") the thread's longest chain of segments (of those as long, the head written first, by creation time to the millisecond). A continuation from a stale copy starts shorter than the line it missed, so it does not take the thread over; one that died off (two nodes continued at once, one gave way, as `…xeqy` did the day before for a minute) is left behind too, which "the continuation written first" at each fork, tried first, got wrong. Every node holding the same segments reads the same line, the same in the TUI, the CLI and the model's history, and `send`'s and delivery's notion of the node that runs a thread (§2.7) is the writer of the main line's head.
- **What a fork left out is said:** a turn's context (§2.3) carries, under "Other continuations of this thread", each branch off the main line: its node, when, the step it left from, and its last messages, since what it did happened (commands ran, commits were made, threads spawned). A thread already continued on a branch that loses goes back to the main line, with that branch told this way: `…xeqy` returns to its 344-message line, and its morning on the 179-message branch is told.
- **Why it forked that morning, from the files:** no step was lost and no chain was broken (every segment's `prev` is there, on the Linux node and on the Mac); the evening is on the long line. The Linux node started at 04:52:52, woke the thread with reports held for it before its first sync had come in, and loaded its history then; the Mac's evening segments landed three seconds later (04:52:55), and the turn, written at 04:53:01, continued from the step it had loaded, the day before's 13:38. Every turn after it, on both nodes, followed that branch as the latest head.
- **Fewer forks:** a turn nobody typed (reports, a message, a detached thread resumed at the node's start) syncs the thread first and runs only where the thread runs; a chat syncs first when another node wrote to the thread (§2.7). Two loops started at the same moment on two nodes still fork it; a lease would prevent that, later.

### 2.5 Storage: records in folders, not a database

Folders of record files are the source of truth:
- files that people, Claude, Codex and grep can read and fix;
- the format code work already uses.

They live in the node's data directory, never in a project's git checkout (decided 2026-10-11, reversing project records in `.stitch/`): chat transcripts and agent records do not belong in a project's history, in every clone of it, and records left uncommitted in a working tree are one `git add -A` from landing there. Git can hold the home workspace's history if the user wants; Stitch does not depend on it.

The store follows the handoff rules, which make the records a grow-only set (the simplest CRDT) that never conflicts, whether it travels by git or by record sync (§2.6):

- **Immutable files** with unique names, created exclusively. A change is a new record that references the old one.
- **Derived state:** status, titles and settings are computed from records. Where a value is genuinely mutable, it is written as a small timestamped decision file, and the latest wins.
- **Transcripts** are stored as compacted segments per checkpoint, never per token. Tokens stream over the live channel (§2.6).
- **Large blobs** (images, attachments, models) go in a content-addressed store (blake3) beside the records, not in git.
- **Nothing stored twice** (decided 2026-10-13, measured on the Mac's store: 1,483 segments, 10.7 MB, of which the provider-native copy of each reply was 40%, its text and tool calls 1.3 MB of it repeating `parts` and `tool_call`, and 44 threads carried 16 distinct system prompts): a thread's system prompt is a blob, the record's `system-prompt.md` attachment named by `system_prompt` (records before keep their `system` field), shared by every thread that has the same one; Anthropic's native content is written packed, its text and tool calls by reference (records.md §5.1). Thinking signatures (a fifth of all transcript bytes) stay: Claude models keep earlier turns' thinking in context, and replay needs them as they were.
- **A segment per turn** (decided 2026-10-13, with the user: a segment per model step left 1,483 files of 7 KB on average, 22% of their disk blocks empty, and every turn read every one of its thread's files again). A turn's steps are appended, as each is done, to its open segment, `journal/<thread>/<segment>.jsonl` in the record root, never synced; it is sealed into `threads/` (same name, same content, then immutable) when the turn ends, before the repository syncs (either side of a sync, under the index's lock), and when the node starts or stops with one open. Readers on the node read the open segment with the sealed ones (history, transcripts, the TUI following a thread, approval reuse, budgets, a spawned thread continuing the conversation here), so a step is seen as it is written, as before; other nodes see it once sealed, and a sync seals first, so a spawn on another node finds the spawning step as before. A turn sealed mid-way for a sync goes on in a new segment after it. Roots from before were migrated once, at a node's first start on this version: runs of consecutive segments merged into the run's last segment (its ID and file, so `prev` and `continues_at` still name it), cut at a turn's start, a fork, a segment a record names, a change of node, and only of segments written before the change, so nothing peers hold in this format is rewritten; a thread whose main line would end elsewhere is left as it was; the files before are kept in `<data>/migrated-segments/`. The protocol between nodes is version 2 since, so a node of either side never syncs with the other.
- **Deleting a thread** (decided 2026-10-22 with the user, who wanted to clean up spawned threads that were of no use; `stitch-store`'s `purge`, `stitch-node`'s `delete`). The one exception to "nothing is removed": a real purge on every node, not a hide, by the user only.
  - **A tombstone:** a `deletion` record, written by the user in the thread's repository, naming the threads it deletes (`deletes`). It syncs like any record and is kept forever, so a node that was offline with the thread cannot bring it back. One written by a node or an agent's run counts for nothing.
  - **What goes, on every node that has the tombstone** (after each sync, at the start, at once where the user deleted): the thread's record and segments, its open segment, and what exists only for it: a record (of any kind but `thread` and `deletion`) whose `thread`, `target`, `answers` or `claims` names the thread or another record going (its reports, its questions and their answers, `set`s on it, its toolset changes, the assignments for it and their claims), and a schedule that reports to it (its self-wakes, its schedules); then the blobs nothing else references. Memory forgets it: the `memory` records holding only its exchanges go too, and the graph is folded again without any observation of its messages (in mixed records, and in what comes later); gists stay, being consolidated across threads. Search (the BM25 archive), lists, the attention view and the dashboard read the index, so it is gone from them. What runs on it here stops first, with no report (it would go with the thread): its loops, a chat's turn, its jobs and shells; its schedules end with their records.
  - **Never back:** each record root keeps the IDs it purged and every deleted thread's in `purged` (local, never synced). Sync takes no file of them, nor of a record that would go with one (an answer an offline node wrote to the thread's question); a loop still running here cannot write to the thread (its open segment is dropped when sealed). A node that has not heard of the tombstone yet still sends them; the receiver drops them, and the sender purges once the tombstone reaches it.
  - **No cascade:** deleting a thread never deletes another. The threads it spawned, its forks, the threads that report to it stay, orphaned: a child's reports to it are kept (they are the child's), and never delivered; a fork or a spawned thread whose source is gone opens, its notice saying the earlier part was deleted, and goes on without it (with its summary, if it has one); the dashboard calls the parent "A deleted thread".
  - **A compaction chain is one conversation** (§2.15): deleting any thread of it deletes the threads it was compacted from and into, listed in the confirmation. A thread compacted on another node after the tombstone was written is not in it: delete it too. Only an automatic compaction (`compacted = true`) joins a chain: a fork the user made, summarized or not, is a thread of its own. **The confirmation says why** (2026-11-15, with the user: a flat list of the same title two or three times, called "what it was compacted from or into", read as a cascade to the parent): the dry run's lines are `<id>\t<title>\t<role>`, the role `this` (named, or spawned by `--spawned-by`'s thread), `earlier` (a part it was compacted from) or `later` (compacted from it); an older node sends no role, and clients then say less. Every surface words it the same (`stitch-cli/src/doomed.rs`, the web client's `actions/doomed.ts`, the app's `DeletePlan`): one thread alone is asked about with no list; a compacted one says "It was summarized and continued in a new thread twice when it grew long, so it is 3 parts of one conversation: all 3 go", lists the parts marked *this one*, *earlier part*, *later part* (CLI, web), and says that the threads it started and the forks made of it stay. The TUI and the app ask the node (a dry run) before asking the user.
  - **A working thread is stopped, not refused:** the user asked for it gone. Running on another node, it stops when the tombstone reaches that node; what that loop writes meanwhile is refused on arrival.
  - **Only the user:** there is no tool for it; `stitch thread rm` asks for approval in an agent's shell like any write (`rules.rs`), and the node refuses a deletion sent from a thread's shell at all (`STITCH_THREAD` set), yolo or not. Surfaces: `stitch thread rm <id>… [--spawned-by <id>] [-y]` (lists the threads with their titles and asks), Delete in the TUI's thread picker (then `y`; it was `d` until typing filtered the picker, 2026-10-29), `Request::DeleteThreads` (`dry_run` lists only), and the phone's thread list (swipe or the context menu, confirmed), through `Node::delete_threads`.
  - **The home workspace under git,** if the user keeps one: Stitch removes the files; what an earlier commit holds is the user's history to rewrite.
- **Indexes are derived** and rebuildable on any node: full-text search, vectors, engram state, the task graph. They live in local SQLite and are gitignored. SQLite becomes a cache, and cr-sqlite is dropped: the records need no general CRDT, because nothing in them is edited in place.

There are two kinds of repository (record store):
- **Project stores:** one per project (§2.3), in `projects/<id>/` in the data directory, holding the threads and records about that project. Its ID derives from the project's first commit (and, for a fork split off, its remote), so nodes that find a project at the same time agree on it. A repository re-created from a new first commit at the same origin is a new project unless the user rebinds it (`stitch project rebind`: a `reroot` record, decided 2026-11-22 with the user, §2.3); rebound, it keeps its project's store: its ID stays the old first commit's.
- **The home workspace:** personal threads, the list of projects, agent and tool definitions (Otter scripts), settings and cross-project views.

A repository's records are identified by the repository's ID, not its path. **Every node holds every repository**, phones included: records are small text, and blobs follow lazily (§2.6). A node indexes every record set it holds into one graph.

**Git in the workflow:**
- a task is a branch or worktree per worker;
- a worker's output is commits plus a report record;
- review is a verification record;
- acceptance is a merge.

Risk, flagged once: **iCloud Drive corrupts `.git` directories.** If iCloud stays a transport, it carries bundles or snapshots, not live repositories.

Git on the phone is not needed. If the phone later needs code (browsing, cloning), libgit2 runs on iOS with SSH through libssh2, as Working Copy shows; that is a capability added then, not the backbone.

### 2.6 Sync and the live channel

- **Record sync** works between any two nodes, phones included: the nodes reconcile the record IDs each holds per repository (range-based set reconciliation: compare fingerprints of ID ranges, split the ranges that differ) and send each other the missing files. Merging is a union. Blobs follow lazily, by hash. It runs over the node protocol.
- **Git** is the code workflow (fetch, push, worktrees, merges), not a transport for records: record sync carries them all. The home workspace syncs first, since it lists the projects; a node then holds a store for every project listed and syncs each.
- **A sync reaches every peer at once, an offline one once** (decided 2026-10-14, after a sync on the Mac and the phone took eight minutes with two peers offline: each repository waited on each peer in turn, and reaching an offline one gives up after 30 seconds, again for every repository): each repository syncs with every peer in parallel; a peer has 10 seconds to be reached, and one not reached is skipped for the rest of that sync's repositories, said once (a session reached still had 120 seconds; since 2026-10-27, 30 seconds without a frame, below). The app's refresh during a sync already under way waits for it rather than ending at once. Since 2026-10-20 (the phone at times syncing nothing until the app was killed and opened again): a peer whose session times out is skipped for the rest of the sync too (it was kept, and each of a phone's dozen repositories waited its 120 seconds again: half an hour); the app no longer waits for a sync begun before it was suspended (its connections may be dead) but starts one afresh; back in front it first tells iroh the network may have changed (`Endpoint::network_change`: iroh does not notice by itself that its relay connection and paths died while the app was suspended, no link having changed); a phone's clones are fetched in the background after a sync, one pass at a time, rather than inside it (GitHub unreachable held every sync up); and what went wrong in a sync is written to the node's log (an unreachable peer on a phone only).
- **What a sync did is seen** (2026-10-27, after sync was found slow with nothing in the log to say why): every sync writes one line to the node's log, desktops too: its time, each peer's repositories, time and files in and out, and the peers not reached with why. The node keeps, in `peers-seen.toml` beside `peers.toml` (local, never synced), when each peer was last reached, on which path (direct, with its address, or relay) and its round trip, its last sync's time, and the last failure since; `stitch peer ls` shows them. A peer of the local list reached on other direct addresses than it lists gets them in `peers.toml` (addresses from a network left months ago only slowed the next dial). The trusted peers are kept 5 seconds rather than read afresh for every incoming connection (read again when `peers.toml` changes; a node not among them is looked for afresh, and a sync that brings home records drops them).
- **Sync is incremental, and pushed** (decided 2026-10-27, with the user: "it should be incremental"; measured before on gamer and geek, LAN, 20 repositories, nothing new: 0.8 s a sync, a new QUIC connection and four or five round trips per repository; a turn's transcript reached the other nodes only at the next 5-minute interval; the phone's spawn reached gamer in 25 s, behind every other repository). Records stay files, the set stays grow-only: what changed is what is incremental, not the store.
  - **A change feed** (`stitch_store::changes`): the index tells, as it learns of them, which files came into which repository and from which peer (`Index::refresh_from`); what another process indexed (the index is shared) is found by polling SQLite's `data_version` every 5 seconds against each repository's fingerprint. Each repository's whole fingerprint is kept by the index and brought up to date as files come (XOR: adding one is one hash), so comparing a repository costs nothing to compute. Anything else that ships changes (the Vault's node adapter) reads the same feed.
  - **Pushed within seconds:** the node gathers changes until a second passes without one (three at most) and pushes the repositories changed to every peer; what came from a peer goes on to the others, not back to it (a phone that can only call out reaches geek through gamer at once). One push at a time per peer, the next taking what came meanwhile. **A push that does not reach its peer is tried again** (decided 2026-10-29, after an approval answered on gamer at 06:42 waited for geek, whose machine was rebooting, until the next interval's sync: a failed push was dropped, and changes for a peer that failed within the minute were not even queued): what failed and what came for that peer meanwhile wait for its retry, 5 seconds after, doubling to a minute, for half an hour, at once when the peer reaches this node; a peer that failed under a minute ago is first tried when that minute is up. After half an hour, the interval's sync, the peer reaching this node or the next change takes it from there. The retry holds the node only while it pushes. **A thread resuming at start with an open approval** catches up with its peers before it goes on, trying again for 30 seconds while none is reached (a machine just booted, its network not up), so an answer given while the node was down is there. **Its waiting step is replayed** (same date): a loop's step is written only once its calls ran, so a loop stopped while a call waited on the user (the node stopped, crashed, or its machine rebooted) used to resume by asking the model again, which asked a slightly different command, withdrew the question the user had answered meanwhile and asked anew. Now the step in progress (its events: the model's reply, its calls, the results some had) is kept for this node alone (the index's local values, `pending-step:<thread>`, dropped once the step is written) and a resumed loop that finds it on the head it continues runs those calls again, without a model call: the same command finds the same question, and the user's answer counts. Calls of it that had their result keep it and do not run again. **A call executing when the node stopped is replayed once** (decided 2026-11-02, issue n6rx: a thread's `shell_run` ending in `pkill -f "stitch node"` stopped its node, a SIGTERM, which stops it as cleanly as a Stop, and every start replayed the call and stopped the node again, every ~10 s for 40 minutes). The call the loop stopped in is the step's first without a result, however the node stopped (a clean stop aborts the loops, whose guards clear `running`, so that mark cannot tell). One that waited on the user (an approval asked, a `wait`: marked `waiting` in the kept step while it waits, cleared only when the wait ends, never by a guard, so a stop during it leaves the mark; `chat::waiting_on_user`) is replayed as often as the node stops there, its question with it, as restart-when-idle expects of those waits. One that executed is marked `replayed` as it is replayed (kept through the step's later rewrites); found again unresolved and so marked, it is not run a third time but gets an error result for the model ("not run again: the node stopped while this call ran, twice …"), logged to stderr, and the loop goes on. **`threads` says an undelivered answer** (same date): a thread on another node whose latest approval was answered after that node was last reached is listed `blocked: approval <q> answered (<choice>, on <node>, <when>), not delivered to <node> yet: …`, not `working`. A push seals nothing: a turn's steps go when it ends (its sealing is itself a change), so a busy repository is not cut into segments by every record another thread writes. The interval's sync (still 5 minutes) is the backstop, for a file written where nothing indexes it (`stitch new` writes a record without indexing it: it goes at the interval).
  - **A summary first** (protocol version 3): a sync opens with one `Summary` frame, a fingerprint for every repository it names, answered with the peer's (`None`: not held there, a folder it has not joined, never tried again in that sync); only those that differ get a session, four at a time, the home workspace first and alone (it lists the projects; projects new to this node are then held and synced). A sync with nothing new is one round trip per peer. A sync someone asked for (the interval's, `stitch sync`, a nudge) asks the peer to seal its open turns first, as before; a push does not.
  - **One connection per peer, kept:** dialed once (or accepted) and used by either side for every stream: summaries, sessions, blobs, questions. A kept connection that brings no first answer in 15 seconds is taken for dead (a peer gone to sleep keeps it open until QUIC's idle timeout), dropped and dialed afresh, once; a question is never asked twice. A node revoked loses its connections when the revocation comes. The phone drops them when the network may have changed.
  - **Blobs out of the session:** in version 3 a session carries files only; afterwards each side that keeps every blob fetches what it lacks from the peer on that connection, 8 at a time, each resumable (`BlobStore::resume`: what came is kept in `tmp/partial-<hash>` and `GetBlobAt` goes on from its offset); a blob the peer does not hold is not asked of it again for 10 minutes. Sessions and transfers are given up after 30 seconds without a frame, not after 120 seconds in all: a large catch-up that moves takes as long as it needs.
  - **Measured after** (2026-10-27, scratch nodes on gamer and geek with copies of their records, 20 repositories, about 11,000 files, LAN through iroh's hole punching, `tools/p2p-bench`): a sync with nothing new, 40 ms of exchange with the peer (the summary of all 20, sealing and refreshing on both sides), 0.11–0.15 s in the node with what follows a sync, 0.3 s for `stitch sync` from the shell (0.5 s before; the rest is the command starting); against a node of version 2, 0.16–0.2 s (one kept connection, four at a time). A thread renamed on one node is on the other 1.0 s later (the second of quiet, then 30 ms), either way; before, at the next interval, up to 5 minutes.
  - **Rollout:** a node of version 3 still serves version 2 sessions as they were (blobs inside them). To a node of version 2, a `Summary` is an unknown frame: it drops the stream unanswered, and the new node syncs every repository with it in version 2 as before (a version-3 `Hello` it refuses is retried in version 2). A node of version 2 serves only connections it accepted, so the new node opens streams on a connection that node dialed only once it has shown version 3.
- **A small live channel** (milestone 6) carries streamed tokens, presence and "sync now" nudges. Its first piece is built (2026-10-22, for §2.7 "Continue on another node"): every loop on a node (a chat, a detached turn) streams what it does (text and thinking deltas, tool calls and results, notes, retries, its start and end) into an in-memory feed per thread (`live::Live`, the last 4,000 events, numbered), and another node follows it with `Question::Follow { thread, after, assignment }` over the existing question stream: a long poll, held up to 20 seconds until something comes, gathered 200 ms once it does, answered with what came since `after` (consecutive text joined), whether the turn that took `assignment` is over, and whether some of it is no longer kept (`gap`: the transcript has it once synced). Nothing of it is stored: segments are still sealed at a turn's end and synced (§2.5). Presence and nudges over it are still to come.

**Transport (decided 2026-10-11): our own reconciliation over plain iroh** (Rust, QUIC, NAT traversal, optional relays), not iroh-docs or iroh-blobs. Between nodes:
- **Identity:** each node has an iroh key, kept in its data directory; its public key is its endpoint ID.
- **Trust:** a node accepts connections only from the user's other nodes: those in its local list (`peers.toml`), and those admitted by `peer` records in the home workspace that no `revoke` record cancels. The records spread by sync, so admitting a node on one node admits it on all, and revoking a lost phone from any node cuts it off everywhere the revocation reaches.
- **Pairing** (decided 2026-10-11): a trusted node shows a ticket, as a QR code and as text, with its endpoint ID, its addresses and a 128-bit secret; it is valid once, for 10 minutes, and is withdrawn after 5 wrong secrets. The new node connects to that endpoint ID on `stitch/pair/1`, which authenticates the inviter (QUIC's TLS proves the key), and sends the secret and its name, which proves it saw the ticket. The inviter writes `peer` records for itself and the new node in the home workspace (only its local list without one), and answers with its name, the home workspace's ID and the nodes it trusts. Names are unique among the user's nodes.
- **Discovery:** either iroh's defaults (number 0's relays and DNS address lookup: they work across NATs, and publish the node's ID and addresses to those servers), or direct addresses only. Running our own relay is an option iroh supports.
- **The protocol** is one ALPN, `stitch/node/1`, with one stream per operation: a summary of every repository (version 3), a sync of one repository, a blob fetch, or a question the node answers (its models), on one connection kept per peer. [records.md](records.md) §7 has the frames.

iCloud is an option, not the mechanism.

**Stitch Vault** (product direction decided 2026-10-27, with the user): an optional, end-to-end encrypted store-and-forward service and backup, with rolling recovery history, so devices need not be online together. The client is here (`stitch-vault`, `stitch vault`, experimental) with its protocol, [vault-protocol.md](vault-protocol.md); the hosted service is Fragcolor's, documented with it.

### 2.7 Weaving threads, across nodes

**As built** (decided 2026-10-12; it replaces `ask_model`, the `delegate` tool, `/detach` and `/delegate`). Every piece of work is a thread, and a thread can start another and talk to any: the point of Stitch is to stitch through contexts.
- **`spawn`** starts a thread toward a goal: on this node or another of the user's (`node`), on a model that node runs (`model`, checked by asking that node: one it cannot run is refused with its list and default; an offline node is not refused, its result says it was not reached and who could run it now: "A spawn to a node not reached" below), with or without its tools (`tools`: off is a question to another model), starting from no conversation, this one (`this`), or any thread's (its ID), out of memory unless `remember`, recalling unless `recall: false` (§2.9). It returns the thread's ID at once. A spawned thread lives with the conversation it continues, else with its parent.
- **Where a spawned thread works** (decided 2026-10-16, with the user, after a thread spawned from the phone to work in `~/devel/otter` on the Mac got the phone thread's place, a sandbox without that checkout, and went around it: issue #2): `spawn` takes `place`, a checkout or folder on the node that runs it (`~/devel/otter`), placed as a chat started there is (a checkout seen for the first time becomes its project); its sandbox, instruction files and skills are that place's. The spawner resolves it there first (here, or by asking that node, `Question::Place`): a path that is not a directory there is refused at once; a node that cannot be asked (unreachable, or older) resolves it when it takes the assignment (its `place` field), and one gone by then runs the thread in the spawner's place, its goal saying so. A thread placed elsewhere cannot also start from a conversation (`context`), which it would read from its own place's records: refused, the goal carries what it needs. Without `place` it lives with the conversation it continues, else its spawner, as before; not guessed from the goal. `spawn`'s result names the place it got ("in otter"), so a mismatch shows. **Handing work on** (issue #4): the root prompt (both defaults, a machine's and a phone's) says how: one thread per repository at a time, spawned with it as its `place`; the existing checkout, with worktrees, clones and new folders only when the user or the repository's instructions call for them, said in the goal; nothing on top of what the user asked (cleanups, merges, housekeeping); and a correction to a running thread says whether it goes on or stops, and what it may create or change. Guidance, not a rule the node enforces: two threads on one repository are sometimes right (a review beside a build).
- **`send`** continues any thread with a message, on the node that owns it (its shells, its checkouts), with that thread's model and tools: a follow-up to a spawned thread keeps all it did.
- **A thread's model** (decided 2026-10-26, after a chat on `gpt-6-astra`, picked in the app, was woken by reports and answered on the node's default, Opus 5.5, and a thread spawned on gamer with `openai/gpt-6-astra`, which did run on it, was told by its `spawn` tool "This thread runs on anthropic/claude-opus-5-5" and reported a routing bug that was not there). A thread's model (`meta::thread_model`) is the latest `set` of `model` the user's turns wrote, else its record's `model` (a spawned thread's, carried by its assignment), else whatever node runs it uses its default. A turn typed with a model its client picked (`Request::Chat`'s `model`: the app's picker, the TUI's `/model`; on the node that runs the thread, a chat passed on to it too) writes that `set` when the model changes, so it syncs: the turns nobody types (reports, messages, `send`), a chat that names no model, a compacted continuation and other nodes continuing the thread all run on it. A chat started with no model picked writes nothing and follows the node that runs it, as before. The tools' descriptions name the model the loop runs on (`NodeTools.model`), not the node's default. Not done: the app's picker still shows the client's choice (`ThreadModels`, else Settings'), not the thread's model when it has none of its own.
- **Interjecting** (decided 2026-10-13, after a message to a thread busy for an hour arrived an hour late): a `send` to a thread whose loop runs waits for that turn to end, then starts the next. With `interject` (the tool's option, `stitch send --interject`), a loop running on that thread on the node that runs it takes the message between its steps, as the user's typing and job notes are (§2.3), the transcript naming its sender (`thread <id>`), the model told who it is from and that its last reply goes back to it. The reply goes back by report: when that loop ends (whatever loop it is: the thread's first turn, one woken by a message, a resumed one, the user's chat), each thread whose message it took gets a report of its own with the loop's last reply and outcome, marked `woven = true` (and `wake = false` when sent so), except the one its own report goes to already; that sender's own report then has news (no `[nothing new]`). Each sender gets its reply from the loop that read its message, and only those that took one, so a reply never comes from a turn that did not see the question; the thread's own report (to whoever started it) is unchanged. A woven reply is not the thread's own: its state, whether it reported, and a cancellation are read from its other reports. With no loop running it starts a turn, as without. A thread run by another node gets an `assignment` with `interject = true`, and that node does the same when it claims it. A message pushed to a loop that ends before taking it starts a turn of its own, its reply to the sender, as a plain `send`. The tool's result says which happened ("woven into its running turn", "no turn runs on it, so it starts a turn", or, for another node, passed on to it to do either).
- **Reports:** when a spawned or messaged thread's turn ends, its last reply is a `report` to whoever spawned or messaged it. It is delivered on the node that owns that thread, once (the delivered message's header carries the report's ID first, then the node and the thread it is from, by ID and title, so the agent can `send` to it, and who started that thread, since a report can come from a thread another chat or a sub-thread started and pointed here: `[Report <report> from <node>: thread <thread> "<title>", started by this thread | thread <id> "<title>" | the user]`): by **`wait`**, which returns reports as they come in (and counts what those threads spent toward the waiting thread's budget), or by waking the thread with it. A `wait` ends on an event, never on a guessed time (decided 2026-10-13, after waits of a default ten minutes held a chat while the user wrote to it): when the reports are in, or as soon as something else comes for the waiting thread (the user's message, a job's note, which follows the result, "Cut short: …"); `minutes` is a deadline the work has, given only then (at most a day). A spawned thread gets at most what its parent has left of its budget.
- **Reports that say what they are** (decided 2026-10-13, after a night away: reports held for hours arrived unmarked, one thread sent five, some came from threads the receiver never started, and some said done when no shell had run):
  - **Late:** a report delivered 10 minutes or more after it was written says so under its header: `[Late: written <local time>, <how long> ago, and held until now: what it says may be out of date.]`.
  - **Together:** the reports due to a thread at one delivery go to it as one message (one turn woken, not one each); reports its loop left untaken wake it once, together.
- **How reports wake the thread they go to** (decided 2026-10-13 with the user, after a night of late reports cost a model turn each for nothing):
  - **Merged, by default.** A report that comes while the thread works is woven into its loop, as before. One that comes while it is idle is gathered: the reports that follow join it until none came for `[detached] gather_secs` (default 5 seconds, a minute at most in all), then they go to the thread together, as one message, once: into a loop that runs on it by then (the user's chat), else waking it for one turn. Each keeps its header, and its late mark when it was held. A thread idle for long is woken all the same, not held until the user opens it: a report is often what it ended its turn waiting for, and work that should not wake it says so (below).
  - **Kept, on request:** `spawn` and `send` take `wake` (default true); with `wake: false` (the CLI's `--no-wake` on `stitch chat --detach` and `stitch send`) the work runs as any other, but its report is kept for the user and never wakes the thread it goes to. The report says so (`wake = false`); the thread's TUI shows it as it lands while the thread is open, and on opening the thread shows those written since it last ran. `wait` for that thread still returns it (the agent can ask for it); `wait` without threads does not count it. A spawned thread's own reports to whoever started it follow the spawn (its record says `wake = false`); a reply to a `send` follows that send.
  - **Nothing new:** a detached thread that already reported and is woken by reports alone (the replies to what it spawned or sent) is told it may answer just `[nothing new]`; then its turn writes a report with `news = false`, keeping its last outcome, which is never delivered (a `wait` for it still sees it). Its own words decide, because only it knows whether a reply changed what it said.
  - **Outcome:** `done` unless the agent's last reply starts with `Blocked:` (`blocked`) or `Not done:` (`not-done`), which a detached thread's prompt asks for when the work was not done, or every tool call its turn made failed (`not-done`, the report starting `Every tool call it made failed (n of n).`: the work never ran, whatever the reply says). The agent's words come first since it knows; the count catches the case where it does not say so. `failed`, `stopped`, `over-budget` and `cancelled` are as before.
- **`models`** lists what a node runs, with the default and quick models, prices (input/output per million tokens: hardcoded in `pricing::list_price`, Anthropic's, Google's, Moonshot's and DeepSeek's known ones, since no provider's model list carries them; a setting gives the rest) and the reserved ones marked.
- **The user's models** (decided 2026-10-26, with the user; `modelset`). *Why:* agents spawned everything on the default; a first cut ranked every model in tiers (top, mid, light) and told the agent to pick the cheapest that fits, which made each spawn a choice among dozens: replaced the same day by two names. **The default model and the quick model**, synced: `model-choice` records in the home workspace (`role` `default` or `quick`, `model`; `removed = true`), the latest of a role standing, as variables are (§2.19); `stitch model default|quick [<provider/model> | --unset]`. A project's `.stitch/settings.toml` may set its own (`[models] default`, `quick`; synced with the `place` record, §2.14), which wins there.
