A thread naming no model runs on: its project's default, else the node's `default_model` (*2026-11-03, with the user:* a node's own, set by hand in its `config.toml` and unset by default, wins over the synced one, so one node can run another model than the fleet; `quick_model` beside it does the same for the quick model, a node's default alone leaving the synced quick one standing; `stitch model default|quick` and `ls` say when this node keeps its own), else the synced one, else a built-in one (*2026-10-31:* the default of the first provider this node has credentials for, `modelset::BUILTIN`: signed-in subscriptions before API keys, each in the order anthropic, openai, gemini, grok, kimi, deepseek; custom endpoints are never picked; a model marked reserved, or missing from a provider list this node already holds, is skipped, and no list is fetched for it; nothing persists, so `stitch auth login` or `logout` changes it at once; with no credentials at all, `DEFAULT_MODEL`); one this node cannot run (its provider not here, or an official endpoint without credentials) gives way to the node's own, said in its log. The quick model, when none is set (project, synced), is the mid tier of the resolved default's provider when that default is the provider's own built-in one (a Haiku or custom-endpoint default keeps itself as quick) (anthropic `claude-sonnet-5-5`, gemini `gemini-3.5-flash` (*2026-11-04:* the gemini pair was `gemini-3.1-pro`/`gemini-3-flash`, ids Gemini's list does not have; now `gemini-3.1-pro-preview`/`gemini-3.5-flash`), kimi `kimi-k2.7-code`, deepseek `deepseek-flash`), else the default itself: openai has none named, since nothing in the code says which of its `gpt-5.6-*` is the mid tier, and grok none, no model name being known. `stitch model ls` and `stitch model default|quick` mark a built-in choice `(built-in)`. `spawn`'s `model` takes `quick` (the quick model, else the default) and `default` besides `provider/model`; an alias goes to the node that starts the thread as is and is resolved there, for its place. The default prompt says one thing: quick, self-contained work (a lookup, a summary, a small check or edit) is spawned with `model: "quick"`, else the model is left out. **Settings by pattern**, synced too: `model-setting` records (`pattern`, `price`, `note`, `reserved`), `stitch model set <pattern> [--reserved [false]] [--price in/out] [--note …]` (only what it is given; an empty price or note clears it), `stitch model rm <pattern>`, `stitch model ls [--node]` (the list and all that is set). `*` globs; where patterns overlap, the most specific says first, field by field. An agent's `stitch model` asks, as `stitch var` does. **Reserved models** (the user: argon, astra and fable "never, unless I ask"): `stitch model set '<pattern>' --reserved` marks a model, and an agent's `spawn` on it is refused unless a message the user wrote (`from` `user@…`) in the spawning thread or a thread above it (`spawned-by`) names it: its ID, its name, or a word of its name that is its own (`fable`, `astra`, `argon`; not `claude`, `pro`, `flash`, numbers). The message the spawning step answers is written only with that step, after its calls, so the loop holds the step's user texts for its calls meanwhile (`chat::unwritten_user_texts`): "ask fable…" and the spawn in the same turn count. Matching patterns merge field by field, the most specific first, so a price for one model keeps a family's `reserved`. Not checked: the user's own picks (the app, `/model`, `/spawn`, the CLI), schedules, and `default`/`quick` (the user's own choices). The TUI and phone show the effective model resolved by the node (2026-10-27, §2.15), not the node-local config alone. The TUI has `/spawn [@node[:model]] <goal>` (alone: pick the node, then its model) and `/send [thread] [message]`; the CLI `stitch chat --detach [--on <node>] [--context this|<thread>] [--no-tools]` and `stitch send <thread> [message] [--from <thread>] [--interject]`.
- **Work for another node is an `assignment` record** in the repository of the thread it concerns: `node`, `goal` (the message), `thread` (the ID a new thread takes, or, with `continue = true`, the thread to continue), optional `model`, `budget`, `tools = false`, `memory = true` (the new thread stays in memory), `recall = true` (out of memory, it recalls; an assignment without either recalls nothing, as one from before 2026-10-29 meant), `interject = true` (a message for the turn running), where the conversation was (`continues` with `continues_call`, the spawning call, whose step is written right after, or `continues_at`, a segment), and `reports-to`. **Naming the node is the placement:** only it claims, so no lease: a new thread by starting it (its system prompt, tools and checkouts), with its history starting from the conversation it continues up to the spawning call; a message by a `claim` record, then a turn of that thread.
- **A thread at work reads so on every node** (decided 2026-10-17, after a thread on the Mac, sent a follow-up from the Linux box, worked for minutes while `threads` there and the app's thread list still said "idle: reported done": a detached thread's state was its last report's outcome, and nothing written said a new turn had started). A thread that reported is `running` again, from its records alone (`chat::turn_open`, so `threads`, `stitch threads`, the TUI and the app all read it so): from a message sent to it (an `assignment` with `continue = true`, which now also references the thread) until a report of any kind after it (its turn's own, or the reply to a message woven into a loop such as the user's chat), and from a turn the node that runs it starts (a turn nobody typed: a message, reports) until its own report after it. Such a turn writes, as it starts, a `claim` of the thread (`thread`, with `reports-to` and `wake = false` when the turn reports elsewhere than its spawner or is kept) and syncs its repository with the peers at once. Before its first report a thread reads as running as before, so nothing is written. A turn cut off by its node stopping is resumed at that node's next start like a first turn, its report to the claim's `reports-to` (a turn cut off before its first step was written reports `stopped`, saying to send again), so a thread never stays "working" with nothing at work. Clocks: the comparison is by creation time across nodes, so a skew of seconds can misread a turn of seconds.
- **A turn runs under the rules of the node that runs it** (clarified 2026-10-17, after a suspicion that a message sent from a node in yolo made the receiving node's turn yolo too: it does not, and did not). Nothing of the sender's approval mode travels: an `assignment` (a spawn or a message) carries only `approval = "read-only"` for a spawn, never looser than its spawner, and a report is text. The node that takes it runs the turn with its own `[shell] approval`, its own allow lists and grants, and the thread's own read-only mode (the user's switch, else its record's). The records of that case (`…mbp-ikkk`, 2026-10-17) agree: the Mac was in `sandbox` mode throughout; its later turns asked nothing because their commands ran in a sandboxed session (named "outside", but started without `outside`) inside `~/devel/stitch-browser`, which the sandbox writes as a `<checkout>-<name>` sibling, while the earlier `cp` and `git commit` ran in a session really outside the sandbox and were approved by the user.
- **Where a thread runs** (decided 2026-10-13, after one thread, used from two machines, ran on both at once: its histories forked, a report woke the copy the user was not looking at, and commands ran on the Mac while the user chatted on Linux). A chat runs on the node of the client it is typed into (its shells have that client's environment). A turn nobody typed (a message sent to it, reports) runs on the node that continued the thread last: the writer of its latest segment as this node knows it, else the node that started it; `send` goes there, reports are delivered there and written reports nudge it. Before any turn the node catches up: a turn nobody typed syncs the thread's repository with the peers, in parallel, then runs only if this node still runs it (a message is passed on as an assignment to the node that does, reports are left to it); a chat syncs first when another node has written to the thread. After a chat, reports held for the thread are delivered here, where it now runs. Every turn's context says where it runs ("On node linux: its shells, files, checkouts and tools are linux's"), since the system prompt names the node the thread started on. **How long a turn's start waits on a peer** (decided 2026-11-03, branch `catchup-fast`: one sleeping phone held every turn's start 5 seconds): 1 second for a peer last reached on the local network (a direct path to a private, link-local or loopback address, `Seen::on_lan`; one never reached, by the addresses `peers.toml` lists), 3 seconds for one reached through a relay, a tailnet (100.64/10 is not local) or the internet, and none for one whose last attempt failed under a minute ago (`peers-seen.toml`'s `failed`). A catch-up not over by then is not cancelled: it goes on in the background (reached within 10 seconds, over within two minutes, `weave::catching_up`), what it brings is indexed by its session as it lands (a sealed segment of the thread's, or another node's, is merged as any sync's is, mid-turn too), and once it ends one a turn stopped waiting on is taken in as a peer's sync is (`after_sync`: answers, assignments, a rewind elsewhere). One that could not reach its peer is noted failed, so the next turns do not wait on it. One catch-up runs per peer and repository: a turn starting meanwhile waits on the one running. The trade: a turn may start before a slow peer's latest steps of the thread are here, and fork it as two loops started at once do (below). A thread resuming at start with an open approval keeps its patience (each peer 5 seconds, tried again for 30 seconds until one is reached, §2.6). Two loops started at the same moment on two nodes still fork the thread (its main line, the longest, is followed and the other continuation is told to the model, §2.4); a lease would close that, later.
- **A message typed into another node's turn is refused** (decided 2026-10-18, after `…isev`: gamer's detached loop sat in a long `shell_run`, the phone's Fork & Resume failed unseen, and the user's next message, typed into the thread on the phone, ran a second loop of it there beside gamer's). A chat (`Request::Chat`, every client: the TUI, the CLI, the app) on a thread whose turn another node's loop has, as `chat::unfinished` says it for a Resume (`elsewhere` naming another node: a detached loop at work there, or a turn whose last step it wrote and never ended), is refused before anything is written (before compacting too), and again once caught up with the peers; the error names the node and the two ways on: send it to that turn (`stitch send <thread> --interject`, `send` with `interject`, the app's composer) or fork the thread. A turn of this node's own (its detached loop) is unchanged: the chat waits for it. A turn cut off on a node that is gone stays that node's: a message sent to it starts a turn there once it is back, or the thread is forked.
- **Continue on another node** (decided 2026-10-22, with the user: a chat started on the phone, whose node runs no programs, ran for a while; later the user wants to keep chatting from the phone but have the turns run on gamer, where the shells, checkouts and tools are, the phone a remote for that thread on that node; the same for any thread and node pair, from the app and the TUI). Built on what exists, not beside it:
  - **Recorded as the user's `set` of `runs-on`** on the thread (records.md §6; only the user's counts, as for read-only): a node's name, or `""` when brought back. Every node reads it; it travels by sync like any record and works offline. Not a field of the thread's record (immutable) and not a lease: naming the node is the placement, as for assignments.
  - **Where its turns run:** `weave::runner_in`, which `send`, report delivery and the turns nobody typed already follow, gives the named node first; brought back, the node that brought it back, until a later segment of another node's. A chat (`Request::Chat`, every client) on a thread set to run on another node is not run where it is typed: the message goes there as an `assignment` (`continue = true`, `interject = true`, new `chat = true`, from the user, no `reports-to`), woven into the loop running there or taken as a turn of its own, as the app's composer already sends to another node's turn. That turn is a chat's: labelled `user@<node>` as typed, its reply kept (`wake = false`) rather than waking whoever spawned the thread, a detached thread's budget lifted as when the user writes in it. A Resume there is refused (its turns are that node's). Since 2026-10-22 (the user's follow-up, closing the first version's gaps) the message carries what the client gave it: its files, as the assignment's attachments (their blobs reach that node by sync, and it fetches any still missing before the turn), and the model the client picked (the app's choice, the TUI's `/model`), as the assignment's `model`: that node uses it when it runs it (its provider resolves there), else says so in the turn's feed ("gamer does not run …: this turn runs on … instead") and falls back to the thread's model, else its default. A message woven into a loop already running keeps that loop's model. Messages the client types while that turn runs go to it the same way, each said woven as it is passed on; the client's stop asks that node to interrupt its loop (`Question::Interrupt`); the app suspending only stops following (the turn goes on there).
  - **Following the turn live:** segments are sealed only at a turn's end (§2.5), so the typing node follows the turn through the live channel (§2.6): it asks where the thread's feed stands there, writes the assignment, then asks `Follow` from that point with the assignment's ID, streaming what comes to its client as `Text`, `Thinking`, `ToolCall`, `ToolResult`, `Note` and `Retry`, the replies a chat here streams, so the app and the TUI show it with no change to how they chat. The assignment is tied to the loop that takes it there (`Live::serve`, woven or a turn of its own) and marked done when that loop ends, however (cancelled, over budget, passed on again: `Served`). Then the thread's repository is synced from that node, so the transcript has the turn, and the chat ends (`ChatDone`, `end_turn`, or `interrupted`).
  - **Moving it back** ("Continue here", `/run-on here`): a `set` of `runs-on` to `""`. Chats run where they are typed again; what nobody types runs on the node that brought it back until another continues it. A message still waiting for the other node (it was offline) is taken there once it is back and, finding the thread runs elsewhere now, passed on to where it runs (`turn_with`'s runner check), as a chat still.
  - **The node offline:** the choice is still written. A chat's message is written as its assignment all the same and waits for that node (synced to it when it is back, taken then); the chat ends at once with a note saying so and `stop = "queued"`, nothing run here. Reached but older (no `Follow`): the message is sent, said, the reply shows once synced (`elsewhere`). Not taken within a minute, or three asks in a row failing mid-turn: the chat ends saying the turn goes on there and shows once synced. Nothing is refused for an offline node: the user chose where it runs, and the message is not lost.
  - **Only a node that runs programs** (decided 2026-10-22, the same follow-up): a thread is set to run only on a node with shells, since that is the point of moving it (a phone would run its turns with none). The node is asked (`Question::Runs`, as `models` and places are): it answers `no` with why when no program starts there (iOS, visionOS) or its `[shell] approval` is `off`, and `RunOn` is refused with that reason; a node not reached (or older) is accepted, said so ("not reached to ask …; if it runs none, bring the thread back"). `Request::Runners` asks every peer at once (`<node>\t<yes|no|unknown|other-build>`), and the app's menu offers those that said yes, and those not reached, marked offline. **A peer of another build is said so, not offline** (decided 2026-11-23, after `Reply::Rebind` was inserted above `Lines`: every peer of the older build answered with what the newer one read as another reply, and the phone showed every node offline): an answer to `Question::Runs` that is not `Lines`, or does not decode, is `other-build`, and the clients mark it "(other build)" — the fix is updating one of the two. Peer replies are numbered by position (postcard): new ones go at the end of `Reply`, pinned by `the_replies_peers_of_other_builds_decode_keep_their_indices`.
  - **A new chat on another node** (decided 2026-11-04, the user: a new chat on the phone always started on the phone, to be moved after its first turn; the app and the web client now pick the node before the first message, beside the place and the model). `Request::Chat`'s `node` (local-54), for a new thread only: the node the client talks to writes the thread itself with no step (as a fork is: its title from the message, a system prompt of this node's with no context, so the node that runs it adds its own instructions and skills with the first message and says where each turn runs; the client's tool, skill and memory choices), sets its `runs-on` to that node, and passes the message on as any chat into a thread continued there (above): the client is told the thread at once and follows the turn as it streams back. Built on the existing path, so the node that runs it needs nothing new (a node from before it takes the assignment as any). Refused before anything is written when the node is not one of the user's or says it runs no programs; one not reached is taken at its word, its message waiting for it. The default stays this device (the phone, the web client's node). **The app:** a new chat's toolbar has a node menu beside its place (`NodeMenu`, shown when another node runs programs: Here, then each runner, offline ones said so; fixture `newchat`); once its thread is known its header says "Runs on <node>". **The web client:** an "on" picker beside "in" in the new thread's header (`Runners`). Tested with `a_new_chat_put_on_another_node_runs_its_first_turn_there`.
  - **Clients:** `Request::RunOn { thread, node }`, `RunsOn` and `Runners`; the TUI `/run-on <node>` and `/run-on here`, its status line `· runs on <node>`; the app's thread menu, a "Runs on" section (Continue on each of the user's other nodes that runs programs, Continue here), and "Runs on <node>" under the title. **The owner is the default for the app too** (decided 2026-11-02, the user, as for the web client, §2.24): a chat from the in-process app (`Peer::App`) into an unpinned thread another node owns runs on its owner when that node runs programs (`Peer::owner_default`: `App` and `Web`; the TUI and other socket processes run it where typed). The app shows the thread's owner as "Runs on <owner>" (`owner_elsewhere` in the FFI, when the owner is among the runners offered) and offers the other runners, and always "here" while the thread runs elsewhere (decided 2026-11-02, the user: the phone is often just asked about a thread, as before): "Continue here" on a device that runs programs, **"Ask here (no shells)" on one that does not** (`runs_programs_here`: a phone), a `RunOn` none (`runs-on` = ""), which has the turn run on the phone with its models and no shell tools; the other runners then offer the way back. Before this the phone ran such a thread itself, with no programs. Tested with `the_web_client_continues_a_foreign_thread_on_its_owner_by_default` (web, app and TUI peers); the `owner` UI fixture shows the header. Not done: a thread compacted there goes on in a new thread the follower does not follow (it shows once synced); a message woven into a loop that ends without taking it starts a turn there that the follower no longer follows.
- **Spawning into a busy place** (decided 2026-10-18, after two writable threads ended up in one checkout): a writable spawn into a place where another writable thread works (on the same node, in the same directory: a detached thread working or blocked, run there, not read-only; another node's by its record's `cwd`, else its repository) is started all the same, and `spawn`'s result names that thread and says the two may write over each other. Never refused (a review beside a build is sometimes right, §2.7 "Handing work on"); read-only spawns say nothing. `Found` (a peer's answer to `Question::Place`) carries the directory for it.
- **The 2026-10-19 incident** (`…iphone-sryi` → `…mbp-vyvm` → `…mbp-grpx` → `…mbp-45ts` → `…mbp-6379`, read from the records): vyvm, a thread sryi had spawned with a 40-minute budget and that the user then chatted with from the phone, spawned grpx on the Mac with `context: this`. The copy grpx got was cut correctly before the spawning call (it never saw the call or its result), but it carried the phone's turn notes ("# Where this turn runs: On node iphone … no programs run here"), the latest thing in it, and grpx's goal came as a plain user message. grpx reasoned "I'm on iphone, which has no shells" and spawned its own goal on the Mac again (45ts), which did the same (6379). 45ts cancelled 6379 on seeing grpx at work in the same folder and stopped; grpx then saw `grpx … working` in `threads`, did not know its own ID, and reported `Blocked:` "to avoid a duplicate", naming itself. Meanwhile every child had been capped to the 6 minutes vyvm had left, unsaid; grpx's blocked report reached the phone after vyvm's deadline, so vyvm's turn did not run, and the report went only into vyvm's over-budget report to sryi: the thread the user was talking to never showed it. A correction sent to grpx later was refused the same way. What follows was decided from it (2026-10-19, the five fixes the user asked for):
- **Who a thread is** (decided 2026-10-19): every turn's context, beside "Where this turn runs", says `This thread is <id>: that ID is you` (the node in an ID being where it started), so a new thread has it in its prompt and every turn of any thread on any node has it; a fork or a compaction, a thread of its own, is told its new ID at its first turn (the prompt it took names the original's). `threads` marks the calling thread `(you)`.
- **Who a spawned thread is** (decided 2026-10-19): a spawned thread's first message carries, after its goal (read last), Stitch's line: you are thread `<id>`, running on node `<node>`, spawned by thread `<parent>`, the goal above is yours; with a conversation, that it is the spawner's (or that thread's), copied for its context, its turns, nodes, tools and spawns not yours, and, for `context: this`, that it ends where the spawner called `spawn` to start it, so the thread started for this goal is you and nobody else is doing it; then "do it yourself, here, with your own tools", spawning only for a separate part or for another node, else `Blocked:`. The goal's sender is `thread <parent>`, not `user@<node>`. The copied conversation drops the spawner's turn notes (`Part::Context`: where its turns ran, its instructions), which were its own, and the message that made the spawning call is replaced by a line ("here this thread called `spawn` …; what it said with the call is left out"), with the calls its step made before it: what the spawner said as it delegated read as the child's intent. A thread restarted before its first step gets the same line.
- **Delegation does not recurse** (decided 2026-10-19): a `spawn` with tools whose goal is the spawning thread's own, or that of a thread above it (`spawned-by`, as far as the records go), word for word or nearly (Jaccard similarity of their words at least 0.8: the incident's goals, reworded a little at each step, were 0.94 to 0.99 alike; a goal and its spawner's distinct one, 0.35), is refused with why and what to do instead (do it, spawn a separate part, or report `Blocked:`). One exception: a thread on a node that runs no programs (the phone) hands its goal to another node, which is how work for a machine gets there. A backstop: a thread 6 spawns below one nobody spawned spawns nothing more. **Not barred:** a spawned thread's writable spawn into its own place (on its own node) is started, and the result says it was spawned to work there and should hand on only a separate part, never its goal. Barring it would also bar an orchestrator spawned to split a large job across parallel threads; the duplicate goal, which the refusal catches, is what recursion looks like, and the header and the thread's own ID take away what drove it. Questions to another model (`tools: false`) are never refused.
- **A budget cut to the spawner's remainder is said** (decided 2026-10-19): a spawned thread never gets more than its spawner has left (unchanged), and `spawn`'s result now says so when that cut what was asked ("Its budget is this thread's remainder: it must end by 14:06 (6 minutes from now)").
- **The user writing in a spawned thread lifts its budget** (decided 2026-10-19, after vyvm, which the user chatted with from the phone, kept the deadline sryi had spawned it with: its chats ran, but every thread it spawned after 14:06 was over budget at once (`…xed6`) and every report to it was held): a chat message from the user into a detached thread that has a budget writes a user `set` of `budget` (docs/records.md §6) to no limits, once. A thread's budget is that `set` when there is one, else its record's `budget` field (`weave::budget_of`); a compacted thread carries a lifted budget as lifted, and what its whole compaction chain spent counts against it (2026-11-20 with the user, §2.15 "A chain is one budget"). From then on it is the user's chat: reports and messages that wake it run, and what it spawns gets the budget it asks for (an explicit `budget_minutes`/`budget_usd` still applies to that child). A thread only the agents talk to keeps its spawn budget, and its children never get more than it has left.
- **A spawn that could not run is refused** (decided 2026-10-19): the `spawn` tool refuses, with why, when the budget asked for or the spawner's remainder leaves the child nothing, instead of starting a thread that is over budget before its first step; the refusal says a message from the user lifts the budget. (`Request::Detach` with a parent, the CLI's, is not refused: the user asked for it.)
- **Reports are never dropped unseen** (decided 2026-10-19). Delivery traced: a report written (here, or arrived by sync) is delivered by `deliver` on the node that runs the thread it goes to, once (`delivered`), unless a `wait` there takes it; while a loop runs on that thread it is woven into it (taken between steps; one the loop ends without taking wakes the thread after it, `Steering`'s drop); idle, it is gathered for a few seconds and wakes it for one turn (`gather`, then `turn`). A turn that runs elsewhere hands the reports back for that node's `deliver`. Two paths lost a report from the thread's sight: a turn not run because the thread is past its budget (the report went only into the thread's own over-budget report to its spawner: the incident), and a report to a cancelled thread (logged, nothing else). Now, in both, what came is written into the thread itself as a step with no model call: the report (or message) as it would have been woven in, then Stitch's line on why no turn ran, so the thread shows it on every node, the user's next message continues from it, and the report counts as had (`has_report`). The over-budget report to the spawner still carries it, except reports written by the thread it would report to (its spawner's replies to what it sent there while it ran): those are only kept in it, and with nothing else left no over-budget report is written, or the spawner would get its own replies back as new reports and answer them again (issue 2026-11-11T154611Z-beast-5vp5). A wake carries only reports not had already: a report a loop did not take is handed to a turn that waits for the thread's lock, `deliver` may hand it again meanwhile with what came since, and the later wake then says only the new ones (`detached::take_reports`), instead of the same reports again in each wake (same issue). The parent's running turn is not a loss: a report woven into it and not taken (its last step called no tools) wakes the thread when the turn ends. Not fixed here: a phone asleep holds what comes for its threads until it wakes (the incident's report arrived 6 minutes late), which the late mark already says.
  - **Delivered once, across restarts and compactions** (decided 2026-10-31, issue evuq: two reports to a user's chat woke it again at every restart of gamer, each "[Late: … held until now]", six times in a day). `delivered` is in memory only; what makes delivery durable is the thread's own transcript, where a delivered report is always written (`has_report`, the text `[Report <id>`). The report's `reports-to` names the thread it was written for, but once that thread is compacted, reports go to its newest continuation (`fork::current`), and `deliver` looked only in the thread named: never found, the report was delivered again after every restart. `deliver` now looks in the whole lineage (`fork::lineage`: the thread and those compacted from it). No separate delivery record: the transcript already is one, on every node.
- **Thread IDs and nodes** (decided 2026-10-19): an ID names the node a thread started on, as every record ID names its writer, and a thread runs where it was continued last (above), so the two differ for a thread continued elsewhere. IDs stay as they are: immutable and referenced everywhere, they cannot follow a thread that moves, and a format without the node would lose the uniqueness the node gives them across nodes that write offline. What runs it is said apart instead: `threads` lists `<id>: runs on <node> (started on <node>)` (the second part only when they differ), `spawn` says `Spawned thread <id> to run on <node>`, `send` `Sent to thread <id>, which runs on <node>`, and the turn's context says what the node in its own ID means.
- **A spawn to a node not reached** (decided 2026-11-09, with the user, after spawns to a node that was off returned "Spawned thread … to run on <node>" and their spawners then read "start unconfirmed" for up to an hour, `UNCONFIRMED`). `spawn` naming another node first asks it `Question::Runs` (`elsewhere::reach`, reached within `CONNECT`, 8 seconds); one not reached is asked nothing more (the model and place questions would each wait out their own timeouts; the place is labelled "unchecked: <node> not reached"). Its assignment is written and queued as before: nothing refused, nothing rerouted, since the user named the node. The result says so first and at once: "<node> not reached (last seen <age> ago | never reached from here): thread <id> is queued there and starts when <node> is back; nothing runs it until then. `cancel <id>` withdraws it. Reached now and able to run it: phone (this node, linux x86_64, holds otter, runs anthropic/claude-opus-5-5); …. Leave it queued, or `cancel` it and spawn it on one of those.", then what a spawn's result says otherwise (its place, where its report goes), or "No other node is reached now that could run it." The alternatives are the other nodes (this one included) asked all at once (`elsewhere::alternatives`, 5 seconds at most for all, `ALTERNATIVES`): `Question::Runs` (which now also answers `platform: <os> <arch>` and `default: <model>`, lines an older asker ignores and an older peer omits, so its entry just has less), `Question::Place` when a place was asked for (holds it, or not there, or unchecked; those holding it listed first), and the model's list when one was named (`node::lists_model`; a node whose list rules it out is left out). Nodes that run no programs (a phone, a shell turned off) are left out of a spawn with tools. **No automatic fallback:** the model decides, to leave it queued or `cancel` and spawn elsewhere. Tested by `a_spawn_to_a_node_not_reached_says_so_with_who_could_run_it`. **Proposed, not built:** node capability records or tags (the "Capabilities record" below) and node selectors with fallback in `spawn` (`node: "gamer|linux"`: the first reached that matches), which would let a spawn route itself instead of the model choosing.
- **A spawn is reachable at once:** `send` to a thread spawned on another node and not synced back yet goes through its assignment (the node named there takes the message once the thread runs, new threads before messages in one pass); one about to start here once its spawning step is written is waited for a moment. `stitch threads` lists such a thread as `[starting on <node>]`.
- **Nudged, not polled:** the spawning node syncs with the named one as soon as the step is written; a report for a thread owned elsewhere is synced to its owner right away; a node that receives records by sync folds memory, wakes approvals waiting there, stops detached threads cancelled elsewhere (a `report` written by another node), claims its assignments and delivers the reports for its threads. Answering an approval syncs with every peer.
- **Cancelling** a thread running on another node writes its report here and nudges that node, which stops it. **An unconfirmed remote start can be withdrawn too** (2026-10-26, after `…mbp-knnd` stayed at "starting on another node" and `cancel` failed with "no thread here"). When only the spawn assignment is held, cancellation writes a `cancelled` report referring to both the future thread and that assignment, using the assignment's repository and reporting parent; no fabricated thread record or model call. Repeated cancellation is harmless. The target checks this withdrawal before claiming the assignment and again after setup before its first model call; if it already started, the usual synced cancellation stops its loop. The result says the assignment was withdrawn, not that the remote process has already stopped. A disconnected target cannot be stopped until it receives the record. `threads` (including `all`) lists assignment-only work and its withdrawal; `wait` says "start unconfirmed", not "still working", when no thread record has arrived. That is not an assertion that the target is offline or stalled: its start may not have synced back. **A start that never comes ends** (2026-10-27, after `…gamer-bjsh`, an update job spawned to gamer at 04:18, stayed "working" on the dashboard for six hours with no title, node or place: gamer had taken the assignment, written its memory fact, and failed to start it, at each pass, saying so only in its log: "`quick`: name a model as provider/model", its build being from before those names, the update it was asked to install; two read-only checks spawned with `quick` that morning stayed unconfirmed the same way). A spawn naming `quick` or `default` for another node now writes the model this node resolves it to as `model`, the name as `model_as` (resolved by the node that starts it, as before; an older one reads `model`). On the target, a new thread's start that fails (`detached::start`: the model or its credentials, the place) is tried again at each pass as before, but once it has failed three times over ten minutes (`START_GIVE_UP`) the target gives it up: a `failed` report (`error` field: the error), referring to the thread and the assignment, goes to the spawner, and no later claim (after a restart either) tries it again. On the asking side a pending start stays `working` ("start unconfirmed") for an hour (`UNCONFIRMED`), then reads `idle`, outcome `unconfirmed` ("not started: <node> has not confirmed it in <time> …; `cancel` withdraws it"), no longer counted as working; a `failed` report makes it "ended: failed to start on <node>: <error>". Rows of record-less threads (`threads`, `stitch.live.threads`, the dashboard) take their goal, title, node, place, parent and start time from the assignment, so they no longer show as "working… · ? · last step"; the dashboard counts `unconfirmed` with the failed and says "sent …" rather than "last step" for a row with no step.
- **The rest of the jobs proposal is not built** (decided 2026-10-21, with the user, closing issue #5): `watch_command` (a command polled until a condition), JSON-aware `watch_file`, `silent_secs` and a Claude Code adapter (`claude -p --output-format stream-json` sessions as jobs). Every hand-written polling loop in the transcripts (about 30, 2026-10-16/17) watched Claude Code (`claude agents --json`, `tmux capture-pane`); after screen watches (`shell_watch` with `screen`, `screen_gone`, `quiet_secs`, which also catches a command gone silent) and coding moving to Stitch's own threads (the claude-code skill removed 2026-10-19), there was one, a foreground `pgrep` wait for another process's `just quick`, and `watch_file` was used only on plain logs with `quiet_secs`. Polling that remains is `wake_at` or a schedule with an Otter check. Taken up again if transcripts show loops coming back.
- **The model lists and cancels its threads** (decided 2026-10-17, with the user, from the proposal that was `docs/jobs-spec.md`, after an orchestrator kept six threads in its head and could only ask them to stop). Tools of the `weave` group, on every node, the phone included:
  - **`threads {all?, status?}`**: the threads this one spawned (`spawned-by`) or sent to (reports or assignments to it, and those still out), newest first, 30 at most; with `all`, every detached thread this node's records know, on any node. Each: ID (the calling thread's marked `(you)`), the node that runs it (`runner`, said apart from the ID: `runs on <node> (started on <node>)`), its place, its state, its goal's first line, when it started and last stepped, its model calls and their cost so far (usage events), and the first lines of what its model said last. States: `working` (a loop runs on it here, its records say `running`, or it is starting), `blocked: approval <question>: <exact command> (asked on <node>, in <place>; in session …)`, `idle: reported <outcome>` (`send` continues it), `ended` (cancelled, compacted into another). `status` filters on those four words. From the store's records, as `stitch threads` is, plus the node's live state.
  - **`cancel {thread, reason?}`** stops a thread's running turn now, as `stitch cancel` does (`detached::cancel`): its jobs end, its report is written, its loops stop, its sessions close with what runs in them (the command's process group too, §2.3); on another node, the report goes there and that node stops it. The report says who cancelled it and why, then what it did so far, from its records and not a model call: its last words, its last 12 tool calls, and a call waiting on the user's approval (in no written step yet), whose question is withdrawn. The result says the same; that report, going to the canceller, is not delivered to it again. A thread it did not spawn or send to asks the user first (yolo allows it, a read-only thread is refused), as does `job_stop` of another thread's job. A thread cannot cancel itself. `send` with `interject` stays for changing course.
  - **Approvals told precisely:** when a spawned thread asks an approval (a new question on this node), the thread it reports to is told at once if a loop runs on it here, woven between its steps, never woken (the user has the question on every node's attention list, and pushed to the phone): the exact command or call (600 characters), the question, the asking node, the place and session, and how the user answers it. `wait` says the same when it ends on a blocked thread. Each question is told to each thread once, by whichever comes first: the note, `wait`, or `threads`. Answering stays the user's alone. **Proposed, not built:** `deny {question}` for the spawner, declining (never allowing) a child's question at once, so a mistaken push or clone stops without the user; it needs the answer to name who declined (a thread, not the user), and whether a node's own threads may answer questions at all is a rule worth deciding with the user first.

Still to come:
- **Capabilities record.** Each node publishes one: whether it is always on, its power and network, its tools, its local models, and the repositories it holds. Built so far: its MCP servers and the tools of those it shares (§2.14).
- **Assignment to any node that fits,** claimed with a lease (`claim` records with `lease_until`), by placement rules.
- **Pinning.** Tools tied to a machine (shell sessions, SSH, a checkout, a Claude Code session) pin a thread to their node.
- **Phones:** iOS runs work in the foreground and during background processing time (a turn running keeps the app awake for iOS's grace, then stops to resume, §5 milestone 4), and hands long work to nodes that can run in the background. A phone holds every repository too (§2.5): what it does not hold is blobs, fetched when a conversation needs them.

Still open:
- the placement rules;
- what happens to a thread when its node disappears mid-lease;
- how two nodes whose leases conflict across a network split show up. Proposal: as a fork in the graph, like any concurrent work.

### 2.8 Agents as programs

**Deprecated** (decided 2026-11-18, with the user): threads no longer hand work to Claude Code or Codex; the default prompt's instruction to do so is gone, and Stitch's own threads (`spawn`) do the work. The read-only guard on `claude` in `rules.rs` stays, as defence while it is installed. What follows is the earlier decision, kept as history.

Claude Code, Codex and other coding agents are programs a thread runs in its interactive shell (§2.3), like `git` or the test suite (decided 2026-10-11, replacing the worker contract):
- **One-shot:** `claude -p "…"` (with `--permission-mode acceptEdits` to let it edit) or `codex exec "…"`; the final answer is the command's output, which the thread reads, checks (`git diff`, tests) and reports.
- **Interactive:** the thread starts `claude` or `codex` and drives it with `shell_send` and `shell_view`, as a person would.
- **Why:** their harnesses are good, improve without effort here and run on existing plans; driving them as programs needs no per-vendor adapter, event translation or brief format, and the thread's own transcript records what it asked and what came back.
- **Sessions:** both resume a session by ID (`claude -p --resume <id>`, `codex exec resume <id>`); the thread notes the ID it got and resumes it later. Sessions live on the machine that ran them (`~/.claude/projects`, `~/.codex/sessions`), which pins the thread to its node (§2.7); elsewhere it starts a new session and gives it the context from its own transcript.
- **Permissions:** the program runs under the thread's shell rules; its own permission modes (Claude Code's `--permission-mode`, Codex's sandbox) are flags the thread chooses, and the approval of the command line that starts it is the user's say over them.
- **Structured output when it helps:** `--output-format stream-json` / `--json` give machine-readable events; whether threads should parse them (progress, usage for budgets) is decided when budgets need the numbers.

External workers still run through their own CLIs and SDKs. **Native subscription access (decided 2026-10-11):** at the user's request, carry edge talk's managed Anthropic, OpenAI/Codex and Grok OAuth flows and request profiles into the Rust provider layer as well. These compatibility profiles depend on provider endpoints and client identifiers used by edge talk, and may need updates when providers change them; mock tests verify the implemented protocol, not account entitlement.

- `stitch auth login anthropic|openai|grok` prints a browser authorization URL and waits up to ten minutes on an IPv4 loopback callback. PKCE S256 and callback state checks apply to all three. Anthropic uses its verifier as state and JSON token exchange; OpenAI and Grok use independent state and form encoding. `auth status` never prints tokens; `auth logout` removes local access without touching API keys. The TUI exposes `/login <provider> [--file]`, `/logout <provider>`, and `/auth [provider]`; its temporary sign-in panel is outside the transcript.
- Tokens live in data-directory-scoped keychain entries, with atomically replaced owner-only `oauth-<provider>.json` files for `--file` or keychain write failure. They never become records or sync. An OS lock serializes login, logout and refresh across processes. Refresh starts fifteen minutes before expiry, persists rotated credentials before inference, and retains the existing refresh token if the provider omits a replacement. A local sign-out tombstone prevents an unavailable keychain's old credential from reappearing.
- **Tokens are asked for per request, and refreshed on a 401** (decided 2026-10-18, after turns running past an access token's eight hours failed with `401 OAuth access token has expired`: a turn had copied the token once, at its start). A provider on a managed subscription holds a token source (`oauth::TokenSource`, the node's `auth::tokens`) instead of a fixed token, and `Provider::stream` asks it before every request, so each model call of a long turn (and its model and context-window lookups) gets the stored credentials, refreshed when within the margin. A request refused with 401 before anything streamed gets a forced refresh (`auth::credentials_forced`) and is sent once more; a second 401 is the turn's error, never a loop. The forced refresh runs under the lock and only if the stored token is still the refused one, so turns refused together refresh once and the rest take its token. Pasted tokens and API keys are sent as they are, and a 401 on them is not retried.
- A subscription the user signed in to always wins over an API key, stored or in the environment (decided 2026-10-12, reversing edge talk's order; confirmed 2026-10-22 with the user, after a Claude Max limit ran out and nobody could tell which route a node billed: the code had kept edge talk's order, a key winning, and now follows this, `stitch auth status` saying per provider `billed by subscription | API key | none`). Only the built-in provider names at their exact official base URLs can inherit a managed session; custom endpoints and aliases do not. Login takes effect in an already-running node. No request falls back from a failed subscription to an API key or another billing route.
- **An account out of usage is said, not retried** (decided 2026-10-22, from the incident in §5 milestone 4, "Out of usage"). `stitch_providers::Error::OutOfUsage` (`what`: `Limit`, a plan's window that comes back by itself, or `Credit`, credit or quota that does not; `resets_at` when the provider says) is recognised from what the providers answer (`http::out_of_usage`): Anthropic's subscription limit, a 429 with `anthropic-ratelimit-unified-status: rejected` and the reset in `anthropic-ratelimit-unified-reset` (the window in `…-representative-claim`), or "usage credits" for long context; a spending cap (`details.error_code` `enforced_spend_limit_reached`), a 400 "credit balance is too low", a 402; OpenAI's `insufficient_quota`; the Codex backend's `usage_limit_reached` (`resets_at`, `resets_in_seconds`) and `usage_not_included`, also as a `response.failed` event; xAI's "used all available credits or reached its monthly spending limit" (403 or 429); Gemini's 429 whose `QuotaFailure` names a per-day quota. A 429 with a `retry-after` over ten minutes counts too. Per-minute rate limits (a short `retry-after`, Gemini's per-minute quotas) are still retried. The user is told which provider and plan (`Provider::label`), when it resets in the node's time zone, and what to do: wait, or switch model with `/model` and go on; Stitch never switches provider or model by itself.
- Anthropic uses bearer authentication, the OAuth/Claude Code beta headers merged with model betas, `x-app: cli`, edge talk's Claude CLI user agent, and its required cached leading system block. The user's system prompt follows it unchanged. Pasted `sk-ant-oat` tokens get the same profile, but have no managed refresh. Normal API keys retain the ordinary Messages profile. *2026-11-09:* the user agent's version follows Claude Code's releases (`oauth::CLAUDE_USER_AGENT`, now 2.1.321): the subscription refuses new models to older clients (Claude Fable 5.2: "version 2.1.290 or newer is required", a 400 at 2.1.280), so a model launch may need it bumped.
- **Thinking blocks whose prefix changed are dropped, not a 400** (decided 2026-11-09, with Claude Fable 5.2). The API binds each thinking block to what came before it (`system`, `tools`, earlier messages); Fable 5.2 and Mythos refuse a request whose replayed block fails that check on every account ("bound to a different conversation"), and every 5.x model on accounts from 2026-08-31. Stitch keeps messages append-only, but a thread's `tools` can change between turns (it moves to another node, its tool sets, an MCP server that connects), so every Claude request with adaptive thinking sends `thinking.block_binding.prefix_mismatch_behavior: "drop_block"` (beta `thinking-binding-controls-2026-08-01`; models without the check accept and ignore it). The cost: the dropped turns' reasoning, and the cache restarts at the change. *Not built:* tool changes as `tool_addition`/`tool_removal` messages, which would keep the blocks; counting the `input_transformations` the API reports. *Checked live* (`live_vendors.rs`, `fable_5_2_live`, subscription): a tool loop with its thinking replayed, then the same history with a tool's description changed: answered, the block dropped.
- OpenAI subscription requests go to `https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/responses` with the account ID extracted from the access JWT, originator and client headers. Responses requests carry nonempty `instructions`, `store: false`, and replayable encrypted reasoning. Codex omits unsupported `max_output_tokens`, temperature and prompt-cache fields other than `prompt_cache_key`, which it routes by along with the `session-id` header (both the thread's ID, as the Codex CLI sends its session's). The Responses adapter streams text, reasoning summaries and tool arguments, preserves output items for replay, and handles images, PDF files, tool results, usage, refusals and incomplete/failed streams. Completed output items (`response.output_item.done`) are kept by `output_index`: when the terminal response has an empty output array, they supply the saved response and its native replay, without duplicating a populated terminal snapshot. This occurs on Codex PDF requests (reproduced 2026-11-12); relying on the terminal snapshot alone made an answer disappear after streaming, on both iOS and Linux. A stream that emitted text or started a tool call but supplies no completed output fails explicitly rather than saving an empty answer. OpenAI API keys use the public Responses endpoint.
- Grok uses the CLI subscription proxy, `https://cli-chat-proxy.grok.com/v1`, and edge talk's client surface/version headers, for either Responses or Chat Completions. Unlike edge talk's probe, proxy rejection does not silently switch to `api.x.ai`; it reports the error. API keys continue to use the configured public endpoint.
- Subscription usage keeps token counts without presenting API dollar estimates as subscription charges.
- **A tool call's input is always an object on the wire** (decided 2026-10-21, after a model's `send` arguments came as a JSON string holding the object, were stored so, and every later request failed with Anthropic's `tool_use.input: Input should be an object`, the thread stuck for good). Every adapter decodes, as it reads a response, a string holding a JSON object (encoded once or more) into the object: the tool runs on it and the history keeps it (`decode_tool_input`). Every adapter, as it sends a history, sends a call's input as an object (`replay_tool_input`): a string holding one decoded, anything else (an input cut off by `max_tokens`) wrapped as `{"_raw": <input>}`, in the portable content and in Anthropic's native blocks alike, so histories stored before this replay and their threads resume.
- **GitHub sign-in** (decided 2026-10-14, with the user; built the same day, `github.rs`): `stitch auth login github` (`/login github` in the TUI), Accounts in the app's Settings, as edge talk's: the OAuth device flow (RFC 8628) of an OAuth App, so no client secret, no redirect and no server of ours, and it works on a headless Linux node or a phone alike: the node asks for a code, the user enters it at `github.com/login/device` (opened for them where there is a browser), the node polls (`slow_down` adds five seconds) and keeps the token in its keychain like any key (`github`, so an MCP server's `key = "github"` uses it, §2.13), then reads the account (`/user`). Each node signs in on its own; tokens never sync. Scopes: edge talk's (`repo`, `user`, `gist`, `notifications`, `workflow`, `read:org`, `project`, `security_events`, `read:audit_log`; no admin or delete scopes), since the GitHub MCP tools need them and GitHub has no read-only repository scope. The OAuth App keeps token expiration off: refreshing an OAuth App's token needs its client secret, which a device-flow client does not hold. From edge talk's bugs: a reply without `expires_in` is a token that does not expire (edge talk assumed eight hours and then thought valid tokens expired), every form value is percent-encoded, and no part of a token is ever logged. One account per node, on `github.com`; GitHub Enterprise hosts later. Uses: fetching projects on the phone (§2.16), the GitHub MCP server, and later what else needs GitHub. **The OAuth App is edge talk's for now** (`Ov23liZBMqn9tfgy2tzu`, decided with the user), so GitHub's consent page says edge talk; **it must change** to one registered for Stitch (device flow on, token expiration off) before Stitch is used by anyone else.
- **Prompt caching, context windows and token counts** (decided 2026-10-13, with the user, after compaction was found cutting Claude threads at 160k of a 1M window, and OpenAI threads counting cached tokens twice):
  - *Anthropic:* a breakpoint at the end of the system prompt (on the last tool when there is none), so threads with the same tools and prompt share it, and automatic caching of the conversation after it. Every breakpoint has the provider's `cache_ttl`, `5m` or `1h` (an hour's breakpoint may not follow one of five minutes); one-hour writes are priced at twice input.
  - *OpenAI and xAI* cache unasked; a thread's requests carry its ID where the server routes by it: `prompt_cache_key` on the Responses API and OpenAI's Chat Completions, `x-grok-conv-id` on xAI's Chat Completions, Codex's `session-id`. Other compatible servers get neither, since some refuse fields they do not know. `cache_ttl = "30m"` sends `prompt_cache_options` on OpenAI's API (GPT-5.6 and later); the deprecated `prompt_cache_retention` is not sent.
  - *Token counts* mean one thing for every provider: `input_tokens` is the prompt neither read from nor written to the cache (Anthropic's sense; OpenAI's adapters subtract cached and cache-write tokens), so the whole prompt is the sum of the three. Clients show the whole prompt and its cached part. Usage written before this by OpenAI threads counts cached tokens in `input_tokens`.
  - *Context windows* are what the provider says of the model, asked once per node and kept while it runs (a lookup that fails is retried after ten minutes, the guess standing meanwhile): Anthropic's `max_input_tokens`, the model list's `context_window` (Codex) or `context_length` (xAI, OpenRouter, following its pages), llama.cpp's served `n_ctx` (`/v1/models`, else `/props`), Ollama's running model (`/api/ps`), else its `num_ctx` or trained length (`/api/show`), Gemini's native `inputTokenLimit`. OpenAI's API lists none, so its windows are guessed by name from the documented ones. A provider's `context_window` overrides it all. Nothing is looked up for a thread that has sent under 2,048 tokens.
- **Gemini** (decided 2026-10-18, built the same day, `stitch-providers/src/gemini.rs`): a built-in provider, `gemini`, of its own kind, `gemini`, over Google's **native** API (`generativelanguage.googleapis.com/v1beta`), not its OpenAI-compatible endpoint, which `kind = "openai"` can still reach. Why native: the compatible endpoint is a subset, with no video or Files API, no images in the reply, and thought signatures (which Gemini 3 checks on every function call sent back, a 400 without them) only through a Google extension field that the Chat Completions adapter would have to special-case anyway; and only the native API describes its models (`inputTokenLimit`, `outputTokenLimit`, `thinking`). What it does, with edge talk's `prompter-google.shs` and `ChatViewModel.uploadVideoToGemini` as the reference:
  - *Keys:* `GEMINI_API_KEY`, then `GOOGLE_API_KEY` (after `STITCH_GEMINI_API_KEY` and the data directory's file, before the keychain), sent as `x-goog-api-key`, never in a URL (edge talk put it in the query). No subscription sign-in (edge talk had none for Google either). Like `openai` and `grok`, it is asked for models whenever the node lists them, and without a key it is "not listed" (`gemini: no API key configured`).
  - *Models:* `models.list` (`pageSize=1000`, following `nextPageToken`), keeping `gemini-*` models that `generateContent`, without embeddings, speech (`tts`, `transcribe`), live and native-audio models, retrieval (`aqa`), Imagen and Veo, computer-use and robotics models, and `omni` models (they list `generateContent` but answer only Google's Interactions API, seen 2026-10-18). Image models (`*-image*`, `nano-banana*`) stay. What the list says of each model is kept for the process: windows (the bullet above), output limits (a request's `maxOutputTokens` is clamped to the model's; unknown, it is left out unless small) and whether it thinks.
  - *Requests:* `models/{model}:streamGenerateContent?alt=sse`; the system prompt as `systemInstruction`; tools as `functionDeclarations` with `parametersJsonSchema` (JSON Schema as it is: `parameters` takes only an OpenAPI subset, which MCP servers' schemas often leave); tool results as `functionResponse` parts in a `user` turn (Gemini 3.6 and later refuse the old `function` role, edge talk's lesson), named from the call; consecutive turns of one role joined. Thinking models get `includeThoughts` (thought summaries stream as thinking), and `effort` sets `thinkingLevel`. No temperature (Gemini 3 recommends the default) and no safety settings (the defaults).
  - *Replay:* a model turn with a thought signature or an image keeps its parts as Gemini sent them (`Native`, the images without their bytes, which the reply's attachments hold), and goes back unchanged to the same model; to another model, or one with no native copy, it is sent from its content, its first function call carrying Gemini's documented `skip_thought_signature_validator`. Both forms were checked against the live API (2026-10-18, Gemini 3.8 Flash, Flash Latest, the 3.1 Flash and Flash-Lite image models). The text of a native copy is stored twice, as Responses' is (Anthropic's alone is packed, §2.5).
  - *Audio and video in:* inline (`inlineData`) up to 8 MB a file and 14 MB a request (Gemini takes 20 MB of request, base64 included); larger through the Files API (resumable upload, then polled until `ACTIVE`, up to 10 minutes, as videos are processed), remembered per process for 46 of the files' 48 hours, so a thread's video is uploaded once, not on each turn (edge talk kept the file's URI in the message, where it outlived the file). PDFs go the same way; images inline as everywhere.
  - *Media read only where it is taken* (decided 2026-10-18, with the user, closing §4's question 9): an audio or video attachment (up to 2 GB, the Files API's limit) reaches the providers as a file left where it is (`Content::File`: its blob's path and size), not as bytes. Gemini alone reads it: a small one each turn, to send inline; a large one once, to upload, its upload remembered by the blob's path, which names its content, so the file is not even read to know it was sent. Every other adapter sends a line naming it and never opens it. So the history is built for each provider without being built twice: what differs is resolved by the adapter, not the node. Before this, a 100 MB video was read into memory on every turn of every thread holding it.
  - *Images out:* an image model's request asks for `responseModalities: ["TEXT", "IMAGE"]` and carries no tools (they take none, so such a turn is one step; function calls in its history, another model's, are told as text, since these models refuse them even there). The images it answers with are stored as blobs and become the reply's attachments (`image-m3-1.png`), so the user gets them as files: the app shows them in the reply (below, *In the app*), the TUI names them, they sync like the user's attachments, and they go back to the model when the conversation goes on (an edit: "now make it blue"). Interim images of the model's thinking are dropped. This standalone path stays, next to the tool (below).
  - *Images in a model's turn, for every provider* (2026-10-18): the images of a model's turn (an image model's, or `generate_image`'s, below) go to every provider. Gemini takes them in the model's turn (its own, from its native parts); the others refuse images there, so `lift_images` moves each, with the `[image img:…]` line naming it, to the end of the user message after that turn, past its tool results, under a line saying they were made for the turn above (a message of their own when none follows). A Gemini turn replayed natively that holds images its parts do not (a tool's) carries them the same way, after its function responses. Before, other providers dropped them.
  - *Not built:* pricing (no estimate in dollars for Gemini; Anthropic, Kimi and DeepSeek have theirs), Google's explicit context caching (implicit caching is counted: `cachedContentTokenCount`), grounding with Google Search and code execution, Imagen and Veo, speech out.
- **Kimi** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user; built the same day, `stitch-providers/src/anthropic.rs`): a built-in provider, `kimi`, of kind `anthropic` at Moonshot's Anthropic-compatible endpoint (`https://api.moonshot.ai/anthropic`; China's, `https://api.moonshot.cn/anthropic`, is a `base_url` setting), for Kimi K3.1 Pro (`kimi-k3.1-pro`, `kimi-k3.1-pro-highspeed`; `kimi-k3`, and the K2 models until Moonshot retires them at the end of 2026): 1M tokens, images (and video, below), always thinking. *Why that endpoint and not Chat Completions:* Moonshot requires every earlier assistant message back whole, its reasoning included (a 400 without it), and the Anthropic adapter already sends a model's own turns back as they came, signed thinking blocks and all; Chat Completions would have needed the reasoning round trip built first (DeepSeek's, below, now gives it). The adapter knows Moonshot by its host (`is_moonshot`; a Claude proxy at another address stays Claude's) and sends it none of Claude's own fields: no `thinking` (it always thinks; it refuses turning it off), no `eager_input_streaming`, no fallbacks, no Claude Code profile, and only the top-level `cache_control` (the only one it reads; `5m` or `1h`, `cache_ttl` as for Claude). Its effort is `low`, `high` or `max` (default `max`): `minimal` is `low`, `medium` (Stitch's default) `high`, `xhigh` `max`. A bearer key; `MOONSHOT_API_KEY`, then `KIMI_API_KEY` (never `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, though the kind is Anthropic's). Its Messages take no documents: a PDF is a note naming it. Models and windows come from its OpenAI-compatible list (`{root}/v1/models`, `context_length`), not `/anthropic/v1/models`, which it does not document. Prices: $2/$10 per million (K3.1 Pro; Highspeed $4/$20, K3 $3/$15), cache hits a tenth of input, cache writes at once input for five minutes and twice for an hour (not Claude's 1.25 times; `pricing.rs` prices each model's writes as its provider bills them). Its `exceeded_current_quota_error` (a 429: the balance spent) is out of credit; its other 429s are rate limits and retried. *Not built:* video (only Chat Completions takes it, uploaded and named `ms://<file-id>`), Moonshot's own tools (web search). *Checked live* (2026-10-29, `stitch-node/tests/live_vendors.rs`, by hand: `kimi-k3.1-pro` at `low`): a turn with a system prompt and the hour's `cache_control`, a tool loop of three steps with its signed thinking sent back, an image, a history whose tool call was Claude's (no thinking block: answered, no 400), `/v1/models` (its windows) where `/anthropic/v1/models` is a 404, cache writes and hits read. Not seen: a write in the hour's tier (the prefixes were too short to write).
- **Kimi's subscription: not built** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user). The Kimi Code plan (Kimi membership, Plus and above) has an API of its own (`https://api.kimi.ai/coding/` for Anthropic's shape, `/coding/v1` for OpenAI's; `api.kimi.com` in China), with its own model IDs (`k3.1-pro`, `k3.1-pro-highspeed`, `k3`, `k3-256k`, `kimi-for-coding…`) and keys made in the Kimi Code console. *The plan, when built:* a separate built-in provider, `kimi-code`, on such a console key, billed as a subscription (no dollar estimates), its User-Agent Stitch's own; not one `kimi` switching to the plan, since the model IDs differ. **No OAuth** unless Moonshot registers a client for Stitch: its CLI's device login (`auth.kimi.com`, a registered client ID, `X-Msh-Platform: kimi_code_cli`) is allowed for its own clients only, the server checks the platform, and its terms make spoofing a client's identity a suspendable violation (third-party agents asked for registration in MoonshotAI/kimi-code#1795, unanswered). *Risks to handle then:* the plan is for interactive use only ("Don't use Kimi Code for non-interactive automation"; enforced by limiting concurrency, then suspension), so detached threads and schedules on it risk the account: confine it to chats or warn; a request's messages may not pass 2 MB (images must be smaller there); plans below Pro take 256K tokens, not 1M (a 403 naming the limit, to be compacted at); its limits (a 5-hour window, a month's total) are said in words without a reset time.
- **DeepSeek** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user; built the same day, `stitch-providers/src/openai.rs`): a built-in provider, `deepseek`, of kind `openai` at `https://api.deepseek.com` (Chat Completions), key `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY`. Models (October 2026): `deepseek-flash` (DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash; images, JPEG, PNG, GIF and WebP, in user messages only) and `deepseek-v4-pro` (text), both 1M tokens and up to 384K out, thinking by default. *Why Chat Completions:* its thinking mode needs every earlier assistant message's `reasoning_content` back once a request has tools (a 400 without it), which its documentation specifies for Chat Completions; its Anthropic-compatible endpoint takes thinking blocks but says nothing of carrying them. So the Chat Completions adapter now **keeps a turn's reasoning and sends it back** where the server needs it (`reasoning_back`, on for DeepSeek's host): the streamed reasoning is the turn's native content (`{"reasoning_content": …}`, named by the model asked for), and goes back on that model's assistant messages; another model's turns (a thread begun on Claude) go with an empty `reasoning_content`, never another model's thinking. Other servers keep nothing and get nothing back, as before. The same mechanism serves Kimi's Chat Completions when its video is built. `effort` is sent as `reasoning_effort` when set (DeepSeek maps `minimal`…`ultra` to its `low`, `high`, `max`; default `high`); nothing is sent unset, since other servers may refuse it. Cache hits are read from `prompt_cache_hit_tokens` (its caching is automatic, on disk, no writes billed). *Checked live* (2026-10-29, `stitch-node/tests/live_vendors.rs`, `deepseek-flash` at `low`): a turn, an image, a tool loop of three steps with the reasoning sent back (the steps that had none sent an empty one), a history whose tool call was Claude's with an empty `reasoning_content` (answered), and a long prefix asked twice, read from the cache the second time (3,072 of 3,235 tokens). Prices are its peak rates (off-peak hours cost half; a budget rather overestimates): Flash $0.30/$1.20 per million, hits $0.006; V4 Pro $1.32/$3.96, hits $0.044. Out of balance is a 402 (out of credit).
- **OpenRouter** (decided 2026-11-17, asked by the user: "like edge talk, any model string, routed by OpenRouter"; built the same day, `stitch-providers/src/openai.rs`): a built-in provider, `openrouter`, of kind `openai` at `https://openrouter.ai/api/v1`, key `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` (`stitch key set openrouter`). A model is `openrouter/<any OpenRouter ID>`, slashes and all: `openrouter/anthropic/claude-sonnet-5.5`, `openrouter/~openai/gpt-sol-latest`, variants (`…:nitro`, `…:floor`); none needs listing first. *Why Chat Completions and not its Anthropic Messages or Responses endpoints:* it is the one shape OpenRouter normalizes for every model, and its extensions there carry what an agent needs: tools, streaming, `reasoning_details` (Claude's signed thinking, OpenAI's encrypted reasoning) that go back with tool results, `cache_control`. Routing Claude to the Messages endpoint through the Anthropic adapter would add a second path per model family for little more (Claude's fallbacks, eager tool input). edge talk did OpenRouter the same way (a Chat Completions prompter, `HTTP-Referer` and `X-Title`), but dropped the reasoning; here it goes back. The adapter knows OpenRouter by its host (`openrouter`, settable for a test server) and then: sends `reasoning` (`{"effort": …}` from `effort`, else `{"enabled": …}` from `thinking`, else nothing: each model keeps its own default effort, which `enabled: true` would make medium), never `reasoning_effort`; keeps a turn's `reasoning_details` (merged by `index` as they stream: text joined, the signature kept) or, without them, its `reasoning` text, as the turn's native content, sent back on the same model's assistant messages only; gives Claude models (`anthropic/…`, `~anthropic/…`) a breakpoint at the end of the system prompt and the top-level `cache_control` for the conversation (as the Anthropic adapter does; `cache = false` turns them off, `cache_ttl` `5m` or `1h`), other models caching by themselves (Gemini's explicit caches bill storage: not asked for); sends `session_id` (the thread) for its sticky routing to the provider holding the cache, and `prompt_cache_key` too on OpenAI's models; sends PDFs as `file` parts (other documents stay a note); asks models named `…-image…` for images (`modalities`) and keeps the images a model makes (`delta.images`) as the reply's attachments; reads the cost it bills (`usage.cost`, in credits, which are dollars) into the response (`Response::cost`), which the turn's spend, budgets and the app's guard take over the node's own prices. Attribution headers: `HTTP-Referer` `https://github.com/sinkingsugar/stitch`, `X-OpenRouter-Title: Stitch`, categories `cli-agent,personal-agent`, and no `X-OpenRouter-App-Visibility`: the app is public, listed in OpenRouter's rankings and app pages (decided 2026-11-17 by the user, before any live call; it was first built sending `hidden`). Only the request that creates the app decides its visibility, so changing it later goes through OpenRouter's support. Its model list is public, so a node without the key lists none of it (`NoKey`); with one, the list is the models a thread can run: those taking `tools`, answering in text over Chat Completions, not `:batch` (about 320 of 470, November 2026). That is too long for an agent's `models`: a provider listing more than 100 shows only the default, the quick model and those with settings, and a line counting the rest ("any of its model IDs works"); the pickers (TUI, the app) get the whole list, and the app's model menu and settings take a typed **Custom Model** (any `provider/model`). Windows come from the list's `context_length` (a variant's from its model). A response's model is named with the provider (`openrouter/anthropic/claude-sonnet-5.5`, `OpenAiCompat::router`, the provider's name in the config), so the records (`msg` and `usage`), the transcript, the cost line and the spend never read an OpenRouter model as the maker's own provider's; the native copy keeps the ID as asked, which it is matched by (decided 2026-11-17, from the first live check). A hosted Chat Completions API (OpenRouter, DeepSeek, OpenAI, xAI) with no key is not asked for its models: `NoKey`, not the 401 a keyless request got (which read as a key found somewhere). With only an OpenRouter key, the built-in pair is `openrouter/anthropic/claude-opus-5.5` and `…/claude-sonnet-5.5`, after every direct provider. Also fixed on the way: a built-in endpoint (kimi, deepseek, openrouter, and openai, grok, gemini) counts as official, so a synced default this node has no key for gives way to the node's own, as the rule always said. *Not built:* OpenRouter's provider routing preferences (`provider.order`, `sort`, data policies: a `[providers.openrouter]` setting when needed; the `:nitro`/`:floor` variants and the `~` aliases cover the common cases), its web plugin and server tools, audio and video input, per-model prices in `models` (the list carries them; the cost of each call is exact without them). *Checked live* (2026-11-17, `stitch chat` on a throwaway node): Claude Sonnet 5.5 in a tool loop with its signed thinking sent back, the second turn of the thread read from the cache (66,939 of 67,429 tokens), the cost OpenRouter billed; GPT-5.6 Luna's encrypted reasoning and Gemini 3.8 Flash's sent back over tool steps; DeepSeek V4.1 Flash with a tool; an image to Gemini.
- **Images: `generate_image`** (decided 2026-10-18, with the user, closing §4's question 9; built the same day, `stitch-node/src/imagegen.rs`, `stitch-providers/src/images.rs`): a tool any thread's model calls, whatever its provider, next to the standalone image model:
  - *The tool:* `prompt`; `images`, the `img:…` IDs of images to edit or to work from (the user's, a tool's, ones it made), read from the blob store and fitted to 2000 px; `aspect_ratio` (Gemini's ten); `model`, another image model (`provider/model`). Its group is `images` (§2.14), on by default, offered only when the node has an image model it can ask: `[images] model`, else `gemini/gemini-3.1-flash-image`, whose provider has a key. Its result names each image made (`img:…`, its size, its media type, and the blob's file on disk, so shell tools can use it: copy it, never change it in place; decided 2026-11-20 for issue 2026-11-04T140457Z-gamer-8pu7, where an agent searched for `generated-<hash8>*` and found nothing, the blob store having no extensions) and says the user sees it in the reply; usage counts toward the thread's budget as a tool's model calls do.
  - *Its images are the reply's,* as an image model's own are: the tool stores them, and the loop adds them to the attachments of the step's reply (the message that made the call), and to that message as the model sees it, so the next step sends them (lifted after the results where a provider needs it, above), and every later turn replays them for edits. So the user gets one kind of generated image, whichever way it was made, and the app shows both the same way.
  - *Backends:* one request shape (`ImageRequest`: model, prompt, images, aspect ratio) and one answer (`Generated`: images, text, usage), behind `Provider::generate_image` and `Provider::makes_images`. Gemini's is a one-turn `generateContent` on an image model with `imageConfig`. Another provider's image API (OpenAI's Images, xAI's) is one more arm there; the tool, the reply's attachments and the app stay as they are. Edge talk had `generate_image` and `edit_image` (OpenAI's, by image hashes, a mask); here one tool does both, an edit being images named in `images`.
- **Images in the app** (decided 2026-10-18, with the user, closing §4's question 9; built the same day, the app's `ImageViewer.swift`): a model's reply with images is a bubble of its own, the images large inside it (up to 360 points wide), under its text. A tap opens the image full screen, as edge talk's image editor (`ImageEditorOverlay.swift`) does: pinch and drag, double tap to zoom, a tap hides the controls, Share, Save (to Photos, add-only), Copy. Edge talk's editing was the model's (`edit_image` on an image's hash); the app adds two ways to ask for it: **Edit** puts the image in the composer (named `Edit of …`), the field focused and saying "Say what to change in the image", and **Markup** opens Quick Look's markup on a copy (drawing, text, shapes) and puts the marked-up copy there instead (`Markup of …`). Either way the message is an image and words: the model edits with `generate_image` (the image's `img:…` ID is in its line) or, on an image model, natively. Nothing there knows the provider: every image attachment, the user's too, opens the same viewer. Looked at with `just ui-shot image`, `imageviewer` and `imageedit`.
- **A model per chat in the app** (decided 2026-10-18, with the user): above the composer, the model the chat's turns use (`ModelMenu`); tapped, the node's models by provider, or the default (Settings' model, else the node's). The choice is the client's, as the TUI's `/model` is, kept per thread in the app's defaults (`ThreadModels`, not synced), a new chat's once its thread is known, and a fork starts with its parent's; every turn the app starts there (send, resume, a suspended turn going on) passes it as `Request::Chat`'s `model`. A thread opened on another client uses that client's choice. Since 2026-10-26 the node keeps a picked model as the thread's (a `set` of `model`, §2.7, "A thread's model"), so the turns nobody types run on it too. The node's model list now keeps each provider's list for ten minutes rather than the whole answer, and asks again a provider without credentials each time, so a key added in Settings lists its models at once.

Survey sources: edge talk's `EdgeTalk/Utilities/{Anthropic,Codex,Grok}SignInManager.swift`, `OAuthCredentialsStore.swift`, `ProviderOAuth.swift`, and `Scripts-Src/Prompters/prompter-{antro,oai,oai-responses}.shs`. Other OAuth there: generic MCP discovery/dynamic registration and a GitHub device-flow preset for the GitHub MCP service; X/Twitter account OAuth. Those are tool/service authorizations, not additional model subscriptions, and remain with their future integrations.

**Stitch's own loop moves to Otter** when Otter is ready (milestone 5): the loop policy (when to detach, what to ask, how to report) as checked, hot-reloadable Otter code, permissions from effects, with the shell and the other node primitives as its tools. The phone, which cannot spawn programs, runs threads on its own loop with remote shells (§2.3) to the user's other nodes.

### 2.9 Memory: engram

- **The engine is a library the node owns** (built): the engram crate is pure Rust, linked by `stitch-memory`.
- **The policy is Rust for now** (as built, below): ambient recall, observe sweeps, cues, feedback, sleep and gists, ported whole from edge talk's scripts. It moves to Otter, over a few shards for the engine (observe, recall, feedback, sleep, pack) in place of edge talk's 21 `Engram.*` shards.

The current design already syncs **facts, not state**.
