The observation log and embeddings sync; every device folds them deterministically into the same graph (edge talk's `docs/engram-architecture.md`: fixed handles, a seeded fly-hash, an int8 round trip, fleet-wide sleep stamps). That carries over directly:

- **Log entries and embeddings become records,** batched into segment files so a busy day is not thousands of files.
- **The episode graph is a derived index.**
- **The prime directive still holds:** a node observes only what happens in front of it.

Engram's associative links can also surface in the orchestration graph as suggested relations between records.

**As built** (decided 2026-10-12; the policy is Rust for now and moves to Otter). edge talk's memory went through months of field use and its rules are ported whole, each with its field reason in the code (`stitch-memory`, `stitch-node/src/memory.rs`); its build log is edge talk's `docs/engram-integration.md`, its current state `docs/engram-architecture.md`.
- **Encoder: EmbeddingGemma 2** (released 2026-10-06; text, images, audio and video in one 768-dimension space) through llama.cpp in-process (`stitch-llama`: a submodule built with CMake, Metal on Apple, iOS cross-builds), Q8_0, with its vision projector for images. **CUDA on Linux** where the build machine has the toolkit and an NVIDIA GPU (decided 2026-10-31: on gamer's CPU path ggml's spinning workers held all 32 CPUs at 100% through each batch), for that machine's architecture only (`STITCH_LLAMA_CUDA_ARCH`), the toolkit's libraries linked dynamically with its directory in the run path; `STITCH_LLAMA_GPU=cuda|vulkan|off` overrides; elsewhere the CPU, with half the logical CPUs, at most 8. On the RTX 4090 a context of 8192 takes about 2.5 GB of VRAM. Installed by `stitch memory install` (the app: Settings → Memory), never by a tool call.
- **One encoder fleet-wide, its context per node** (decided 2026-10-14, measured on an M4 Max with llama.cpp's own buffer sizes): every `memory` record carries the encoder's tag and a node folds only its own tag's, so a phone cannot run a smaller quant alone; Google publishes no QAT build of EmbeddingGemma 2 (only the 300m had them), and unsloth's `UD-Q4_K_XL` (176 MB against 310) would save 190 MiB of weights where the compute buffers, sized by the context, dominate: 1.5 GB at 8192 tokens, 280 MB at 2048, 130 MB at 1024 (weights 416 MiB, Metal and the CPU-mapped embeddings). `[memory] context` sets it: 8192 by default, 2048 on iOS and visionOS (about 700 MB in all), where a longer message is encoded by its beginning; what fits encodes alike at either (cosine > 0.999, `tests/real.rs`). On the CPU the encoder takes seconds a message (llama.cpp's CPU path, against 8 ms on Metal): `gpu = false` is not for a phone.
- **On the phone** (2026-10-14): the app's Settings → Memory downloads the model (the vision projector, 555 MB more, on request) or removes it, and starts its node again, as the CLI's install does. The encoder is suspended while the app is in the background (`set_foreground`): unloaded, refusing, because iOS refuses the GPU to an app in the background and llama.cpp's Metal backend then fails for good; a computation that fails anyway drops the model, loaded again on the next use. What goes unobserved meanwhile is caught up on the thread's next exchange; a sleep due waits for the foreground (its gist is encoded). Background refresh syncs, and a sync folds memory. **Images are bounded** (decided 2026-11-18, after an iPhone crashed in ggml's allocator embedding a 1206×2622 screenshot while catching up a thread: a Metal compute buffer refused, then dereferenced): every image is scaled to 768 px on its long side before the projector (`stitch_llama::IMAGE_SIDE`, the gemma4v projector's own warmup size: 256 tokens of 48 px), and the projector's `image_max_tokens` is 256 as a backstop; catching up (`heal`) embeds an image only while the OS leaves the app 1 GiB (`os_proc_available_memory`, iOS), else it waits for a later catch-up. Live observations are not held back.
- **Facts are `memory` records:** an exchange's observations (a message's or image's place, `repo:thread#msg[:aN]`, its int8 embedding, and two flags its writer decided: `own` and `supersedes`), what it showed and what the answer used, what it dismissed, a thread leaving or rejoining memory, in one record in the thread's repository; a sleep (every twenty hours, fleet-wide) and a gist in the home workspace. Every node holds every repository, so every node folds every fact.
- **The graph and its log are a deterministic fold:** facts folded in record-ID order; an older fact arriving late folds again from the newest checkpoint before it (below; from the start when there is none). Nothing mutable syncs: no episode rows, no snapshots. The log is edge talk's `engram_log` in memory: decisions, targets, supersession, forgetting, and how often each memory was shown. A place observed twice (two nodes catching up on one thread) counts once. Queries run on a copy of the graph (engram's `Clone`), so asking never changes the shared state; feedback replays by payload (engram's `credit`).
- **Costs follow the live set, not all history** (decided 2026-10-31). Measured that day: about 5.4k facts, 1.3k living episodes, some 250 facts a day. Engram forgets at sleep, so the episodes stay bounded, but two costs grew with history. (a) The cortex (the cosine scan of ambient and asked recall) kept every embedding ever stored. (b) A fact arriving late by sync folded every fact again from the start, at all facts × living episodes, on every such sync. Now:
  - **The cortex holds only remembered memories.** A memory forgotten (by a sleep, or by a gist's DROP) leaves it, and so does **what merged into it: the Strengthens into its episode and a gist's kept members, now forgotten with it.** Before, those stayed recallable forever after their episode was gone: about a quarter of all observations, and half the cortex on the field data. This changes behaviour on purpose: forgetting forgets. A thread out of memory has its memories set aside: never scanned for proposals, still counted by asked recall's relevance floor as before, and back when the thread is. The relevance floor's "linked" test and `gist_due` now walk the near memories and the living episodes, not the whole log. The log still keeps every handle, because a place observed again must still count once.
  - **After each sleep the graph passes through engram's own snapshot** (`Fold::settle`). Engram's `snapshot`/`restore` is not exact: it drops the recently written episodes that Hebbian binding links the next one to, and the order free slots are reused in. Folding through it makes every fold start each day from exactly what a snapshot holds, so a restored checkpoint goes on bit for bit. The same step drains engram's flush material (`drain_dirty`). Stitch writes no rows, so that material grew with all history and was cloned with every query.
  - **Checkpoints** (`stitch_memory::Folding`): the fold's whole state (the engram snapshot, cortex, log, shown times, threads switched, gists; canonical bytes) after each of the last three sleeps. A checkpoint is named by how many facts it holds and a digest chained over them in order (each fact with its record, position and time). It is used only while the facts before it are unchanged, so the result is the from-scratch fold bit for bit, in any order of arrival (`tests.rs`: `checkpoints_fold_bit_for_bit_in_any_order`, and the disk test). It is a local cache, never synced, kept under the data directory's `memory-fold/`. Its file names carry a tag of the tuning, the encoder and the build (`stitch_node::build()`), and another tag's files are deleted on open, so a parameter, model or code change discards them. A build that does not name its commit, or has local changes, keeps them in memory only. At most three are kept, within 256 MB. A late fact folds from the newest checkpoint before it, and a deleted thread from the newest before its first fact. At start, every fact is read again but folding begins at the newest valid checkpoint.
  - **Measured** (`cargo run --release -p stitch-memory --example foldbench`): synthetic histories shaped like the field's (`synth.rs`: about 70% Link, 29% Strengthen, a sleep every 250 facts, gists, feedback, threads leaving memory), on gamer (24 threads) while other builds loaded it (load 5–22). The late fact is a record about a day back. "Before" is engram 981299b. "After" is this change on 981299b (the binary built just as the inverted index landed; its timings match a 981299b build) and on 8e96e0e (engram's exact inverted index). Times are 10k / 50k / 200k facts:

    | | before (981299b) | after (981299b) | after (8e96e0e) |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | cortex size | 8,534 / 42,791 / 171,088 | 2,833 / 3,074 / 2,916 | same |
    | full rebuild | 0.97 / 5.2 / 22.3 s | 0.88 / 5.4 / 20.9 s | 0.74 / 3.8 / 16.6 s |
    | one late fact | 0.98 / 5.2 / 22.5 s | 36 / 44 / 58 ms | 31 / 43 / 61 ms |
    | start (all facts, checkpoint on disk) | (= rebuild) | 18 / 38 / 120 ms | 16 / 43 / 127 ms |
    | observe | 0.12 / 0.13 / 0.13 ms | 0.12 / 0.13 / 0.12 ms | 0.11 / 0.13 / 0.11 ms |
    | ambient (peek + cortex) | 2.6 / 12.1 / 59 ms | 1.8 / 2.2 / 2.0 ms | 2.3 / 3.7 / 2.4 ms |
    | asked recall | 4.8 / 21.1 / 97 ms | 3.3 / 4.0 / 3.6 ms | 3.8 / 8.2 / 3.7 ms |
    | checkpoint size | – | 3.2 / 5.2 / 12.1 MB | same |

    Living episodes stay at about 1,250–1,280 at every size. The iPhone is about 3× slower (estimated, not run). Before, a late fact there took about 3 s at 10k facts and about 67 s at 200k, and recall about 0.3 s at 200k. After, at 200k: a late fact takes about 0.2 s, a start about 0.4 s, recall under 15 ms.
  - **iOS budget:** disk holds at most three checkpoints, about 3 × 12 MB at 200k facts and a few MB today. Memory holds no checkpoint resident; one is read only while folding again. The fold itself is about the checkpoint's size, mostly the log: about 70 bytes plus its place per handle ever observed. Engram's index adds about 190–255 bytes per episode, about 0.3 MB. **Still O(history), open:** the node keeps every fact in memory for refolds (about 830 bytes per observation: about 4.5 MB today, about 165 MB at 200k facts). Each refresh also reads every `memory` record from the index to find the new ones. The next step is to keep facts only from the oldest checkpoint on, read older ones from the index when a refold needs them, and list record IDs alone when refreshing.
- **Thresholds transferred, not re-guessed:** edge talk's bands were measured on its field data with EmbeddingGemma-300m. `stitch-memory/examples/transfer.rs` re-embedded 7,324 of those messages (edge talk's own store, locally) with EmbeddingGemma 2 and moved every band to the same quantile of the new model's distribution, over pairs dense around the thresholds (each sampled message's 40 nearest under the old model plus 20 random). The models rank pairs alike (Spearman 0.87 cosine, 0.82 jaccard, 0.85 query→document). Jaccard: Strengthen 0.30 → 0.267, Link 0.05 → 0.045, recall anchor 0.07 → 0.058, surfacing 0.18 → 0.164. Cosine distance, message → memory (unasked): strict 0.55 → 0.310, middle 0.60 → 0.322, wander 0.70 → 0.374. Query → memory (asked): fusion 0.60 → 0.327, relevance floor 0.65 → 0.340. The encoder's own fit (`examples/fit.rs`: anisotropy 0.257 raw, 0.033 centered) agrees: the transferred Strengthen band sits above every related pair it measured (max 0.202). `stitch memory audit` prints decisions by novelty band and strengths: the drift check after any change.
- **Writing (observe):** a node observes the exchanges it runs (text and images; not tool output, not memory's notes, not a message with the skip marker U+2063 or the dismiss marker U+2060), and catches up in the background on what it never observed of a thread it touches (history feeds no unasked recall: ingestion, not experience). Same-thread messages share words, so distinct replies land in the Strengthen band: a Strengthen into a sibling of the same thread keeps its own memory unless the two share half their distinctive words (the mutable fact). A Strengthen of a message carrying numbers supersedes: recalling the old one shows the newer. The user's message is observed before the turn on a copy of the graph (its novelty, what it surfaces) and for real after it, with the same embedding.
- **Recall, unasked (ambient):** what a node's recent observations surfaced parks per thread (eight at most). Before a turn, the message is the cue: it must carry a topic (four distinctive words; reactive turns sit next to their tone-twins), and a thread whose last note went unanswered (a cancelled exchange) gets none; otherwise the candidates wait. The cortex also proposes (top five eligible by cosine, filtered before the limit), first in line. Each candidate must be eligible (not forgotten, not this thread's, not a thread out of memory), within the ceiling its cue's novelty sets (familiar topic strict; new territory: one labeled distant association) and within 0.49 of the cue with both centered (provisional, decided 2026-10-12 on the first few field threads, to revisit as notes accumulate: raw, any two short chatty messages sit 0.20–0.30 apart, inside every transferred ceiling, and the first field notes brought another thread's small talk to a generic request; centered, the relevant notes came in at ≤ 0.458 and the irrelevant at ≥ 0.528, at every cue length, where raw split them by 0.011; over every substantive cue of the first field threads against every eligible memory, cortex proposals under the strict ceiling went from 69 memories in 19 notes, 7 of them relevant, to 11 in 6, the same 7 relevant, 3 irrelevant; under the wander ceiling from 79 to the same 11. The rest of the pipeline matches edge talk's after its own fix for irrelevant notes: verification against the cue under the novelty ceiling, the cortex proposer for topic-bearing cues only, the four-word cue and substance floors, the unanswered-note guard, per-thread dedup, the budget after the filters. Its thresholds were raw distances in EmbeddingGemma-300m, where its short chatter sat farther apart; the quantile transfer kept their place in its traffic, not in short messages). Every note keeps what recalibrating takes, so labels come with use: its cue's distinctive words, length and novelty, the ceiling and whether it wandered, and each memory's raw and centered distance; the feedback on it (credited, dismissed by the marker or by citing it unused) names the note. `stitch memory notes` joins them: one row per memory shown, `helped`, `dismissed`, `ignored` or `unknown`. To re-measure: once a few hundred rows are `helped` or `dismissed`, compare their centered distances (and raw, by cue length), move the cutoff to where dismissals take over, and replay the field threads offline (each substantive user message as a cue against the earlier memories of other threads, as was done for 0.49) to see what the new cutoff shows and drops before shipping it. Keep the gate a single number until the data asks for more, not already given to the thread (its notes' handles, its `recall` results' `[id …]`), substantial (four distinctive words; a question judged on its own words), and not a text already in the note. Four at most; quotes capped at 120 words; a user's message is shown with the reply that followed. The note is part of the message (not the system prompt) in edge talk's words, with each memory's provenance (`[id, age, speaker, thread, seen, strength, helped before]`) and the invisible dismissal invitation.
- **Recall, asked:** the `recall` tool fuses the graph's associations (full demand) with the closest memories; one survives when it, or one it is linked to, is within the relevance floor, so an off-topic store returns nothing. Not floored, not capped. A keyword search (BM25) over every thread follows: what memory does not span. A thread out of memory gets the keyword search only, unless it was left recalling (a spawned thread's default, below).
- **Feedback:** a shown memory (the note's, the tool's) earned credit when max(3, min(⌈0.3 × its distinctive words⌉, 8)) of them come back in the answer, lines citing an `[id …]` excluded (quoting is not using), and only when its own words pass the floor. An answer with U+2060 dismisses what it did not use, and so does citing a memory without using it: knocked by half. Sleep audits before it decays: a memory shown three times without ever helping, or, having helped, shown eight times in fourteen days, is knocked.
- **Gists (REM):** after a sleep, the sleeping node condenses the largest quiet cluster (five or more linked memories, the newest a week old) from its twelve strongest members with `[memory] gist_model` (the quick model when unset): one paragraph, and a `DROP:` line for chaff. Kept members resolve to the gist; dropped ones are forgotten.
- **Afterthoughts:** when an exchange's observations surface something new, the thread may get one follow-up: memory's note as its own message, an unwritten instruction to add a short afterthought or answer with U+2060 alone. At most once per message of the user's, a minute apart; a decline is written, never shown.
- **Off for a thread** (edge talk's per-conversation toggle): `stitch memory off|on`, `/memory off|on`. Off, nothing of it is observed or recalled anywhere, its tool recall is the keyword search only; on again, it is caught up. On a new thread (none written yet), `/memory` switches no other thread: the TUI keeps the choice and sends it with the first message (`Chat`'s `memory`), and the node writes it before the first step, over the place's default (fixed 2026-10-20: it used to switch the latest thread of the place, often a spawned one already out).
- **Off for a place** (decided 2026-10-18): `memory = false` in a place's `.stitch/settings.toml` (§2.14) keeps its threads out of memory. Before a turn, a thread in that place whose memory was never switched (no `{ thread, remember }` fact, by anyone) gets `remember = false`, as `/memory off` writes it: new threads before their first step, existing ones from their next turn (what they said before leaves memory with them). `/memory on` brings one back for good, since it is then switched (written even for a thread not yet taken out). What the TUI's status line and the app show already counts the place: a new thread in it, and one of its threads not yet switched, show memory off before any turn (`Request::Memory` with the client's `cwd`, or `global`). No default by kind of place: every git checkout is a project, and most of the user's own conversations are in one.
- **A place's `memory = false` wins over an agent's `remember`** (decided 2026-10-31, after measuring that the stitch project still took in 341 observations, two other projects 866 and 209 since they said `memory = false`: chats and default spawns stayed out, but threads asked to be remembered did not, since only the chat path read the place's setting). A thread asked to be remembered by an agent or a default (`spawn`'s `remember: true`, `stitch chat --detach --memory`, an assignment's `memory = true` on the node that claims it, a schedule's thread, remembered by default) starts out of memory in such a place, recalling as a default spawn does (`{ thread, remember = false, recall }` before its first step). Only the user's own switch brings a thread back: `/memory on`, `stitch memory on`, or the TUI's `/memory` chosen for a new chat. Agents are told, so they do not count on what is not kept. Threads remembered before this are left as they are (no sweep). *As built:* `detached::start`, which every detached thread goes through (spawns here, claimed assignments, schedules), writes the fact when the place is off and the thread was never switched (node log: "out of memory, as its place's `memory = false` says"), and tells the thread so with its first message (`detached::PLACE_MEMORY_OFF`, said, not written). The `spawn` tool's result says "Not remembered: …" when it starts here; for another node, whose place's settings are read there, it says the place there may keep it out. Chats keep `Node::place_memory` before each turn. A thread compacted into a new one (`fork::compact`) carries the user's `/memory on` as it carries `/memory off`, so a place's `memory = false` does not take the continuation out.
- **Off for spawned threads by default** (decided 2026-10-13): a thread started by another thread or for one (the `spawn` tool, `stitch chat --detach`, `/spawn`, an assignment another node claims) is out of memory from its start, exactly as `/memory off` leaves a thread: its `{ thread, remember = false }` fact is written by the node that starts it, in the thread's repository, before its first step, so nothing of it is observed, it gets no unasked recall or afterthought, its tool recall is the keyword search only, and other threads are never shown its messages. Its work is mostly tool chatter and handoffs that crowded the user's own conversations out of recall. `spawn`'s `remember: true` (`--memory`; an assignment's `memory = true`) keeps it in memory; `/memory on` brings one back later, caught up. Chats the user starts are unchanged.
- **Spawned threads recall; remembering is chosen** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user, issues `…ye9m` and `…mu6n`): the one `memory` flag tied recalling to being remembered, so a thread spawned with the default could not search memory and repeated what earlier threads had learned, unless its spawner pasted the history into its goal; and its hint ("worth recalling later") read as "worth it to the user", pushing agents to leave out exactly the agent-to-agent work whose lessons later threads need. `spawn` now has two: `recall` (default true: the thread's `recall` tool searches memory as a remembered thread's does) and `remember` (default false: its conversation is kept). Out of memory but recalling is the fact `{ thread, remember = false, recall = true }`: nothing of the thread is observed, nothing of it is shown elsewhere, it gets no unasked recall or afterthought, but its asked recall is whole; a build from before reads the fact without `recall`, as out of memory altogether (no format change). `/memory off` writes `recall` false: off is still off. An assignment carries `memory = true` (remembered, every build reads it) or `recall = true`; one with neither recalls nothing (what an older spawner meant). **`memory` is kept, unadvertised:** `memory: true` is both, `memory: false` neither (the old behaviour), and an explicit `remember` or `recall` wins over it; it left the schema so new calls say what they mean, and stays read because agents, goals and transcripts written before still use it, and a refused call would cost a turn for nothing. `stitch chat --detach` recalls, `--memory` remembers too; schedules' threads are remembered as before (unless their place says `memory = false`, which wins: 2026-10-31, above); app threads and fixers recall nothing (unchanged: app threads cannot declare memory, and fixers' powers are the user's to change).
- **Agent-to-agent conversations rank below the user's** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user): a thread is agent-to-agent while none of its messages is the user's own (`chat::by_user`: typed, not a spawner's goal, a schedule's, a report or another thread's message), tagged as the search archive indexes it, so a thread the user later writes into is theirs from its next indexing, and threads from before are tagged too. When the user searches (`stitch search`, `/search`, the app's search) and when the `recall` tool runs for a thread the user is in, every result from an agent-to-agent thread comes after every result from the user's own conversations, each part in its own order; a spawned or scheduled thread's `recall` sees one ranking. So remembering spawned work no longer clutters what the user finds. Unasked recall is unchanged (its candidates pass a distance gate, not a ranking).
- **Not carried over,** because Stitch has no such thing: personas and their grants, group chats, fork copies (a Stitch fork does not copy messages, §2.15), the distilled `memory` table tier, the legacy A/B log, provisional handles and ghost tombstones (Stitch names a message by where it will be written before it is), fleet-once migration sentinels (a refold under new parameters replaces them).

### 2.10 Embeds

Mini-apps and canvases are HTML and JS that agents write. The node serves them, so the same embed works:
- in the iOS app's WebView;
- in a desktop browser;
- next to the CLI.

Their logic and data access go through node APIs and Otter, not through Swift shards.

**Apps** (decided and built 2026-10-14, with the user, pulled forward from milestone 5: nothing of it waits on Otter; `apps/` in `stitch-node`). Edge talk's embeds and Mini Apps, rebuilt on the node:
- **Made with a tool, not a marker.** The agent writes an app's files (a directory with `index.html`, or one HTML file) and calls `show_app` (group `apps`) with a name and, when it needs the network, `networking`. That takes a **version**: every file as a blob, directories as trees (a folder's format, §2.16), and a manifest blob naming the tree, the name, `networking`, and the thread whose state it keeps. The manifest is the tool message's attachment (`application/vnd.stitch.app+json`), so the conversation shows each version where it was shown (edge talk's frozen `<artifact>` snapshots); the tool result lists every blob, so nodes that pull all blobs pull them. Binary files travel, as folder sync's do. Inline `<html_embed>` is not carried over: one way to make an app.
- **Pinned** apps are `app` records in the home workspace (name, an SF Symbol, the version); pinned again from the same thread and name, a newer record supersedes the last, so an app's versions are its history (edge talk kept none). Unpinning is a record too. **A pin follows its thread** (decided 2026-10-17): when the thread it was pinned from shows a newer version under the app's name (its version's name, so a pin renamed still follows), `show_app` moves the pin there, keeping its ID, origin, name, icon and data, as pinning again by hand did; the move is written by the node that ran `show_app` and syncs with the home workspace, and the phone's list and widget pick it up on their next refresh (the widget names apps by ID, so it opens the new version unchanged). A pin made at a version (`stitch app pin blake3:…`) records `fixed` and stays; pinned again from its thread, it follows again. Pins from the phone (from the version in the conversation) follow, and so do pins written before the distinction. The phone shows them above its threads, full screen, and in a home screen widget (`stitch://app?id=`); a desktop browser lists them at `localhost` and can install one as an app (a web manifest).
- **State is the thread's:** a page's `localStorage` (and `stitch.storage`, JSON values) is `set` records on the thread, field `app:<key>`, the latest standing as for any `set` (records.md §6). Every version and the pin share it. A page starts with it inlined (synchronous reads: edge talk's lesson); its writes are gathered for a second, written, and synced at once; a page hears other devices' writes by a long poll (no event stream crosses a URL scheme handler).
- **One server, two transports.** `apps::serve` answers a request (host, path, method, body) for both: the iOS app's `WKURLSchemeHandler` (`stitch-app://<host>/`, through the FFI, in process, no socket) and, on a desktop, HTTP on loopback (`[apps] port`, 7177). Each app is an **origin of its own**: a version's host is its manifest hash in two labels, a pinned app's `p` and a hash of its ID (stable across versions); on a desktop under `.localhost`, which browsers and the system resolve to loopback. The desktop lets in only a link's token, then a cookie of that host; a request must name the server as its host (no DNS rebinding) and a POST come from its own origin. **Links with the token go only to local clients** (decided 2026-10-19, after an app `show_app` returned could be opened by `browse_web`, which the node admits by adding the token, but not by the user's browser until pinned): the tool result, which syncs and goes to the provider, keeps the bare address, so a chat's client is told of a shown app apart (`Reply::App`, its name and the address with the token, from `ChatEvent::App`); the TUI and `stitch chat` print it, `/app` opens the open thread's latest app, and `stitch app open` takes a thread or a version as well as a pinned app (`Request::AppOpen`). The phone shows the app from the transcript's attachment, as before.
- **The bridge** goes first in an entry page's `<head>`, with the state inlined: `localStorage`, `stitch.storage`, `stitch.report(data)` (wakes the thread, rate-limited per thread; a pinned app's goes to the thread it was pinned from, as "Pinned app … (version …)", under the same limit, and a pin with no thread, or whose thread is not on the device, answers 409 saying so), and a page's first fatal error told to its thread, once per version, only for the thread's latest version, at most two fixes per thread while the node runs (edge talk's auto-fix limits). **A report, and an error, is a message sent to the thread** (decided 2026-10-17, after the user's Refresh in a pinned dashboard answered `ok` and woke nothing: its thread was a spawned one that had reported, and the report went as a late note, which by design (§2.3) wakes no thread after its report): `weave::send` with `interject`, from the user, so it is woven into a loop running on the thread, else starts a turn, reported or not; on the node that runs the thread, which may not be the one serving the page (the phone's pinned app of a thread the Mac runs: an assignment there, synced to it at once). The page hears where it went (`{ok, said}`), or why not: 429 too soon, 409 no thread, 503 when the node running the thread could not be reached now (the assignment waits for the next sync). The late-note rule stays for what it is for (jobs, approval notes after a thread reported). Pinned apps tell their first error on the same terms (decided 2026-10-17): a pin now follows its thread, so a pinned app failing is usually the latest version failing where the user meets it, and a fix shown again moves the pin; the same limits keep it from spending without end, and a pin fixed at an old version asks for nothing. In the phone's web view it also takes downloads and `window.print()` to the system and reports its height.
- **Libraries:** edge talk's whole set (decided with the user, 2026-10-14; files the node serves, never linked: `applibs/README.md`): Chart.js, D3, Mermaid, Leaflet, KaTeX, Three.js, JSCAD and its STL serializer and renderer, MeshRepair (WebAssembly), pdfmake, SheetJS, the leadsheet compiler with SpessaSynth and the GeneralUser GS soundfont, … They are in the node's binary, gzipped (9 MB), at `/_lib/` of every origin, included by the page explicitly (no detection: edge talk's false positives). The bundled ES modules (Three.js, leadsheet, SpessaSynth) come through an import map Stitch gives every page without its own (edge talk inlined them as `data:` URIs because its pages had no origin and WebKit would not load their modules through its scheme handler; an app has an origin, and modules load: the UI test checks it, MeshRepair and leadsheet included).
- **Without `networking`** a CSP keeps a page to its origin (WebAssembly allowed).
- **Skills:** edge talk's, built in and read with `read_skill` (no shell on a phone), their files too (`file`): `apps` (pages, libraries, state, 3D printing with MeshRepair, documents) and `leadsheet` (music as `.ls`, its spec, an example and the player template). Stitch's own beside them: `google-flights` and `youtube` (§2.17). A `claude-code` skill (briefing, starting, watching and answering Claude Code sessions, decided 2026-10-16) was removed again: coding is done by Stitch's own threads now, and running `claude` stays possible as any program in the shell. A skill of the user's of the same name replaces one. Edge talk validated songs with `run_js`; here the player checks on load and reports, and a desktop agent can `web_eval` the app. Edge talk's `widgets` (Shards 1.x views) wait for the Otter loop.

**Live state for apps** (decided and built 2026-10-17, with the user, steps 1–2 of `docs/live-apps-spec.md`, after an agent-made dashboard summarised stale thread reports into a `data.json`: stale at once, a model call per refresh, and wrong). The node serves the facts; a page renders them; an agent adds only judgement (later, spec §4). `apps/live.rs`:
- **Bridge calls,** GETs under `/_stitch/live/`, so they work through the phone's scheme handler as through HTTP: `stitch.live.threads({all, status, since, limit})` (the code of the `threads` tool, `weave::row`: id, title, goal, the node that runs it and the one it started on, place, parent (`spawned-by`, else `reports-to`), state `working`/`blocked`/`idle`/`ended`/`cancelled`, the latest report's outcome, the approval it waits on with its exact command, started, last step, model calls, cost, the first 300 characters of its last words; without `all`, the threads the app's own thread started; `since` drops threads that neither stepped nor started since, unless running), `stitch.live.attention()` (the attention list's questions: thread, node, place, session, exact command, when asked), `stitch.live.jobs()` (this node's jobs, every thread's, as `jobs {all}` lists them: running, commands left running, ended within the hour; a phone has none), `stitch.live.schedules({all})` (added 2026-10-22, the user asking for schedules on the dashboard: every node's schedules, self-wakes included, the soonest due first: name, goal, when, node, place, state, whether its node paused it (the standing `state` set by no user and no run: failed firings in a row), the thread a self-wake continues, its threads, check, budget, daily cap and today's spend, the next three times (from its timer's last firing on its own node, from now on another's), whether it is firing, and on its own node what its timer did (`here`: last firing, fired, quiet, skipped, failures in a row, last said); `all` adds the ended ones). Read-only (a POST is 405), from the index and memory; a thread's model calls and last words are read from its transcript once per step (a cache by last step). **`conversations: true` (2026-11-11, for the Atlas prototype, §2.25):** with `all`, the listing is the user's own (the client's `every`: every conversation and thread, compaction chains folded to their newest, deleted ones left out), `limit` up to 5000 for that call only (500 otherwise), rows without `in_flight` and `app` and with `goal` cut at 300 characters; `detached` tells a conversation (false) from a spawned thread. A row's `parent` is followed to the newest of its chain, so it names a listed row unless the parent was deleted (`parent_title`: "A deleted thread") or is in a repository this node does not hold (an id with no row). An older node ignores the flag (detached threads alone, which an app can tell by no row with `detached: false`). Cost on a copy of gamer's data (1,550 rows, release): 3.5 s cold, 0.5 s warm for the loop before; places were read three times per row (now once) and the cold transcript reads (model calls, cost, last words, a whole transcript each) go on up to 8 threads at once (`weave::warm_rows`): 1.2 s cold after a node start, ~0.5 s warm (0.3 ms a row, the index lock taken per call, never held across rows).
- **`stitch.live.on(kind, cb)`** (`threads`, `jobs`, `attention`, `schedules`, `*`): the existing long poll (`/_stitch/events`) takes `live=N` and answers when live state moved too, with each kind's generation. The node watches only while a page with consent listens (and 30 s after): every second it takes a mark of each kind (the index's count and latest row of thread, report, question, answer, assignment and `set` records and of segments, with the loops running and starting; the open questions; the jobs' keys; the index's mark of schedule and `set` records with this node's timer state) and bumps the kinds whose mark moved. Polling marks instead of hooks in every writer: cheap (two SQL counts), never misses a writer, and no model is involved (a test asserts a page listening makes no model call).
- **Consent per app, off by default.** A page without it gets 403 with `consent` `ask` (never answered) or `denied`, the reason, and the command that allows it. The answer is kept on the pin (`live` on its `app` record, carried as pins move; a new pin takes its conversation's answer) or, for an app in its conversation, on its thread (a `set` of `live:<name>`), so it syncs. It is given only by trusted UI: on the phone the bridge posts `live: ask` and the app shows a system alert naming the app (by the node's record of it, not the page's word), "Don't Allow" or "Allow", kept through `Request::AppLive`; in a browser, `stitch app allow-live <name> [--thread <id>] [--deny]` (the page hears of it through the long poll and loads). Agents' shells ask the user before `stitch app allow-live`. A denial is kept too, and only `allow-live` (or a new answer on a computer) lifts it: the phone does not ask again.
- **The built-in dashboard,** in the binary (since version 8 the web client's second entry, below; before, plain JS in `apps/dashboard/`): "What's going on?" (counts, the working threads, one sentence each, this node's background jobs folded to a line each) beside "Needs you" (approvals with their exact command and how to answer them, blocked threads, schedules their node paused after failures, with the command that resumes them) and "Scheduled" (version 3, 2026-10-22: the active schedules and self-wakes, soonest first, each with how often, node and place, its goal's first sentence, its timer's last word, and when it fires next; the paused ones folded; a count among the numbers; a schedule's last firing on the timeline); "Threads", a lane per conversation the threads came from (the first parent of a spawn chain that is not in the list, by its title), each thread a card (status colour, a short title, one line of what came of it, node and place chips, age, calls; a click opens its goal and last words) with the threads it spawned hanging under it, across nodes; lanes quiet for a day fold into "Earlier", a line each; and a timeline, a line per thread at its latest move, by day. Threads of the last three days and those working or waiting; with `all`, `live.threads` also gives the conversations a turn runs on here or an approval waits on (a conversation is a lane's first card then, its spawns under it), since a dashboard missing the chat at work answers "what's going on?" wrong (a conversation running on another node shows once it spawns or asks; open). Redone 2026-10-20 (version 2) after the user found v1's node columns and feed unreadable: a column per node split one effort over three columns and wrapped every row, titles were briefs' openings, and the feed repeated the reports. A spawned thread's title is often its goal cut at 80 characters, so the page makes a short one from the goal's first sentence that says what to do (dropping asides, "In ~/x on gamer," and "You are…" boilerplate); a title the node gives spawned threads would do better (open). Responsive to 360 px, no force map. `stitch dashboard` (`Request::Dashboard`) pins it (a pin with `builtin = "dashboard"` and its version, no thread) allowed to read live state (the user asked for it by name), and opens it in the browser; the phone's thread list offers it as a dashed tile until it is pinned. A node whose built-in is newer than the pinned one moves the pin at start, never back. Its CI, places and answers are the spec's steps 3–4; insights became loose ends (below).
- **The dashboard is the client's home view** (decided 2026-11-04, with the user, who found the dashboard needed some love while the web client's home (§2.24) did most of what it showed, better, in a second codebase drifting from it). Version 8: the dashboard is a **second entry of the client's bundle** (`apps/client/src/dashboard/main.tsx`, built by `just client` into the committed `apps/client/dist/dashboard/` beside the client's `dist/`, embedded by `include_str!` as `live::DASHBOARD`), rendering the home view's own components (`src/home/Home.tsx`: Needs you, What's going on, Loose ends, Scheduled, Timeline) against **a data source** (`src/home/source.ts`): the thread list and the home flag as signals, and a `Source` with `live(what)` and `looseEnds(op)`, and the capabilities the host has: `open` (a thread), `href` (a link that opens one), `answer`, `looseAuto`, `schedule`. The client's source is the WebSocket and the user's reach (`initLive`, from the store); the dashboard's (`src/dashboard/source.ts`) is the apps bridge, `stitch.live.*` (`/_stitch/live/*`), reloaded on its change events (300 ms) and every minute, the threads of the last three days (`all`, 300 at most). It keeps working as a pinned app on the phone and in the browser, with the same pin, consent and bridge. **Writes stay as they were:** the dashboard's only POSTs are loose ends' check and dismiss (a blocked thread's Dismiss is the same call); where the client shows Allow/Always/Decline, Resume, or the auto-handling toggle and rules editor, the dashboard shows the fact and how to do it (`stitch answer <id> y`, `stitch schedule resume <id>`, `stitch loose-ends --auto …` / `--rules edit`, or the client), since its endpoint can be called by any local process. Auto-handling is shown read-only (its mode, the rules folded). Titles open the thread where the host can: the client selects it; the phone's web view follows `stitch://thread?id=` (`window.webkit.messageHandlers` present); a browser shows them as text. **What only the dashboard has** stays, in `src/dashboard/`: the numbers (each a link to its panel), the "Threads" lanes by conversation with spawn trees (`lanes.ts`, cards opening on a click to goal, last words and Open), "Earlier" folding, the live pill. The look is the client's (dark; the light palette of version 7 went with it). One column under 860 px (Needs you, What's going on, Loose ends, Scheduled), the numbers three across under 520 px, down to 360 px. **The client's narrow layout** came with it (it was broken under ~720 px: the 320 px sidebar left the home view a 60 px column): under 720 px the sidebar sits above the view, its list capped at 30% of the height with the home view scrolling as one page under it; a thread open folds the sidebar to its top line ("‹ Stitch" goes home), the spawn panel hides, and a row's button goes under its text under 520 px. A preview on the fixture with no node: `bun apps/client/tools/dashboard-preview.ts` (the phone's `live::fixture`, which also serves `app.css` now). *Open:* a light theme for the client and the dashboard; the old dashboard's notes of approvals and jobs seen while the page was open are not in the timeline.
- **Loose ends** (decided and built 2026-10-29, with the user, who works on many things in parallel and lost track of where they were needed; this is the spec's §4, insights, narrowed to one question). `loose.rs`. *What counts:* conversations, the threads the user started (not detached, not an app's or an end user's): a spawned thread's loose end shows up as its parent's, which its report woke. *Free facts:* a conversation is **unread** when its latest sealed segment is newer than the user's `opened` (a `set` the user's clients write through `Request::Opened`, the time they saw it, never before the step it saw, written only when the thread moved since: the TUI each time what it shows changed, the phone when its chat loads (`opened` in the FFI), and the node itself when a client's chat turn ends, the client having watched it); before any marker, moved after the node first told loose ends (`loose-ends-since`, local) counts. A spawned thread that ended `failed`, `over-budget` or `unconfirmed` in the last three days, unopened, whose parent has not moved since, is one too. *A judgement:* the quick model (`[loose_ends] model`) reads a conversation that stopped (its title, first message, the user's last message, the agent's last words, the failure a turn ended with; bounded) and answers `waiting-on-you`, `continue`, `done` or `stale` with one line of what the user is to do; kept as a `set` of `loose-end` on the thread (a table: `verdict`, `line`, `step` = the latest segment's time, `at`, `model`), so it is judged once per step and syncs. *Since 2026-11-14 the decision model judges first* (§2.29, the `loose-ends` profile: when sure, its verdict with the agent's own words as the line; the quick model where it is unsure). *The gate* (`loose::due`, Rust, no model): a pass asks the model only for conversations not working (no open segment, no loop here), quiet five minutes (the timer's; on demand none), moved within seven days, not judged nor dismissed at their latest step, at most 20 a pass, the latest first. *Who judges:* a node judges the conversations it runs (`runner`); a phone's (a node with a `device` record) by the first of the user's other nodes by name, so one conversation has one judge and one push. **Failover** (2026-11-04, with the user, after seeing that a `beast` that is down left every phone conversation unjudged): a later node by name judges a phone's conversations only when no node before it was reached within 30 minutes (as this node's `peers-seen` says), and then only once one stayed unjudged its rank × 90 minutes (`loose::phone_rank`, `FAILOVER`): the first node, up after all, has judged by then and its judgement has synced, so two nodes rarely judge the same step, and a judgement written twice is the same `set`, the latest winning. *When:* the node's timer every `[loose_ends] every_minutes` (60; 0: off; never on a phone), first five minutes after the start; on demand, `stitch loose-ends` (judges every node's due conversations, then lists; `--list` only lists; `--dismiss <id>`), and the dashboard's button. This replaces the spec's "not on a timer": the gate makes a quiet hour free. *Push:* the timer's pass pushes once for the conversations it newly judged `waiting-on-you` that the user has not seen (several in one push; one opens its chat), `[loose_ends] push = false` turns it off; a step is judged once, so told once. *Dismissed:* a user's `set` of `loose-end-dismissed` (the step) hides it until the thread moves. *Shown:* `stitch.live.looseEnds()` (`/_stitch/live/loose-ends`: items, the latest first, with thread, title, kind `conversation`/`thread`, verdict, line, since, unread, node, place; `checking`, how many are not judged yet, the node's last pass with its model and cost); listed: judged `waiting-on-you`/`continue`, and unread ones (judged or not). The only POSTs under `live/`: `loose-ends?check` and `?dismiss=<id>`, from the built-in dashboard alone (405 for any other app). The dashboard (version 5) has a "Loose ends" panel under the top row (in one column, a phone's, right after "Needs you", Scheduled after it): a row each (verdict colour, title, unread chip, "needs you to: …", node, place, since; Dismiss; on the phone Open, `stitch://thread?id=…`, which the app opens), older than a day folded, a "Check loose ends" button, and a count among the numbers.
- **Loose ends, auto-handled** (decided and built 2026-11-04, with the user: many loose ends are a "go ahead" or a "drop it" they would say anyway). Off by default. *The setting* is the user's, synced: a record of kind `instructions` with `place = "loose-ends"` in the home workspace (`instructions::keyed`, so any node edits it and the latest wins), its `mode` field `off`, `dry-run` or `on`, its text the user's **rules** in their words (what may be continued and what to tell the agent, what to dismiss, what never to touch). `[loose_ends] auto = "off"|"dry-run"|"on"` on a node wins over it there (a node kept off whatever the phone says). Set with `Request::LooseEndsAuto { mode, rules }` (the user's alone, `peer::the_user_alone`: a thread's commands cannot turn it on nor write its rules), `stitch loose-ends --auto <mode>` and `--rules show|edit|clear|<file>|-`, and the web client's toggle and rules box; the built-in dashboard only shows it (its POSTs come from a page any local process could imitate). Shown in `live.looseEnds()` as `auto` (`mode`, `synced`, `here`, `rules`). *The judge* (the same pass and gate, no new model calls when off or with no rules) is given the rules after the excerpt and asked also for `act`: `none`, `continue` with a `message` (what to tell the agent), or `dismiss`; only on a rule that clearly covers the case, never to approve payments, purchases, deletions, deploys, messages to others or credentials the rules do not name. A `continue` only for `waiting-on-you` or `continue`. *Guards* (free, before any confirmation): no unanswered question of the thread (an approval or the agent's `ask`: those are never answered by loose ends); not a step that an auto-continue produced with no message of the user's since (`chat::last_prompt_from`: the latest message from the user's side is loose ends' own), so it never runs a conversation on by itself; at most 3 auto-continues a conversation in 24 hours and 3 a pass. *Confirmation:* a continue that passed the guards is checked by the **default** model against the rules, the excerpt and the message (`confirm` true/false, `why`), so the quick model's misreading of a rule does not act. *Acting:* on, a confirmed continue is sent into the thread through `weave::send_as` (the owner's node runs it, forwarded as an assignment with `from = "loose-ends"`), written as from `loose-ends` (not the user: `chat::by_user` is false for it) and starting with a note that loose ends sent it on the user's rules, so the agent and the transcript say so; the turn runs under the thread's own approval rules. A dismiss writes the user's `loose-end-dismissed`. A dry run proposes, guards and confirms the same way and only records. *Kept:* a `set` of `loose-end-auto` on the thread (`act`, `message`, `outcome` = `continued`, `dismissed`, `would-continue`, `would-dismiss`, `declined`, `held`, `failed`, `why`, `mode`, `step`, `at`, and `continues`, the day's auto-continue times), logged by the node, said by `stitch loose-ends`, and shown on the item (`auto`) while it is about the step shown or a continue of the last day. *Told:* one push per pass for what was done (`Loose ends handled`: the title and the message sent); a conversation acted on is not also pushed as waiting. *Clients:* the web client's loose ends panel has an Off / Dry run / On segmented toggle, the rules (folded, an Edit rules box saved with Save or Ctrl/⌘-Enter), and on each row a chip with what auto-handling did or would do and its message and reason; the dashboard (version 7) shows the mode among its facts and the same line per row. *Open:* the iOS app's own toggle (it reads the same `live` payload), per-place rules, an undo for a dismissal from its push.

Not yet: Otter behind an app's data (milestone 5), the headless `run_js` of edge talk (a desktop agent checks its app with `browse_web` and `web_eval` at the address `show_app` gives).

### 2.11 Chats and attachments

Starting a chat on the phone, asking questions and attaching images, videos, PDFs and other files must stay as direct as it is today. The same conversation, attachments included, must be resumable from the CLI on a Linux node, and the other way round.

- **A conversation is a thread of records** in the home workspace (or a project repository when it is about that project): messages, model replies, tool calls and results, in compacted segments (§2.5). Any node that holds the repository can show it and continue it.
- **Attachments are blobs referenced by content hash:** a record holds the hash, media type, name and size; the bytes live in the content-addressed blob store beside the records (§2.5). Derived forms (thumbnails, extracted PDF text, video frames, transcripts) are cached per node and rebuildable.
- **Blob sync is lazy on phones:** a phone uploads what it creates and fetches other blobs when a conversation needs them; always-on nodes hold everything. What a phone creates reaches the others when an always-on node syncs with it (that node asks for every blob it lacks), so until then it is on the phone only.
- **Fetching asks every peer at once** (decided 2026-10-14, after a turn on the phone waited on peers one after another, two minutes each): each known peer is asked whether it holds the blob (20 seconds to answer, so one offline costs no more), the first that does sends it, the others are let go before they send anything; eight blobs are fetched at a time. The app fetches a thread's missing attachments in the background when it is opened (`FetchBlobs`), so they are there to show and the next turn does not wait for them.
- **Model input is the provider layer's job** (Rust): it turns an attachment into what each provider accepts (images inline, PDFs as documents or text, audio and video natively where supported, otherwise a note naming the file; sampled frames are not built). Scripts pass attachment references, not image values. Since 2026-10-18 audio and video attachments (up to 2 GB) reach Gemini as they are, read only by its adapter; every other adapter gets a line naming them and never reads them (§2.8, Gemini). Images a model makes, or `generate_image` makes for it, are the reply's attachments, shown in its bubble (§2.8, Images).
- **Media types from the bytes** (decided 2026-10-21, after a `Photo.jpg` from a WiFi scope app, PNG inside, made Anthropic refuse every later request of its chat: "specified using the image/jpeg media type, but the image appears to be a image/png image"): an image's type is what its signature says, not its name, extension or a picker's word. One function reads it (`stitch_providers::media::sniff_image`: PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, and HEIC, HEIF and AVIF by their `ftyp` brand), used at both ends: when a file is stored as an attachment (`media_type_of`, the node's and the CLI's) and the app writes a picked or pasted image (ImageIO's type, anything but PNG, JPEG, GIF and WebP drawn as JPEG), and again when a request is built, so records stored with a wrong label are sent right without being rewritten. Where it is known it wins (the node's `fit` and history, every adapter: `portable` for Anthropic, OpenAI-compatible and Responses, Gemini's parts); unknown bytes keep their label. HEIC and HEIF go as images: Gemini reads them, every other adapter sends a note saying it cannot read that type and to attach JPEG or PNG (the node has no HEIC decoder; the app converts its own).
- **Capture sources stay native in the clients** and produce attachments, so nothing about them is device-bound after capture:
  - camera, photo library, files and screenshots;
  - **room scans** (RoomPlan with LiDAR): today edge talk's `EdgeTalk/Views/RoomScanView.swift` converts the `CapturedRoom` into plain value types and `EdgeTalk/Models/RoomScanPlan.swift` (platform-independent, no RoomPlan import) produces a measured text summary and a rendered top-down floor plan, attached through the ordinary document path. Keep that, and also attach the structured scan (the value types as JSON, and the USDZ model) so agents on any node can compute with the geometry, not only read the summary;
  - later, other sensor captures the same way (dictation, screen recording, location).

  **Built in the iOS app (2026-10-15):** the composer's `+` menu has Photos, Videos, Video Frame (pick a video, scrub its filmstrip or step frame by frame, keep stills as images), Scan Room (LiDAR devices; iOS only, RoomPlan is not on visionOS), Take a Picture (where there is a camera), Files, and Paste Image (when the pasteboard holds one). Its items are plain buttons; the pickers are presented from the chat view, since a `PhotosPicker` inside a `Menu` never opens. Every capture is a file in the app's outbox (`Outbox`), which the node stores as a blob when the message is sent. Images are written as types the models read, named for what their bytes are (the picker's type can be wrong, §2.11, Media types from the bytes): photos come from the picker as JPEG (a HEIC photo would reach the model as a name only), camera shots and frames are JPEG drawn upright (the node scales large images without reading EXIF orientation), a pasted screenshot stays PNG. A room scan attaches four files: the floor plan (JPEG), the measured summary (Markdown, naming the other three), the scan as JSON (`RoomScanPlan.json`: every surface and object as RoomPlan gave it, metres, transforms column-major, and the walls as the summary labels them) and RoomPlan's parametric USDZ export. The pending attachments show as thumbnails (Quick Look's: images, videos, PDFs, models; an icon and the name otherwise), each with a remove button; a message's own show the same way while its turn runs, until the transcript is read again with their blobs. Take a Picture shows wherever `UIImagePickerController` reports a camera (the Xcode 27 simulator does). Not built: pasting an image into the message field itself (SwiftUI's `pasteDestination` is iOS 27).
- **Continuing on another node:** Stitch's loop continues any conversation from its records. A Claude Code or Codex session a thread used lives on the machine that ran it; continuing elsewhere starts a new session given the context from the thread's transcript, which stays canonical.

### 2.12 Keep and drop

**Keep:**
- chat, with images, videos, PDFs and other files as attachments, resumable on any node;
- room scans (RoomPlan) as a capture source producing attachments;
- provider access with streaming, multimodal input included (in Rust);
- tools: the shell (local, and remote between nodes) and the `stitch` commands now; tools as Otter scripts later;
- SSH and local shell;
- engram;
- local llama.cpp on nodes (embeddings first);
- BYOK;
- embeds;
- text tools;
- the actor and persona idea, as worker configurations.

**Drop:**
- UI built from shards, and RealityKit;
- canvases as shards (embeds replace them);
- per-provider script variants;
- cr-sqlite as the source of truth;
- `ext/*` bindings;
- widgets at first;
- audio;
- watchOS at first;
- MCP for agents: other agents are driven as programs in the shell (§2.8). Tools from MCP servers are kept (§2.13, decided 2026-10-12).

### 2.13 Tools from MCP servers

**Decided 2026-10-12**, reversing "no MCP client until something needs one" (§2.12): services now publish their tools as MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers (issue trackers, error trackers, databases, the user's own), and wrapping each in a script for the shell is the per-service work Stitch avoids elsewhere. Coding agents stay programs in the shell (§2.8); MCP is for tools, not agents. The client is a node primitive in Rust (§2.2) over the `rmcp` crate (the protocol's official Rust SDK: its stdio and streamable HTTP client transports, initialization, JSON-RPC IDs, pagination and cancellation); what Stitch adds is configuration, lifecycle, naming, approvals and turning results into model input.

- **Configuration is per node,** in `config.toml`: `[mcp.servers.<name>]` says how to start or reach a server on this machine (a command and its paths, an endpoint, secrets), so it is machine-specific, and running a command is the node's policy: a server is never defined by a checkout (a cloned repository naming a command would run code on every node that opens it) nor by a synced record. Names are `[a-z0-9-]`, and every setting and secret is keyed by that name (edge talk's GitHub server broke when a display name and a keychain account disagreed).
- **Which threads get which tools is an enable list,** so the tool list stays small: nothing is offered until listed. `[mcp] enable` holds what every thread with tools on this node gets; `[mcp.projects."<dir>"] enable` adds what threads get whose shells start in that directory or below it (a checkout, a folder; `~` allowed). An entry is a server (`github`: all it offers) or a pattern over namespaced tool names (`github__get_*`, `*` matching any run of characters). A server's own `tools = [...]` (its unprefixed tool names, same patterns) limits what it offers anywhere. The configuration is read when a loop starts, so a change applies from the next turn without restarting the node; a server whose definition changed is stopped and started anew on next use. These are the node's defaults; a place's own settings and each thread's own set go on top of them (§2.14, decided 2026-10-12).
- **Transports: stdio first, streamable HTTP next** (both built). Stdio: `command`, `args`, `env`, `cwd` (default the home directory). The server is a child of the node, with the node's environment plus `env` (not a client's: one server serves every thread), its stderr in the node's log line by line. It is spawned, never forked (§2.3: the command is resolved to a path first, so the standard library uses `posix_spawn`), as the leader of its own process group, so stopping it stops what it started (`npx` and `uvx` wrappers). Streamable HTTP: `url`, `headers`; the session ID is the transport's business in `rmcp` (edge talk cached session IDs by URL in a global and cleared them by hand). The 2024-11-05 HTTP+SSE transport is not supported. Secrets: `key = "<name>"` names a key stored with `stitch key set <name>` (keychain, or the owner-only file; `STITCH_<NAME>_API_KEY` too), sent as `Authorization: Bearer` over HTTP, or put in the environment variable `key_env` of a stdio server; `headers` and `env` may also hold them literally, since `config.toml` never syncs.
- **Lifecycle: started lazily by the node, shared across threads, restarted after a crash, stopped with the node.** One hub per node holds a connection per server. A server starts the first time a loop whose thread enables it prepares its tools, before a model call: initialize, then `tools/list` (every page), within `startup_timeout_secs` (default 30). Every thread on the node shares that connection; concurrent calls are multiplexed by JSON-RPC IDs, as the protocol intends. The tool list is cached and listed again after `notifications/tools/list_changed`; one listing at a time is shared by whoever needs it, and calls to the server go on while it runs (a server slow to list held up every thread that used it, fixed 2026-10-13); a listing that fails or takes longer than `startup_timeout_secs` backs off as a start does, the last known tools standing meanwhile. A server whose process exits or whose transport closes is started again on next use; failures back off (2 s, doubling to 60 s), during which its last known tools stay listed and calls to it fail at once, saying why. **A server enabled for the thread that is down with no tools ever listed is not left out silently** (fixed 2026-10-30, issue a2vf: after a reboot the mail server's port refused, and threads simply had no `mail__*` tools, so the helper reported them missing rather than the server down): it is offered as one stand-in, `<server>__unavailable`, whose description carries the error and which, called, tries again (asking nothing) and says the error or that it is back, its tools offered from the next step; a call of one of its tools by name says the same. A call in flight when the server died fails, saying so, and is not retried: it may have taken effect. The node stops every server when it stops, and waits for them, having first stopped answering other nodes (§2.14); from then on none starts again, whatever still asks for it (a loop under way, a call, a publication): the session closed, then the stdio process group sent SIGTERM and, once its leader has exited or after 3 s, SIGKILL in any case, so nothing that outlived a wrapper exiting on SIGTERM survives (fixed: the group was killed only when the leader outlived the 3 s; the leader is waited for without being reaped, `WNOWAIT`, until the group was killed) (as built, 2026-10-12: the node spawns the process itself rather than through `rmcp`'s child transport, which killed the group only when the leader outlived its closed input, so a wrapper that exited cleanly left its server running; the group is signalled before its leader is reaped, so its ID is no other process's). A start that fails or times out kills the group too, and so does letting go of a server whose definition changed once no loop holds it. The handshake is `initialize` (protocol 2025-11-25 offered, older versions accepted), the one servers speak today; the stateless `server/discover` lifecycle of the 2026-07-28 revision can follow once servers do. Servers are not stopped when idle: they are small, and some keep state worth keeping.
- **How the model sees them:** after the built-in tools, named `<server>__<tool>`: the separator is unambiguous because server names have no `_` (edge talk's single `_` was not), and no built-in name contains `__`. Characters outside `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` in a tool's name become `_`, and a full name is at most 64 characters (the strictest provider's limit); a name that still clashes or is too long is left out, with a line in the node's log. The description is the server's, prefixed with `MCP server <name>:` and cut at 4,000 characters; the input schema is passed through as the server gave it (`"type": "object"` added when missing). Tools are sorted by name, so the list, and with it the provider's prompt cache, changes only when a server's tools do. A server that fails to start offers no tools in that step, its error in the node's log; `stitch mcp` checks the configuration by starting each server and listing its tools.
- **Calls go through Stitch's rules and approvals,** as shell commands do (§2.3): an MCP tool acts outside Stitch with the user's credentials. A call runs without asking when the server's `allow = [...]` patterns (unprefixed tool names) match it, when an `always` answer allowed it in the thread's place (§2.3), when the server has `approval = "allow"` (trusted whole), or when the node is in yolo (`[shell] approval = "allow"`); otherwise the user approves it in the TUI or CLI (shown as `run [mcp:<server>]: <server>__<tool> <arguments as JSON>`), or a detached thread asks a `question` record and waits, exactly as for a command. Servers' annotations (`readOnlyHint`, `destructiveHint`) are the server's claims, not the user's rules, and grant nothing. A declined call fails with the user's reason; an approved one is marked so, as commands are.
- **Results:** the content blocks in order: text as text; images into the blob store and the conversation as tool images (with `img:` IDs for `zoom_image`), as `browse_web`'s are; embedded text resources inlined with their URI; resource links and binary resources listed by URI and type, never fetched; audio named and left out. `structuredContent` is given as JSON when there are no blocks. `isError` makes the result a failure, as do protocol errors and timeouts, each naming the server. Text past 32,000 characters is cut, the whole saved under `<data>/downloads/` with its path in the result, as the web tools do.
- **Timeouts and cancellation:** a call waits at most `timeout_secs` (per server, default 120, at most 3600); then the node sends `notifications/cancelled` for it and the result says it timed out. A call the user interrupts (Esc), or whose thread is cancelled, is dropped and cancelled the same way, so the server can stop the work. Progress notifications do not extend the wait (later, with a cap).
- **Out of scope for now:** resources and their subscriptions (beyond what a tool result carries), prompts, sampling (a server asking Stitch's model), elicitation, roots, completion, the tasks and multi-round extensions, server logging beyond stderr; OAuth (authorization discovery, dynamic client registration, device flows: static tokens only; in edge talk that was a large share of the MCP code and of its fixes); the legacy SSE transport; MCP on the phone (no stdio there; HTTP servers once the iOS node has tools); Stitch as an MCP server for other agents. MCP tools are Rust node primitives now; with Otter (milestone 5) they become tools that Otter scripts call, under effect-based grants, like the shell.

What else edge talk taught: its servers were HTTP or SSE only (iOS), their tools copied into a database table by a manual sync and stale until synced again; Stitch lists them live and follows `list_changed`. It let each actor enable tools by name, which is what kept its tool lists usable; Stitch keeps that as the enable lists.

### 2.14 Tool and skill sets per thread, places' own settings, tools on other nodes

**Decided 2026-10-12**, with the user, for a case like this: a folder `~/Mail` where mail is handled, whose conversations get a Mail.app MCP server and a skill on handling mail, also when they are started on the iPhone, a full node, while the server runs on the Mac. Node-wide and per-directory enable lists (§2.13) cannot say that: the phone has neither the server nor the folder, and a thread cannot change what it has.

**One mechanism for tools and skills.** A thread has a tool set and a skill set: an ordered list of entries, each turning something on (`+x`, or `x`) or off (`-x`), where the last entry that matches an item decides.
- **Tool entries:** a built-in group, `shell` (the `shell_*` tools, `watch_file`, `unwatch`), `web` (`browse_web`, `web_eval`, `search_web`), `viewer` (`view_file`, `zoom_image`), `weave` (`spawn`, `send`, `wait`, `models`; `spawn` also names it), `memory` (`recall`; whether the thread is remembered at all stays `/memory`) or `images` (`generate_image`, §2.8), `calendar` (the calendar and reminders tools) or `maps` (`find_nearby`; both where the device lends them, §2.3); an MCP server (`github`, all it offers); or a pattern over namespaced MCP tool names (`github__get_*`). A bare name is a group when it names one, else a server; `<name>__*` always means the server (one named like a group: the reference `memory` server). In `config.toml`'s `[mcp]` lists, which only ever named servers, a bare name is the server, so a configuration from before keeps its meaning.
- **Skill entries:** a skill's name, or a pattern (`*`). A skill's own tools (§2.17) are listed or lazy as its SKILL.md says (`tools: lazy` in its front matter; decided 2026-10-20): lazy ones are not in the tool list, called with `Tool.Call` in `otter_run`, and `read_skill` gives their parameters.
- **Layers,** in order: the built-in default (every group the node runs is on; MCP tools are off until listed, §2.13; every skill listed for the thread is on), the node's `[mcp] enable` and `[mcp.projects."<dir>"] enable` (§2.13, kept as they were), the place's settings (below), then the thread's own changes in the order written. Defaults are worked out when a turn starts, on the node that runs it, so a changed configuration or place setting applies to existing threads from their next turn, as before; the thread's own changes follow it to every node.
- **Why changes, not a stored list:** two nodes changing a thread's set offline both take effect (each change is its own record, folded in order), where a `set` of the whole list (records.md §6) would keep only one. A thread's changes are `toolset` records in its repository (`tools = ["+github", "-web"]`, `skills = ["-release"]`, a `thread` reference), so they sync with the thread.
- **From the next turn:** a loop keeps the tools it started with; the next turn lists the new ones. The skills listed, and a short "Tools" note (tools that run on another node, entries that are on but unavailable here) are part of the thread's context (§2.3), so a change reaches the model the way an instruction file's does: as a `context` part with the next message.
- **Changing a set:** the TUI's `/tools` and `/skills` (an overlay listing every item with its state; Enter toggles; a new thread's changes are kept by the client and sent with its first message), the CLI's `stitch thread tools <id> +github -web` and `stitch thread skills <id> -release` (without changes, they list), `stitch chat --tools … --skills …`, and `spawn`'s `toolset` and `skills` (the CLI's `stitch chat --detach --tools …`): the spawning node writes the new thread's `toolset` before the thread or its assignment exists, so it is there wherever the thread starts. A spawned thread starts from its own defaults (its place is usually its parent's) plus what `spawn` names; it does not inherit its parent's changes.
- **Calls to what is not on:** a call to a built-in tool whose group is off, to an MCP tool configured here or shared by another node but not enabled, or to a server no node has (or has without sharing it), fails with a result that says which and how to turn it on (`/tools`, `stitch thread tools <thread> +x`), never an unknown-tool error or a shell call.

**A place's own settings and skills** (a project's checkout or a folder alike): `<place>/.stitch/settings.toml` and `<place>/.stitch/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`.
- `settings.toml`: `enable = ["mail"]` and `disable = ["web"]` (tool entries, as above), `memory = false` (its threads out of memory, §2.9), `approval = "ask" | "auto" | "yolo"` (the place's own approval mode over the node's, *2026-11-20, with the user*, §2.28) and `[skills] disable = [...]`. It can only turn on what a node configures: servers are still defined only by `config.toml` (§2.13), so a cloned checkout's settings start nothing a node does not already run; but its `approval` can loosen the approvals its threads' calls go through (to yolo, or auto), a trade-off taken knowingly (§2.28, with its open mitigation).
- Place skills are listed only for that place's threads, next to the home workspace's; a place skill with a home skill's name replaces it there.
- **They sync as records:** a node that has the place's directory reads its files at each turn and, when they differ from what it last published, writes a `place` record in the home workspace (`place = "folder:~/Mail"` or `"project:<repo>"`, its `node`, the settings, and each skill's name, description and text, at most 64 KiB each). A node with the directory uses its own files (none when it has no `.stitch/`: deleting it turns the place's settings off, published as an empty record when its last one was not); one without it (the phone) uses the latest record from any node. Records are per node, so two nodes holding different files never write over each other in turns.
- A skill that is only in a record (its files are on another node) is listed with "read it with `read_skill`", a tool offered when there is such a skill, which returns its text (files next to it are not carried).
- `[mcp.projects."<dir>"]` in `config.toml` keeps working: it is the node's own per-directory default, not synced.

**Tools on another node.** A server that opts in, `[mcp.servers.mail] share = true`, is offered to the threads of the user's other nodes.
- **Published as the node's `capabilities` record** (records.md §7: one per node, in the home workspace, the latest from a node standing): the names of the servers it configures, and, for each shared one, its tools as it lists them (name, description, input schema), after its own `tools` filter. A node starts its shared servers in the background when it starts (one that fails, to start or to list its tools, is tried again after its backoff, §2.13, and published once it lists them), and writes a new record when that list changes (a server's `list_changed`, once its tools have settled for 2 s; a configuration read at a turn's start), never twice the same (each server's tools in name order).
- **Listed for a thread that enables it** when this node does not configure that server, as `mail__search` with the description starting `MCP server mail on mbp:`, exactly where a local server's tools would be. A local server of that name wins; between two nodes sharing one, the first by name. A server another node configures without sharing it is unavailable here, and a call says so ("on mbp, not shared").
- **A call is sent to that node** as a question on its own stream (`stitch/node/1`'s `Ask`, as `models` is, §2.7): the thread, its repository, the server, the tool, the arguments. It runs there, under that node's rules: `allow`, `approval = "allow"`, its yolo; else the approval is a `question` record in the thread's repository (that node syncs it to the asking node at once, and any client of any node answers it), as for a detached thread (§2.3). A repository that node does not hold yet fails the call, saying to sync it there first, rather than asking where no client of the thread would look. Only an unanswered approval question is taken up again for such a call, never an answered one: that node cannot tell from the thread's steps, which it may not have yet, whether an answer was had, so an old answer never approves a new call (decided 2026-10-13). When the asker goes away while the call runs (its turn interrupted, its node stopped or gone), the stream closes and that node drops the call; a question it left open is answered there, declined by the node, "withdrawn: the caller is gone" (a queued call's, when its node stops: "withdrawn: the queued call was interrupted"). The result (text, images as bytes) comes back on the stream; the asking node shows it as a local call's. The connection keeps alive while the call runs; the server's own timeout bounds the call there.
- **Offline:** when the node cannot be reached within 5 seconds the call fails at once, saying so and how to queue it: `queue_call` (offered when the thread has remote tools) with the tool and its arguments writes a `call` record in the thread's repository (`node`, `server`, `tool`, `input` as JSON text, `thread` and `reports-to` references). That node, after its next sync with any node that has the record, takes it (a `claim`, then the call, under its rules and approvals as above) and writes the result as a `report` with a `call` reference (`failed`, saying why, when it could not run it), delivered to the thread like any report (§2.7: into its loop, else it wakes it), headed `[Report <report> from mbp: queued call mail__search (<call>)]`. A call claimed but not reported when its node starts again is reported as interrupted, not run again: it may have taken effect.
- **Not built:** progress of a remote call, cancelling one from the asking side other than by going away (an interrupted call closes its stream, and the remote node drops it, its MCP call cancelled on the server as an interrupted local one is), images in a queued call's result (a report carries text; they are counted), and resources, which MCP tools do not need here. An asking node of an older build is answered "a question this node does not know".

### 2.15 Forks, summaries and compaction

**Model defaults across clients and auxiliary calls (2026-10-27):** the TUI and phone ask the node for the effective next-turn model (`EffectiveModel`, also exposed through FFI), using the same resolver as chat: an explicit client pick, else the thread's deliberate choice, else its project's/synced runnable default (node-local config and built-in defaults remain fallbacks; a built-in choice is shown as such and never pinned, §2.7). They refresh it as sync arrives, without listing providers or pinning a displayed default. A concrete pick remains explicit even if it equals today's default. `default` and `quick` are durable role choices, resolved again on each turn, including detached wake/resume; selecting Follow default overrides an old thread pick without pinning today's model. Existing phone `model` (Settings) and `threadModels` choices are not deleted: their original intent cannot be inferred.
