Settings labels the former as a device-only **App override**, with None to follow the thread/synced default; a chat can explicitly follow default or quick. The TUI similarly offers the roles in `/model`. Models lists report the runnable default rather than a synced choice unavailable on this node. A turn explicitly routed to another machine is still resolved on that machine; the local display is this node's resolution, not a promise about remote credentials.

Titles, fork/compaction summaries and memory sleep gists now default to **quick**, resolving project then synced roles (gists are global). `[threads] title_model`, `summary_model` and `[memory] gist_model` still override that policy, and can name `default`, `quick` or a concrete model. No quick configured/available means the runnable default. Summaries keep their comprehensive structured prompt and full source: never cut history to fit quick. Their output allowance is a tenth of the summary model's window, at least 8192 and at most 64k (`fork::summary_max_tokens`; decided 2026-11-22 with the user: a fixed 8192 cut off every summary of a long compaction chain, whose summary carries more each link, and a cut-off summary is thrown away, so the chain could no longer compact). For an *automatic* quick summary only, a conservative content-byte estimate plus output reserve exceeding quick's window, an actual context-length rejection, or an empty/incomplete response gives way once to the thread's model. Explicit summary overrides do not silently switch. Only a nonempty, normally completed summary is published; a truncated/refused summary fails without creating the fork. No automatic billing/network-error fallback. This preserves continuity mechanically, but cannot score semantic summary quality: choose an explicit stronger summary model when needed. Memory retrieval, embeddings, sleep folding and window/model listing make no generative auxiliary calls; memory afterthoughts stay in the answering model's context, and image generation keeps its separate image-model setting.

**Decided 2026-10-13**, with the user, from edge talk's fork, summarize-and-continue and summarize-and-truncate, which Stitch lacked, and because detached threads that run for days outgrow their model's context window with nothing to stop them.

- **A fork is a thread that continues another, read from it, never copied** (`continues`, as a spawned thread's conversation is, §2.7): up to a message (`continues_msg`, with the tool calls that followed it) at the main line's head when forked, without attachments (each said as a line) or tool calls (what was said only), or its last N messages (counted back to one of the user's, so no reply or tool result starts it). It takes the thread's system prompt, place, and tool and skill changes. Edge talk copied rows, with new IDs; records here are immutable and synced, so a reference costs nothing and the original stays the source.
- **Summarize & continue, summarize & truncate:** a `summary` on the fork stands for what it leaves out: all of it, or what comes before its last N messages. Edge talk's prompt (structured for retrieval: topics, an executive context, a breakdown, verbatim anchors, files, a continuity cheat sheet) with a section on work state (what changed, what ran, what is next), by `[threads] summary_model` or quick (with the context/continuity safeguards above); one prompt in Rust, not one per provider. The summary is the fork's first message, as a user message saying it continues an earlier conversation, and is shown at the top of its transcript.
- **Titles** carry edge talk's prefixes: ⑂ a fork, ↻ a summary, ✂ a summary and the last messages. A new chat is titled from its first exchange, in the background, by `[threads] title_model` (else quick; `titles = false` turns it off), as a `set` of `title` written by the node; one the user gives (`rename`) stands, and wins over a generated one written later. Edge talk did the same with eight provider scripts.
- **Pins** are a `set` of `pinned` on the thread: pinned threads list first, on every node.
- **Quoting** (edge talk's quote) writes the thread's transcript to a Markdown file framed as material from another conversation, "do not continue it", attached to a new thread's first message.
- **Search for the user** (`stitch search`, `/search`): every thread's messages by their words (the BM25 archive the `recall` tool searches, §2.9) and, where memory runs, the messages memory holds by meaning (EmbeddingGemma 2, cosine distance within 0.36, looser than asked recall's floor since a list is skimmed, not put in a prompt), fused by reciprocal rank (k = 60), agent-to-agent threads after the user's own (§2.9); a place's threads only on request. Edge talk's search bars were per persona, project and global; the archive is rebuilt in memory per thread as threads move, and becomes the derived full-text index of §2.5 (SQLite FTS) when it needs to persist.
- **Rewinding a thread** (decided 2026-10-22 with the user: edit-and-resend goes on in the same thread, not a fork, as Esc Esc does in Claude Code; `rewind.rs`). A `rewind` record, by the user, names the thread, the message its conversation goes on from (`upto`, a message ID) and the segment holding it (IDs are per branch; neither: from the thread's start). The next turn's segment follows that segment (`prev`) and says so in its header (`rewind`, the record; `upto`, the message): its branch. **The main line** is the latest rewind's branch (by the record's time, then its ID), the longest chain within it when two nodes continued it; while nothing continues the rewind yet, the line up to its message; with no rewind, the longest chain (§2.4). A rewind whose segment is not here yet waits, the one before it standing. Every node holding the same records reads the same line. Only the user's rewinds count, as with `set`s of a thread's mode.
  - **What came after stays stored, off the line:** not in the transcript (TUI, phone, `stitch thread`), the model's context, the "other continuations" note (§2.4, which tells only forks within the current branch), search (the BM25 archive reads the line) or what memory recalls (its messages no longer resolve). Messages after a rewind are numbered past every message the thread holds on any branch (`m5` after a rewind to `m2` from `m4`), so a message off the line and one on it never share an ID, which memory and search name messages by. Showing "earlier versions" later needs nothing new: the branches are there, each a rewind's.
  - **Edit and resend, Retry, rewind to here:** edit (the TUI's Esc Esc `e`, the phone's Edit & Resend) and Retry rewind to the message before the user's (the start, for a first message) and then send the text again, in the composer or at once; "rewind to here" (`r` in the TUI's picker) goes on from the message picked, and on one of the user's sends it again (a retry). Fork from here stays the way to a second thread. The phone sends the message's attachments again (it reads their blobs as files, as before); the TUI does not (it attaches paths), said in its status line.
  - **The TUI after a rewind** prints nothing again, consistently with forks (§5, "What a fork shows"): a line `── rewound: the thread goes on from m3 "…"; what came after it above is set aside ──`, then what follows; its count of what is shown, its message picker and Ctrl+Y take the line.
  - **Running turns:** a rewind is refused while a turn runs on the thread on the node it is asked of (stop it first). A turn running on another node from before the rewind is stopped there when the rewind reaches it (`rewind::stop_overtaken`, after each sync: turns that started before a rewind another node wrote); what it wrote meanwhile lands off the line, stored. After a rewind the thread runs on the node it was rewound on until a turn continues it (`runner`), and an unfinished turn at the rewind (a retry's message not yet answered) is that node's to resume.
  - **Forks and compaction follow the line:** a fork made after a rewind takes the line (up to the rewind's message, while nothing continues it); one made before keeps what it took (its `continues_at` and `upto` name it). Compaction summarizes the line. A thread spawned from a turn keeps the conversation up to its spawning call, on whichever branch that was.
- **A fork shows what it continues** (decided 2026-11-24, with the user: a fork's chat showed only "⑂ continues thread …", so the history it went on from could not be read, nor rewound into, from the fork). The model's context is unchanged; what clients show changes, as compaction chains show theirs ("A visible boundary", below):
  - **What is shown:** the history of the thread it was forked from, up to the fork point: that thread's compaction chain, each compaction's `compacted` divider between its parts, and what the chain's first thread was forked from, recursively, with its own `forked` divider (`chat::fork_past`). Then the fork's `forked` divider, in place of the fork notice, and the fork's own messages (its own compaction chain, as before). A fork of the last N messages, or a summarized one, still shows the whole history up to the fork point.
  - **Read-only, and from where:** every entry of that history is `ancestor`, with no `id` (Fork and Rewind offer only the fork's own messages, as with an earlier part of a compaction chain), and names its `source`: its thread, and a message's ID there, so a client offers "open in the original" (and forking or rewinding there).
  - **What the model reads:** an ancestor the fork's model reads is marked (`inherited` in the web client's entries, `context` in `chat::Shown`, `in_context` over FFI). They are known by their keys, a message by its time and ID and a call by its ID, matched against `chat::inherited`, so options that reshape what a fork takes (no tool calls, no attachments) still match. Where that reading starts later than the history does (a fork of the last N, a compaction's kept messages), a `context` entry stands before its first event ("── the fork's model reads from here on ──"). A summary the model reads is the `forked` (or `compacted`) divider's.
  - **Nothing twice:** what a fork inherits is that history, marked, never added again; a fork's own segments hold only its own events.
  - **The divider** (`ForkPoint`): `from` (and its `title`), `to`, `at`, `upto` (the message it was forked at, `None` for the latest), `how` (`full`, `last`, `summary` or `summary+last`), `last`, `summary`, `no_tools`, `no_attachments`, `shared` (the thread forked is a conversation another user shared: its manifest's hash, the sender's key, whether that key signed it, whether that is the user's own key; design §2.31), and `said`, its line (`⑂ forked from “title” at m12: its last 4 messages (a conversation shared with you, signed by key 1a2b…)`).
  - **Surfaces:** the web client's transcript (`Request::Transcript`: ancestor entries with `ancestor`, `source {thread, id}`, `mid`, `inherited`, `shared`; `{n, kind: "forked", …}` and `{n, kind: "context", text}` dividers; shown dimmed, with "Open in original", a rule with the summary folded and a link for the divider); `chat::lineage_shown` (the TUI: a dimmed divider as a compaction's, the `context` line as a notice; the app over FFI: `Entry.fork`, `ancestor`, `source_thread`, `source_id`, `in_context`, `shared`, and `Entry.compaction` now set from the entry itself, so a parent's compactions are told too). A message of a conversation another user shared is `shared` wherever it is shown, and styled as foreign.
  - **Tested:** `tests/fork_history.rs` covers a fork of a compacted thread (the parent's chain, its compaction divider, the context starting at the compaction's kept messages, the fork's own messages with their IDs), and a fork of that fork of its last 2 messages (two `forked` dividers, the context marked on the last 2, nothing twice, no IDs but the fork's). It checks the same on `lineage_shown`. `tests/share.rs` covers a fork of an imported thread: its history read-only and `shared`, the divider saying whose. `root.rs`'s `a_forks_transcript_shows_what_it_inherits` covers a fork of the last 2 and a summarized fork.
- **In the TUI** (2026-10-30): `/fork` and `/compact` with no arguments open a form of these options with a preview of what is kept (§5, "The fork form"); the phone's options sheet getting the preview is open.
- **Compaction is a new thread, never a rewrite** (decided with the user: a new thread, not the thread's history replaced in place). Before a turn, a thread whose latest step sent its model more than `[threads] compact_at` percent (80) of its context window continues in a new thread: summarize & truncate, keeping `compact_keep` messages (10; a thread shorter than that is summarized whole), marked `compacted = true`. A prompt the provider refuses as too long is compacted the same way and tried once more. **Midway through a turn too** (fixed 2026-11-19, issue 2026-11-19T095120Z-gamer-rwg5: a detached turn of heavy tool use grew from under the threshold past a 1M window and ended `failed: prompt is too long`, since a detached turn compacted only as it started and only a chat retried): the refused step is not written (`chat::failed`; what came since the last step written is, though: a message woven in meanwhile, whose sender was told it went to the model, or Stitch's `NO_REPLY` note, so the compaction keeps it rather than losing it; a step that said nothing is written as it comes, so `NO_REPLY`'s extra call counts in `left_after` like any other), so a detached turn (`turn_with`) compacts and tries once more on the new thread within the same turn (its lock taken, `moved_to`, its steering, report and live feed the new thread's, the askers of both tries answered); the first turn of a spawn and a resumed loop compact and hand the rest to a turn on the new thread (`goes_on_compacted`), with that turn's limits given explicitly (`TurnOpts::budget`, and a schedule's capability profile), never the thread's own budget in their place. When steps of the turn were already written (the message with the first), the retry does not send the message again (it is in the summary or the kept messages) but a note that the thread was compacted midway and to go on from where it stopped (`MIDWAY_NOTE`); when nothing was, the message goes as it was. Once per turn; an app's thread only as `may_spend` allows; a compaction that fails (nothing written yet to summarize) leaves the failed report as before. **Limits carry across** (2026-11-19, the user, rejecting a first version that gave the retry the turn's limits afresh): the continuation runs under what is left of the turn's own limits (`left_after`), whatever they were (the thread's remaining budget, a schedule firing's smaller cap, a typed chat's steps): its steps less the model calls taken (the steps written since the turn began and the refused call, which counts as one: `Limits::steps` is the model calls a turn takes, so with the step limit's wrap-up call the two tries together take no more calls than the turn would have), its money and tokens less what its written steps spent (their `usage` events, its tools' included; the refused call spent nothing), its deadline as it was. The hand-off's turn can compact once more (`turn_with`'s retry), under what is left again: two compactions at most, never more calls or money than the original turn had. With nothing left (no step, no money or tokens, past its deadline) it neither compacts nor goes on: it reports as the limit does, `stopped` ("most steps") or `over-budget` (`finish_said`). **The summary is paid from the turn too** (2026-11-19, the user: no cap may be exceeded): a midway compaction's summary is a model call of up to the window's input, so with money or tokens limited the turn compacts only when the most the summary may spend (`fork::compaction_bound`: every summary model it may ask, quick and the thread's, each reading at most the bytes it would send and at most its window, writing at most its output allowance (`summary_max_tokens`), at its price; a subscription's free, as the turn's own steps are) is under what is left, and not when a price is not known beforehand; else it reports `over-budget`. Compacted, what the summary's calls spent (`fork::compact_counted`) is taken off the continuation's limits; nothing left, the new thread reports `over-budget` without a step. It is written as `usage` on the thread compacted, as every compaction's summary is (below, "A chain is one budget"), so the chain's budget over later turns and a schedule's caps count it, once (the turn's own limits took it off directly and read nothing back). **A chain is one budget** (decided 2026-11-20 with the user, "option A", issue 2026-11-19T193029Z-beast-ijcq: a compaction copied the budget to the new thread whole while spending was read per thread, so every compaction, as a turn starts, by the user's `/compact` or midway, gave a thread its total budget back, and a schedule's `spent_since`, reading only the newest thread of each, forgot what came before): whatever means a thread's total reads its whole compaction chain (`detached::spent_by_chain`, `spent_by_chains`: `Index::compaction_lineage`, each thread once, each reading only its own steps, since what a compaction takes of the thread it continues is read there, never copied): `weave::remaining` (later turns, resumed loops, a subscription's turns, apps), a schedule's `spent_since` and so `cap_left` and its firings' costs, a room's money, a report's cost, the hazard guard's `budget_left`, the `thread_usd` a chat's end shows, and self-improvement's tree and triage spend (`selfimprove.rs`). The budget field stays as it was (copied, a lifted one lifted); what changes is the reading. A turn whose budget is already spent no longer compacts as it starts (no turn runs, so no summary is paid for one). **Every summary is spent on the chain** (2026-11-20, the follow-up to option A): whatever compacts (as a turn starts, detached or a chat; a chat's retry after a too-long refusal; the user's `/compact`; midway; a room's speaker), its summary's model calls are written as `usage` on the thread compacted, a step of their own after the compaction (`fork::compact_as` → `chat::write_spent`, under the lock its caller holds), so the chain's budget, a schedule's caps and the cost shown count them exactly once; a summary paid for whose compaction then fails (an incomplete summary with nothing to fall back to, a failure after its call) is written there all the same (`summarize` and `fork_with` add each call to the caller's `SummarySpent` as it is made, whatever follows). Only a midway compaction also takes it off the running turn's own limits; one as a turn starts is paid before the turn's limits are read (`weave::remaining` then counts it), and one before a turn given explicit limits (`TurnOpts::budget`) is counted for later turns and caps, not against that turn's. A plain fork's summary (`/summarize`, a fork of part of it) is a new conversation and still spends outside the thread (a known gap). Known effect: the summary's `usage` is the latest on the old thread, so after a failed compaction `last_prompt_tokens` reads the summary call's prompt until the next step. `spent_by` (one thread's own) stays for what is a turn's (`left_after`, since the turn began on that thread). Known limit: a typed chat's turn whose steps the user extended (`more_steps`) is counted against its first grant only, so it stops rather than ask again. The new thread is the old one's from then on: a detached one keeps its goal, budget, model, policy and parent; what is sent to the old one, its notes and reports, `wait` for it, `cancel` and resuming at start all go to the newest; it reads as one conversation (below). **Never while a turn runs on it** (issue 2026-11-04T102014Z-gamer-mfnh: a child's note to a parent waiting on it compacted the parent at once, and the compacted thread's turn ran beside the original's, two supervisors on one goal): a message, report or note for it (`detached::turn_with`) takes the thread's lock first, then the locks of its newer compactions, and only then decides to compact, taking the new thread's lock too for the turn; a chat compacts only when no loop here holds the thread (else it waits for that loop or goes into it, uncompacted), holding the old thread's lock, and a compaction after a too-long refusal takes the new thread's lock. Locks along a compaction chain are always taken oldest first, so they cannot deadlock. Context windows are the provider's `context_window`, else what the provider says of the model, else a guess by model name (§2.8, prompt caching and context windows).
- **One conversation (decided 2026-11-09 with the user, "option b"):** a compaction made a second chat, beside the first in every list, with the pin left on the old one, and only a TUI chat that compacted was told: a compaction on the detached path (a report, a note, the web client's `Send`, a schedule) was silent, and a client open on the old ID saw nothing until it was reopened. Now a compaction chain is one conversation in every client; the threads stay as they are (no migration), the reading changes:
  - **Lists show a chain once**, as its newest thread: `Index::listed_threads` (the TUI's picker, `stitch threads`, `Request::Threads` and so the web client's sidebar and the dashboard, the app's `threads()` over FFI, `last`) leaves out every thread compacted into another; `weave`'s list of detached threads too. Its **pin** is the chain's: the latest `set` of `pinned` on any of its threads (`Index::lineage_pinned`), so a pin on an old thread carries over, and `Pin` (written on the newest) wins over it either way. Its **title** likewise: the latest `set` of `title` on any of them (a `rename`, or the one generated for the first exchange), else the newest's own without the ✂ a compaction's carries (the same conversation; a fork the user makes keeps ⑂/↻/✂, though a ✂ at the latest message is a compaction since 2026-11-16, below). `Rename` writes on the newest too. A row of `Request::Threads` has `earlier`, the threads it was compacted from, oldest first; asked for one of those (`thread`), it gives the newest's row.
  - **An old ID is the newest**: a chat, `Send`, reports and notes already went there; now so do `InFlight`, `Pin`, `Rename`, the web client's `follow` (the newest's feed, said under the ID followed), and the TUI on its poll and when it reopens the last thread of a place. Old links and notifications open the conversation.
  - **One event, on every path:** `fork::compact` (every compaction comes there) records the model whose window was outgrown, that window and what its last step sent (`compacted_model`, `compacted_window`, `compacted_tokens` on the new thread), pushes a note on the old thread's live feed while the summary is written, then `LiveEvent::Compacted(Compaction {from, to, model, window, tokens, kept, summary})` on both feeds; a chat's own client also gets `Reply::Compacted` (then `Thread`). A follower of the old thread hears it and goes on following the new thread's feed from its start (`apps::ws`), so a web view open on the old ID streams the new turn at once; a chat passed to another node relays it. Clients switch to `to` when they show `from`.
  - **A visible boundary:** the transcript of a chain (`Request::Transcript`, `chat::lineage_shown` for the TUI) is the old threads', then at each compaction a divider entry, then the newest's own messages: in the web client's transcript `{n, kind: "compacted", at, said, from, to, model, window, tokens, kept, summary}` (`said` is the line, `Compaction::said`: "compacted here (162k of 200k window): summary + last 10 messages"; `n` is the event it comes before), shown as a rule with the summary folded and a link to the earlier thread; in the TUI a `compacted` entry, printed as a dimmed `── compacted here (…) ──` with the summary's first lines under it. What the newest inherits (the summary as its first message, the kept messages) is not shown twice. The messages of the earlier threads carry no `id`: message IDs are per thread, so the message picker, Fork and Rewind offer only the newest's. `Transcript` of an earlier thread reads the chain up to it, as it was, and every `Transcript` says the newest as `current`: the web client opens the newest when it is asked for an old one, and opens the old one as it was (read-only in effect: a message typed there goes to the newest, which it switches to) only from the divider's link. A compaction is not a fork: `forked_from` is none for it.
  - **iOS** (2026-11-09) follows the same contract over FFI. Its list is `threads()`: one row per chain, its `earlier` IDs with it, a spawn's `parent` taken to its newest, so nothing lists or hangs under an old ID (the app looks a thread's row up by either). `newest(thread)` (`Index::compaction_newest`) resolves an ID wherever one comes in: the selection (a notification, a `stitch://thread` link, a pinned app's conversation, a parent; one place, `MainView`), the chat opened for it, a `PendingSend` kept from a killed process; the per-thread model choice and kept message move with it. `transcript()` reads the lineage (`chat::lineage_shown`), each divider an entry `who == "compacted"` with its `compaction` (`CompactionInfo`: from, to, model, window, tokens, kept, summary, `said`); the app draws it as a rule with the line, the summary folded under a Summary button, and an "Earlier thread" link that opens that thread as it was (a banner over it, its "Latest" button; what is typed there goes to the newest, which the chat switches to). A chat's listener gets `compacted(CompactionInfo)` (no longer a note): the divider goes in before the message being sent and the chat moves to `to` at once (the view, the list's selection, the running turn's bookkeeping). A compaction the app did not run (a detached turn, another node's) is found when the open chat reads again on the index's revision: its thread's newest differs, and it moves the same way.
  - A follower on an older node (during a rollout) cannot read `Compacted` in a peer's follow answer: its chat on that node loses touch and reads the turn once synced.
  - **A turn that compacts is the new thread's at once** (fixed 2026-11-16): `detached::turn_with` listed its loop (`running`, what `is_running`, `cancel` and `stop_reported` read) under the thread it was asked for, then compacted it and ran the turn on the new one, whose steering is keyed by the new ID; so for that whole turn `is_running(newest)` was false: an event's ask for it was not woven in but batched behind the turn (the flusher thinking nothing ran), and `cancel` (which resolves the newest) answered "not running". A loop is now listed under the thread it runs on: `Registered::moved_to` re-keys it when the turn follows a compaction made while it waited and when it compacts (the turn's own `listed` key is shared with `register`, which runs after the spawn, so a move before the listing lands where it should; the new thread's reports taken as known, so `stop_reported` does not read an old `cancelled` as new). `is_running` also answers for an old ID (it checks the thread and its newest). Tested with `a_turn_that_compacts_as_it_starts_is_the_new_threads` (an ask woven into such a turn; `cancel` by the old ID and by the newest stops it).
  - **"Summarize & truncate" is a compaction** (decided 2026-11-16, after a real case: the app's button made a ✂ fork that looked like a compaction, but the pin, the title, the listing, the thread's event subscriptions, its schedules, `wait`, `cancel` and reports stayed on the old thread, and the user's messages went into both). On the node, so every client gets it: a `Request::Fork` that summarizes, keeps the last N, is cut at the latest message (no `upto`) and drops nothing (neither `no_attachments` nor `no_tools`) goes through `fork::compact_by_user` (`fork::requested` decides): the newest of the chain is compacted, keeping N, with everything a compaction carries (`compacted = true`, detached fields, model, memory switch, taint, `LiveEvent::Compacted` on both feeds); `compacted_model` is the thread's own model, with its window and what its last step sent, and the record says `compacted_by_user = true` (the divider reads as an automatic one's: the figures are true either way). Never while a turn runs on it: refused when this node's lock of the thread is held (a chat or a loop at work), or another node has its turn, as a rewind is; the lock is held while the summary is written, so a turn sent meanwhile waits and then runs on the new thread. The reply is still `Forked(new)`, which clients open (the app's `started`, the TUI's `/compact`): the same conversation. A plain fork, a summary of all of it (`/summarize`, "Summarize & continue") and a fork up to a message or without attachments or tool calls stay forks the user made. Tested with `summarize_and_truncate_compacts_the_thread` (the pin, the listing and the thread's subscription follow it; a summary of all of it is not a compaction).

### 2.16 Folder sync

**Decided 2026-10-14, with the user; built the same day** (`folders.rs`). A folder place (§2.3) holds working files the user and agents edit on every node: its `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`, `.stitch/`, notes, common docs. Only a place's settings and skills reached other nodes before (§2.14), as records written one way by the node with the directory, so a phone could read neither the folder's instructions nor its docs, nor write them. The folder's files sync instead, both ways, on the record and blob infrastructure, the way iCloud Drive syncs: no CRDT, nothing to resolve by hand.
- **Not git** (considered with the user, and dropped): git suits histories people curate, not unattended sync. Commits would be made up from guesses at when an edit settled; a conflict is a state git leaves for a person; a clone takes everything where a phone wants content on demand; binaries and large files need LFS; and packs would be a second sync system beside records, with a second identity and history. What git would give, diffs and three-way merges of text, comes from libraries (`similar`, `diffy`) over the history records keep.
- **Snapshots as records, trees and contents as blobs:** each change is a `snapshot` record in the folder's own store (records.md §3), naming the root tree, the snapshots it follows (two or more for a merge), the paths it changed, the node, and the thread when a turn made it. A tree is a directory as a JSON blob, content-addressed as git's are, so an unchanged directory costs nothing again and the same content is the same tree on every node. A snapshot attaches the blobs it adds, so always-on nodes pull them with it; a phone fetches what a merge needs. The store's ID derives from the folder's identity, as a project's from its first commit: every node finds it from the folder's threads, with nothing to register (a `link` that joins two folders under a new identity starts a new store, merged from the files as any first sync).
- **A pass** scans the folder (size and modification time first, hashes for what changed; `state.json` keeps what the last scan saw and the snapshot the files are at), publishes what changed since that snapshot, merges in what the other nodes published, writes the result, and syncs the folder's store with the peers at once when it published, so they merge it in their pass after the sync. A pass runs before each turn on the place (so the turn reads the files as the others left them), when the turn ends (its changes credited to its thread), after every sync, and when asked (`stitch folder sync`, the app's Files). **Changes are applied** only while no turn runs on the place, and a file is written or removed only if it is still as the scan saw it: an edit made meanwhile is taken up by the next pass.
- **Conflicts, iCloud's way:** a text file edited on two nodes since their common ancestor is merged three ways when the edits do not overlap; otherwise the later edit wins and the other is kept beside it as a conflict copy (`AGENTS (mac, 2026-10-14).md`). "Later" is by the snapshots' times (the node's name breaking a tie), so two nodes merging the same changes at once decide alike (found by CI on Linux: dated "now", each node's own change won on it, and the two never agreed). A deletion against an edit keeps the edit. Another node's change that follows this node's is taken as it is (a fast-forward), so merges happen only when both changed.
- **What syncs:** any file up to 25 MB, binaries too (`[folders] max_file_mb`; decided 2026-10-22, with the user, after a PDF in a folder stayed on its node: text files up to 1 MiB were all that synced before, too little for the documents a folder holds). Only text (UTF-8, no NUL) merges three ways; a binary changed on two nodes keeps the later one and a conflict copy, and `stitch folder diff` says "Binary files … differ". Minus ignore rules (`.DS_Store`, `node_modules`, `target`, `build`, `dist`, `.venv`, editors' leftovers, and the folder's own `.stitch/ignore`: a pattern a line, `*` and `?`, against a name, or against the path when it has a `/`). What a node has but does not carry (a larger file, a link, a checkout inside the folder; a binary too on a node from before 2026-10-22) has no say in its trees: there it publishes and keeps what the others share, and never writes those paths. (Found while testing: listing such paths in a node's tree set its trees apart from the others' forever, and each node merged the other's merge in turn.) Phones hold what syncs: a phone has no folders of the user's, so it keeps a copy in its data directory (`folders/<id>/files/`), which is the folder's directory there (its threads' instructions are read from it).
- **A new folder asks first, and no folder grows without bound** (decided 2026-10-20, with the user, who saw that a thread started or spawned in the wrong directory, `~/.cargo` or `~/Downloads`, would publish everything in it to every node, past taking back: snapshots are records, and nothing limited a folder's count or size). Three guards, in the pass, so they hold whatever made the place (a chat, `spawn`'s `place`, a schedule, a phone): (1) *A folder that never synced syncs only once the user lets it.* Its pass publishes and merges nothing until a `folder` record says `sync = <key>`, `allow = true`; the first turn on it asks, as an approval of that thread (a `question`, session `folder sync`, in the attention list and pushed to the phones, with what it holds: "3,412 file(s), 210 MB"), without holding up the turn, whose files then stay where they are. Yolo does not answer it: what it guards is past the sandbox's reach. Allowed, the folder syncs at once; declined, it is `allow = false` and never asked again; `stitch folder sync --allow` (or `--stop`) in it answers from a shell, the latest answer counting. A folder with a snapshot already (from before this, or from a node where it was allowed) goes on syncing, unasked. (2) *Some directories never sync unless allowed, and do not ask:* a hidden directory in the home directory (`~/.cargo`, `~/.cache`, `~/.config`), a temporary directory, the system's (`/usr`, `/etc`, `/var`, `/opt`, …). They are still places (instructions, skills), only their files stay; making them global instead was weighed and left, since a thread's directory there is often the user's choice and the danger is only the sync. (3) *Limits, for every folder:* at most 2,000 files it would carry and 250 MB of them (`[folders] max_files`, `max_mb` in config.toml; 50 MB until binaries were carried, 2026-10-22), counted from sizes before each pass reads anything; a folder over either syncs nothing at all (never a part of it, which would merge as deletions elsewhere) and says so, pointing to `.stitch/ignore`.
- **A global thread's working folder is a folder too** (decided 2026-11-01, with the user; §2.3): identity `workdir:<origin>`, its directory `<data>/workdirs/<origin>/` on every node (phones included: no copy apart), synced with no question. Until it holds a file nothing is held for it. Its first snapshot also writes a `folder` record (`sync = workdir:<origin>`, `allow = true`) in the home workspace, pushed at once: that record, not a thread, is what makes every node hold the folder's store (a node holding every global thread's store would sync hundreds of empty ones), so the always-on nodes take a phone's changes, blobs too, while it is still online, before any turn on the thread runs there. A node that begins to hold a folder's store (this, or a folder place's first thread arriving) syncs that store with its peers at once rather than at the next interval. The directory itself is made on a node only by a turn on the thread (or `stitch folder sync` in it); elsewhere its store is records and blobs alone. `stitch app export <thread | pinned app | blake3:version> [dir]` writes an app version's files from its manifest's tree (default `./<name as a slug>`, refusing a directory that is not empty without `--force`): the way to get an app out when its source is on another node or was never a file.
- **Reviewing and writing:** `stitch folder log [path]` lists the changes (when, which node, which thread, what), `stitch folder diff [path] [--from] [--to]` shows one as a unified diff; the app's Files (a chat's toolbar, in a folder) lists a folder's files, edits a text file (saved, it syncs), and shows a file's or the folder's history and each change's diff.
- **Agents edit files without a shell** (decided 2026-10-14, with the user; the desktop prompt says to prefer them to `cat >`, here-documents and `sed -i`, after an agent's here-document waited for an approval that could not offer "always", writing forms having no prefix to grant): `write_file` (a file whole) and `edit_file` (exact string replacement, unique unless `replace_all`, the change shown as a diff), the `files` group, on every node; on a phone they are the only way an agent changes a file, so a phone's turn in a folder edits its copy and the change syncs. Inside the thread's place (its folder, a phone's copy, its checkout) they run without asking, since a folder keeps every version and a checkout has git; outside it they ask, as a writing shell command does, unless yolo is on; a read-only thread refuses them, and so does a phone's clone of a project. A path is resolved through what exists of it, so `..` or a link cannot make a file seem inside; files in `.git` are never written. Files are written through a temporary file beside them, keeping their permissions.
- **A thread continued on another node** (decided 2026-10-16, with the user, after a thread started on the Mac and continued on the phone looked for its files at the Mac's paths): its prompt and its earlier turns are the node it started on's (§2.4: "This node", the rules, every path). So each turn's context says where it runs: the node, its system and home directory, the place's directory there (a phone's copy of a folder, its clone of a project), that earlier turns may have run elsewhere with that node's paths and tools, and on a node that runs no programs, that there are no shells or browser and how files are read and changed. And on a phone, a folder's paths on the other nodes (`~/home-notes/…`, `/Users/<name>/home-notes/…`, a linked location's) name its copy for `view_file`, `edit_file` and `write_file`, so paths from earlier turns work as written. A project's checkouts are each node's own (not synced), so for a phone's clone the context says that a path inside a checkout elsewhere is the same path inside the clone, and the agent maps it.
- **Projects on the phone are clones it keeps itself** (decided 2026-10-14, with the user; built the same day, `clones.rs`): a git work tree is never file-synced (below), so a phone, which has no checkout, clones a project from its `origin` and fetches it on its own, needing no other node online. The clone is in `checkouts/<project>/` in the data directory and is the project's checkout there, so the project's threads read its instructions and files. It is fetched before a turn on the project (20 seconds at most, then the turn goes on with what is there; with no clone yet, the first is made in the background, since it may take long), after every sync (the clones there are, in the background: the sync does not wait for GitHub), and when the app opens a chat of the project. Clones are lazy: a sync fetches the clones there are and makes none. **A first clone asks** (decided 2026-10-21, with the user, after a large repository was cloned on the phone just by opening a chat of it; a size limit in config.toml was built first and dropped: the user wants to choose, not a threshold): a project is cloned on a device only once the user says yes there, kept per device in `clones.json` in the data directory (yes or no; a clone made before this is kept and fetched). Opening a chat of a project never cloned and never answered shows a dialog, "Clone github.com/o/r to this device (123 MB on GitHub)?" (GitHub's repository `size`; "its size not known" otherwise), Clone or Don't clone (`ProjectCloneQuestion`, `ProjectClone`); a yes clones at once, so the chat's first message finds it. Opening the project's Files asks again after a no. A turn on the project with no answer yet (a thread spawned onto the phone, a schedule) asks it as an approval of its thread (session `clone`, like a new folder's sync; yolo does not answer it) and waits; declined, or a new chat with no thread to ask for, it goes on without a clone. **A turn waits for a first clone** (the same day): a turn on a project being cloned for the first time, or allowed and not cloned yet, waits for the clone (10 minutes at most) before its context is made, and its start directory and place are taken after it (they were taken before, so a new chat's first turn had no place directory); an existing clone is fetched within 20 seconds as before. gitoxide over HTTPS, through the node's own `reqwest` and rustls (ring), so no C or OpenSSL and it builds for iOS and visionOS like the rest; it is built only for them (and for tests): a desktop never clones this way (its projects are the user's checkouts; one with no checkout of a project clones it with `git` once the user says yes, §2.3), so there gitoxide would be build time and binary size for code that never runs. A clone is opened with an identity of its own for its reference logs, since a phone has no git configuration. **One operation at a time per clone, stale git locks cleared** (decided 2026-10-30, after the phone's clone of stitch kept an empty `packed-refs.lock` from an update cut short, the app suspended or killed, and every fetch since failed with "Failed to update references…"): each fetch, switch, discard or push holds the clone's lock until its blocking work ends, even when its caller stopped waiting (`before_turn`'s 20 seconds dropped the wait and freed the lock while the fetch ran on, so a second could start beside it); and before changing a clone, with that lock held, the lock files git takes in its `.git` (`packed-refs.lock`, `index.lock`, `HEAD.lock`, `config.lock`, `refs/**/*.lock`) are removed, each said in the log: nothing else in the process is working on that clone, so any lock there is stale, and the user cannot remove files in the app's container. The remote branch followed is the one of the branch the clone is on: `origin/HEAD` is written once at the clone and a fetch does not move it (found by the test). Credentials are the node's GitHub sign-in (§2.8), sent to `github.com` only; public repositories need none. It sees what is pushed, not a Mac's local branches or uncommitted work; fetching from the user's nodes over iroh can come later, for those and for repositories without an `origin`.
- **Branches, edits and pushes from the phone** (decided 2026-10-14, with the user, the same day): the app's Files shows a project's clone with its branch (any branch of `origin`; switching is refused while the clone has changes of its own), its changes from the branch (each as a diff, discarded with a swipe), and its files, edited in the app or by the phone's agents (`edit_file`, `write_file`). A fetch merges the clone's changes three ways onto what arrived; where they overlap it touches nothing and names the files. gitoxide cannot push (0.89 reads push settings but has no push), so a commit goes through GitHub's API: each changed file a blob, a tree on the branch's, a commit (authored as the GitHub account, its noreply address), then the branch moved on only if it has not moved on GitHub meanwhile (fetch and commit again), or a new branch, with a pull request into the clone's branch when asked; the clone is then at the commit. Each commit says where it goes: the branch the clone is on, or a new one (the default on `main` or `master`, with a pull request). Phone agents commit with `commit_push`, which always asks the user (with the files and message), yolo or not; desktops' agents use git in their shells. GitHub repositories only.
- **A git work tree is never file-synced.** Git rewrites a work tree wholesale (checkouts, rebases, stashes), which a file sync would spread as edits and could mix between branches; a work tree is a project, synced by the user's git. So: a folder that has a `.git` (a directory, or a file as worktrees and submodules have) is a project from the start (§2.3, as before). One that gets a `.git` stops syncing at once, before its first commit (a pass there does nothing), and is folded into the project once it has one (as before): other nodes leave their copies as they are, never deleting them, and their threads in the project are told, in the turn's context, that the folder became the project on that node and the copy here no longer syncs, so the agent there can reconcile them (compare with a clone, carry edits over, or ask). A checkout inside a folder is left out, its files never written. A folder whose `.git` goes away syncs again from its files as they are, merged with its last snapshot as any change is (a node with no snapshot of its own merges from nothing: files on both sides that differ are kept, one as a conflict copy, never deleted). **Unfolded** (decided 2026-10-16, with the user, after `~/home-notes` lost its `.git` once folded and synced no more, its store refused by the phone): once folded, too: the node whose location it is, seeing the directory there without `.git` (when a place is resolved, after every sync, at `stitch folder sync`), writes a `folder` record (`location`, `unfold` = the project) that ends the fold, so every node takes it for a folder again and holds its store. Every thread goes with it, those started in the project too (decided the same day, after the phone's turns in such a thread still looked for a clone): a project whose every fold was ended is the folder's, its threads placed there (their records stay in its store, held and synced as before), and it is no place of its own, left out of the place lists (the app's, `stitch project ls`) and of a phone's clone refreshes. A location that is not a directory there stays folded; a new `.git` with a commit folds it again.

### 2.17 Tools written in Otter

**Decided 2026-10-16**, with the user: milestone 5 starts now, with tools, before Otter's Tracks A2 (concurrency) and A3 (runtime compilation) are done. A tool is the smallest thing that needs the language and nothing else that is missing: Otter already compiles a script while the host runs, describes it and calls it (`otter_lang::Tool`, the host side of H8 in its smallest form, `otter/docs/embedding.md` §6), and edge talk's tools, rewritten (§2.3), are the first users. The loop and its policy stay Rust until A2 and A3 (§2.8). Built the same day (`shards/`, `otter/` since the rename below).

- **The name** (decided 2026-10-17, with the user): the language is Otter, as Otter itself renamed it (`c1afb6e`). Shards names the old system (1.x, edge talk's scripts), and a shard is still a building block. Everything Stitch shows the model or the user says Otter: the module (`otter/`), `[otter]` in config.toml, `run [otter:<skill>]` approvals, `grant_otter` on questions. A `[shards]` table is converted, not aliased: the node renames it `[otter]` when it reads config.toml, once, its headers renamed in place so the rest of the file stays as it is (merged into `[otter]` and written again from its values when both are there, or when written otherwise), and logs it. A question asked before, with `grant_shards`, grants as `grant_otter` does (records are not rewritten).
- **Where they come from: skills.** A skill's scripts in `tools/` are its tools (`*.otr`, `*.otter` or `*.shs`: Otter made `.otr` the extension for new files on 2026-10-17, `8dc2f6f`, with `.otter` and `.shs` still accepted, and never checks an extension itself; Stitch's `google-flights` scripts are `.otr` since): the home workspace's skills, a place's (`.stitch/skills/<name>/tools/`), and Stitch's own (compiled into the binary, read from memory). They are offered while the skill is on for the thread (§2.14), so turning a skill off turns its tools off and the skill's text says when to use them; there are no tool entries of their own. A skill whose files are another node's brings none here. A place's scripts are a checkout's code, but a script only runs when called, and a call with effects asks (below), as a command in the shell does.
- **A script** declares functions and ends with its definition, a table its top-level code outputs, `{definition: {name description parameters} use: "Function"}`; the function takes `{input env working-dir}` (the model's arguments, an empty `env` for now but for the `edge-talk` skill's tools, below, the thread's directory) and outputs text, or a value given as JSON. The catalog is the `otter` command's with its `sqlite` feature: core, `otter-io` (HTTP, files), `otter-text` (regular expressions, hashes) and `otter-sqlite` (`Db.Query`, read-only, since 2026-10-20), so what `otter tool` runs runs in Stitch, plus Stitch's own `Tool.Call` (below, which works only in `otter_run`); host shards over the node's services (shells, engram, the store) come with Track C.
- **Checked before offered, compiled once:** a script is parsed, lowered, composed and type-checked when a loop first offers it, then kept per node with what it read (the file, its `tools/` directory and every file in it, what it includes) and compiled again when any of that changes, from the next turn. One that does not compile is not offered and its diagnostics go to the node's log, once per change; a file without top-level code is a library others include, skipped quietly. Names are the definition's (`[A-Za-z0-9_-]`, at most 64, no `__`, no built-in tool's); of two skills' tools with one name the first by skill name wins, the other logged. They are listed after the built-in tools and before MCP tools, by name, descriptions cut at 4,000 characters.
- **Approvals follow effects** (§2.2): Otter infers a tool's effects through everything its function calls (`Tool::effects`). A tool with none only computes and runs without asking, in read-only threads too. Any other (network, files, the clock, waiting, randomness, or a shard's undeclared effects) asks as an MCP call does (§2.13): it runs without asking when `[otter] allow` patterns match its name, when an `always` answer granted it in the thread's place (`[otter.places."<place>"] allow`, its own table rather than `[mcp.places]`, read at each check and freeing waiting questions), or in yolo; otherwise the user approves it, shown as `run [otter:<skill>]: <tool> <arguments>`, and a read-only thread is refused it. Effects are declared by shards, a trusted contract, not a sandbox: they say what a tool may do, the approval says whether it may.
- **Calls** run on a blocking thread (`spawn_blocking`; a waiting call sleeps, costing no CPU), at most `[otter] timeout_secs` (120, at most 3600), and are cancelled (Otter's cancel token) when the user interrupts them or the thread is cancelled: the call's future is dropped and the script stops at once. Files are confined to the thread's directory, read-only in a read-only thread (Otter's `FsAccess`). A failure is the script's message with what it logged; what a successful call logged goes to the node's log. Long results are cut at 32,000 characters, the whole saved under `<data>/downloads/`, as MCP results are.
- **Where: every node** (decided 2026-10-17, with the user; desktops only the day before, while Otter was not built for iOS). The phone and visionOS link Otter too and are full Otter tool hosts: the same code, no second implementation, and Stitch's own skills that bring tools are listed there as everywhere. Otter's crates build for those targets as they are (its catalog's I/O through `reqwest` and rustls with ring, as the node's own), so nothing in Otter changed for it. On the phone a thread's directory, which a tool's files are confined to, is its place's copy in the app's container (a folder's copy, a project's clone, §2.16), else, for a global thread, its working folder in the node's data directory (`Library/Application Support/stitch/workdirs/<thread>/`, §2.3), never the container itself, which cannot be written; a call that asks is a question like any other, answered in the app's approvals or from another node; the app being suspended stops a running call as an interruption does (the call's future dropped, Otter's cancel token set), told to the model as such. Otter is a path dependency at `../otter`, as engram is, checked out beside Stitch in CI too; its build flag (`-C llvm-args=-align-all-nofallthru-blocks=4`, so the VM's speed does not hang on code placement) is in `.cargo/config.toml` for the desktop targets only, which rebuilt every crate once: on the phone a tool's time goes to the network, and the flag costs about 5% of binary size. Otter added 2.6 MB (3.1%) to the iPhone app's binary (Release, 85.95 MB to 88.58 MB; its code, `__TEXT`, 1.8 MB).
- **The first tool:** Stitch's `google-flights` skill, edge talk's Google Flights tool ported to Otter (`otter/examples/tools/flights`, copied at Otter `fbd548d`, again at `fe9fc48` for its A4 port and at `57366f8` for the catalog defaults): `google_flights_search` and `google_flights_cheap_dates`, reading the public website's search page (not the JSON RPCs, blocked for cookieless callers), one request at a time, a degraded page retried. No key.
- **YouTube** (2026-10-20): Stitch's `youtube` skill, edge talk's `youtube-captions` tool (the edgetalk extras repository's `tools/`, Shards 1.x) ported to Otter on Otter's `Http.Post` (added for it): `youtube_video` takes an ID or any YouTube URL and gives the details, a timestamped transcript and the top comments. As in edge talk, the player is asked as the ANDROID app (innertube `/player`; the embedded TV player as fallback), since the website's caption URLs need a proof-of-origin token and come back empty, and comments come from the WEB client's `/next` continuation. New here: a track by people before an automatic one, a related code (`en`, `en-US`), else the video's own language, said so (YouTube's translations, `&tlang=`, answer 429 without a session, so the model translates); transcripts cut at `max_chars` with the second to continue from (`from_seconds`); the captions request retried on transient failures (YouTube answers 429 at times). No key.
- **Edge talk's history, read in place** (decided 2026-10-20, with the user): Stitch's `edge-talk` skill reads the user's edge talk chats and distilled memories from edge talk's own database, never copying or changing it: `edge_talk_search` (FTS5 over messages, bm25-ranked: conversation id, title, date, sender, a snippet; all the words, else any), `edge_talk_memories` (FTS5 over the `memory` table, or the latest), `edge_talk_conversations` (by title words and active dates, paged) and `edge_talk_read` (one conversation in order, paged, tool and system rows only when asked, long messages cut). Otter scripts over `Db.Query` (`otter-sqlite`: read-only connections, an authorizer, WAL-safe beside edge talk writing, cancellable), the queries edge talk's own (`Scripts-Src/db.sql`, `Utils/convo-search-common.shs`): its FTS tables are contentless, so snippets are cut from the message with `instr`. Edge talk is not a dependency: only its schema is.
- **Written in Otter's A4 forms** (2026-10-24, Otter `fe9fc48`, `docs/iteration-and-access.md`): the built-in skills' scripts loop with `ForEach`, `Map`, `Filter` and `Reduce` instead of a counted `Repeat` over `Take(i)`, read nested JSON with lenient paths (`v.runs?`, `data.3.0?`) instead of hand-written `Key`/`At` helpers, and bind fields with patterns (`= {code: t-code url: t-url}`); the `otter` skill's primer teaches those forms. Their behaviour is unchanged (the offline tests, and the live flights and YouTube ones). Since Otter `57366f8` (PR #19, catalog defaults) they read optional values with `Expect*(default:)` instead of hand-written `IntOr`, `StringOr`, `BoolOr` and `SeqOr` helpers, take a sequence's end with `Last(default: none)` and pad with `String.PadStart` instead of `Pad2`; the primer teaches those shards. Edge talk's `StringOr` also trimmed, so its tools trim the arguments the model gives (and a message's text) explicitly; a float where an Int is expected now gives the default, where its `IntOr` truncated it.
  - **Where the database is:** `edge-talk.db` in `Documents/` of the app group `group.fragcolor.edge-talk`'s container: on macOS `~/Library/Group Containers/group.fragcolor.edge-talk/` (940 MB on the Mac, about 9k conversations, 265k messages, 5.2k memories); on iOS the app joins that group (same team, `project.yml`) and gives the node the container `FileManager.containerURL` resolves (`set_edge_talk_container`, before `Stitch.open`). The skill, and so its tools, are listed on a node only while the file is there; elsewhere (Linux, a phone without edge talk) they do not exist.
  - **Only its tools read it:** the path is not in the scripts but given by the host in the call's `env` (`edge-talk-db`), with a read grant on that one file (`FsAccess::grant_read`; SQLite opens the `-wal` and `-shm` beside it itself), to the built-in skill's tools only: no other skill's tool (one of the user's named `edge-talk` replaces the text, and its scripts get neither), and no `otter_run`, can read it (tested).
  - **They run without asking** (in read-only threads too): they only read the user's own data from one fixed file, as a built-in tool that only reads does. `[otter] ask = ["edge_talk_*"]` (patterns over names) makes them ask as any Otter tool with effects does.
  - **Not done:** importing edge talk's chats as threads, or its memories into engram (§4, question 4); attachments (hashes of files in edge talk's storage); edge talk's semantic search (its embeddings) and personas beyond their names.
- **The language's own tools** (decided 2026-10-17, with the user): edge talk taught its threads Shards with four tools (`shards_search`, `shards_help`, `shards_check`, `run_shards`); Stitch's are rewritten over Otter's Rust API, not ported, and answer as the `otter` command does, so what an agent learns in one works in the other. They matter most on the phone, which has no shell for the `otter` command.
  - `otter_search {query, limit?}`: Otter's catalog `search` (the catalog skills' tools compile against), ranked here (an exact name, or a dotted name's last part, first; then names that start with the query, that contain it, then summaries; several words with no match as a whole: the shards matching most of them), each `Name (input -> output): summary`. `otter search` prints `name: summary` in catalog order: ranking and signatures belong in Otter's catalog, so the CLI says the same (a request for Otter, not made yet).
  - `otter_describe {name}`: `describe_json`, as `otter describe` prints it; on a miss, `no shard named X (did you mean: …)` from `otter_lang::problem::closest`.
  - `otter_check {code | file, input?, json?}`: `Program::load_with` and `analyze`, as `otter check`: rendered diagnostics (`code:line:column: …`), or `code: ok` and the effects of the entries a run starts; `json` gives `CheckReport::to_json`. `file` is a script in the thread's directory, named by its path there, so a skill's tool is checked with its includes before a turn offers it.
  - `otter_run {code | file, input?, json?}`: checked, then run as `otter run` runs a program (its entries, `@run`'s mesh, iterations and rate), its output `otter run`'s (the log, then `root: <value>`, `failed: …`, `still running after N ticks`; `json` gives `run --json`'s object). A runtime failure says its own message, which names the shard, without the runtime's `activation error:` in front (2026-10-22, as Otter's tools do); it has no line or column (Otter locates only compose-time failures; a run with effects cannot be run again to locate one). Chats show a call's code on its own lines, not escaped in JSON. `input`, a JSON value, is the top-level code's input. `Program::run` takes no services, cancellation or limit, so the node drives a mesh of its own from Otter's public API (`Mesh::tick_budgeted` under a `Budget` with the cancel token and the deadline, so a loop that never waits stops too; `Mesh::wait` between ticks, so a waiting run costs no CPU), as `Tool::call_with` does; a `Program::run_with(options)` in Otter would replace it.
  - **Approvals follow effects exactly as skills' tools do:** a run whose entries have no effects runs without asking, in read-only threads too; any effect asks, shown as `run [otter]: otter_run <code>` (the code, and its input), honouring yolo, `[otter] allow` (`"otter_run"`) and "always" grants in the place (`grant_otter`); a read-only thread is refused it. `Log` is I/O in Otter's contract, so a run that logs asks: the skill says to make a value the code's last instead. Same time limit (`[otter] timeout_secs`), cancellation (the call's future dropped sets the token), files confined to the thread's directory (read-only in a read-only thread) and long output cut at 32,000 characters as tools.
  - `@include` and `@read` (in all three) read only in the thread's directory: names relative to it, a path that resolves outside refused.
  - **With the `otter` skill, not always offered** (§2.14): Stitch's own `otter` skill (`apps/skills/otter/SKILL.md`) brings the four, as other skills bring their scripts, so `-otter` turns them off with the text that teaches them, and a skill of the user's named `otter` replaces the text and keeps the tools. Skills are on by default, so threads have them unless turned off. The skill says what the language is, the loop (never guess a shard: search and describe it; check before running; run), the language in brief, how to write a skill's tool script (`{definition: {...} use: "Function"}`, the call table `{input env working-dir}`, effects and approvals, where the files go) and points to the `google-flights` scripts. Every snippet in it is checked, run or loaded as a tool in a test.
- **`Tool.Call`: the thread's tools from Otter** (decided 2026-10-20, with the user; `otter/calls.rs`). `{video: "dQw4w9WgXcQ"} | Tool.Call("youtube_video")`: a host shard of Stitch's own (Otter supports them, `otter/docs/embedding.md` §2; nothing in Otter changed), in the catalog `otter_run` and `otter_check` compile against. Its input is the arguments (a table, or none for none), its parameter the tool's name (a literal or a String variable), its output the tool's text result as a String; a failed call fails the shard with the tool's message (`Maybe` catches it). So code can call any tool the thread has, one or many in a script ("transcripts of these five videos", "flights on these five dates").
  - **Dispatch:** `otter_run` runs inside the thread's tool dispatcher, so it inserts a service into its mesh (`ToolCalls`, the sending end of a channel); the shard sends each call on it and waits, and `otter_run`'s own call, still in progress, serves the channel by calling the dispatcher (`NodeTools::call`, the same tool and skill sets, approver and rules as the model's calls; several at once when the script's wires make them). Where no service is (a skill's tool script, a schedule's check) the shard fails, "no tools here". The service holds no tools itself, only the way back to the call that serves it, so nothing outlives `otter_run`.
  - **Approvals stay per tool:** each call goes through that tool's own approval exactly as the model's call would (the rules, yolo, `[otter] allow`/`ask`, "always" answers, read-only threads); its question's command starts `[from otter_run, Tool.Call] ` (a task-local the dispatcher sets, read by `ApprovalRequest::new`), so the user sees it comes from a script. `otter_run` itself never asks because of `Tool.Call`: its approval reads the effects of the code compiled against a second catalog whose `Tool.Call` declares none (the one scripts run on declares `io, time, suspends`, so compose and analysis stay honest); the rest of the script asks for its own effects as before. `otter_check` says which effects are `Tool.Call`'s. Otter's `Maybe` declares I/O (it may log), so a script using it asks once for itself: Otter's contract, not changed here.
  - **Privileges stay with the tool:** a call runs as the tool does when the model calls it, so edge talk's tools keep their read grant on its database and the script around them never gets it.
  - **Not callable:** `otter_run` (no recursion), and `spawn`, `send`, `wait`, `cancel`, `wake_at`, `schedule`: their work outlives the script and belongs to the model's turn. Refused with a message saying so. A tool the thread does not have fails as a model's call to it would.
  - Images a tool returns are left out of the String, with a note (`[1 image not shown: call view_file directly to see it]`), as is the "(approved by the user)" note. A call the script stops waiting for (cancelled, `otter_run`'s time limit) closes its answer's channel and is dropped, as an interrupted call of the model's is (a shell command it entered gets Ctrl+C); `otter_run` interrupted drops them all. A question cannot outlast the run: when `otter_run` runs out of time while calls of its script wait on the user's approval (a detached thread's user away), its error names those tools and says to call them directly, where the approval waits for the user, or have them allowed (issue 2026-11-09T080739Z-mbp-y24w: before, the model saw only "timed out after 120s").
- **Lazy skill tools** (decided 2026-10-20, with the user, with `Tool.Call`). A skill's tools are **listed** (in the tool list, as before) or **lazy**: not listed, called with `Tool.Call` in `otter_run`. A field of Stitch's own skills (`BuiltInSkill::lazy`), and a front matter key of a user's or place's SKILL.md: `tools: lazy` (`tools: listed`, or no key, lists them). A lazy tool needs `otter_run`: with the `otter` skill off, a lazy skill's tools are listed as before.
  - **Why:** every listed tool costs its description and schema in every request, and a crowded list makes the model pick worse; most skill tools are used in few threads. Through `otter_run` the tool list never changes, so the provider's prompt cache holds (adding tools mid-thread when a skill is read would break it), and a script can combine calls (five videos, five dates). Approvals and privileges stay per tool (`Tool.Call` above), and nothing in Otter changed. **The costs:** an extra step before the first use (`read_skill`, then `otter_run`), and the model must think to read the skill: so a lazy skill's line in the prompt's skills list names its tools ("its tools youtube_video, called with Tool.Call in otter_run: read_skill gives their parameters"), and `read_skill` (offered for a skill here with lazy tools too, its SKILL.md read from its file) adds, after the text, how to call them and each one's description and parameter schema. The tools stay callable directly too (the model sometimes does).
  - **Default for the user's and places' skills: listed.** Their tools were written to be at hand, and making them lazy silently would change how existing skills behave; `tools: lazy` is one line when a skill's tools are rarely needed.
  - **Stitch's own skills, reviewed:** `google-flights` lazy (two tools with long descriptions, rarely needed; several dates in one run is the common case anyway), `youtube` lazy (one tool, used when a link is shared: one extra step then, and several videos in one run), `edge-talk` lazy (four tools, read now and then; a search and the reads of its hits in one run; they still run unasked, `[otter] ask` still applies). `otter` keeps its four listed (they are the way in); `apps`, `leadsheet` and `browser` bring no tools of their own (the browser tools are a group). Each lazy skill's SKILL.md says to call its tools through `otter_run` with `Tool.Call`, with an example (checked and run against stand-ins in a test).
  - **Not skills, not changed:** the built-in groups and MCP servers. Candidates to make lazy later: `images` (`generate_image`, rare), `browser` (fifteen tools, only while a browser is connected; but a session wants them directly, so perhaps the rarer ones), `memory` (`recall`, one tool; keep), MCP servers with many tools (a per-server `lazy = true` would do the same for them, their calls already approved per tool).
- **Not yet:** an `env` for tools (keys, settings: `[otter.tools.<name>]`); calling a tool script before a turn offers it (`otter tool`'s call: a thread can write a skill's `tools/` folder and check it with `otter_check {file}`, and it is offered from the next turn); fuel limits on `otter_run` (time is the bound); Otter tools shared with other nodes, as MCP servers are (§2.14); grants per effect rather than per tool; a `stitch otter` command that checks every skill's scripts, as `stitch mcp` does servers.

### 2.18 Browser control

**Decided 2026-10-17**, with the user. The plan, with its reasons, sources and the parity table against Claude in Chrome and OpenAI's agents, is [browser-tool.md](browser-tool.md); this section records what was decided. B0, the spike, was run on 2026-10-18 and confirmed the extension path (browser-tool.md §15); B1 is being built (browser-tool.md §16: the extension in `apps/chrome/`, `stitch browser-host` relaying to the node's socket as a browser link, the engine in `crates/stitch-node/src/browser/`, the `browser` tool group and skill). Two findings changed the plan: the agent's own input is told apart by a mark on its CDP events (`timestamp: 1`, which pages see as `event.timeStamp` 0), and snapshots with their refs are built in the page by an isolated-world script rather than from CDP's accessibility tree.

- **The user's own Chrome first:** the real profile and its logins, driven by **Stitch's own extension**. The extension uses `chrome.debugger` (the DevTools protocol, per tab) and native messaging to `stitch browser-host`, a subcommand that relays to the node's local socket. **A node it starts is launchd's** (decided 2026-10-19, after a node the host started as Chrome's child inherited Chrome's quarantine: every file it wrote, cargo's build scripts included, got `com.apple.quarantine` from Chrome, Gatekeeper refused to run them ("Not Opened", builds killed), and its keychain prompts showed as Chrome's): on macOS the host starts the node as a launchd job of the user's GUI session (`node.plist` in the data directory, one-shot, with the host's environment), not as its child; other clients still start it as theirs. Chrome 144+'s consent attach (`chrome://inspect/#remote-debugging`, an Allow dialog per connection, reaching the whole browser) is the fallback with no install. The headless browser (the web tools' Chromium, made into sessions), profiles Stitch launches, and CDP addresses come later, as backends of the same engine. The web tools (§2.3) stay stateless and logged out.
- **One engine in the node** (Rust, desktop only) over a transport trait: sessions per thread and browser, tabs, accessibility snapshots with `ref_N` refs, screenshots in one coordinate space, trusted input through CDP `Input.*`, waiting, dialogs, uploads and downloads. The tools are the `browser` group (§2.14), on for desktop threads while an extension is connected (default).
- **Cooperative sessions are the default** when the user is at the browser:
  - the agent works in a tab group of its own in the user's window, marked in the page (a glow, its cursor, labels, Stop / Take over / Hand back);
  - **the user always wins:** their input in an agent tab pauses the agent there, and **it resumes by itself about 4 s after they go idle, from a fresh read**;
  - every action is checked against the tab's epoch, so a change the user made is re-read before the agent acts;
  - the user points the agent at a tab, a selection or an element.
  **Agent-alone** runs in the same Chrome in an unfocused window of its own (1280×900; some window managers ignore the request not to focus it, so right after creating it, if Chrome had the user's focus and the new window took it, the previous window gets it back (never when Chrome was not focused, which would bring it to the front): a mitigation of the suspected cause of phantom "the user typed" refusals, issue 4nwi, not proven). A tab that leaves the session (the user closed it, or dragged it out of its group) is remembered with the reason (the last 16): this explains the loss, it does not prevent it: an explicit call on it says why, and the next tool result says so when it was the current one. An action by x,y is refused when the viewport differs from the one of the agent's last screenshot. **Detached threads may use the user's Chrome while the user is away, in agent-alone mode.** A move into the user's window needs the user.
- **Profiles are the user's pick, none by default:** the agent can use no Chrome profile until the user enables it, and **enabling is a click in that profile's extension popup**, never a command, since agents run `stitch` commands in their shells. `stitch browser profiles` lists connected profiles and can disable one.
- **Installed by `stitch browser install`,** sideloaded for now: it writes the extension's files (with a pinned key, so its ID is stable) and the native messaging host manifest for every Chromium browser found (Chrome, Brave, Edge), then says how to "Load unpacked". The headless Chromium download it did before moves to `stitch browser install headless`. A Web Store listing comes later.
- **Safety:**
  - the extension attaches only to the agent's tabs and never reads cookies;
  - while the user is watching (cooperative, the agent's tab in front, recent input), in-site actions run and the remaining questions appear in the page;
  - otherwise questions are `question` records with a screenshot, pushed to the phone;
  - navigating to another site, and a fixed set of consequential actions (paying, sending, account changes, deleting, uploads, risky downloads), always ask, **yolo included**: the first rule yolo does not override;
  - passwords, OTPs, card numbers and CAPTCHAs are handoffs to the user;
  - devtools-style tools (eval, console, network) are off by default;
  - turns that used the user's Chrome get no unasked recall and are not observed into memory;
  - **screenshots of logged-in pages sync with the thread to all nodes,** as other tool images do.
- **The live channel is pulled forward** (milestone 6 in part, §5): watching and taking over the user's Chrome from the phone (screencast frames, touches and keys back) is the phase after the first cooperative sessions, together with driving the Mac's browser from other nodes.

### 2.19 Secrets and variables

**Decided 2026-10-21**, with the user, after reading edge talk's: one flat global list of "environment variables" in `UserDefaults` (plain text, not the keychain), configuration (`ssh-host`) and secrets (`ssh-password`) mixed, every script handed all of it as `ext/env`, entered again on every device. Stitch so far had provider keys only (§2.1), per node, never synced.

- **Two kinds, both by name:** *variables* (not secret: `HF_HOME`, a region) are records in the home workspace and sync as records do; *secrets* are kept in the keychain (§2.1's stores and scoping) and are never a record in clear.
- **Secrets are on all the user's devices by default** ("this device only" is the choice per secret): synced sealed to each trusted node, with the X25519 keys and the sealing push already uses (`device` records, §5 milestone 4): a `secret` record carries the name, its scope and one ciphertext per node; a node opens its own and keeps the value in its keychain. A newly paired node gets them re-sealed by a node that has them; removing a node suggests rotating the secrets it held. Entered once on the phone, there on the Mac.
- **Scoped and injected, never shown to the model:** a secret is global, a project's or a folder's. A place says what its shells and MCP servers get (`OPENAI_API_KEY = "secret:openai"`), and they get it as an environment variable; tools take `secret:<name>` where they need one (an SSH password, §2.20; an MCP server's `key`, §2.13). Tool output and transcripts are scanned for secret values and show `«secret:<name>»` instead, before the model or a record sees them.
- **Provider keys become secrets** (same store, same names: `openai`, `github`), so Settings' Keys and `stitch key` become Secrets and variables (`stitch secret`, `stitch var`; the TUI and the app the same): which devices hold each, where it is used, when last; revealing one needs the device's owner authentication.
- **Locking the app** is a separate option, for the whole app (Face ID, with a timeout, a day by default), not per secret or per use.

**As built (2026-10-21)**, refining the above:
- **Where values are:** in `keys`' stores, unchanged: the data directory's keychain entries (the data protection keychain on iOS, this device only, after first unlock), or `credentials.toml` where there is no keychain or the user chose it. A global secret is kept under its bare name, so a provider's key *is* the global secret of the provider's name and nothing moves: the migration loses nothing because it copies nothing. A place's secret is kept as `<scope>/<name>` (`project:p-…/openai`). What this node holds, and where each came from, is `<data>/secrets.toml` (names, scopes, `device_only`, when and where set; owner-only, never values). Keys stored before secrets existed (or by `stitch key`, `stitch auth login github`, `stitch apns set`, the app's old Keys) are listed the first time the node looks (the known providers' names, the configured providers', MCP servers' `key`s, `credentials.toml`'s entries) as global secrets of **this device only**: two nodes may hold different keys of one name on purpose, and neither is overwritten by the other until the user shares one. A new secret is synced by default. Every existing lookup keeps working in its order (`credentials.toml`, `STITCH_<NAME>_API_KEY` and the provider's usual variable, the keychain).
- **Names and scopes:** letters, digits, `_`, `-`, `.` (at most 64); scopes are place keys (`global`, `project:<repo>`, `folder:<key>`). A place gets its own secret of a name, else the global one; another place's never.
- **Variables** are `variable` records (`name`, `scope`, `value`; `removed = true`), the latest of a name and scope standing. Shells get the global variables and their place's by name (those whose names are environment variable names), without being mapped.
- **The mapping lives in the place's settings** (decided 2026-10-21): `[env]` in `<place>/.stitch/settings.toml` (§2.14), which already syncs as the `place` record and holds only references, so a committed checkout carries no value: `OPENAI_API_KEY = "secret:openai"`, `"var:<name>"`, or a value as written.
