Before it, `[env]` in `config.toml`, the node's own default for every shell (not synced). `PATH` and `STITCH_DATA` are not taken from either. A reference that does not resolve leaves the variable unset, said in the node's log. Read as each shell session starts, not once a turn (2026-10-30, issue k6ng: settings written during a turn reached no session until the next), the place as it is then (a folder folded into a project since, by `git init`, is the project's). MCP servers, one per node for every place, take global ones: `key = "secret:github"` (a bare name as before), and `secret:`/`var:` values in their `env`.
- **Redaction:** every value the node holds (its secrets, and providers' keys from wherever, the environment included) of at least 6 characters is replaced, longest first, by `«secret:<name>»`: in a tool's output before the loop gives it to the model or writes it (the `Tools::redact` hook, so every tool and MCP server is covered), in a shell command's output before it is cut to its limit (so no value is cut in two and half shown), in a job's notice and woven text, in a tool's result run for another node before it leaves, and in a shell attached in the TUI or app. Output read in chunks (an attached shell) goes through a stream redactor that holds back what could start a value until the next chunk shows whether it does, or 100 ms pass. Not scanned: what the user types into a chat (they chose to say it) and what models write.
- **Sync, sealed:** every node has its own X25519 sealing key (made the first time it has a secret to sync or sees a `secret` record, so a node that never syncs a secret never touches its keychain for one), its private half kept as a secret is (`seal-key@stitch`, a name no secret can have), its public half a `seal-key` record (`node`, `key`; the latest of a node stands). Not the push key of `device` records: that one is the phone app's (CryptoKit's, in the app's own keychain items), and desktops have none. A synced secret is a `secret` record: `name`, `scope`, `set_at`, and `sealed`, one ciphertext per node with a key (this node's own included), each sealed as pushes are (an ephemeral X25519 key, HKDF-SHA256 with info `stitch secret v1`, ChaCha20-Poly1305) over the name, scope and value together, so a ciphertext moved into another record does not open. The record that stands for a name and scope is the one whose value was set last (`set_at`, then creation), so a node re-sealing an older value never wins over a newer one. After each sync (and at start) a node opens what was sealed for it into its own store, drops what a `removed = true` record removed, and, holding the standing value, re-seals it for every trusted node with a key the record leaves out (a node paired since): a new record with the same `set_at`. Two nodes may both re-seal; their records agree. A secret set to sync before there was a home workspace is sealed once there is one. Turning "this device only" on writes a removal (the others drop their copies); a device-only secret is never touched by records of its name. Removing a node (`revoke`) leaves its ciphertexts where they were: the secrets sealed for it list it under `rotate` (carried into re-sealed records) until a new value is set. No record holds a value in clear, and a sealing key never leaves its node. Tested with three nodes in one process (`tests/secrets.rs`).
- **The app:** Settings → Secrets & Variables replaces the Keys section (a provider's key is added there by its name, offered as a suggestion; GitHub's sign-in stays under Accounts). The list shows each secret's name, scope, the machines that hold it (or "this device only", or not here yet), where it is used and a rotation warning; a secret opens to its value behind **Reveal** (the device owner's authentication, `LAContext` `.deviceOwnerAuthentication`: Face ID, else the passcode, each time), the machines, a "This device only" switch, a new value and Remove. Variables are listed below, edited in place. The **app lock** (Settings → App Lock, off by default) covers the app until the owner authenticates when it starts or comes back after being away longer than its timeout (every time, 5 minutes, an hour, a day (the default), a week); a cancelled prompt is not asked again by itself, the lock screen's Unlock asks. It hides the app, not the node: the node keeps running and syncing under it.
- **Commands:** `stitch secret set <name> [--scope … | --here] [--device-only | --sync] [--keep-value] [--file]` (read without echo, or from standard input), `stitch secret get <name> [--reveal]`, `stitch secret ls` (where each is held and used, never values), `stitch secret rm`, and `stitch var set|ls|rm`, all through the node (so it redacts a new value at once). `stitch key set|rm <provider>` are `stitch secret set|rm <provider>`. An agent's `stitch secret` or `stitch var` other than `ls` asks, as `stitch key` did. The TUI had no keys command, so it has none for secrets either.

### 2.20 SSH

**Decided 2026-10-21**, with the user. edge talk's `ssh_shell_cmd` / `ssh_shell_wait_for` (Shards' SSH module over russh) had one global session to one host configured by env variables, a key file in iCloud Drive, no host key check, and the session dropped when the conversation changed. Stitch's is native Rust, not Otter: a `stitch-ssh` crate over russh with ring (pure Rust, so it builds for iOS and visionOS).

- **A kind of shell session, not new tools:** `shell_run(host: "devbox-1", …)` opens or reuses a session on that host, with everything local sessions have (persistence, background jobs, watches, `idle_secs`, `screen`, `shell_view`/`shell_send` for full-screen programs). It gives the phone shell tools: remote ones, where it has no local shell. A machine that runs Stitch is better reached with `spawn` on its node; SSH is for the rest (servers, VMs, a Pi, routers).
- **Hosts are records, credentials are not:** a host (name, address, port, user, jump host, which secret or key) is a record in the home workspace, defined once for every device; desktop nodes also read `~/.ssh/config`. Host keys are trusted on first use and kept as records (fingerprints are public), so every node knows them; a changed key fails.
- **Each device has its own key:** on the iPhone a Secure Enclave P-256 key (`ecdsa-sha2-nistp256`), which never leaves the phone and is used without asking each time (the app lock covers the device, §2.19); on desktops the SSH agent and the user's keys. The app shows the public key, and "Authorize on host" has a node that already reaches the host add it to `authorized_keys`. Passwords are secrets (§2.19).
- **Approvals as relaxed as local ones:** a host has no sandbox, so its commands go through the same parser and the same list of what reaches beyond (§2.3: pushing, `rm -r` of a tree, `sudo`, `systemctl`, …), which asks; the rest runs. A host can be trusted whole (`approval = "allow"`) or kept to asking.
- **Reconnecting:** the phone drops connections in the background; a session reconnects when next used, said in the result. **Sessions run inside `tmux` where the host has it** (decided 2026-10-21, with the user): `tmux new -A -s stitch-<thread>-<session>`, so a running program outlives a dropped connection (the phone in the background, a network change), the session is picked up where it was from any node, and the user can `tmux attach` to watch; a host without `tmux` gets a plain shell, said once; a thread's `tmux` sessions are closed when it ends or its session is closed.
- **Then SFTP:** `view_file`, `read_file`, `write_file` and `edit_file` on `host:path`.

**As built (2026-10-21)**, refining the above:
- **One session, two terminals:** `stitch_shell::Session` has a backend: a local PTY as before, or a `Remote` (a trait: resize, put a file, signal the command's group, close, how it ended) with the terminal's output and input as async streams (`Session::remote`). Everything above the terminal is shared: the line log, the screen, the marker, `idle`/`screen`/`screen_gone`/`quiet`, waits, jobs, attaching. What a local session reads from the PTY a remote one cannot: whether a program runs comes from the marker alone (a line entered, its prompt not back), so a command a person types into an attached remote session is not seen as running; `command_group` is never a remote group (its number means another process here), and `signal_remote` signals the group holding the terminal there through a channel of its own (`/proc`, else `ps`; `job_stop` uses it after Ctrl+C). A long command (over 768 bytes) is put in the session's directory there and sourced.
- **The shell there** is bash (a host without bash is said, not worked around) with an rc of its own (`remote_rc`): the user's login profile read quietly first (so their `PATH`), then the local default's plain prompt, no history file, no pager, and the marker with its secret written in (no environment passes over SSH). The rc, the nonce, the shell's pid, the scripts that start the shell (`start`, and `tmux` for a session in tmux) and long commands live in `~/.cache/stitch/ssh/<name>/` (owner only), removed when the session is closed. **No login shell's quoting is relied on** (found on devbox-1, whose login shell is fish, where `\'` inside single quotes is an escape): scripts go as `sh -s`'s input, and the command lines sent are unquoted words (`sh .cache/stitch/ssh/<name>/start`, `cat > …/command-N`). `<name>` is `stitch-<thread>-<session>`, made safe unquoted.
- **tmux in control mode** (decided 2026-10-21, while building): a session in tmux is a `tmux -u -C new-session -A -s <name>` client on a channel without a terminal, not `tmux attach` on a PTY: tmux redraws a pane for a terminal (cursor moves, its own scrolling), which would make the line log a picture of tmux's screen and swallow the marker; in control mode `%output` is what the pane's program printed, unchanged, so the marker and the log work as locally. Input is `send-keys -H` (every byte a key in hex, nothing taken for a key name), the size `refresh-client -C`, and replies (`%begin`/`%end` with flag 1) are matched in order. A setup script (its own channel) says whether the host has bash and tmux and whether a Stitch tmux session of that name is there with its nonce: then it is **picked up**: the pane drawn as it is (`capture-pane -p -e`, output held until the capture is in so nothing is drawn twice), busy with what holds its terminal (`ps`/`/proc` of the shell's terminal group, said by its command line) or at its prompt. A tmux session without the nonce file is not Stitch's and is replaced.
- **`stitch-ssh`** (russh 0.62, ring, rsa, flate2; builds for iOS and visionOS): `Connection` (through a jump host by a direct-tcpip channel; keepalives every 30 s, three missed is a drop), `Pool` (one connection per host, made again when it dropped), `open` (a session: tmux or plain), `HostKeys` (a trait: what is trusted per `[address]:port`, OpenSSH's form, and trusting a first key), `config` (`~/.ssh/config`: `Host` blocks with patterns and negation, first value wins, `Include`, `%h`; `Match` skipped), `device` (this device's key). Authentication tries, in order: the agent's keys, key files without a passphrase (one with a passphrase is the agent's), a password, this device's key (in memory, or a `DeviceSigner` that signs elsewhere); a refusal names what was tried and how to add the device's key. A dropped connection ends its sessions with "the connection to <host> dropped" (`Link::dropped`), told from the shell's exit or a close.
- **The shell tools take `host`** (offered when there are hosts, an `enum` of their names with how each is reached and approved): a session there is keyed `<name>@<host>` among the thread's sessions (a name given with `@` is refused), so jobs, watches, `shell_view`, `shell_send`, `job_stop` and `jobs` work on it as on a local one; `watch_file` is local only (a host's file is watched with `tail -f` as a job). The node has one `Ssh` service: a pool of connections, the far end of each remote session, this device's key. A session whose connection dropped is opened again on its next use (`shell_run`, `shell_send`, `shell_view`, `shell_wait`), the result starting with what happened ("the same tmux session, picked up where it was", or a new shell); a host without tmux is said once. A remote session opens without the sessions' lock. Closing a session (or the thread's sessions when it ends, or the node's when it stops) ends its tmux session and directory; a session whose connection is down when closed leaves its tmux session there (a gap: nothing cleans it later). The system prompt lists the hosts (`# SSH hosts`), and says to spawn on a node for a machine that runs Stitch. **With none, it says how to add one** (decided 2026-10-22, after a thread on gamer asked to install Stitch on devbox-1 found no host offered, reached for a plain `ssh` that failed on an unknown host key, and the user asked why the tool did not work): `stitch ssh host add`, `stitch ssh authorize` when the host refuses this device, rather than a plain `ssh`.
- **The phone** gets the shell tools when there are hosts: `host` required, `outside` and `watch_file` gone, local calls refused with what to do.
- **Approvals on a host** (`Rules::allows_remote`, by the host's `approval`): `relaxed` (the default) reads the line as the sandbox does (lenient where a prefix cannot vouch, `bash -c` looked into) and asks for what reaches beyond the sandbox (`beyond`: pushing, publishing, `sudo`, other machines, `systemctl` but its reading verbs, `kill`, `tmux` but listing, …) and what a sandbox would have stopped there (`beyond_remote`: `rm -r` of anything but relative paths below where it runs, `apt`/`dnf`/`pacman`/… but listing, `service`, `mkfs`, `dd`, `fdisk`, firewalls, users, `mount` with arguments, `reboot`/`poweroff`); cloning and worktrees run (no place to keep them in). `ask` is an unsandboxed session's rules (read-only commands and the user's prefixes run); `allow` runs everything. Yolo is yolo there too; a read-only thread keeps to the read-only rules on every host. An "always" answer grants the prefix for the place, here and on hosts alike. `stitch ssh` but `key`, `authorizations` and listing asks.
- **Hosts** are `ssh-host` records (`name`, `address`, `port`, `user`, `jump`, `auth`, `approval`, `tmux = false` to keep to plain shells; `removed = true`), the latest of a name standing. A desktop also lists `~/.ssh/config`'s hosts (not git forges, nor `User git`), and the config fills what a record of the same name leaves out (address, port, user, jump, identity files); a record's empty user is this user's. `auth`: `auto` (desktop: the agent, the config's identity files and `~/.ssh/id_{ed25519,ecdsa,rsa}`, then this device's key; phone: this device's key), `agent`, `device`, `secret:<name>` (a password, a global secret, §2.19), `key:<path>`. Jump hosts are host names (or `ProxyJump`'s `user@host:port`, by its host), at most four deep.
- **Host keys** are `ssh-host-key` records (`host` as `[address]:port`, the host's `name`, `key`, `fingerprint`; `removed = true` forgets a host's keys), written by the node that first saw the key and synced at once; a changed key fails with both fingerprints and how to forget the old one (`stitch ssh hostkey rm`, the app). A desktop checks the user's `known_hosts` too (a key it records that differs is a change).
- **This device's key:** on an iPhone a Secure Enclave P-256 key (`kSecAttrTokenIDSecureEnclave`, `kSecAccessControlPrivateKeyUsage`, after first unlock, this device only, found by its label), signing `ecdsa-sha2-nistp256` through Security.framework (`SecKeyCreateSignature`, the DER signature made SSH's `mpint r, mpint s`); made with attributes of its own, as security-framework's `GenerateKeyOptions` sets the private key's attributes on macOS only. Where there is no Secure Enclave (the simulator) and on desktops: an Ed25519 key kept as a secret is (`ssh-key@stitch`, this device's only). Its public half is an `ssh-device-key` record (`node`, `key`, `held`).
- **Authorizing a key** (`stitch ssh authorize <host> [--node <n> | --key <line>]`, the app's "Authorize on host"): this node's key by default. Done here when this node gets in (`umask 077; mkdir -p ~/.ssh`, the line added once, `grep -qxF`); else an `ssh-authorize` request record (`host`, `key`), which each desktop node that knows the host tries once after a sync, answering with `ssh-authorized` (`request`, `ok`, `said`); `stitch ssh authorizations` lists them, pending, done or failed with what each node said.
- **The app:** Settings → SSH Hosts: the hosts (each with how it is reached, through which host, and a badge when it is trusted whole or kept to asking; swiped away to remove), this device's key (its `authorized_keys` line, fingerprint, where it is kept: the Secure Enclave on an iPhone; Copy and Share), the authorizations asked and how they went, and the host keys trusted (by whom, when; swiped to forget, after a confirmation). A host opens to edit (name, address, port, user, the host it goes through, signing in: automatic, this device's key, the agent, a password secret by name; approvals: relaxed, ask, trusted; tmux) with **Authorize This Device on <host>** (from here when this device gets in, else through a machine that reaches it). Over `stitch-ffi` (`ssh_hosts`, `set_ssh_host`, `remove_ssh_host`, `ssh_host_keys`, `forget_ssh_host_key`, `ssh_device_key`, `authorize_on_ssh_host`, `ssh_authorizations`); fixtures `ssh` and `sshhost` (`just ui-shot`).
- **Commands:** `stitch ssh host add <name> [--address --port --user --jump --auth --approval --no-tmux] | ls | rm`, `stitch ssh key`, `stitch ssh authorize`, `stitch ssh authorizations`, `stitch ssh hostkey ls | rm`; over `Request::Ssh` (`local-33`).
- **Tested** against a russh server in the test's process that runs what it is asked with this machine's `sh` (on a PTY when asked), a home and tmux socket of its own: commands, exit statuses, the directory kept, long commands, `idle`, a full-screen program's screen, resizing, signals; a host key trusted on first use, a changed one refused (from the records and from the user's `known_hosts`); passwords, a key, an ECDSA key signing through `DeviceSigner`, a refusal; a tmux session that outlives a dropped connection and is picked up busy, then closed with its tmux session; a plain shell that is new after a drop. In macOS's sandbox `ps` cannot run, so what holds a remote terminal is not checked there (said); outside it, it is. `localhost_sshd` (ignored) runs against a real `sshd` (here, or `STITCH_SSH_TEST_HOST=user@host`): a plain shell, `uname -a`; run against devbox-1 on 2026-10-21 (the agent's key, the user's `known_hosts`, fish as login shell). The server is lent to other crates' tests (`stitch-ssh`'s `testing` feature): `stitch-node/tests/ssh.rs` runs a node with a host record and a password secret, the model's `shell_run` with `host` (run unasked; `sudo` asked and declined; the tools and prompt offering the host), the host key kept as a record, a drop reconnected and said, this device's key authorized on the host (once, however often asked). The rules on a host are unit-tested (`rules::tests`).
- **Not built yet:** SFTP (`view_file`, `read_file`, `write_file`, `edit_file` on `host:path`), the step after this one. Gaps: a tmux session whose connection is down when its session or thread is closed is left on the host; a command a person types into an attached remote session is not seen as running; a duplicate control client opened by two calls at once stays attached until its connection goes; `cwd` does not apply on a host (sessions start in the home); hosts without bash are refused; a profile that `exec`s another shell takes the session with it; the Secure Enclave key is built and type-checked but not yet run on a device (the simulator has none; it falls back to a keychain key there); keyboard-interactive authentication and passphrase-protected key files outside the agent are not supported; `Pool::get` holds the pool's lock while connecting, so a slow host delays connecting to the others.

### 2.21 The Discord agent: a node in a container, the key proxy, the Discord bridge

**Decided 2026-10-25**, with the user: a node run as a service for other people, driven from a Discord server ("guild" is Discord's word for one), in a container, its model calls through a proxy on the host that holds the key. It is a general assistant, its purpose a persona the host gives. How to run it: `stitch discord --help` and `stitch proxy --help`.

**The deployment** (the container image, a dedicated user with a firewall by UID, instances, per-server instructions, approvers) is Fragcolor's own and documented with it; what follows is what this repository builds: the proxy, attribution and the bridge.

**Reading a busy thread** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user): the agent reads mentions as names (`@Name` from the message's resolved mentions, `@Role` and `#channel` from the guild's roles and channels at `GUILD_CREATE` and their create/update events, a channel not known asked of Discord once; one not found stays `@user`, `@role`, `#channel`, never an ID), the bot's own mention as `@<its name>`, `(/ask)` for the command and `(replying to Name: first 100 characters…)` for a Discord reply. Every message in a mapped thread is a turn, so the agent may answer exactly **`[pass]`**: the bridge posts nothing ("typing…" shows while it decides, which is the simplest honest signal). Its instructions say when (people talking to each other, chit-chat, a question to someone else) and that a mention or `/ask` always gets an answer; the bridge holds to that for those (a pass on one posts "*(no answer)*"). **Bursts**: one turn at a time per Discord thread (two people in one thread used to start two turns on one Stitch thread at once); messages that come while it runs are queued and go together in the next turn, a line each, answered once, so the per-user one-at-a-time rule now applies only to starting conversations and nobody in a thread is told to wait. Every message counts toward its author's hourly limit; one over it is dropped silently unless it is for the bot. **Pings**: replies allow mentions of exactly the users the agent named `@Name` among the conversation's people (authors and those mentioned there, kept in memory since the bridge started), turned into `<@id>`; `@everyone`, `@here` and roles never ping. **Turns it did not start** (2026-10-29, after a child's report reached the bot but never Discord): the bridge used to post only the replies of turns it started (`Node::chat`); now it follows its mapped threads by reading the node's files in the shared volume every 3 s (no node API streams thread events; a listing per thread a poll, the segments read only when the sealed set changed): an open journal segment means a turn runs ("typing…"), and once sealed, the assistant's text after the thread's mark is posted like its own replies (split, pings, `[pass]` dropped), replying to the last message for the bot in that channel. The mark (`posted`, Stitch thread → last message number) is saved in `threads.json`, set after each of its own turns (so they are not posted twice) and skipped while one of its own runs; a restart neither reposts nor drops; a thread mapped before is marked where it stands.

- **`stitch proxy`** (crate `stitch-proxy`, a subcommand of the one binary): an HTTP server on the host forwarding model requests to the provider with the real API key from the host's stores (`keys::get`: `credentials.toml`, the environment, the keychain). **API keys only**: a subscription's sign-in is never used, since sharing one with others is against the providers' terms. The client sends a **token** of its own instead (`stp_…`, shown once, kept as a SHA-256 hash in `<data>/proxy/proxy.toml`, which is read again when it changes), as `x-api-key` or a bearer token. Requests are `/<upstream>/<path>`, so a node's provider `base_url` is the proxy and the upstream's name (`http://host.docker.internal:7178/anthropic`) and its key the token; upstreams `anthropic`, `openai` and `gemini` (OpenAI-compatible) are built in, others configured (`kind` `anthropic` or `openai`, `base_url`, `key`: the secret's name). **Only the model endpoints** pass (Anthropic `POST /v1/messages`, `/v1/messages/count_tokens`, `GET /v1/models[/id]`; OpenAI-compatible `POST /chat/completions`, `/responses`, `GET /models[/id]`); the rest is 404. Hop-by-hop headers, the client's credentials, `x-stitch-*` and `accept-encoding` stay behind (the body is read as it passes). **Streams pass through unbuffered**: each chunk goes on as it arrives and is fed to a meter on the way (`stitch_providers::sse`, now public), which reads the usage the stream carries (Anthropic's `message_start` and `message_delta`, the last Chat Completions chunk, Responses' `response.completed`; the largest of each field, as streams repeat it cumulatively) or a JSON body's `usage`; OpenAI's prompt count has its cached tokens taken out, so `input` means what it does in `stitch_providers::Usage`. A response cut short by the client is counted with what passed. **Budgets** are budget tokens a UTC day: input, output and cache writes in full, cache reads at a tenth (what they cost, so a long cached thread does not spend the day rereading itself), per token (`daily_tokens`) and per end user (`daily_tokens_per_user`); a spent one answers 403 until midnight, not 429, which clients retry. **Rates**: requests a minute per token (30 by default), over it 429 with `retry-after`. Usage is kept in `usage.json` (40 days, by day, token and end user) and every request is a line of `usage.log` (token, end user, thread, upstream, path, model, status, ms, tokens). It **listens** on loopback and Docker's bridge gateway (`172.17.0.1`) by default, never every address; an address not there yet is tried again every 30 s (Docker may start after it at boot).
- **Attribution**: `Request::Chat` has `end_user` (the bridge sends `discord:<user id>`); the node runs such a turn inside `stitch_providers::with_attribution`, a task-local set of headers every provider request in the turn carries: `x-stitch-end-user`, and `x-stitch-thread` once the thread is known. Task-local rather than a field on every provider (decided while building): it reaches the requests the turn makes without threading a value through the loop, and nothing outside such a turn carries it. Work a turn starts elsewhere (a detached thread, a title) is counted as nobody's (`-`).
- **`stitch discord`** (crate `stitch-discord`, over twilight 0.17: maintained, modular, rustls with ring like the rest): the gateway with the `GUILDS`, `GUILD_MESSAGES` and `MESSAGE_CONTENT` intents (refused intents, 4014, end it with what to turn on). A **guild allowlist** (`STITCH_DISCORD_GUILDS`; none: nowhere, the guilds it is in logged so the user can add one); DMs and bots are ignored. A **mention or `/ask`** (a guild command, registered in each allowed guild at `READY`) starts a Stitch thread (global place) and a Discord thread from the message (or from `/ask`'s answer), where it replies; anything said in a Discord thread mapped to a Stitch thread continues it, without a mention (the map in `<data>/discord/threads.json`). The text sent is `<display name>: <text>` (several people share a thread); the bot's instructions say so. Those (`/data/home/AGENTS.md`) are written at each start from the image's generic ones (a Discord assistant: who speaks, Discord's formatting, the container, never revealing credentials, not reaching other machines) and an optional **persona** on the host (`~/.config/discord-agent/AGENTS.md`: what the bot is for on that server), after them or instead (`DISCORD_AGENT_PERSONA_MODE=replace`); with the dedicated user it reaches the node as its Podman secret `discord-agent-persona` (decided 2026-10-25, with the user: the bot is generic, a server's purpose is configuration, changed without rebuilding the image). Replies are split under 2000 characters (at lines, then spaces; a code block cut in two is closed and opened again with its language), posted with mentions off; "typing" every 8 s while the turn runs. **Per Discord user**: one turn at a time, 20 an hour by default. An **approval** the node asks (rare: yolo) is declined with a reason for the model, and the reply says which command was declined. A turn is a connection to the node of its own (`LocalNode`), so turns in several threads run at once.
- **Tested**: the proxy against a mock upstream speaking raw HTTP (a stream's second event held back while the first is read through the proxy, the key swapped, usage, budgets, rates, a token added while it runs); the bridge with a fake Discord and node; a real node behind the proxy in front of a mock Anthropic, a turn counted for its Discord user and thread (`stitch-discord/tests/end_to_end.rs`); on gamer, the image built with Docker 29, the node container answering a turn through the proxy, from the mock upstream and from Gemini (`gemini-3.1-flash-lite`, the user's key on the host), the bridge connecting to Discord with the Message Content intent, registering `/ask` in the user's test server and posting there, and (`tests/live.rs`, ignored) answering in a thread it started, in pieces. Gamer's ufw drops the Docker bridge's traffic to the host, so that run used the host's network for the node container; the bridge network waits on the rule above. With rootless Podman 6.1 (pasta), installed afterwards, the forwarding works: `discord-agent.sh up` and a turn through the proxy to Anthropic's API (claude-sonnet-5-5), the bridge logged in. Gemini through the proxy (the `openai` kind on Google's OpenAI-compatible endpoint) answers plain turns but refuses a turn's second step after a tool call ("Function call is missing a thought_signature"): a gap of the `openai` adapter with Gemini, not of the proxy; the bot is meant for the `anthropic` upstream.
- **Not built yet**: attachments from Discord (images to the model) and images in replies; streaming a reply into an edited message as it comes; a global `/ask`; budgets in money rather than tokens; the proxy for Gemini's native API.

### 2.22 App threads: apps that run threads of their own, within a grant

**Decided 2026-10-27**, with the user, for Atelier (a user's studio: a garment design app): its designer directs garment projects with an agent, from an app (project cards, a new project, a conversation with that project's thread beside a 3D mannequin, its replies shown in the app). An app (§2.10) was a window on one thread: `stitch.storage`, `stitch.report` to that thread, `stitch.live` to read. Atelier needs to start threads, talk to them and show what they say, without the user approving each call, and without the page (agent-written JavaScript, untrusted) reaching anything else of the user's. So: **an app runs threads of its own, a family, within what its manifest declares and the user granted once.** Nothing parallel is invented: the threads are detached threads (§2.3, §2.7), their tools a tool set (§2.14), their shells the sandbox and the approval rules (§2.3), their questions the user's attention list.

- **The family.** An app's threads are those it created, recorded on each thread (`app` = the app's conversation, `app_name` = its name, fields of the thread record, written when it starts; its first message is `from` `app "<name>"`). The key is the one live consent already uses (§2.10): the app's conversation and its version's name, so every version, and the pin that follows them, is the same app with the same threads; a pin with no conversation (the built-in dashboard) has none. An app sees and drives **only its family**: every call naming another thread answers 404 (not 403: no oracle of which IDs exist). It never sees the user's other threads, their questions or their transcripts (that stays `stitch.live`, with its own consent). The family's threads are the user's like any other: listed by `stitch threads`, the dashboard and the phone, cancellable and answerable there, attributed to the app.
- **The manifest** is `stitch-app.json` in the app's directory, taken by `show_app` into the version's manifest blob (`needs`), so it is versioned with the app and shown with it; `show_app` refuses one that does not parse or declares what cannot be declared, saying why. Under `threads`:
  - `places`: where its threads may work, as `spawn` names them (`~/devel/studio`, a folder, `global` for a thread's own working folder); the first is the default. Resolved on the node at each create, compared by place, not by spelling.
  - `tools`: tool entries (§2.14) turned on, the rest off: built-in groups `web`, `viewer`, `files`, `images`, MCP servers and patterns (`github__get_*`). **Not declarable:** `weave` (spawning, sending and cancelling would reach outside the family), `memory` (`recall` reads every conversation of the user's), `apps` and `browser` (the user's own Chrome, logged in as them), and **`shell`** (decided while building: the sandbox confines writes, not reads, and any process of the user's account can talk to the node's socket, which has no notion of who asks; a shell under an app's goal could read every conversation through `stitch show`, or answer its own question. Work an app needs done on the machine goes through Otter or MCP tools the user installed; a shell for apps waits for the node to tell its own shells' requests apart, by peer credentials on the socket, open). `skills`: the skills it has (none unless listed).
  - `otter`: Otter tools of its granted skills (§2.17) that run (never `otter_run` and the language tools, which are off for app threads: code the thread writes); `mcp_allow`: MCP tools (`server__tool`, patterns) of this node's own servers that run. No other Otter tool with effects and no other MCP call of a family thread runs, asked or not. Not declarable either: the `edge-talk` skill (it reads edge talk's conversations).
  - `models` (`default`, `quick`, `provider/model`; the first is the default), `budget` per thread (`usd`, `minutes`, `steps`, and `per`: `thread`, the default, for the thread's whole life (minutes from its create), or `turn`, each turn afresh, for long-lived conversations such as a project's; the app can neither raise nor renew a budget, only ask less), `daily_usd` for the whole family, `concurrent` (threads at work at once, default 2). Creates may also give a `title`.
- **The grant.** The user grants the app what its manifest lists, once: a `set` on the app's conversation, field `threads:<name>`, its value the needs granted and their hash (or a denial), written by the user only (`Author::user`), syncing with the conversation like live consent. Given by trusted UI only, seeing the list: on a computer `stitch app allow-threads "<name>" --thread <id>` **shows** the needs (the places as resolved here, a warning for a place that is a checkout, below) and the needs' hash, and writes nothing; `… --needs <hash>` writes the grant, refused when the version named so has other needs by then (decided 2026-10-27 after review: the first version wrote before the user saw anything). The page shows the command with `--needs`; agents' shells ask the user before it, as for `allow-live`. The name is the app's exact name (case counts) in that conversation, its latest version there, or a pinned app's. On the phone, a sheet naming the app by the node's record and listing the needs (the bridge's `stitch.threads.ask()`, as `live: ask`; not built yet: the page is told the command). The grant is read as the user's current value (`Index::current_user_value`: by the user, not by a run), and its needs are checked again as a manifest's are, so an old or hand-written grant cannot carry what can no longer be declared. **The grant binds the skills it names**: each by its directory and a hash of its files (SKILL.md, its tools and what they include), taken when granted; a family thread loads a skill only while it is that one, so a skill changed since, or another of the same name (a place's, which would otherwise come first), is left out until granted again. **A changed manifest needs a new grant**: the page's version must have the needs granted (by hash) to create threads (`consent: changed`, with the new list, otherwise). The grant standing **at each call** applies (below): a newer grant reaches every thread of the family at its next call, and a denial also cancels the family's turns at work.
- **Enforcement: one check on every path** (`apps/guard.rs`; decided 2026-10-27 after two reviews: the first version confined the *approvals*, and much of what a thread does never asks: a place's skills and their tools, `[otter] allow` and `[mcp] allow`, compile-time `@read`, image IDs, the web by URL spelling). A family thread's tools carry its `Guard`, and **every tool call passes `Guard::authorize` before anything of it runs** (nothing read, nothing dispatched), reading the grant standing at that call; revoked, denied, or its place no longer granted, everything is refused.
  - *Only these tools,* whatever a tool set says: `view_file` and `zoom_image` (viewer), `write_file` and `edit_file` (files), `search_web` and `browse_web` (web), `generate_image` (images), the Otter tools of granted skills, MCP tools of `mcp_allow` on this node's servers, `read_skill` of a granted skill. The tool set is still `-*` then the grant's (appended at every turn by `selection`, so nothing written later widens it), so the model is offered only these; the guard is what holds. It replaces the first version's separate list of confined tools and the shell rules its approver kept (a family thread has no shell).
  - *Files:* read and written only in the place (or a global thread's own folder), the path resolved (links, `..`) and authorized before anything is read, so `edit_file` tells nothing of a file it may not write. Writes outside the place go to the user. **Writes that would steer the user's own agents go to the user too, inside the place, never at once**: `.stitch/**` (a place's skills and settings), `.claude/**`, `.github/**`, `.vscode/**`, `.cargo/**`, `.devcontainer/**`, `.husky/**`, `.idea/**`, `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `GEMINI.md`, `.mcp.json`, `.envrc`, and tools' settings that run code (`.npmrc`, `.yarnrc[.yml]`, `.pre-commit-config.yaml`, `.gitmodules`); `.git` stays refused. Build files (`build.rs`, `justfile`, `Makefile`, `package.json`) stay writable: they are an app's ordinary work in a project of its own, but in a checkout the user's own threads build they would run as the user. So `allow-threads` **warns** when a granted place is a git checkout (or inside one): grant an app a folder of its own.
  - *Skills and Otter:* a family thread loads only granted skills still bound to the grant (above). Its scripts compile against a catalog **without `Http.*`** (a script's network would pass the web's filter by) and with `@include`/`@read` confined to their skill's directory; the language tools (`otter_run`, …) are off. A tool with effects runs only when `otter` names it; `[otter] allow`, a place's grants and tools that run unasked by default (`edge-talk`) count for nothing.
  - *MCP:* only `mcp_allow` names, on this node's servers (other nodes' shared servers are not offered to family threads), checked before `[mcp.<server>] allow`, `approval = "allow"` or any dispatch.
  - *Images:* `zoom_image` and `generate_image` take only images of the thread's own lineage (attachments, and images in tool results, of its transcripts), by full ID, no prefix match; the app's files endpoint serves that same set.
  - *The web, after DNS:* `browse_web` for a family thread is a fetch by a client of its own: no proxy, a resolver that drops every address not public (loopback, unspecified, private, CGNAT `100.64/10`, link-local, multicast, broadcast, documentation and benchmarking ranges; IPv6 loopback, ULA, link-local, site-local `fec0::/10`, multicast, documentation, and mapped, compatible, NAT64 and 6to4 addresses whose IPv4 is not public), so a name resolving here (`nip.io`, `localtest.me`, `.internal`), a rebinding or a redirect is refused at connection time; each redirect hop to an IP literal is checked by the redirect policy. Pages are read without JavaScript (their HTML as Markdown), files as downloads, at most 10 MiB. **`web_eval` is off** for family threads: the headless browser has no such filter (subresources, a page's own fetches); it comes back with a filtering proxy for that browser. `search_web` asks only the search provider.
  - *No yolo, no borrowed grants:* a family thread never runs as yolo, and the user's own allowances (`[shell] allow`, "always" answers, `[otter] allow`, `[mcp] allow`) do not apply to it: they were given for the user's threads.
  - *Approvals:* what remains the user's (writes outside the place or to the files above, a clone) is a question like any (§2.3) whose command names the app (`[app "Atelier"] write_file …`), on the attention list, in `stitch threads` and on the phone; never answerable from the app (no app call answers a question; the app sees `state: approval`).
  - *Its prompt:* a family thread's system prompt is short: that it is an app's thread whose requests come from an untrusted page, its place's instructions, its granted skills and the turn's own notes; not the user's home instructions, the node's names, SSH hosts or the home directory.
  - A place that is, holds or is inside the node's data directory (home, the root) or another thread's folder is never granted.
- **The bridge** (POSTs JSON, same origin; GETs; all under `/_stitch/threads`, through the phone's scheme handler and HTTP alike): `stitch.threads.grant()` (the consent state, the needs and the command that grants them), `create({goal, place, model, budget_usd, budget_minutes, max_steps, title, images})`, `list()` (the family, newest first: id, title, goal, state `working`/`approval`/`idle`/`done`/`cancelled`, the outcome, place, model calls, cost, last words; with the family's spend today and its caps), `get(id)`, `send(id, text, images?)` (woven into a turn at work, else a new turn), `messages(id, {after})` (the transcript as the app sees it: the user's and the agent's messages with their images, a line per tool call by name, never tool output; `n` to ask for what follows), `cancel(id)`, and `stitch.threads.on(cb)`, the existing long poll (`/_stitch/events` with `threads=N`), where N is the **family's own mark** (a hash of its threads' states and steps): it answers only when one of the family moved, so its timing tells the page nothing of the user's other threads (the first version answered when any thread moved). Images: at most 4 a message, 5 MiB each and 10 MiB together (a request holds 16 MiB), PNG, JPEG, WebP or GIF by their bytes, kept as blobs and attached to the message (§2.11); a thread's files are served to its app only when its lineage's transcripts hold them as images (`/_stitch/threads/<id>/files/<hash>`, the same set its thread may name in `zoom_image`). Text at most 32 KiB. Answers carry `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin`: on a desktop every app is a `*.localhost` host, one site, so another app's page could otherwise embed them.
- **Money, at every step** (decided 2026-10-27 after review: the first version checked the day's money only when a thread was created or sent to). The loop asks its tools before every model call (`ToolRunner::admit`) and tells them what each step and each tool cost (`ToolRunner::metered`). A family thread's guard keeps a **family ledger** (per app, on the node that runs the family, below): the family's spend since local midnight, read from its usage events once a day, then kept by what its turns meter. A step is admitted, under the family's lock, only while the day's spend plus what the family's other turns reserved is under `daily_usd` (each admitted step reserves its likely cost, the last step's, until it is metered), and while the thread is under the **current** grant's per-thread budget (its lineage's spend, or this turn's with `per: turn`; minutes likewise): lowering a grant reaches threads at work, and a budget the user raised for a turn of their own does not carry to the app's. Costs are priced from usage at list prices when the provider gives none (a subscription, other providers); a step with no known price stops a family turn, and a create naming a model with none is refused. `generate_image` is priced (at its model's list price) or refused for a family thread. A turn may pass the day by at most one step of each turn at work.
- **Admission.** Creates and sends are admitted under the family's lock, which counts the threads at work and the slots claimed by admissions not yet started, so parallel calls cannot pass `concurrent` together; the claim ends when the turn is listed as running. Each create is capped by the grant's per-thread budget (a create may ask less, never more), and refused (429) when the family spent its day or has its threads at work; at most one create every 2 seconds and a send a second per thread. The model, the place and the budget a create asks are checked against the grant before anything is written (403 saying what the grant allows). Images are decoded (PNG, JPEG, WebP or GIF, at most 8192 pixels a side and 40 megapixels) as a whole batch before the call is admitted, and stored as blobs only once it is, within a family upload quota of 100 MiB a day (kept by the node in memory: a restart resets it).
- **A thread is its lineage.** A family thread compacted goes on in a new record; the app keeps the first one's ID (`fork::lineage`): list, get, messages (every transcript of the lineage, `n` counted across them), files, send and cancel resolve it, and spend is summed over it, each record's own usage once. A user's fork of a family thread is the user's, not the family's (only compactions carry the app).
- **One node runs a family** (decided 2026-10-27: the ledger and the admission lock are a node's, so the family's money is kept where it runs). A family thread runs only on the node it started on, the one serving the page: a turn of it on another node is refused by nodes that know app threads, and a node older than this, which would run it unscoped, reads its tool set from its record as `-*` alone (the grant's entries are added at each turn by a node that enforces them), so it would run it with no tools. A send from a page served by another node answers 409. **Not built:** the phone's Atelier with its threads on gamer (the phone serves the page, gamer runs and keeps the ledger): an assignment carrying the app, the running node enforcing with the grant synced to it; it may go only to a node whose `capabilities` say it enforces app grants. Until then an app pinned on the phone runs its threads on the phone.
- **A web adapter later** (for the designer's own login, outside the user's devices): a client of the node like the Discord bridge (§2.21), `stitch web`, with its own accounts (a passkey, sessions), each account given a set of pinned apps; it serves those apps' origins by passing requests to `apps::serve` unchanged (the same family, the same grant, the same 404s), adds attribution (`end_user` = `web:<account>`, as `discord:<id>`) and rate limits of its own, and never relays approvals: those stay the user's, on the user's devices.
- **Security, with the page untrusted:** a page reaches only its own origin's calls (§2.10: a token cookie per host, POSTs from the host's own origin, no DNS rebinding; the phone's scheme handler has no other origin); the family scope is checked on the node, per call, from records, never from what the page says; the grant is written only by trusted UI; questions are never answerable by an app; the page never sees tool output or the user's other threads. What a grant does give, the app can read through its threads' replies: what its place holds, what its Otter and MCP tools return, what the web gives. `stitch app allow-threads` says so; grant a place of its own where it can. Left: a filtering proxy for the headless browser (then `web_eval`, and pages run with their JavaScript), and Otter's `Http.*` through the same filter (it needs a resolver hook in Otter: open); the upload quota and the claims kept in records rather than memory; the long poll's mark is computed from the family's rows on each move (cheap for a family, not indexed).

**Built 2026-10-27** (`apps/threads.rs`, the bridge's `stitch.threads`, `stitch app allow-threads`; the demo `examples/app-threads/`, "Studio", with `tools/app-threads/try.sh`: a node of its own in a temporary data directory and a mock model): the manifest, the grant, create, list, get, send, messages, cancel and files scoped to the family, the tool set, confinement and approvals enforced, the limits. **Tested:** unit tests of the needs, the tool entries and the sorting of approvals; `tests/app_threads.rs` (a node and a mock model: refused until granted, what the grant does not cover refused before anything is written, a thread created with an image, only its tools offered, a read outside its place and a local address refused, the family alone listed and reachable (the user's thread 404), a message from the app, a changed manifest asking again while the family stays readable, a denial; and on a yolo node a write outside the place waiting on the user, the concurrency limit, a cancel); the demo driven in a headless browser against a test node on gamer. **Not yet:** the phone's grant sheet (the page shows the command); threads on another node; the web adapter; a shell for apps (above); streaming a reply as it is written (a page reads the transcript when the long poll says something moved); files a thread makes reaching its app other than as images in its messages (open: an `app_files` tool, or the thread's folder served read-only to its app); the app named in `stitch threads` and on the attention list (`stitch.live.threads` gives `app`).

**Reworked 2026-10-27, after two independent reviews** (`apps/guard.rs`, `web/public.rs`; the decisions are in the bullets above): the confinement was one of approvals, and many paths never asked. Now: one guard on every call of a family thread (the tool list, files with the paths that steer agents routed to the user, images of its lineage only, the web filtered after DNS with `web_eval` off, Otter compiled without `Http.*` and with includes kept to the skill, the language tools off, MCP only as granted and only this node's, the user's `[otter]`/`[mcp]` allowances void); skills bound to the grant by the hash of their files; the grant shown before it is written (`--needs`), read as the user's current value and checked again, empty grant lists granting nothing; money admitted before and metered after every model call against a family ledger, priced at list prices when the provider gives none, unknown prices stopping the turn, `generate_image` priced; admissions under a family lock with claims; the lineage as the app's thread; a denial cancelling the family's turns; one node running a family (creates refused elsewhere, turns refused on other nodes, the record's tool set `-*` for older ones); a minimal prompt; images decoded before admission and stored after, with a daily quota; a compaction's summary of a family thread counted to the family (and none while its grant is revoked or its day spent); a review of the rework found a `..` after a missing directory passing the place check of `write_file` (the user's threads too: paths are now resolved component by component) and wanted the content of a write in its question (shown, cut at 2000 characters). Otter tools' files at run time were already confined to the thread's place (`FsAccess::within`, `Db.Query` included); `title` on create; budgets per turn; the long poll's family mark; rate maps pruned; a user's fork no longer the family's; places in threads' folders refused. **Tested:** the guard's verdicts for each tool (`guard.rs`), steering paths, images named exactly, public addresses (mapped, NAT64, 6to4, CGNAT, site-local, …) and names resolving here (`web/public.rs`), an app's scripts without network or outside reads (`otter/host.rs`), skills unbound when changed, per-turn budgets hashed apart, images decoded within bounds, approvals sorted with empty grants refused (`threads.rs`); `tests/app_threads.rs`: the grant shown then written with its hash (another hash refused), a mapped-IPv6 loopback URL, another blob's image ID and `web_eval` refused, the family's prompt free of the user's home, nodes and hosts, a write to `.stitch/skills` in its own folder waiting on the user with the app named, a turn stopped before the step that would pass the day's money (none past it reached the model), two parallel sends with one slot admitting one, a denial cancelling the turn at work. The last two fail with the guard's step check and the claim taken out. **Not covered by tests:** compaction of a family thread (the lineage functions are exercised only uncompacted), the revocation mid-turn through the node (the guard's verdict under a revoked grant is unit-tested), the refusal of a family turn on another node.

### 2.23 App containers: declared commands in rootless podman, and app data

**Decided 2026-10-27**, with the user, after two independent reviews of a jail for app commands (GPT-6 Astra and Opus, in threads `2026-10-27T185030Z-gamer-qsjm` and `…-uzkf`): Atelier (§2.22) must rebuild a garment with Blender, and an app's threads run no programs. The reviews found Stitch's shell sandbox (§2.3) cannot be that jail: it confines writes, not reads, the network, sockets (the node's, D-Bus, ssh-agent, X11's abstract socket, Docker's, localhost ports) or the environment, and its macOS profile allows by default. A jail of our own (namespaces, seccomp, Landlock, a deny-by-default Seatbelt profile) would be security code Stitch maintains on two OSes, and apps would still need their tools installed on the host. **So: an app ships a `Containerfile`; the node builds its image in rootless podman, and runs the commands the manifest declares in containers whose whole invocation Stitch owns.** Both reviews chose this. It rests on the same kernel boundary a jail would (user namespaces), so a kernel or runtime escape still lands as a user of the machine: the command runs as a container user that maps to a subordinate ID, owning nothing of the user's, but the boundary is the kernel's. Linux only; macOS refuses (`podman machine` is a VM sharing `/Users` by default: later, opt-in, with no shares and inputs copied in), iOS runs no containers (a phone's app sends its commands to a node that does, with its family, §2.22 "One node runs a family", not built).

- **What an app declares** (`stitch-app.json`, beside `threads`; `apps/containers/spec.rs`):
  - `container.dir`: the directory of the app holding its `Containerfile` and everything the build may `COPY` (the **build context**, default `container`): the app's tools bundle (Atelier's `technical_garment.py`) goes there, so it is baked into the image. It is part of the app's version like its other files (§2.10), so the context is a snapshot in the blob store, never the live directory.
  - `container.build`: `memory_mb`, `minutes`, `network` (`true`, the default, or `false`), `disk_mb` (the app's image budget). `container.cache`: an optional persistent volume (`path` in the container, `mb`); nothing persists between runs otherwise.
  - `container.commands`: each `run` (argv, the first element an absolute path in the image, never looked up), its typed `args` (`file`: a file of the app's data folder under `dir` with one of `ext`; `out`: a folder the node makes; `enum`, `int` with `min`/`max`, `slug`), each substituted as **one whole argv element** and only where it stands alone (`{recipe}`, never inside another); `inputs` (files outside the app, each pinned by SHA-256, copied in: Atelier's Freya mannequin); limits (`timeout` seconds, `memory_mb`, `cpus`, `pids`), output caps (`max_files`, `max_mb`), and `gpu` (step 4).
  - `container.shell`: asks for the jailed shell (step 4).
  - Refused at `show_app` (saying why): a command whose argv is not absolute, a placeholder inside an argument or naming no arg, an input without a 64-hex SHA-256 or inside the node's data directory or a hidden directory of the home (`~/.ssh`, `~/.stitch`, `~/.config`), limits past the node's caps (timeout 1 h, 16 GiB, 16 CPUs, 1024 PIDs, 2 GiB of outputs, 64 GiB of image), a Containerfile whose `FROM` names a tag instead of `@sha256:` (or `scratch`; a later `FROM` may name an earlier stage), an `ADD <url>` without `--checksum=sha256:`, `RUN --mount=type=secret|ssh`, or a missing Containerfile.
- **Pins, not image digests.** Builds do not reproduce (apt), and each node builds its own, so an image digest means nothing to the user and nothing across nodes. `show_app` computes the **pins** from the version's files: the Containerfile's SHA-256, its base images' digests, and the context's tree hash (every file under `container.dir` by path and content blob). The pins are part of the needs, so a changed Containerfile or tool is a changed manifest. The image a node built is recorded on that node (label `stitch.pins`), shown for information.
- **Three grants, each the user's, each by hash** (fields on the app's conversation like `threads:<name>`, written by `Author::user` only; `stitch app allow-container "<name>" --thread <id>` shows all three and writes none; `--build <hash>`, `--run <hash>`, `--shell <hash>` write each; `--deny` takes them back; agents' shells ask the user first, as for `allow-threads`):
  - **build** (`build:<name>`, its hash over the pins, the build limits, the network and the cache): the grant says plainly "the build runs the app's code (its Containerfile) with internet access" and shows the Containerfile, and when a Containerfile was granted before, the diff from it; it is asked again only when the pins change. The granted context's files are in the grant, by blob, so the build uses exactly what the user saw.
  - **run** (`run:<name>`, its hash over the commands, the pins, the inputs with their hashes and the devices): each command with its argv, args, inputs and limits; a GPU command says what it exposes. The commands run only with the image built from the granted pins.
  - **shell** (`shell:<name>`), step 4.
- **Building** (`apps/containers/build.rs`): only after the build grant, started by the grant itself (the user's act), or by a run that finds no image for its pins, at most once an hour per app and pins (failures remembered), one build at a time on the node: an app cannot trigger builds without limit. The context is written from the granted blobs into a fresh directory, nothing else (no secrets, no `--secret`/`--ssh`, no other podman options). `podman build --pull=missing --layers=false --force-rm --no-cache=false` (no intermediate images kept: no build cache to grow; a rebuild starts over, which is fine for something rebuilt when its pins change), `--memory`, `--pids-limit` of the grant, `--network=pasta:--no-map-gw,--map-guest-addr,none` (or `none` when the app declares no network), a timeout that kills it, labelled `stitch.app`, `stitch.node`, `stitch.pins`, tagged `localhost/stitch-app-<app key>:<pins>`.
  - **What the build's network reaches, verified on gamer** (podman 6.1.4, pasta 2026_10_02; `live_tests::the_build_reaches_the_internet_not_the_host`): not the host's loopback (127.0.0.1); not the host's services listening on all addresses through 169.254.1.2 (`host.containers.internal`), **which podman maps to the host by default** (`--map-guest-addr`: the live test reached a port bound to 0.0.0.0 that way until the build said `--map-guest-addr,none`); not the host's own LAN or bridge addresses (pasta gives the container the host's address, so it is itself), not Docker's bridge; the internet yes. **Other machines on the LAN stay reachable** (the router answered): pasta has no destination filter (`--no-tcp` with a spliced port to a filtering proxy fails to start). So the grant says "the internet and your local network (not this machine's own services)". Builds that need no network say `network: false` and run with none. A filtering egress proxy (public addresses only, `web/public.rs`'s resolver) is the way to close the LAN: open.
- **Running** (`apps/containers/run.rs`): the built-in tool `app_command` (`{command, args}`), offered to an app's thread only while its run grant stands and the command is in it, through the guard (§2.22) like every call; money for compute is counted as **minutes**: `container.daily_minutes` beside `daily_usd` (default 60), the family's container time today kept in its ledger. The invocation is all Stitch's: `podman run --rm --init --pull=never <image ID>` (by ID, never a name), `--entrypoint` set to the command's first element and its arguments after the image (the image's `ENTRYPOINT`/`CMD` never run), `--network=none`, `--read-only` with `--tmpfs` for `/tmp`, `/run`, the home and `/dev/shm` (each bounded), `--cap-drop=all`, `--security-opt=no-new-privileges`, Stitch's seccomp profile, `--pid=private --ipc=private --uts=private`, `--user 1000:1000` (not `keep-id`: an escape lands as subordinate ID 100999, owning nothing of the user's), `--memory` with no swap, `--pids-limit`, `--cpus`, `--ulimit core=0`, `--log-driver=none`, `--timeout`, no `--env` but `HOME`, `PATH`, `LANG`, `TMPDIR`, labels on every container (`stitch.app`, `stitch.node`, `stitch.run`). Inputs (the `file` args, the declared `inputs`) are copied into a fresh staging folder and mounted read-only, `noexec`; the output folder is a fresh folder the node makes, mounted `noexec,nosuid,nodev`, owned by the container's user (`podman unshare chown`). Podman runs with a cleared environment (only `PATH`, `HOME`, `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, `USER`, `LANG`), so `CONTAINER_HOST`, `DOCKER_HOST` and connection settings are not inherited; its socket is never exposed nor the API service started.
  - **Seccomp: podman's default allows nested user namespaces** (verified on gamer: `unshare -r` inside a container gave root in a new namespace; io_uring is refused). So Stitch passes its own profile: podman's default (`containers-common`'s, compiled in) without `unshare`, `setns`, `mount` and the new mount API, `pivot_root`, `umount2`, `keyctl`, `ptrace`, `process_vm_readv`, `pidfd_getfd`, `name_to_handle_at`, `bpf`, `perf_event_open`, `userfaultfd` and io_uring, `clone` allowed only without namespace flags, `clone3` answering `ENOSYS` (its flags are behind a pointer; libcs fall back to `clone`).
  - **The engine is checked before every build and run** (`podman info`, cached a minute): `rootless` true, not a remote client (`serviceIsRemote`), cgroup v2 with the `memory` and `pids` controllers delegated (rootless limits are silently ignored otherwise), seccomp enabled; else refused, saying what is missing. Docker is never used (rootful Docker is root-equivalent; refused, not discouraged).
  - **Kill the whole container:** a timeout (and a cancelled turn, a revoked grant) runs `podman kill` then `podman rm -f`, and waits until no container labelled with the run remains: the PID namespace dies with its init, `setsid` grandchildren included. At node start, containers labelled with this node's data directory (`stitch.node`) are removed (conmon keeps them alive past a crashed node); the user's own containers are never touched.
  - **Outputs are hostile.** While the command runs, a watcher (`podman unshare du`, every half second) kills it when its outputs pass `max_mb` or `max_files`. Once the container is gone: ownership taken back (`podman unshare chown -R -h 0:0`, never following links), then read by the node without following links, regular files only (a symlink, FIFO, socket or device is skipped and said), at most `max_files` files and `max_mb` together, names checked. Accepted files go to the app's data folder (below) under `out/<command>-<time>-<id>/`; images (PNG, JPEG, WebP by their bytes, decoded within the image bounds of §2.22) also become the turn's reply's attachments, so the app's `messages` and files show them and the thread can look at its work; `.glb`, `.gltf`, `.blend`, `.json`, `.txt` and the rest only as files of the data folder, which the bridge serves as downloads (`attachment`, `nosniff`, never as HTML or script; `.glb` as `model/gltf-binary` for the viewer). A `.blend` is untrusted when opened later in the user's own Blender with auto-run on. stdout and stderr are cut at 16 KiB each, control characters removed, before the model sees them.
- **GPU (step 4):** a command with `gpu: true` gets `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all` through CDI, with a spec **the node generates with the system's NVIDIA Container Toolkit** (`/usr/bin/nvidia-ctk`, root's and writable by root alone, at least 1.17.8: the fixes for CVE-2024-0132 and CVE-2025-23266, read from `nvidia-ctk --version`; gamer has 1.20.1) into its own directory before each GPU run, `--disable-hook all` then only `create-symlinks`, `update-ldcache` and the hardening `disable-device-node-modification` (gamer's installed spec in `/var/run/cdi` has six kinds of hooks: `enable-cuda-compat`, `apply-cuda-memory-limits`, `update-application-profile` are left out), checked again after (every hook NVIDIA's root-owned `nvidia-cdi-hook`, only those three), passed with `--cdi-spec-dir` alone: an app never supplies a spec or a hook. One GPU command at a time on the node. The run grant says: "the GPU's kernel driver and NVIDIA's container hooks (run on the host as the container starts) are exposed to this command". Default: CPU (Atelier's `rebuild_garment` is CPU: Cycles renders its preview in seconds). **Verified on gamer** (RTX 4090): `live_tests::a_gpu_command_sees_the_gpu_through_stitchs_cdi_spec` (the devices and the driver's files in a container with Stitch's invocation and spec), and `nvidia-smi -L` in a Debian container with the spec, no capabilities, user 1000, read-only, no network (with podman's default seccomp; Stitch's profile was checked with the devices only).
- **The jailed shell (step 4):** the `shell` grant gives an app's threads `app_shell`, a command line run by `/bin/sh -c` in the app's image with exactly a command's isolation and invocation (no network, the image read-only, Stitch's seccomp, user 1000, limits, a timeout per call), each call a container of its own: the data folder's `work/` copied in read-only at `/in/work` (256 MiB, 2,000 files at most, no links), what it writes to `/out` read back as hostile into `out/shell-<time>-<id>/` (never written into `work/` directly: a container's files would be a subordinate ID's, and its links the node's to follow). It **cancels pinning for that app**: the shell runs whatever the thread writes, so the image's pinned tools no longer bound what runs; the grant says so in capitals. A network through a filtering proxy is future work (design only: the proxy must be the only reachable egress, not an `HTTP_PROXY` variable a program can ignore; pasta cannot filter destinations, so it would be a network namespace whose only route is the proxy's socket). Built on the command's path; not exercised end to end (Atelier declares no shell).
- **Storage:** images live in the user's podman storage (`~/.local/share/containers`), 1–3 GB an app. Each app has a disk budget (`build.disk_mb`, default 4 GiB): a build that would pass it is refused; after a build the app keeps its current and previous image, older ones are removed. Stitch removes only what it labelled (`stitch.node` its own data directory), and a base image only when Stitch pulled it (the node records which bases were absent before its build), so the user's own images, containers and volumes are never pruned. The optional cache volume (`stitch-app-<key>-cache`, labelled) is measured after each run and emptied when past its `mb`. `stitch app storage` lists each app's images, cache and data folder with sizes; `stitch app storage "<name>" --rm` removes its images and cache (rebuilt on the next run). Outputs in the data folder count to the app's data quota (below); images attached to replies go to the blob store, owned by the family's threads, within the family's daily upload quota of §2.22 (100 MiB) and the outputs' own cap: blob growth is bounded by the outputs' `max_mb` per run times the runs the daily compute minutes allow, and blobs leave with their threads (§2.12).
- **App data, apart from the app's code** (scope added 2026-10-27, `apps/files.rs`): until now an app's threads wrote into their place, for Atelier the app's own git checkout, mixing the user's designs with code, and the page could not load files its threads made. So **each app with threads has a data folder**, declared as `data.folder` (default `~/Apps/<name>`: the user's files, visible and backed up with home, never under the node's data directory, which `holds_data` refuses, and never a checkout, which is refused too, not just warned about), with `data.quota_mb` (default 2 GiB) and `data.local` (subfolders that do not sync: `out` and `cache` by default). It is a **folder place** (§2.16), so it syncs between the user's nodes like any folder, after the user allows its first sync, within the folder limits (25 MB a file, 250 MB, 2,000 files); the node writes its `.stitch/ignore` with the local subfolders when it creates it (outputs are rebuildable; a file the app wants synced it copies outside them). *What was verified:* the folder is made at the grant with `.stitch/ignore` holding `out/*` and `cache/*`, which the sync's scan (`folders::Ignore`, pruning a directory whose path matches) leaves out with everything under them, so a rebuild's GLB and `.blend` stay on the node that made them; read in the code and seen in the end-to-end run's folder, **not run across two nodes**. Folders sync by their `~` path's identity, so `~/Apps/Atelier` on gamer and on the Mac is the same folder. `threads.places` may name it as `data` (Atelier's threads work there). The grant shows it resolved; its hash covers it.
  - **`stitch.files`** in the bridge, for the page, scoped to that folder alone: `list(dir)`, `read(path)` (bytes, or a URL `/_stitch/files/<path>` a Three.js loader can fetch), `write(path, bytes|text)`, `remove(path)`, and `mark()`, which changes when the folder's files do (a page polls it; folding it into the long poll is left). Paths are relative, without `..`, `.`-prefixed parts or NUL; each is resolved component by component and refused if any part is a link; only regular files are read; a write goes through a temporary file and never replaces a link; writes are at most 15 MiB a file (a request holds 16 MiB) and within the quota; the same origin, POST-only for changes, the token cookie and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin` as the other calls. Granted with the threads (the needs' hash covers `data`): before the grant the page has no files.
- **Not done here:** a "trusted app" mode (fewer restrictions for an app the user wrote) is out of scope: a named mode, if ever, never a silent weakening; the macOS VM; the phone's grant sheets; a GPU other than NVIDIA's; the filtering proxy.

**Built 2026-10-27** (`apps/containers/{spec,engine,build,run,storage}.rs`, `apps/files.rs`, the bridge's `stitch.files`, `stitch app allow-container`, `stitch app storage`; branch `app-containers`): everything above but the macOS VM and the phone's grant sheets. **Tested:** unit tests of the manifest's checks (Containerfiles: tags, unpinned downloads, secrets and SSH, `RUN --network`, `COPY --from` a tag), commands (placeholders whole and named, limits, pinned inputs, hidden directories), the hashes each grant covers, the pins, typed arguments (`..`, other folders, extensions, ranges, extra arguments refused), the data folder (links, `..`, other apps' folders, steering files, the quota), the hostile read-back (links, FIFOs, hidden names, a closed folder, caps), the whole invocation, the engine's refusals (rootful, remote, cgroup v1, controllers not delegated), the seccomp profile, the NVIDIA version and CDI checks; and **live tests on the real engine** (`live_tests.rs`, skipped where podman or a C compiler is missing; CI's devbox-1 has podman 6.1.4): a static probe in a container run with Stitch's invocation reaches none of the node's socket, D-Bus, Docker's socket, Wayland, X11 (file and abstract), an abstract D-Bus name, localhost (bound to 127.0.0.1 and to all addresses), 169.254.1.2, the host's LAN address, the internet, `~/.stitch` and `~/.ssh`; writes only `/out`, cannot run what it wrote there; `unshare` (user and network), `clone(CLONE_NEWUSER)`, io_uring, `mount`, `keyctl` and `ptrace` answer ENOSYS; links and FIFOs it leaves in `/out` are not followed; a `setsid` grandchild dies with the container at the timeout and no container is left; 400 MB under a 128 MiB cap is killed; 200 MB of outputs in a closed folder past a 16 MiB cap stops the run; the build reaches the internet and not the host (the test found 169.254.1.2 reaching services on all addresses until `--map-guest-addr,none`); a GPU command sees the GPU through Stitch's spec. **End to end** (`tools/app-containers/atelier_e2e.sh`, a test node, a mock model scripted as the thread): Atelier shown, its threads, build and run granted by hash, its image built (37–42 s on gamer: the Debian layers were cached; 1.48 GiB), the recipe written by the page through `stitch.files`, a thread edits it (`hemZ` 0.62 → 0.55) and runs `rebuild_garment` (10 s, CPU), the preview comes back as an image of its reply, the page reads `garment.glb` (`model/gltf-binary`) and the `.blend` as a download, and the page's 3D view shows the shorter skirt from the data folder. A test node's images are its own (labelled with its data directory) and removed with `stitch app storage --rm`.

**Storage on gamer, 2026-10-27:** `/home` (btrfs, 1.9 TB) had 105 GB free (95% used). Podman's storage held 2.7 GB of the user's own images before (66 images, mostly untagged builds of other projects: not Stitch's, never touched by it). Atelier's image is 1.48 GiB (Debian bookworm-slim and Blender 5.2.3); with retention, at most two per app (3 GiB) within its 4 GiB budget. A rebuild writes about 3.6 MB into `out/` (GLB 0.3 MB, mannequin 0.6 MB, `.blend` 1.1 MB, preview 1.6 MB) and 1.6 MB of blob (the preview), within the family's 100 MiB a day of uploads.

**Not yet:** the macOS VM; the phone's grant sheets for containers and the data folder; `stitch.files` in the long poll (a page polls `mark()`); the filtering egress proxy (builds reach the LAN); the shell end to end; the per-app disk budget is checked after a build (the build itself is bounded by time and memory, not disk); a failed build's partial storage is podman's to clean (`--force-rm`, no layers kept); the image attached by a command is shown on the reply step that called it, before the tool's line, in `messages`.

### 2.24 The web client: the next app of the apps system (proposal)

Proposed 2026-11-02; the points marked decided are with the user the same day. **Built so far (part B):** the `/_stitch/ws` WebSocket (`apps/ws.rs`, JSON envelope over the socket's own handler; origin of the `builtin = "client"` pin only) and the pin with a placeholder bundle. It is not a new subsystem but the next app of the apps system (§2.10, §2.22, §2.23): the main desktop UI is a **built-in app**, like the dashboard.

- **A PWA served by the node itself** (decided 2026-11-02, with the user). Embedded in the node binary and answered by `apps::serve` on the existing loopback server (`[apps] port` 7177, `.localhost` origins, token-then-cookie, the Host and Origin checks). Users install it with Chrome's "Install as app" (a web manifest). No Tauri, no Electron. **`stitch client`** (decided 2026-11-12, with the user: the web client is a first-class client) opens it: `Request::Client` finds the client's pin by its builtin key (`live::CLIENT_KEY`; the pin's name does not matter), pins it when it is not yet, and answers `Reply::App` with its address; the CLI prints it and opens the browser (`--no-open`: print only). On a node that serves no apps (`[apps] port = 0`, or a phone) the request fails saying so. `--thread <id>` opens it on `#/t/<id>` and `--map` on `#/map` (routes, below): the CLI asks the node for the thread first and says so when there is none; the URL is `…/?k=<token>#/t/<id>`, the node's 303 that drops `?k=` sets the cookie and the browser carries the fragment over (it is never sent to the server), and the client reads it at start. Unpinning it by name says it is the built-in client. **The client's pin is the node's own, not the user's:** `apps::pinned` (every list, `stitch app list/unpin/allow-*/export`, `follow`, the launcher page, the phone's `pinned_apps_now`) leaves it out, so it cannot be listed, unpinned or re-versioned; `apps::pinned_all` is for the node (serving, the WebSocket's origin check, `client`/`ensure_client`, `pin_with`) and `AppOpen`, which still finds it by name. Rare native needs go through the node (a user service; it registers `stitch://`) or the Chrome extension (apps/chrome, §2.18). The TUI stays for SSH and quick use.
- **Only the local node** (decided). Other nodes are reached through its iroh peer links (§2.6, §2.7); no node opens a port to the network.
- **Reused as it is** (decided): `apps::serve` routing and its two transports; an origin per app; the cookie auth; the bundled libraries; state as `set` records synced across devices; the long poll (`/_stitch/events`, change marks) for hearing other devices; and §2.22's thread API; §2.23's containers need nothing from the client, whose iframes show their outputs and data folder through `stitch.files` as now.
- **The thread API is §2.22's, widened** (decided). The page calls `stitch.threads` (`grant`, `create`, `list`, `get`, `send`, `messages(id, {after})`, `cancel`, `on`) on `/_stitch/threads`, with its guard, 404s and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy`. For an ordinary app the family is the threads it created; for the client it is all of the user's threads (every node's, through the local node), and the client may also do what only the user does and an app never can (§2.22: "no app call answers a question"): answer approvals and questions, cancel any thread, delete, fork, switch modes and models. Where `messages` shows no tool output, the client's reads do (transcript, in-flight calls, diffs). Raw `Request`/`Reply` is the wire under it, not a second page API.
- **One WebSocket per client to the node** (decided 2026-11-02, with the user), the one addition to the transport. It carries both directions: `Request`s and their `Reply`s, and streamed turn events (a turn's tokens, steps and in-flight calls: `ChatEvent`, `Question::Follow`, `InFlight`), which §2.22 lists as not built ("streaming a reply as it is written"). The long poll stays only for what ordinary apps use (change marks). **At the upgrade it needs what the HTTP path already has:** the same Host check and Origin check, plus the cookie; a cross-site WebSocket is not blocked by CORS, so the Origin check is what protects it. The channel is also what the **Discord bridge** (§2.21) lacks: it follows its threads by polling the node's files every 3 s because no node API streams thread events. It becomes another client of the same public protocol, which proves the protocol is a real API.
- **Front end** (decided 2026-11-02, with the user): TypeScript with Preact and `@preact/signals`. Solid was rejected: its JSX needs a compile step of its own (a Bun plugin with Babel inside), destructuring props silently breaks its reactivity, and agents know it less well; in a streaming transcript the cost is re-rendering markdown, not the framework. Bun builds it at development time only (`bun build`, one tool for bundling and type stripping, behind a `just` recipe).
