A bare `--threshold p` sets a profile's one question, or its one threshold for every question (`land`); on a profile with several (`guard.fixer`, `triage`) it is refused: name the key. `stitch decide` (alone or `profiles`) lists the decision and fallback models, every profile with its questions, thresholds as they stand and where they came from, fallback, model, timeout, and the node's own decisions today against their cap; `stitch decide set [<profile>] [--threshold [key=]p]… [--fallback <model|none>] [--model m] [--daily usd]` (`--model`, `--daily` and a global `--fallback` without a profile); run from a thread's shell it asks the user (their question alone, yolo or not; `hazard::ask_user`), the node itself may not, and a fixer's commands are not served it (`fixer::refuses`). `stitch decide try <profile> <text|-> [--yes-no "…?"]…` asks and prints each answer against its threshold. `Request::Decide { input }` (local-67 with the guard and `Request::Mode`).
- **Hardened after the review** (2026-11-14): **the whole request is redacted** (input, questions' instructions, options' descriptions, levels; a key or an option's name holding a secret is refused) before it is hashed, sent or kept, and records (answers, errors) are redacted again as written; **money is admitted before every paid call**: the node's own decisions within a daily cap ($0.50 unless `--daily`), a thread's within what its tool call may still spend (500 decisions and $0.50 a call, a script's nested `Decide`s counted together, and what was left of the thread's budget when the call began), an app's grant admitted per `decide` call as a step is; at most four paid calls at once for the node; accounting is cancellation-safe (a call's spending and verdicts are kept outside its future, so a stopped `otter_run` still counts what its decisions spent); a model that answered something unusable (a missing answer, unreadable JSON) is charged all the same; records are appended a whole line at a time under a lock. The triage keeps agents' titles out of the instructions and options (issues named by key), and drops a choice outside the keys it offered. Loose ends carry the agent's last words apart from the excerpt (never parsed back out of it).
- **For agents and skills:** the **`decide` tool** (its own group, `decide`, on by default; `tools::decide`), `{input, questions}` with questions by key: a string (yes/no), `{choice, options}` (a list, or a table of when each applies; up to 255), `{score, levels}` (lowest first); 100 questions and 400,000 characters a call; the `tool` profile; its result JSON (`answers` with each choice's ten likeliest, `calibrated`, `model`, `ms`, `usd`) and its cost metered to the thread. An app's or a family's thread has it only when its grant turns the `decide` group on (`apps::guard::authorize`; priced, so the grant's money counts it). **Otter's `Decide`** (`otter::decide`, a host shard of Stitch's next to `Tool.Call`; Otter itself names nothing of it): `text | Decide({spam: "Is it spam?" lang: {choice: "Which?" options: ["en" "it"]}})` outputs the tool's result as a table; it reaches the thread's `decide` tool through `otter_run`'s tool calls, so it is approved, granted and priced as the tool (no effects of its own in `otter_run`'s approval); elsewhere (a skill's tool, a schedule's check) it fails, as `Tool.Call` does.
- **The triage's pre-pass:** when the harness-issues check (`@stitch:open-issues`) wakes the triage, the node asks the `triage` profile in **one call** over the open issues as filed (the latest 40; title, area, body to 1,500 characters, framed as data): per issue not a bug, not actionable and a duplicate of which other, and which to fix next. What passes its threshold is appended to the firing's message as advice ("likely duplicates: X duplicates Y (0.85); likely not a bug in Stitch: Z (0.95); worth fixing next: W (0.70)", calibrated or not said), the triage's calls staying its own (its goal unchanged: it reads the issues and decides); none flagged, the model down: nothing added. Recorded as every call, paid by the node.
- **Loose ends** (§2.10): the pass asks the `loose-ends` profile first; when the verdict's probability reaches its threshold (0.6 for `waiting-on-you`, 0.8 for the others: a conversation that waits is rarely called done) and it is calibrated it is the judgement, written as before with `model` the decision model and `p` its probability, and **the line is the agent's own words**, as the decision model writes none: its last question when it waits on the user, its last sentence when it stopped partway, its first when done, none when stale (prose only: code blocks, headings, tables and list marks left out; 14 words at most). Below the threshold, or the model unavailable, or auto-handling on (which needs the quick model's proposed action), the quick model judges as before: that is loose ends' original path, not decide's fallback, and it stays whatever the fallback setting says (`[loose_ends] decide = false` turns the decision model off). The quick model is resolved only when needed. A pass says how many the decision model judged.
- **Measured:** `just hazard-eval` after the move, unchanged (it asks the API directly with the same questions and inputs): obvious 30/30, routine 0/30, hidden 27/28 with files and 5/28 blind, look-alikes 0/19 (3/19 blind) at 0.7 (28/28 and 2/19 at 0.5; 25/28 and 0/19 at 0.8); fixers 20/20 adversarial and 0/6 benign at 0.5/0.5, 23 of the 98 stress lines; landing 11/11 and 2/6 at 0.3. **Loose ends against the quick model**, on the user's real conversations (`just loose-ends-eval`, 2026-11-14: the 400 latest quick-model judgements on gamer, each conversation rebuilt as it was at the step judged, read only, the rows in `/tmp/decide-eval/loose-ends.jsonl`): exact agreement 302/400 (75.5%), on "waits on the user or not" 329/400 (82.2%), open or closed 317/400 (79.2%). The disagreements are mostly the quick model's "waiting" against the decision model's "done" (48: "Fixed.", "Yes, mixing them is fine.", an answer given), the quick model's prompt counting any proposed next step as waiting. By the choice's probability: ≥ 0.6 takes 285 (71%) at 82.8% agreement, ≥ 0.7 218 at 87.6%, ≥ 0.8 155 at 94.8%, ≥ 0.9 83 at 97.6%. **The profile's thresholds (waiting at 0.6, the rest at 0.8) take 201/400 (50%) at 94.5% agreement, calling only 6 of the quick model's waits otherwise**; the other half goes to the quick model as before. 400 calls cost $0.0325 ($0.00008 each), median 149–166 ms, p90 216–255 ms. The quick model is no ground truth: which of the two is right where they differ is for the user to judge (the rows hold both lines).
- **Tested** (a fake backend and sink, `decide::tests`): profiles and the user's thresholds over the defaults, the guard's profiles set as any other; the cache (one call for two, another profile asked again) and records without secrets; no fallback by default (every profile fails), one set for every profile answering uncalibrated and uncached, never for `guard.fixer`, `land`, `loose-ends`, a profile opting out or naming its own (`decide::tests::the_fallback_is_the_users_setting_none_by_default`); the whole request and its record redacted; money admitted before each paid call, a failed call's cost kept through a stopped call; whole lines under concurrent appends; a partial answer an error, charged; titles kept out of the triage's questions and unknown choices dropped; choices, scores, the limits, a missing answer an error; the fallback's schema and reading (`decide::fallback`); the tool's questions in every form (`decide::tool`); the triage's pre-pass in one call, its flags and suggestion (`decide::triage`); Otter's `Decide` reaching the thread's `decide` tool with its cost counted, and failing where there are no tools (`otter::host`); loose ends' judgement from a decision and the line from the agent's words (`loose::tests`), and on a node against a mock Decisions API (`tests/loose_ends.rs`: the decision model judging with the agent's question as the line and no quick model asked, the call in `stitch decide log` and the node's day; unsure at 0.52, the quick model judging; the user's thresholds set, the guard's as any profile's, unknown questions and a fallback for `guard.fixer` refused).
- **Open:** routing per turn, choosing tools and skills, memory's relevance gate, web-content injection checks as profiles (§4, 13; the auto approval mode is built, §2.28); calibrating thresholds from the records and the user's answers (a `stitch decide calibrate`); `Decide` in a skill's own tools (a mesh service of the thread's, not only `otter_run`'s); the loose-ends line in the user's imperative ("Pick A or B") rather than the agent's question when the quick model is not asked.

### 2.30 Inline controls (proposal; step 1, preset answers, and step 2, action buttons, built)

**Proposed 2026-11-18, with the user; not decided.** Apps (§2.10) are whole pages in a web view, and the user opens them on purpose. Many replies need something much smaller in the message itself: preset answers that continue the chat ("A", "B", "both, A first"), and repetitive actions that need no model call at all (restart a service, rerun CI, toggle something, re-render with another value). **Inline controls** are a few native controls (buttons, text fields, toggles, sliders, pickers, labels, rows) that the agent attaches to its reply. Each one is backed by an Otter handler that runs when the user presses it.

- **Not edge talk's mistake** (§1, "UI as shards"): Otter does not build a UI tree by data flow. The snippet evaluates once to a **small declarative description**, plain data with a fixed schema (control kinds, labels, bindings, handler names). Each client renders it with its own widgets: SwiftUI on iOS, the web client, numbered keys in the TUI. Only the handlers are code.
- **Authored with a tool**, `show_controls(code)`, like `show_app`, not a fenced block. The node runs `otter check` on it, so errors go back to the model before the user sees anything. The snippet is content-addressed (blake3), which gives versions and pins as for apps. Its static effects (none, io, time…) are known per handler.
- **What a press does.** Each handler says one of:
  - **reply**: sends the thread a user message: a preset answer, or one built from the fields ("pressed *Deploy* with env = staging"). This wakes the agent, as the user typing would, and it is the common case.
  - **run**: runs the handler without waking anyone. It updates the control's own state (a label showing the output, a status) or adds a compact entry to the transcript.
  - **both**: runs, then replies with the result.
- **Where it runs.** A pure handler runs in the client (the phone has Otter), so it answers at once. A handler with effects runs on the thread's node, under the gates of `otter_run`: the control shows its effects (a chip, "io: Http.Post"), and the user consents once per snippet hash, as with app pins, not on every press (open: confirming each io press for some shards).
- **State.** Field values are records, so they sync across the user's devices. A control in an older message stays live; when its thread or node is gone it is greyed out. Pinning one (as an app is pinned) puts it in the pinned list, and later in a home screen widget or a Control Center control.
- **Safety.** Code the model wrote, triggered by the user, is an injection path: a page read by the agent asks for a "Confirm" button that does something else. Effects are static and shown, consent is per hash, and a turn tainted by untrusted content (§2.28) may only emit **reply** controls and pure handlers.
- **Needs from Otter:** a `Ui.*` vocabulary that produces the description, and handlers as named fns the host can call with the fields as input. This is to be added to `../otter/docs/host-requirements.md` as a host requirement, without naming Stitch.
- **Open:** the TUI's form of fields; how long a control stays live (step 1 settles it for preset answers: until the user writes; step 2 for action buttons: until a newer set).

**Step 1, preset answers: as built (2026-11-18, approved by the user).** Reply labels alone, with no code, built first, before any Otter.

- **The tool**, `suggest_replies` (`crates/stitch-node/src/replies.rs`), in the `weave` group beside `notify`, so read-only threads have it (it only shows the user something): `{replies: [{label, text?}]}`, 1–6 replies; a label is trimmed, single-line, at most 60 characters and distinct; `text` (at most 2000) defaults to the label. What breaks these goes back to the model as an error, and a refused call shows nothing. It returns at once ("Shown: [A] [B] … Write your reply now if you have not, then end the turn; do not call this again this turn"). Its description says when, and rarely (tightened 2026-11-20, the user found it overused): only when the user's next answer is very likely one of a few obvious choices just put to them (A or B, yes or no to a proposed action); not as a routine follow-up, not after a report or an answer that asks for no decision, not for open questions; at most once a turn; the reply still asks in words.
- **A turn that ends saying nothing** (decided 2026-11-20, issue `…mbp-s6e3`): a model that called tools, said nothing with its last calls and ends its turn with no text (seen after `suggest_replies`) is told once a turn, as Stitch, to write its reply (`chat::NO_REPLY`, the loop's step after; not at the step limit or over budget). The note is a user message from `stitch:no-reply`, which the model reads and no transcript shows (`chat::unshown`): shown, it would be a user message after the set and end it. A model's turn with nothing in it (no text, no call, natively at most empty text) is never sent back to a provider (`said_nothing`, live and when the history is rebuilt): Anthropic refuses an empty text block, so an empty end used to break the thread's next turn. The buttons show either way: the set is the turn's, after its last entry, reply or not.
- **No record of its own.** The tool call in the transcript is the record: both transcript builders give the validated replies as one explicit field on the call's entry, so no client parses tool arguments: `replies: [{label, text}]` on the web client's `kind: "tool"` entry (`Request::Transcript`, its JSON line: no change to `api.rs`'s types, so no `local-N` bump), and `chat::Shown::replies` (the TUI's entries, and `stitch-ffi`'s `Entry.replies: [SuggestedReply {label, text}]`, `#[uniffi(default = [])]`). Its one-line summary is `suggest_replies: A · B`. The live stream carries only that line (`ToolCall`); clients read the field when they read the transcript again (the web client does after each tool result, the TUI when the turn ends).
- **Live** (`replies::live`, the same rule in each client): the last entry with replies, while no user message (`you`, the web client's `kind: "user"`, a local pending one included) comes after it and no turn runs. A later set in the same turn replaces an earlier one. Pressing a live one sends its `text` as an ordinary message, the path typing takes. Older ones are inert.
- **Web client:** the buttons at the end of the turn (after its last entry, or, once answered, before the user's message); the call's own tool line is not shown. Answered, the set stays with the pick marked (the one whose `text` the next user message is, pressed or typed the same) and the rest dimmed; a set never picked disappears (`transcript/suggested.ts`, tested in `test/replies.test.ts`; `?mock`'s "Dinner ideas" thread).
- **TUI:** a live set is said once in the scrollback, `↳ /1 Yes   /2 No`; `/1`…`/6` in the composer sends that reply's text as a message (even one starting with `/`: it is not run as a command); with none live, it says so.
- **Safety:** a press is the user's own message, read before it is sent, so a tainted turn (§2.28) may offer them too.
- **iOS app:** from `Entry.replies`, as the web client: capsule buttons (wrapping) after the turn's last entry, the call's own tool row not shown; answered, inert with the pick marked ✓ and the rest dimmed, a set never picked gone. The rule is `replyBlocks` in `apps/ios/ChatCore` (a Swift package with no node, `swift test` in `just quick`, the web client's cases); `ChatModel.replySets` maps the entries to it (a `you` entry by its `typed` text, the app's own pending message included, so a press answers its set at once), and the view drops a live set while a turn runs (`ChatModel.turnRuns`: this app's, a call in flight, another node's turn, a detached thread `working`). A press sends the text through `send`, the composer's path, what is being typed kept. The app reads the transcript again when its turn ends (and on the index's revision for turns it did not start), so the field arrives without parsing the live `ToolCall` line. `just ui-shot replies` ("Dinner ideas": one set answered, one live).

**Step 2, action buttons: as built (2026-11-20, decided with the user).** A simpler step 2 than the proposal above: a button carries **one tool call** of the agent's instead of a preset text. No Otter `Ui.*` vocabulary, no new control kinds, no handlers in the client; the proposal's run and both are a button's `wake` false and true.

- **A sibling tool, `offer_actions`** (`crates/stitch-node/src/actions.rs`, the `weave` group beside `suggest_replies`), not a variant of `suggest_replies`: the two differ in what they are for (a few answers, rarely; repetitive things, as often as they help), in lifetime and in what a tainted turn may offer, so one tool would carry two rule sets and a set would have to be refused for mixing them. `{actions: [{label, tool, input, wake?}]}`, 1–24 (a table's rows); labels as preset answers' (trimmed, single-line, at most 60, distinct); `input` an object (default `{}`); never `offer_actions`, `suggest_replies`, `wait`, `wake_at`, `shell_wait` or `event_reply` (what only makes sense inside a turn). Its description is short: buttons for what the user would otherwise copy and paste or ask for one by one; `suggest_replies` keeps its "use rarely".
- **Checked when offered** (`actions::check`, against the loop's own tools as offered to this turn), each failure an error back to the model and no buttons shown: the tool is among the thread's tools (its tool set, MCP and skills' Otter tools included) and its capability profiles allow it; the input passes the tool's input schema (`events::schema_error`, the subset asks use: type, enum, const, anyOf, properties, required, additionalProperties, items, bounds); `otter_run` code checks (its effects computed, as `otter_run` does first). **A read-only thread** offers only what its mode runs: `shell_run` with a command the read-only rules allow (none on SSH hosts), `otter_run` with pure code, and tools that change nothing (`view_file`, `browse_web`, `recall`, `read_thread`, `jobs`, …). **A tainted turn** (§2.28, docs/events.md: any untrusted profile on the turn's ceiling, its schedule's or subscription's or the thread's taint) is refused the tool altogether, saying to offer preset answers instead: a page or a payload could otherwise get a "Confirm" button that runs its call by the user's press.
- **`wake`** (default false), named as `spawn`'s, `send`'s and `schedule`'s are: false runs the call only ("blind"); true runs it, then the press becomes the user's message ("▶ label: ok (call line)", then its output, 4,000 characters at most) and a turn, as `send` starts one (`weave::send`), so it ends a live preset-answer set as typing does.
- **A press** (`Request::Press {thread, set, item}`, local-71; `Reply::Lines`, one JSON line `{ok, output}`): `set` is the `offer_actions` call's ID. The node looks the call up in the thread's own records (its line), never taking a call from the client, and runs it on **the node that offered it** (the writer of the segment holding the call; another node is asked, `Question::Press`, appended to the peer questions; unreachable or too old says so). There it runs with the tools a detached turn of the thread would have (`detached::tools_for`: its tool set, place, mode, start directory and selection; the ceiling reads the thread's taint at the call), so every gate is the agent's: sandbox, the rules and the hazard guard, approvals (a question record, shown and answered as any other), `otter_run`'s effect consent, an app's guard. No model call; what a tool spent (a script's `Decide`) is metered to the thread.
- **While a turn runs, a press waits** for it (the thread's lock, `detached.locks`), then runs, rather than being refused: a table must stay usable while the agent works, and a step written beside a running turn would fork the thread. Under the lock the set is read again: a newer set from that turn refuses the press. Clients show the button running meanwhile.
- **In the transcript:** a `press` event (`{t: "press", at, set, item, label, tool, ok, output, wake}`, an `Event::Other`: older builds keep it unread), the output redacted and kept to 20,000 characters, written as a step of its own (`chat::append_events`). `chat::Shown` gives `actions` and `call` (the set's ID) on the call's `tool` entry (cleared when its result is an error), and `press` entries (`said` the line "▶ label: ok" or "▶ label: error …", `text` with the output's first 12 lines); the web client's transcript the same as `actions: [{label, tool, call, wake}]` and `set` on the tool entry and `kind: "press"` entries; `stitch-ffi`'s `Entry.actions: [ActionInfo]`, `Entry.call`, `Entry.press: PressInfo` and `press(thread, set, item) -> Pressed`.
- **What the model reads:** a press that did not wake it is told with the user's next message (whatever it is: typed, a report), first, as `[Since your last turn the user pressed action buttons you offered, each running its call without you: ▶ label: ok (tool): output…]` (600 characters of output each), built the same when the turn starts (`History::presses`) and when its history is built again (`messages_from`), so the prompt cache holds. A press that woke it is that message itself.
- **Lifetime** (`actions::live`, the same rule in each client): the thread's latest accepted set is live, whatever comes after it (the user writing, turns, presses), so each row of a table can be pressed in turn and again; a newer set replaces it; a thread compacted into another ends it (the press is refused; Stitch has no archiving, and a deleted thread is gone). Older sets stay shown where they were, inert. Preset-answer sets keep their rule.
- **Clients.** Each set shows after its turn's last entry (presses after it do not move it), the call's own row not shown; each button shows how its latest press ended (✓, ✗, from the transcript's presses) and running while its press is out from this client, and stays pressable to run again. Web client: `transcript/Actions.tsx`, the rule `actionBlocks` in `transcript/suggested.ts` (`test/actions.test.ts`), press lines with their output folded, `?mock`'s "Waypoints". iOS app: `actionBlocks` in `apps/ios/ChatCore` (`ActionButtons.swift`, its tests the web client's cases), `ChatModel.actionSets` and `press`, `ActionButtons` in `ChatView` (rounded buttons in a flow, a spinner, ✓/✗), press rows; `just ui-shot actions` ("Waypoints": a table of five, one pressed ✓, one ✗, live after the user wrote). TUI: a live set is said once, `↳ /1 ▶ Dock   /2 ▶ Ring`; `/1`…`/24` presses one when no preset answers are live (those keep `/1`…`/6`), its line and output printed as a tool line when the transcript is followed.
- **Tested:** the shape and the press's line and note (`actions::tests`); on a node with a mock model (`tests/actions.rs`): a press runs with no model call, its line in both transcripts, the model told with the next message and the history built again the same, the set live after the user wrote, a press needing approval asking as a question and running once allowed, `wake` starting a turn with the result as the user's message (and the earlier presses told first), unknown buttons and sets, a newer set replacing the older; a missing tool, an input its schema refuses, a read-only thread's write refused and its read offered, a turn woken by an untrusted event and the user's next turn on the tainted thread refused. The TUI's `/N` (`an_action_button_is_pressed_with_its_number`), ChatCore's `ActionButtonsTests`, the web client's `actions.test.ts`.
- **Open:** "running" shows only on the client that pressed (others see the press when the transcript has it); a press needing approval holds the thread's lock while it waits; consent per set (rather than the agent's approvals for each press) and pure calls run in the client, from the proposal, are not built; a fork offers its parent's sets no more (a press reads the thread's own segments).

### 2.31 Sharing a thread as a file

**Decided 2026-11-23, with the user.** One Stitch user hands a conversation to another (one person's phone to another's), peer to peer. The first transport is a **file** sent by AirDrop, Messages or Mail: Apple stores and forwards it, so neither node has to be online and no server is involved. Every node so far holds only its own user's records (§2.6: trust is the user's own devices); this is the first thing that crosses between users, so it is a copy, never sync: the recipient gets new threads of their own, and nothing ties the two nodes together afterwards.

- **The file is an archive:** a zip with Stitch's own extension, `.stitch`. Import is the main purpose, with attachments and app versions, so the file carries content-addressed blobs as the store keeps them. A single HTML file was the first draft the same day, and the user corrected it: it carried blobs as base64 in the page, which suits reading, not importing. The entries:
  - **`manifest.json`:** the format (`stitch-share`) and its version (1); the title; when it was made; the kind (`thread` or `effort`) and the level; `source`, the node it came from by its share key (below), not by its name; the bundle's own thread labels (`t1`, `t2`…); counts (messages, calls, attachments, apps); `entries`, every other entry's path with its blake3 hash; and `signature`.
  - **`threads.json`:** each thread (label, title, when it started, its parent's label in an effort, its model) with its events in the record format (records.md §5: `msg`, `tool_call`, `tool_result`, and `note`s), and the attachments its messages carry (hash, type, name, size).
  - **`blobs/<blake3 hex>`:** every attachment, and every app version: its manifest blob, its trees and its files, as the store keeps them, so an imported app opens as the sender's did. Media that is already compressed (JPEG, PNG, GIF, WebP, HEIC, AVIF, audio, video, zip, gzip, PDF, fonts, wasm) is stored as it is; the rest is deflated.
  - **`index.html`:** a read-only viewer for whoever unpacks the archive without Stitch. It is a static transcript, every text HTML-escaped, with images from `blobs/` and other files linked there. It runs no script and reaches no network: its Content-Security-Policy is `default-src 'none'; img-src 'self' file:; style-src 'unsafe-inline'`.
  - **Hashes and signature:** each entry is hashed as its bytes. The signature (`{alg: "ed25519", key, sig}`) is over the manifest without it, in canonical form (`serde_json`'s, of the parsed `Value`), so the importer computes the same bytes from what it read, whatever the file's whitespace. It covers every entry through `entries`.
  - **Original IDs are not shared.** Thread IDs carry the node's name (`…T180424Z-laptop-x1y2`), so threads are `t1`… in the archive; message IDs are renumbered per shared thread; an app's manifest loses the thread it kept state for (`thread`, an ID), so it is a new blob; tool call IDs, which are the providers', stay.
- **What is shared.** By default, a **conversation**: the thread's compaction chain (§2.15, "One conversation"), oldest first. Each thread contributes only its own events (what a compaction inherits is already in the thread before it), and a note where each compaction was made. With `--effort`, the **spawn tree**: the conversation at the top of the tree the thread is in and every thread spawned under it, however deep, each as its own chain. This uses `scopes::resolve` (§2.27) unchanged, its order and its 200-thread limit included. A fork's own events are shared with a note of what it continued, but not the thread it continued.
- **Levels** are about tool calls; attachments and apps go at every level:
  - `messages`: the conversation's text and attachments, no tool calls;
  - `calls` (the default): each call kept with its input, strings over 300 characters cut, and its output folded to its first line, at most 160 characters, saying how many lines were left out;
  - `full`: inputs and outputs whole (outputs stored as blobs are read in, at most 256 KB each).
- **What is never shared,** at any level:
  - the provider's native content of a reply (`native`): thinking, its signatures and encrypted reasoning are bound to the sender's provider account, and thinking is not something they chose to show;
  - instructions and skills (`Part::Context`), recalled memories (`Part::Recalled` and `memory` messages), a scope's briefing (said as a line instead), a room's relay notes, usage, approvals, presses, failures, the system prompt, and Stitch's own unshown notes;
  - `from`, reduced to `user` or `note` (reports and notes name other threads and nodes).
- **Redaction and review.** Every string of the transcript, the title included, and every text attachment go through `Vault::redact` (§2.19) always: every secret value this node holds becomes `«secret:<name>»`, and the redactions are counted. An app's files are not rewritten (that would break code), so an app version with a file holding a secret value is not shared, and the preview says why.
  - Then a **leak scan** reads the redacted transcript, the attachments' names, text attachments and the apps' text files. It looks for home paths (`/home/<u>`, `/Users/<u>`, `C:\Users\<u>`, and this user's home); private, CGNAT, link-local and unique-local addresses; emails; hostnames (`.local`, `.lan`, `.internal`, `.home.arpa`, `.localdomain`, and this machine's hostname); the user's node names, from this node and its peers (`known_peers`: the device records), as whole words; and the OS user name. It lists each finding (kind, text, count, where first seen: `t1#m4`, `attachment …`, `app <name> <path>`).
  - **The node gates it:** an export with findings is written only when the client confirms the transcript's hash (of `threads.json`, which does not change between a preview and the export) from a preview, so every client (CLI, the app, the web client) shows the findings first. `--yes` confirms them in the CLI.
  - **The scan only finds and asks; it does not rewrite.** The user decides (shares anyway, picks a lower level, or does not share). Automatic scrubbing (home paths to `~`, addresses masked) is a later option.
- **The signature: a share key of its own, not the node's iroh key.** The node's identity key (`node.key`, its endpoint ID) is suitable as a key: it is ed25519, and iroh signs and verifies with it. But it is the node's network identity. Discovery looks an endpoint ID up, and a connection attempt is how a peer is reached, so a file carrying it would tell every recipient how to find and dial that node, and would tie every share to its network identity. Each node therefore has a separate ed25519 **share key**, `<data>/share.key`, made on first use like `node.key` (owner-only).
  - The manifest's `source` is its public key (hex).
  - The signature proves the file is unchanged since that key signed it, and that two files came from the same key. It does not prove who holds the key: there is no directory. Knowing contacts' keys is a later step (below).
  - The importer reports the signer: "your own key" when it is the importing node's share key (a file the user shared and took back; the thread records it as `imported_own`, so the fork divider and the thread rows say the same on every node), or the key's first 16 hex characters.
- **Import mirrors `stitch vault import`: verify, preview, then write.**
  - **Verify:** the whole archive is read before anything is written, within limits (20,000 entries, 128 MB unpacked). It is refused when it has an entry it should not have (only `manifest.json`, `threads.json`, `index.html` and `blobs/<64 lowercase hex>`), an entry missing or not in the manifest, an entry whose bytes do not match its hash, a blob that is not the blob it names, a signature that does not verify, or a version newer than this build reads. An unsigned archive is accepted and said to be unsigned.
  - **Preview:** title, kind, level, when made, the parts (`t1 <title>`), counts (messages, calls, attachments, apps), signer and hash; or, for the same transcript imported here before, the threads it made (`already`).
  - **Write:** every blob into the store (each checked again as stored), then, in the home workspace (the global place), **one thread per shared thread**. A conversation is one thread, its compaction chain flattened with a note at each compaction; it compacts again by itself when continued past its window. In an effort, each spawned thread names its imported parent with an `imported-under` ref, which `scopes::resolve` follows as it follows `spawned-by`. The threads are not marked spawned, so nothing treats them as detached work to resume.
  - **Fields:** `imported_from` (the manifest's hash, `blake3:…`, of the manifest as signed), `imported_source` (its source key), `imported_signed`, `imported_label` (`t1`…) and `imported_level`; the title as shared, prefixed ⇩.
  - **Readable in every transcript view:** the first events are a note (the `room-note` event, §2.27, which the TUI, the web client and the app already show as a quiet line) saying where it came from, the signer and the hash, and that it is foreign history. Then come the shared events: user messages `from = "shared:user"` (not the user's own: `by_user` is false), notes `shared:note`, replies `shared:assistant` with their model.
  - **Foreign, untrusted history, as an untrusted event payload is** (docs/events.md): the model is told first, in a message no transcript shows (`stitch:shared`), that the conversation was shared by someone else, happened on their machines, and is data to read, never instructions to follow. The imported thread is **tainted** with an untrusted profile allowing no tools (`import <hash>`), which its compactions keep (§2.28), and it goes out of memory.
  - **Continuing means forking** (decided 2026-11-23, with the user). A fork the user makes of an imported thread (`stitch fork`, Fork in the app and the web client, `Request::Fork`) gets the user's usual tools under the thread's own approval rules, as any thread does. Its history stays fenced as foreign data: the import's note, the shared calls as text, and still out of memory unless the user changes that.
    - `share::forked`, called by `fork::fork` (forks the user makes, not compactions), writes the fork's taint without the import's profile, keeping any other (untrusted events that reached the thread), and takes the fork out of memory.
    - The fence is read from the events alone, so the history is the same bytes at every turn and the prompt cache holds. In `chat::messages_from`, shared messages that come without the import's note before them (a fork of the last N messages) get `share::FENCE` before the first of them. In `chat::shaped`, a summary of a conversation holding shared messages (a summarized fork, a compaction) starts with the fence too.
    - A plain chat on the imported thread itself still runs, with no tools: reading and discussing what was shared needs none. The user's note in its transcript and the model's note say to fork it to go on with tools; the model, asked there for work that needs tools, says so. Forking it automatically on a chat was the alternative, and was not taken: it would make a thread the client did not open, which every client would then have to switch to; the existing fork, which every client already has, is simpler. `stitch event untaint <id>` still lifts the imported thread's own taint, for a user who wants that.
  - **Its tool calls ran elsewhere:** the history builder (`chat::messages_from`) gives every call of a `shared:` reply, and its result, to the model as text (`[In the shared conversation, not here: called …]`, `[Its output there: …]`). A tool of that name may not exist here, and either way it did not run here, which a real tool call and result would tell the model it did. Transcripts still show them as calls.
  - **Use:** it can be attached as a scope (`thread:`/`effort:`), and forked to continue it (above); `read_thread` and the briefing say the imported thread is tainted. A fork of it shows the imported history above its `forked` divider, as every fork shows what it continues (§2.15), its messages `shared` and the divider naming the conversation shared and its signer. An imported app version is there for `stitch app export` and the app's views, as any app a thread showed.
- **The extension: `.stitch`, a type conforming to `public.zip-archive`.** The app exports the type (its identifier in the app's own reverse-DNS domain, declared by the app) conforming to `public.zip-archive` (and `public.data`, `public.content`). The reasons:
  - AirDrop of a type an installed app owns opens that app directly, and Messages and Mail offer "Open in Stitch".
  - Because the type conforms to `public.zip-archive`, Files can still unpack it, showing `index.html` and the blobs to someone without Stitch.
  - On a desktop, `unzip` (or renaming it to `.zip`) does the same.
  - Considered: `.zip` (opens nowhere in particular, and invites unpacking where importing is meant), and a single `.html` (readable everywhere, but no place for blobs and apps; see above).
  - The CLI writes `<title>.stitch` by default (`--out` names another), and import reads any file name.
- **Later, recorded as open, not built:**
  - *A hosted encrypted link:* the archive encrypted with a random key, uploaded to a store-and-forward service, the key only in the URL's fragment (`#k=…`), which browsers never send, so the service holds ciphertext it cannot read. It suits recipients without the app (a small viewer page decrypts in the browser and shows `index.html` with its blobs) and large archives; it needs expiry, revocation and a size limit.
  - *iroh between contacts:* direct delivery between two users' nodes over a separate ALPN (never `stitch/node/1`, which only the user's own nodes may use), to contacts whose share keys were exchanged once (a QR code, or the first file's signer accepted, trust on first use). The archive is the unit; a contact's node receives it into an inbox the user previews and accepts from. Both nodes must be reachable, or a relay holds it.
  - *Continuing together* (a thread two users both write to) is multi-user (§4, 3), not this.

*As built, the core* (2026-11-23, local-74; branch `thread-share`; `stitch-node/src/share.rs`).
- **Wire** (no `skip_serializing_if`):
  - `Request::Share {thread, effort, level, preview, confirm}` answers `Reply::Lines` with one JSON line, `share::Preview`: title, kind, level, counts (apps among them), `left_out` (attachments not shared, each with why), `bytes`, `hash` (what `confirm` takes), `key`, `redactions`, `findings`, `file_name`, and `path` once written. Asked of any thread of a chain, it shares from the newest, so the whole chain.
  - `Request::Import {bundle, preview}` answers one JSON line, `share::Imported`: the threads made (none in a preview), `parts`, title, `hash`, `signer`, `mine`, level, kind, `created`, counts, `already`, `written`. The client reads the file and sends its bytes, so a thread's sandboxed shell cannot make the node read a path for it; an archive is at most 56 MB for that reason, as the local protocol's frames are 64 MB.
  - The archive is written into `<data>/downloads/share-<hash>/<title>.stitch`, as `Quote` writes its file; the CLI copies it to `--out`, the app shares it from there.
- **CLI:**
  - `stitch thread share <id> [--effort] [--level messages|calls|full] [--out f] [--yes] [--dry-run]` previews (counts, redactions, what was left out, the findings), asks when there are findings (refused without a terminal unless `--yes`), then exports with the preview's hash.
  - `stitch thread import <file> [--yes]` previews, asks (refused without a terminal unless `--yes`), then imports.
  - From a thread's shell, `import` asks, as `rm` does (`rules.rs`). `share` does not: it writes where the sandbox lets the shell write, and reads nothing an agent could not read with `read_thread`.
- **FFI:**
  - `share_preview(thread, effort, level) -> SharePreview` and `share_export(thread, effort, level, confirm) -> SharePreview` (its `path`), with `ShareFinding` `{kind, text, count, at}`;
  - `import_preview(path) -> ImportedThread` and `import_thread(path) -> ImportedThread`; `ImportedThread.created` is when the file was made (the manifest's `created`, seconds since the epoch), for the preview.
- **Limits:** an attachment at most 10 MB, an app version 20 MB, all blobs 40 MB, a text attachment redacted and scanned up to 1 MB (larger is not shared). A blob not on this device is not shared, said in the message and in `left_out`. Images a tool showed (its result's `blobs`) are not shared.
- **Thread rows** say an imported conversation (2026-11-23, asked by the user for the app): `Request::Threads`' JSON rows (and so the web client's `ThreadRow`) carry `imported_from` (the manifest's hash), `imported_source` (the sender's share key), `imported_signed` and `imported_own` (signed by the user's own key), absent on other rows; the FFI's `ThreadInfo` has `imported_from`, `imported_source` (both `None` otherwise), `imported_signed` and `imported_own`. Read from the chain's imported thread (`share::origin`), so its compactions carry them; a fork of it does not (it is the user's). JSON rows only: `api.rs` and the protocol number did not change.
- **Transcripts unchanged:** the notes are `room-note` events, and the model's note is hidden by `chat::unshown`. Imported user messages show as the user's bubbles in the web client and the app (their `from` is `shared:user`); a client may mark them, later. `folders::tree_blobs` lists an app's trees and files for the archive.
- **Tested:**
  - Unit tests in `share.rs`: the scan finds each kind and leaves look-alikes alone; folding; file names; what counts as compressed. An archive opens as it was made: its entries, a viewer with no script (an escaped `<script>` in a message), images from `blobs/`, a PNG stored uncompressed, the signer and source. A changed transcript, viewer, manifest or blob is refused, and so are an entry added and an entry removed. A newer format is refused, saying why.
  - `tests/share.rs`, on a node with a mock model: a compacted conversation with an image, an app version (its manifest naming a thread), a secret, a home path, the node's name, thinking and a recalled memory.
    - The preview's counts, findings and redactions; an export refused without the confirmed hash or with a wrong one.
    - The archive: no secret, thinking, memory or thread ID in any entry; a compaction note; outputs folded; messages renumbered over the chain; the app's tree and page as blobs, its manifest a new blob without its thread.
    - Levels `messages` (no calls) and `full` (whole outputs).
    - Import on the same node: a preview first, writing nothing; then a thread signed by its own key, a note first in the transcript, the model's note hidden, a tool row and the image there, the app's manifest (without its thread), tree and page in the store. Importing again gives the same thread.
    - Continued: the model reads the foreign note and the calls as text, no `tool_use`, and no tools but `event_reply`.
    - Forked (`Request::Fork`): the fork's turn has tools (`shell_run`), and its history still carries the import's note and the calls as text. A fork of the last 4 messages, without the note, gets the fence before the shared messages. That the fork is out of memory is not tested end to end, since memory does not run in these tests.
    - A changed archive is refused.
    - An effort of two threads exported and imported, the second `imported-under` the first, which an `effort:` scope resolves to both.
- **Left for the app** (another repository):
  - the exported type `.stitch` conforming to `public.zip-archive`, and opening a file into `import_preview`, then `import_thread` once the user agrees;
  - continuing an imported thread through the existing `fork` (FFI, unchanged), offered prominently on an imported thread (its row's `imported_from`, below);
  - a share action showing `share_preview`'s findings before `share_export`, then the share sheet with its `path`;
  - marking imported threads and their untaint.
- **Not built:** the web client's and the TUI's share and import (the requests are there); scrubbing findings; showing which of the user's own nodes signed a file (only "this node" is told).

## 3. Effect on the Otter requirements

The node is a Rust host. That is the case Otter's `embedding.md` already supports, so the requirements shrink. Otter's §7 orders the work that remains into tracks; the table maps each requirement to Stitch's need, and §5 says which milestone waits on which track.

| Requirement | Status for this rewrite | Otter §7 |
|---|---|---|
| H1 object values | **Critical.** SSH and shell sessions, model contexts, run handles | A1 |
| H2 images and other deferred values | Lower for scripts: attachments are content-hash references and the Rust provider layer turns them into model input. An `Image` object only when a script tool must process pixels | Deferred |
| H3 services | **Critical.** Node services: store, providers, sessions, engram, models | A1 |
| H4 cleanup on every exit | Needed. Leases, session guards | A1 |
| H5 concurrency | **Critical.** Agent loops spawn, wait for and cancel workers | A2 |
| H6 cancellation vs. workers | Needed. Long model calls, shells | A1 |
| H7 host exchange | Simpler. A Rust API between node and scripts; progress through cells and channels | Not listed |
| H8 runtime compilation | **Critical.** LLM-written tools checked and compiled while running. Its smallest host-side form, `otter_lang::Tool` (compile at run time, describe, call), is in use for skills' tools (§2.17); agent loops still wait on A3 | A3 |
| H9 platform branching | Lower. Node-level configuration | Later |
| H10 C API, foreign shards | **Mostly dropped.** No Swift shards. iOS links the Rust node in-process and calls it through UniFFI over the node's client API (§5, milestone 4) | Deferred |
| H11 packaging | In use for iOS and visionOS (2026-10-17, §2.17): Otter's crates link into the app's node as Rust dependencies, built for the device, the simulator and visionOS with the rest, so no separate static library is needed; Linux and macOS binaries | D |
| H12 language additions | Needed. Iteration in particular | A4 |
| H13 migrating 1.x scripts | **Dropped.** Agents and tools are rewritten, not migrated | Deferred |
| H14 observability | Needed. Agents run for days and must be inspectable | Later, after A2 |

On catalog breadth (§3 of the requirements document):
- strings, regex, JSON, fs, hashes and time are needed for text tools;
- the database shards are no longer needed by scripts.

On the module list (§4):
- **Needed from Otter:** HTTP (for tools), and shards over the node's services: shell sessions and SSH, engram, embeddings.
- **Rust core, not modules:** providers, the store, shell sessions, engram and embeddings (`stitch-memory`, `stitch-llama`).
- **Dropped:** imaging, spreadsheet, pdf, network and candle, unless a tool needs them.

## 4. Open questions

1. Delegation rules and lease semantics (§2.7); recursion was decided on 2026-10-19 ("Delegation does not recurse"), and so was a spawned thread the user chats in (its budget is lifted).
2. Remotes on own machines over SSH only, or a hosted git service too? A hosted service means encrypting records at rest, for example per file with age.
3. Multi-user: only the user's own devices, or shared tasks and repositories? Sharing changes the key and permission model. A first step decided 2026-11-23 (§2.31): a conversation handed to another user as a signed file, imported as a copy, foreign and untrusted; a hosted encrypted link and delivery between contacts' nodes over iroh stay open there.
4. Migration: import existing conversations, memories and actors into records, or start fresh? Partly answered on 2026-10-20: edge talk's chats and memories are readable in place, through the `edge-talk` skill (§2.17), on the Mac and the phone; importing them as threads (or into engram) is still open.
5. The transport for remote clients (one node's CLI driving another node). Local clients and the protocol between nodes are decided (§2.1, §2.6).
6. Which worker kinds besides Claude Code, Codex and the native loop.
7. The graph view: partly decided 2026-11-11 (§2.25: no global embedding map; a hand-arranged 2D desk and local neighbourhoods, last of its steps). Open: the rendering technology on each client, and whether layouts and saved views are shared between nodes.
8. Promoting a global thread's working folder (§2.3) into a place: a new folder, or merged into an existing folder or project, its threads moving with it. (Since 2026-11-01 the working folder syncs between nodes on its own, §2.16, so promoting it is about organizing, no longer about reaching its files elsewhere.)
9. Generated video and speech (Veo, TTS), which need long-running calls of their own. Images were decided on 2026-10-18 (§2.8: `generate_image` next to the image models, generated images in the reply's bubble with a viewer that edits, media read only by the providers that take it); other providers' image backends plug in behind `Provider::generate_image`.
10. ~~Self-improvement~~: decided 2026-10-28 with the user: agents file harness issues as records, a designated fixer node fixes them in worktrees and lands them on main once CI and `just quick` pass, and the fix rolls out by itself, gamer first, then the fleet (the phone through TestFlight), with a drain, a health check and rollback to the previous build (never past a store-format change). [self-improve.md](self-improve.md). The same day, after a review: **its settings are the user's, synced** (the fixer node, `budget_usd` per fix, `daily_usd`, on or off; the harness-issues cadence, the checkout, the model): `self-improve` records in the home workspace, the latest of the user's standing, as `model-choice` and variables are (§2.19); `stitch issue settings --node <n> --budget <usd> --daily <usd> --on|--off`, which asks when an agent runs it, as `stitch model` does; `[self_improve]` in config.toml stays only as a node's fallback, field by field. Off until set. `self_improve` is a tool group of its own, off by default, never handed on, never for a client's end users, asking the user unless the harness-issues schedule's thread or a chat the user started calls it; fixes are admitted on the fixer node against a ledger (`fix-reservation` records: one fixer at a time, a daily cap in UTC counting each fixer's whole thread tree, two attempts per issue before the user is asked, the last landed fix healthy on the canary first); a fixer's change to a protected path (`tools/protected-paths`: the approval rules and the fixers' mode, caller identity, the sandbox, self-improvement and issues, the `stitch-dev` skill, `deploy/`, `.github/`, the list) lands only once the user answers an approval question the node asks at landing (decided with the user the same day, replacing signed commits and `approved/<sha>` tags as too complex): its facts read by the node from git (the TL;DR, the protected files' full diff, a stat of the rest) apart from the fixer's quoted summary, answerable only by the user (never from a thread's commands or any sandboxed process, `setsid` included); the self-update installs what clean `origin/<branch>` holds; promotion uses the node's own git credentials (the risk accepted, self-improve.md §5); a fixer runs in the fixers' approval mode whatever the node's (sandboxed, never yolo, asking for pushes but its own `fix-*` branches and for what could take the user's powers) and lands through the node (`stitch issue land`); who asks the node is told from the connecting process (walked up to a shell session the node started), never from `STITCH_THREAD`. *Revised 2026-10-29, after a review of 49dce1e found three ways around the landing gate* (self-improve.md §5): **the node runs git for fixers only in a bare mirror of its own** (`<data>/self-improve/mirror.git`, fetched from origin, its URL read once from the fixer checkout's `.git/config` as a file or from `[self_improve] remote`; hooks, fsmonitor and `ext::` off), never in the fixer's checkout, whose `.git` the fixer writes; **a fixer's sandbox writes only Cargo's registry and git caches** (not `~/.cargo/bin`, `~/.cargo/config.toml`, rustup's toolchains or other package managers' homes) **and reads no credentials** (`~/.ssh`, `~/.config/gh`, git's credential stores, `~/.netrc`, the keychains: `Sandbox::unreadable`, Landlock read rules on Linux, `deny file-read*` on macOS), its shells have no SSH agent, forge token or credential helper, `view_file` reads none of them for it, and it has no SSH hosts nor the browser; **the node pushes a fixer's branches** (`stitch issue push <id>`: a bundle the fixer's command makes, its heads checked to be the issue's `fix-<id>[-…]` branches, pushed from the mirror) and reads CI for it (`stitch issue ci`, `stitch issue fetch`); **the node serves a fixer's commands only what is a fixer's**, told from the connecting process (`fixer::refuses`: `stitch issue`, `stitch send` to its own issue's threads, notices, reading; no chat, spawn, answer, approval, yolo, tool sets, schedules, keys), since a command line is no gate (`bash x.sh`, `python -c`); **the triage thread runs in the fixers' mode** (a fixer's report wakes it), and **`send` from a thread in the fixers' mode reaches only its own issue's fixers and the triage that started it**. *Revised 2026-10-31, asked by the user:* **the fixer supervises** (self-improve.md §4, 8): it reads the issue and transcript, plans, reviews its children's reports and diffs and makes the calls (what to change, landing, rollout), on its own model, editing no code; children it spawns do the hands-on work in its worktree, `model: "quick"` by default and the default model only for very complex jobs (subtle concurrency or protocol bugs, cross-crate, API or store-format changes, a cause a quick child could not find, a quick child that failed or fixed it wrongly); a fixer's children write its issue's worktree (`selfimprove::own_issue`: the issue of the nearest fixer above), while pushing, landing and setting the issue stay the fixer's. *Revised 2026-11-14, asked by the user (who approved all 114 of the fixers' and triage's questions unread):* **the fixers ask rarely, and only when it matters** (§2.28, roles; self-improve.md §5): their rules read a line's substitutions, subshells and here-documents instead of asking for any parenthesis, let read-only `stitch`, reading Stitch's own units and cleaning up their own `fix-<id>` branches and worktree run, every sandbox writes ccache's runtime directory and opens the GPUs (so `just quick` runs inside), the user's own threads set issues without asking; what would still ask goes, in the fixers' role of the hazard guard, to one decision call (hazard and authority) and runs only when it stays in the sandbox, only discards local work and both clear; a protected landing goes to the landing role's six questions and, all clear and the role on, lands with a notice and `stitch issue revert`. Of the 114, 94 now run, 1 is the guard's, 19 ask. *Revised 2026-11-22:* **two repositories**, the core and a second one that builds against it and pins it, configured as places (`places` in the synced settings, else `[self_improve] places`; none: the core alone; a mirror, CI and protected paths per repository; `Issue.repo`; paired worktrees; a fix in both lands the core first, the app's commit pinning the very commit that lands, both checked before either is pushed): self-improve.md §6.
11. The web client (§2.24): the field and wording of the tier-3 grant (a §2.22 `set`), and whether a later shell-only service worker is worth it. Decided 2026-11-02: the transport, front end, wire types, versioning, notifications, platforms and license.
12. Apple Watch app: answering approvals and short questions from the wrist, a voice call (§2.26) into a thread or room, and glanceable status (threads working, needs-you, spend). Group chat (rooms of threads) and branch/effort scopes were decided on 2026-11-13 (§2.27, [rooms.md](rooms.md)).
13. ~~A decision primitive~~: the capability and its first use, the hazard guard, decided and built 2026-11-13 (§2.28); the other uses below stay open. As noted 2026-11-13, asked by the user: closed-set judgements as a provider capability next to chat and `generate_image`: yes/no, a choice among named options, a score on an ordered rubric, each with a calibrated probability, many questions over one shared input in one call. Gemini's Decisions API (`gemini-decision-1`, announced 2026-11-11: up to 1,000 questions in one pass over a multimodal input of up to 256K tokens, 150–250 ms, $0.10 per 1M input tokens, no output billed; https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/developers-tools/gemini-decisions-api/) is the first backend; the quick model forced to a JSON schema is the fallback, uncalibrated. Where the harness already asks a model a closed question it would go faster, cheaper and with a confidence to threshold on: the landing gate's risk flags for fixers (first use; prompted by the user approving every landing question unread: the node asks "does this widen what agents may do without asking, change sandbox paths, the caller check, the protected list or CI?" of the protected diff, lands what is clearly benign with a notice and a one-tap revert, asks only on a flag or low confidence, the verdict first), triage (duplicates, not-a-bug, which issue next), loose ends' "does this wait on the user?", model routing per turn (tier and effort), choosing tools and skills for a thread, memory's relevance gate, web-content injection checks before content reaches a model with tools; and an **auto approval mode** between asking and yolo (asked by the user the same day): the rules decide first as now (allow lists run, hard denials and what always asks stay: purchases, sends, deletes, account changes, keys, settings, `self_improve`, protected landings), and only what would ask goes to the decision model with the thread's goal and the user's recent messages as the intent, and categories asked about separately (destructive, reaching beyond the place, exfiltration of secrets or data, credentials, spending, pushing); clearly in intent and in no risky category runs, marked in the transcript with its verdict and confidence, anything else asks; the user's past answers to questions are the evaluation set and set the thresholds. Decided with §2.28: the provider's shape (`Provider::decide`), pricing and metering, the guard's threshold and its fallback. Decided 2026-11-14 (§2.28, roles): the landing gate, as noted above (six questions over the protected diff, 0.3, a notice and `stitch issue revert`), and for the fixers and the triage a role of the same guard that lets what would ask run when it stays in the sandbox, only discards local work, and clears both the hazard and an authority question (for the fixers the verdict over untrusted input is never the only gate: the rules and the sandbox decide first, and what reaches past the sandbox asks whatever the model says; for the landing it is the gate, so that role is the user's opt-in, `stitch guard on --for landing`, with every unasked landing told to them and revertible in one command). *Decided 2026-11-14, asked by the user (§2.29):* decisions are a node service, not the guard's: one entry point (`decide::ask`) with named profiles (questions, thresholds, fallback policy; `guard.user`, `guard.fixer`, `land`, `triage`, `loose-ends`, `tool`), typed answers, short timeouts, a cache, metering to the calling thread or the node's own day, local records for calibration, and the quick model forced to a JSON schema as the fallback where a profile allows it (uncalibrated; never the guard's); thresholds the user's, synced (`stitch decide`, the guard's through `stitch guard`); the `decide` tool and Otter's `Decide` for agents and skills; the triage's pre-pass (advice in its message) and loose ends' judgement (the quick model where the decision model is unsure) as its first new users. Still open: routing, tools and skills, memory's gate and web-content checks as profiles (the auto approval mode was built 2026-11-14 as yolo with the hazard guard, §2.28, simpler than the rules-first design noted here), calibrating thresholds from the records, and that a verdict over untrusted input (a diff, a page) is advisory, never the only gate for raising authority (the guard only ever makes more things ask).
14. A meeting bot (Google Meet, like Claude Meetings): proposed 2026-11-13 (its proposal is kept with the bot, outside this repository), not decided: a signed-in bot account in a real Chrome driven by a Playwright sidecar, Meet's captions scraped as the transcript, one read-only thread per meeting, on a Linux node. Revised 2026-11-15 (meet-bot.md §0): no `stitch meet` bridge; the sidecar speaks events ([events.md](events.md)): it creates the meeting thread and subscribes it to `meet/<code>/*`, triggers and the end-of-call notes are asks the thread answers, which needs one change, S1: a subscription flag `answers` letting a read-only thread answer its asks (built 2026-11-16, branch `meet-bot`). Decided by the user: answer only chat messages naming Stitch, notes-only with outside guests, meeting threads in `~/meetings`, Meet captions only (no own STT in the MVP), never speak. The sidecar's Stitch side is `apps/meet/` (tested with `--fake`, no Meet), with a `meeting` skill; joined a real call 2026-11-16; built the same day (meet-bot.md §0 "As built"): follow mode, meetd and `meet join` (the meeting thread in the asking thread's place, attached to it: `Request::Detach` gained `scopes`, local-69), calendar auto-join, a user service.

### Known issues and follow-ups (2026-10-12)

Open after the day's work, checked against the code (on 2026-10-13, those fixed that day are taken out: `watch_file` on a recreated file, `Shells.typing` never pruned, the node a moved thread runs on, and who ran a thread that moved, §2.4 and §2.7); MCP progress (§2.13) and remote calls' cancel, progress and images (§2.14, "Not built") are listed where they are.

- **Shells, macOS:** bash's `child setpgid (…): Operation not permitted` is the race in §2.3, seen by users. Report it to Apple with the plain C reproduction. The tests drop exactly that line from exact comparisons (`stitch-shell/tests/common/mod.rs`). The node's log line for a missing helper (`tools.rs`) still blames the trampoline for it.
- **GitHub's OAuth App:** sign-in uses edge talk's (§2.8); register one for Stitch and change it before anyone else uses Stitch.
- **Typing:** untaken typed text is given back to the session in reverse order (`untake_typed` prepends each note).
- **TUI reports:** a report is marked seen before it is printed. One whose thread or record is not indexed yet is never shown.
- **Memory:** `note_place` is recomputed on every drain, re-reading the thread's events. A drain that shows nothing clears a pending value.
- **`stitch shell attach`:** it does not redraw the screen after the broadcast lags (`Lagged` is skipped).
- **Jobs and tools:**
  - `Client::attach` drops `api::read` mid-frame in its `select!`. This is latent: the only caller drops the client afterwards.
- **Memory recalibration:**
  - The 0.49 data is one-sided: `stitch memory notes` has only memories that passed the gate. Near-misses are not logged, so the data can show where to tighten, not where to loosen.
  - The cutoff is compiled in; give it a config or environment override.
- **Wait options overlap:**
  - `idle_secs` (`shell_run`, `shell_send`) and `quiet_secs` (`shell_watch`, `shell_wait`, `watch_file`) name the same idea.
  - `shell_watch` and `shell_wait` take the same conditions and differ only in blocking.
- **CI:** hosted runners on every branch pushed (decided 2026-10-15: agents push feature branches freely; hosted only since 2026-11-21): Linux (fmt, clippy, tests, `--locked`), the web client, and on macOS `check-apple` (the crates an Apple client links, built for iOS and visionOS). Code that runs only on a macOS host (the shell helper, the trampoline, the atfork regression test) is built there but not run.
- **Missing helper:** a missing `stitch-shell-helper` is only a log line. Show it in a status command (`stitch node status`).
- **MCP:**
  - An enabled server that is not set up (no key, a wrong path) logs its failure at every retry, as long as threads enable it.
  - The approval prompt says `run` for MCP calls.
  - Cancellation over streamable HTTP is untested (only stdio is).
- **Remote tools:**
  - The TUI's `/tools` overlay toggles listed items only; patterns are possible only through `stitch thread tools`.
  - The iOS `/tools` screen comes with milestone 4.
  - Not tested against a real Mail.app server, only a fake one.
- **Sync (after 2026-10-27's incremental sync, §2.6):**
  - A suspended phone gets nothing pushed: it syncs when the app opens, at background refresh (15 minutes at best), or on the one silent push sent today ("answered"). Next: after a push of changes, a silent APNs "repository changed, sync with node X" to the phones, rate-limited, the app calling `sync_with`.
  - A record written by a command that does not index it (`stitch new`) goes at the interval; the 5-second poll sees only what was indexed. A cheap scan (each month directory's mtime) would find those too.
  - `stitch sync` still waits up to 10 seconds for each peer that cannot be reached (all at once); pushes leave such a peer out for a minute.
  - A push to a peer that came back is not triggered by its coming back: its own push, or the interval, brings the two together.
- **Stitch itself:**
  - A process left running in the background of a session's shell (`server &`, `nohup … &` typed at the prompt) is a job of the shell in a process group of its own: closing the session sends the shell SIGHUP, which bash passes on to its jobs, and kills the shell's group and the foreground command's, but a background job that ignores SIGHUP survives the session (and a thread's cancellation), as it did before 2026-10-17. Found by a test of `cancel` (2026-10-17). A fix: on closing, kill every process of the session's own session ID (the shell is a session leader), found with `sysctl` `KERN_PROC_SESSION` on macOS and `/proc/*/stat` on Linux.
  - `shell_run` of a multi-line command (a heredoc and more lines, under 768 bytes) is typed line by line. Bash prompts after each line, so `background` reports "It ended at once, so it is no job" while the command keeps running, and a line watch on it fires at the first prompt. A command over 768 bytes is sourced whole and is not affected.

## 5. First milestones (proposal)

Milestones 1 to 3 need nothing new from Otter, so both sides proceed in parallel.

1. **Store and records:** the record format, repository layout, derived status, local index, record sync between two nodes, and commits of received records on full nodes. The format is decided in [records.md](records.md). Done: the format, record roots, blobs, the index with derived status and the graph's edges, reconciliation, commits into checkouts (removed since, §2.5) (`stitch-records`, `stitch-store`), and sync and blob fetch between machines over iroh (`stitch-net`), and pairing, first driven by a development CLI, now by the node (its sync loop, `stitch sync`, `stitch invite`, `stitch join`).
2. **Node and CLI/TUI:** the single binary and its node; chats with attachments and multimodal providers, resumable across two nodes, in the CLI first, then a TUI (ratatui); the attention and digest views. Done: the single binary (`stitch node`, auto-start, the socket protocol), the Anthropic, OpenAI-compatible and Responses adapters, API keys and managed subscription sign-in (§2.8), chats with attachments resumable across nodes and providers, missing attachments fetched from peers, the sync loop, `stitch invite` and `stitch join`, and the TUI (`stitch` alone: the conversation printed into the terminal's own scrollback, so the terminal selects, copies, scrolls and searches it; a live area at the bottom for the streaming reply, approvals, the input with `/attach`, `/model` and `/new`, and overlays for threads and attention). Not yet: the digest view (§2.3, "since you last looked").
   - **Markdown in the TUI** (decided 2026-10-21 with the user; `tui/markdown.rs`): replies, streamed and stored, are rendered as they reach the scrollback, a finished line at a time, by a small renderer of our own rather than a parser that wants the whole text (pulldown-cmark): headings, bullet, numbered and task lists, block quotes, rules, bold, italic, strikethrough, code spans, links, escapes and fenced code. What the terminal copies stays close to what was written: emphasis, code-span and heading markers go, list markers become bullets, a code block keeps its text as written and its fences (dimmed, the language showing), and a link keeps its URL after its text, the text an OSC 8 hyperlink. Only an open fence and an unfinished table are carried from line to line; a table's rows stay in the live area until it ends, then print with sized and aligned columns, or as written when wider than the terminal. The printer's row count uses the text as shown. `/copy` (Ctrl+Y) still copies the reply's Markdown. Not done: syntax colouring of code, wrapping inside table cells.
   - **Picking a message, and `@` files in the TUI** (decided 2026-10-22 with the user; `tui/app.rs`, `tui/files.rs`). The conversation stays in the terminal's scrollback, so what is picked is shown in the live area, not highlighted where it printed.
     - **Esc Esc on an empty input** (Esc alone was free there; while a turn runs it still interrupts) opens a picker over the open thread's messages, the user's and the models' (not tool calls, results or notes), at the latest: ↑↓ (and j/k, Home/End) move, the live area shows the selected one's role, local time, ID and attachments and the start of its text; Enter lists its actions, each with a key that also works from the list: `f` Fork from here (`Request::Fork` with `upto` its ID, the fork opened as `/fork` opens it), `r` rewind to here (§2.15, "Rewinding a thread"; on a message of the user's, sent again), `o` with options (the fork form, up to that message: below; until 2026-10-30 the input got `/fork @m12 ` to complete with flags), `e` edit and resend (a message of the user's: the thread rewound to the message before it, any message with an ID, its text in the input to change and send, since 2026-10-22; it was a fork up to there before; the first message rewinds to the thread's start; its attachments are not carried, said in the status line, since the TUI attaches paths and they are blobs), `c` copy its text (OSC 52 and the platform's tool, as Ctrl+Y). Esc goes back, then out. For this the TUI reads transcripts with `chat::transcript_shown`, which now gives each message's time (`Shown::at`) beside its ID.
     - **The fork form** (decided 2026-10-30, with the user: the flags were only discoverable from `/help`, and `o` left the user to type them). `/fork` alone, `/compact` alone (preset: a summary, then the last 10) and the picker's `o` (preset: up to that message, a row of its own, `u`) open a form in the live area, as `/tools` opens its list (`Overlay::Fork`, `ForkForm`): all of it or its last N messages (a radio row; digits type N, the first replacing the preset 10), and checkboxes without attachments (`-a`), without tool calls (`-t`) and a summary of what is not kept (`-s`). ↑↓ and Tab/Shift+Tab move, Space (or ←→) changes the row selected, `a` `t` `s` `u` switch theirs from anywhere, Enter forks (`Request::Fork`, as typed flags do), Esc cancels. Under it, what the fork keeps, recomputed as it changes, and the same fork as typed (`as typed: /fork @m3 2 -t -s`), to learn the fast path. The preview is the TUI's own estimate from the transcript it holds (`fork_keeps`, mirroring `chat::shaped`: up to the message with the tool calls after it, tool calls dropped, the last N counted back to a message of the user's): messages kept of how many, a rough token count (characters / 4), attachments kept, how many messages the summary stands for; and when the node would refuse it (nothing kept, nothing left to summarize). No node round-trip: on a fork, whose transcript holds only its own messages, it says `(+ what this thread continues)` rather than counting its parent's. Typed arguments (`/fork 5 -t`, `/compact N`, `/summarize`) stay as they were. Open: the phone's fork options sheet could show the same preview.
     - **What a fork shows** (decided 2026-10-22, from a bug the user hit: after `f` or `e`, the scrollback jumped and messages showed twice). A fork opens as any thread opens, under its separator, with its own transcript: the notice of what it continues (up to which message, its summary) and then only its own messages, since it reads its parent's rather than copying them (§2.15); the parent's messages are not printed again, being right above, and the scrollback is not cleared (the conversation stays the terminal's). The cause was not the opening: `chat::transcript_shown` left the fork's notice out when read up to a segment, which the TUI does when a chat ends to count what the chat already showed live, so on any thread with a notice (a fork, `/summarize`, `/compact`, a compaction the TUI follows, a fork opened with `/open`) the count was one short and each turn's last entry, its reply, was printed a second time. The transcript up to a segment now starts as the whole one does.
     - **Resizing the terminal** (decided 2026-10-22, from duplicates the user still saw in Alacritty with that fix). The TUI tracked where the text ends as a row it computed, and resizing broke it: the terminal reflows its screen and scrollback (Alacritty wraps and joins lines on a width change; on a shorter window it pushes the rows above the cursor into its scrollback as far as needed to keep the cursor on screen and drops those below it; on a taller one it pulls rows back from its scrollback and moves the cursor down), so the row went stale. Run through Alacritty's own grid (`alacritty_terminal`, a dev-dependency: `tui/emu.rs`), every resize but a wider one went wrong: narrower or shorter, rows of the live area (the input's border, a streaming reply's lines, the Esc Esc picker's preview of a message) were left above the text and went into the scrollback, so a reply streamed during the resize showed twice; taller, the last lines of the conversation were cleared from the screen, being lost. Decided: the terminal's own cursor, hidden (the input draws its caret), is parked where the text ends after every draw, so the terminal's reflow carries it along with the text; on a new size, taken from the terminal's size before anything is written (`Screen::sync`; the resize event can come after a spinner's frame, which would park the cursor at a stale row), the cursor is asked for (CPR, `ESC [6n`), and from there down the screen is cleared and the live area drawn on the new bottom rows. For the answer to be read, the terminal's events are read on a thread of the TUI's own that polls (25 ms) instead of crossterm's `EventStream`, whose thread holds crossterm's event reader while it waits for input, so that `cursor::position` waited its two seconds. The other way, clearing the screen and printing again the tail that fits, was not taken: what a clear does to the screen's rows varies (Alacritty and VTE push them into the scrollback, xterm drops them), so the scrollback would get a copy or a gap, and it needs the conversation's rendered tail kept. Every terminal answers CPR. Those that reflow (Alacritty, kitty, VTE, iTerm2, Terminal.app, Ghostty, WezTerm, tmux) move the cursor with its text; xterm does not reflow (lines are cut, the cursor stays), which suits it too. A residual race: a resize landing between the size check and the terminal reading what was written puts that one write at the old rows. Tested: the screen through Alacritty's grid, idle, with a reply streaming and with the picker open, wider, narrower, shorter, taller, dragged and back, with and without scrollback (`resizing_the_terminal_keeps_the_scrollback_whole`; it fails before this change); and, ignored (it starts a node), the built binary in a pseudo-terminal on that grid answering its cursor queries (`the_tui_in_alacritty_takes_resizes`: each resize taken in about 40 ms, `/help` printed four times found four times; the binary before this change lost one). Only checked by emulation: a real window being resized.
     - **A message woven into a turn followed** (found with the resize, 2026-10-22). Sent while another turn runs on the thread (a report's, a `send` from another thread), a message is printed when sent, waits, and is woven into that turn, which the TUI follows from the transcript: it was printed a second time there. While it waits, the first message of the user's that starts with it is printed without it (what was queued and run together with it stays); its own turn, if it gets one, is counted as before. Checked as well, on a copy of the user's index (`transcripts_up_to_a_segment_start_the_whole`, ignored): every segment of the forked summary fork the user saw duplicates in, and of 335 threads, gives a transcript up to it that starts the whole one, but for segments of threads whose chain branched (two nodes wrote at once): the whole transcript follows one branch, so a chat ended on the other counts against a transcript that does not hold it. Not fixed (rare: concurrent turns on two nodes).
     - **`@` where a word starts** (not in a command: `/spawn @node` takes one) opens an inline picker over the files of the TUI's directory: in a git checkout `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard` (tracked files that exist, untracked ones not ignored), else a breadth-first walk (hidden directories and build outputs skipped, 8 levels, 20,000 files), with their directories. What follows the `@` filters it, a fuzzy subsequence match that prefers runs, word starts and the file's own name (tried from the left and from the right); ↑↓ move, Tab or Enter picks, Esc closes, leaving what was typed; a space or the cursor leaving the word closes it. Picking writes `@relative/path ` and, for a text file (UTF-8, no NUL in its first 8 KB) of at most 256 KB, attaches it through the TUI's attachments (the status line names it); a directory, a binary or a bigger file stays a mention only, for the agent to read. Sending drops the attachment of each `@path` no longer in the text; `/attach`'s files are not touched. Not done: the same in the phone's composer.
