     - **Images from the clipboard and dropped files** (decided 2026-10-27, with the user, who found the TUI had no way to attach a pasted image; `tui/clipboard.rs`): a terminal pastes only text, so **Ctrl+V** (and an empty bracketed paste, which some terminals send for an image) reads the platform's clipboard: on macOS through `osascript`, a file copied in Finder as its path (`«class furl»`), else the image as PNG (`«class PNGf»`, which macOS converts to from the other image types); on Linux `wl-paste`, then `xclip`, for `image/png`. The image is written to `$TMPDIR/stitch-paste-<pid>/image-<n>.png`, which the node stores as a blob when the message is sent, and attached as `@image-<n>.png` at the cursor: a mention as the `@` picker's, so deleting the name takes it back. A paste that is nothing but paths to existing files that are not text (a file dropped on the terminal: escaped, quoted or a `file://` URL) attaches them the same way, `@name`, instead of typing the path; a path to a text file stays text, more often meant as a name. Over SSH the clipboard read is the remote machine's (as in Claude Code); OSC 52 cannot read images.
3. **Stitch as the root:** the agent loop in chat and interactive shell sessions as its tools, with rules and approvals (done); instruction files and skills in every thread's context, updates carried with the next message (done); the web tools: `browse_web`, `web_eval` and `search_web`, in Rust with a shared headless Chromium (done); `view_file`, `zoom_image` and page screenshots (done); threads that weave: `spawn`, `send`, `wait`, `models`, here or on another node (done); memory: engram over EmbeddingGemma 2 in-process, a fold of synced facts, recalled unasked and on request (done); threads placed in projects (every git checkout, by its first commit; forks split off on request), folders (one across nodes by their `~` path, linked or unlinked on request, folded into a project after `git init`) or nowhere, every node holding every project, the TUI reopening the last one per place (done); detached threads (done: `stitch chat --detach` and `/spawn`, approvals as `question` records answered with `stitch answer` or from the TUI's attention view, budgets in money, tokens and time, reports into the starting thread that wake its agent, `stitch cancel`, resumed by the node that started them after a restart); tools from MCP servers, stdio and streamable HTTP, with enable lists and approvals (done, §2.13); tool and skill sets per thread, places' own settings and skills, tools shared between nodes (§2.14); schedules; remote shells between nodes. Used daily for the work now done by hand with handoff records.
4. **iOS client and node** (decided 2026-10-12: the phone is a full node from the first build, not a thin client; Otter is not on its path: it joined the app on 2026-10-17, for Otter tools, §2.17). The node links into the app as an XCFramework of the Rust crates, all but the CLI; `stitch-llama` and `stitch-memory` are in from day one. visionOS too, built with a pinned nightly (`-Zbuild-std`: Rust ships no standard library for it). What a phone cannot do is left out by target, not by feature (decided 2026-10-13), so no build can forget it: `stitch-shell` builds everywhere but starts no program on iOS or visionOS (the sandbox forbids it; `CAN_SPAWN`), so the node offers no shell tools there; Chrome is desktop only, so `browse_web` and `web_eval` render in the app's WebKit there (§2.3, since 2026-10-29; before, only `search_web`); the `keyring` crate has no iOS store, so secrets go to the data protection keychain through Security.framework, readable after the first unlock (background refresh runs locked) and on this device only, under accounts without the data directory's hash (an app's container path changes when it is updated or reinstalled). libc 0.2.190 dropped `net/route.h` and `netinet6/in6_var.h` items on those OSes (their SDKs lack the headers), breaking iroh's `netdev` and `netwatch`, neither of which had visionOS code; forks of their released versions under the user's account fix both, patched in the workspace until upstream releases do. `just check` runs `just check-apple` on macOS (clippy for the iOS device and simulator, a visionOS check), as iroh's own CI covers no Apple mobile target. Swift reaches it through UniFFI over one narrow client API (threads, attention, blobs, pairing and sync, provider sign-in), a Rust trait that also has an implementation over the local socket and later one over the iroh remote-client protocol, so SwiftUI never knows where the node is. The control flows are records that sync: approvals asked and answered, delegation asked and claimed, progress in transcripts; the iroh remote-client protocol (§4, question 5) comes after, as a fast path for live steering that writes the same records. The phone runs work in the foreground (its own loop in Rust, with delegation and record tools; a loop in Otter replaces it) and syncs on returning to the foreground and in background refresh; push for attention comes later. First step: the node built for iOS on a device, writing a record, pairing with a Mac by QR and syncing.

   **Built (2026-10-13), with a basic chat:** the app (SwiftUI, in its own repository, one target for iOS and visionOS 26, `com.fragcolor.stitch`, its project made by XcodeGen from `project.yml`) links `stitch-ffi`, an XCFramework built by `just ios`. Decided with it:
   - **The app's node takes requests in process.** `NodeHandle::client` serves a connection over an in-memory stream exactly as one on the socket, so the app sends the same requests as the CLI and the TUI, and the trait over both is still to come with the remote-client protocol. On iOS and visionOS the node binds no socket: no other process could reach it. Reads (threads, transcripts) come from the store and index, as the TUI's do.
   - **Async without a second runtime:** an exported `async` call spawns its work on the node's runtime and awaits the handle, which any executor can poll, so UniFFI's own polling serves Swift's `async` and no Swift thread is held.
   - **Setup and joining are library calls** (`stitch_node::setup`), the CLI's and the app's. The app sets its node up on first start under a name the user picks (`phone`, `vision` suggested), with blobs fetched lazily, and a home workspace of its own: every home workspace is the repository `home`, so what it holds before joining merges with the fleet's.
   - **Pairing:** the QR code of `stitch invite`, read by VisionKit's scanner on iOS, a pasted ticket, or a `stitch://join?ticket=…` link. A name another node already has is refused by the inviter, as on the desktop.
   - **A moved data directory finds its home workspace again:** an iOS app's container moves when the app is updated or reinstalled, and the index keeps root paths; with no home workspace where the index says, the node indexes the one at `<data>/home` again.
   - **Subscription sign-ins work as on the desktop,** as edge talk's do: the node listens for the redirect on this device (`auth::login`, the provider's loopback port), and the app shows the page in Safari inside the app, so it stays in the foreground for it; closing the page cancels.
   - **Forks, as the TUI and the CLI have them** (§2.15, 2026-10-14): a chat's menu forks it, summarizes and continues, summarizes and truncates (a summary, then the last 10 messages), or opens the options (the last N messages, without attachments or tool calls, a summary of what is not kept); a message's own menu forks up to it (`upto`, so transcripts carry each message's ID), and the fork opens.
   - **The app's own UI and operations** (attachments shown, formatted replies, message actions, the thread list; its UI tests, Apple account and deployment; local refresh, background refresh, apps in the app) are documented with the app.
   - **Spawned threads hang under their parent** (decided 2026-10-29, with the user, who found the flat list of chats and spawns very hard to follow). `ThreadInfo` carries `parent` (`meta::parent`, shared with the TUI's `/parent`: who spawned it, else whom it reports to, followed to its newest compaction) and the first line of a spawned thread's `goal`. The list is a tree (`ThreadTree`): a thread whose parent is listed hangs under it, indented, its goal under its title when it says more; a conversation's section and order go by the latest activity in it or below it. A conversation with spawns has a pill with their count that opens or closes them; closed, a line says how many below are working, need the user or failed. Open by default only while something below works or waits on the user; a tap's choice holds for the session (not kept). Searching shows the matches flat, each saying what it hangs under. **A pinned spawn is a root of its own** (decided 2026-11-04, with the user, after a pinned child stayed folded under its parent, in its parent's day, in the app, the web client and the TUI alike: only roots were looked at for pinning): it is listed with the pinned, saying what it hangs under, its own spawns under it; the same in the web client's sidebar (whose trees now also go by the latest activity in them, not the root's own) and the TUI's picker. A detached thread's `running` state now shows the working badge (the list looked only for `working`), `waiting` reads "needs you". In a chat, its title is the navigation title (was "Chat"), and a bar over the transcript links to the thread that spawned it and lists those it spawned, with their state.
   - **The TUI's thread picker is the same tree** (2026-10-29, with the user): spawns hang under their parent with tree lines (`├ └`), a parent marked ▾ open or ▸ closed with the closed summary (`21 below · 1 working · 1 needs you`), open by default while something below works or waits on the user; → opens (or steps into the first spawn), ← closes (or goes to the parent), the choice kept for the session. Typing filters by every word in the title or place, the matches flat, each with `↖` the thread it hangs under; with no match Enter runs `/search` on the words over the messages. Letters type, so `j`/`k` no longer move and Delete (not `d`) deletes; Esc clears the filter, then closes. A spawn's goal shows under the list when it says more than its title. Read again (every poll), the picker stays on the thread it was on, not on the row. `ThreadItem` carries `parent` and `goal` as the app's `ThreadInfo` does (`tui/tree.rs`).
   - **Prompt:** where no program can start, the default system prompt says so, and hands work that needs a machine to the user's others (`spawn`); the web tools are all there, `browse_web` and `web_eval` in the app's WebKit (§2.3, since 2026-10-29).
   - **Approvals in the app** (2026-10-14): the attention list, a bell in the thread list with a badge, each question allowed, allowed always (what it grants shown), or declined with a reason; the answer syncs to every node at once, as the question came (a node asking syncs it to all at once). Read every three seconds while the app runs; new ones are notified (a local notification) when the app is not in front, and opening one shows the approvals. **New ones pop up while it is in front** (decided 2026-10-22, with the user, who chatted from the phone, saw no notification and no bell in a chat, and answered an `otter_run` approval on the Mac): a small sheet over whatever is open, one question at a time, with Allow, Always, Decline and Later (left to the approvals; not swiped away, so a dismissal never answers); it goes when answered here or elsewhere, and none pops while the approvals are open.
   - **Push notifications** (decided 2026-10-14, with the user; `apns.rs`, `relay/`): a question asked while the app is not running reaches the phone through Apple's push service. The phone registers for pushes once notifications are allowed and writes a `device` record in the home workspace: its device token, `env` (`sandbox` for a development build), and the public half of an X25519 key it made (the private half stays in its keychain, shared with its notification service extension), synced at once. The node that asks the user pushes to every phone (so a question is pushed once): the content (the question, the thread's title, the node) **sealed for that phone** (an ephemeral X25519 key, HKDF-SHA256 with both public keys as salt and `stitch push v1` as info, ChaCha20-Poly1305), with the waiting count as the app's badge; the extension opens it with CryptoKit and shows it, and opening it shows the approvals. What APNs, a relay and anyone between see is a placeholder, "Something waits on you". `apps/ios/tools/push-interop.sh` checks that what the node seals CryptoKit opens. Two ways to APNs, taking the same sealed content:
     - **With the app team's key on the node** (a build signed by the user's own team): `stitch apns set <AuthKey.p8> --key-id <id> --team-id <team>` (the team named: no default since 2026-11-22, so no team ID is built in) keeps it in the keychain (the key `apns`) and its IDs in `config.toml` (`[apns]`). APNs takes HTTP/2 requests with a JWT (ES256, the key's ID and the team's), signed with ring, made again after 50 minutes.
     - **Through the relay** (the store's app: only the team that owns an app's ID can push to it, so users' nodes cannot hold the key): a Cloudflare Worker (`relay/`, deployed by the app's team) holding the key, keeping nothing: it takes a device token, an environment, the sealed content and a badge, builds the payload itself (its placeholder; it cannot put arbitrary text on a phone) and forwards it. The device token is the credential, as for APNs itself (a per-device secret handed out by the relay would add nothing: whoever has the token could ask for it); pushes are capped per token. It is the default: a node without a key of its own pushes through the app team's relay with nothing to set up; `stitch apns relay <url>` names another, and a key of the node's own, when set, comes first.
     - `stitch apns test` pushes one; `stitch apns status` says how the node pushes and to which phones.
     - **Notices** (§2.3) go the same way: sealed content with `thread` and no `question`; the extension puts them in their thread's group (`notices` for none), and opening one opens that chat.
     - **Calls** (§2.3, "Calls") go as VoIP pushes to the phone's second token (`voip`), sealed whole: the relay's `POST /voip`, or the node's own key to the topic `<app>.voip`. One APNs key serves both topics when it is team-scoped.
     - **A notification lives as long as its question** (decided 2026-10-18, from the user's reports: notifications of questions answered elsewhere stayed, the approvals opened from one were empty, and answering meant opening the app). A question's notification is named by the question: the app's own by its ID, a push by `apns-collapse-id` (the relay's `collapse`), and both carry it as `question` (with `node`, the asker). The app takes away the notification of every question its node holds as answered or withdrawn (`Stitch.answered`; one it does not hold yet stays, since a push can come before its question), each time it reads the questions. When the asking stops (answered anywhere, withdrawn; not when its node stops), the node that pushed sends a **background push** (`content-available`, sealed `{answered, badge}`; through the relay at `POST /answered`, so a relay from before answers 404 rather than showing its placeholder), which wakes the app (`remote-notification` background mode) to take it away and sync; iOS delivers these at its discretion and not to an app the user force-quit, so the next launch or refresh does it otherwise. **Allow and Decline on the notification** (category `question`, set by the extension once it opens a push): they answer without opening the app (Allow asks for the phone unlocked); the app, launched in the background if need be, fetches the question first when it is not here yet, syncing with the asker alone (`Stitch.syncWith`: it just pushed, so it is reachable, and unreachable peers hold nothing up), then all, 15 s at most each, answers, and syncs the answer to the asker; what fails is said in a notification. **Opening a notification** shows the approvals with the question as "Arriving from mbp…" until that sync brings it; the approvals sync each time they are shown.
   - **Memory** (2026-10-14, §2.9): installed from Settings → Memory, the same encoder as every node's with a 2048-token context; a chat's menu takes it out of memory or brings it back.
   - **Turns and the background** (decided 2026-10-15, after turns on the phone died whenever the user switched apps, and a chat left and opened again showed neither its streaming reply nor that it ran). What was found, in the simulator with the mock model, the app frozen as iOS suspends it (`STITCH-FREEZE`, apps/ios/README.md) or killed: with no background task iOS suspends the app within seconds of leaving it; the provider then drops the stalled stream, and on waking the read fails at once (`http: error decoding response body`, a reset under it; or a stream ended before its last event), never retried since output had streamed, so the turn ended in that error. Nothing hung there, but a connection gone silently (a network switched while asleep) would have waited for the 10-minute read timeout. The user's message is written with the turn's first step, so a turn cut in its first model call (most chats are one call) lost the message from the transcript, and the composer gave it back only for a new chat; a turn killed after steps had run kept them, the transcript ending in tool results with no reply and no way to go on. Now:
     - **Kept awake while a turn runs.** The app holds a `UIApplication` background task while a chat or a detached loop works on its node (`busy`, as the node's own idle check: not a thread waiting on an approval or `wait`), checked as chats start and end and every two seconds while held, and ends it as soon as none runs: a turn finishes when the user answers a message elsewhere (iOS gives about 30 seconds). A reply that ends while the app is not in front is a local notification, which opens its chat.
     - **Stopped cleanly when the grace ends.** iOS's expiration handler stops the chats running with `Suspend` on their connection: as an interrupt, what the model was writing is dropped and a tool call cut (its result says the app was suspended, not the user), but the stop is written as `interrupted` with `why = "suspended"` and the chat ends with stop `suspended`, within two seconds, before the process sleeps. Detached loops are left to iOS: killed, they resume at the node's next start as before; woken, their cut call is retried (below).
     - **A call the network cut is sent again** (every node): a model call that failed on the network (no connection, a dropped or stalled stream, a stream ended before the provider's last event: `stitch_providers::Error::is_network`, `Cut`) is sent again after 2, 5 and 10 seconds; nothing of it was written, and clients are told (`Reply::Retry`) to drop what streamed. An error the provider answered is not retried here (the HTTP layer already retries its 429s and 5xx before streaming, but not an account out of usage, §2.8).
     - **Resume.** `chat::unfinished` reads from a thread's records whether its last turn never ended: `suspended` (stopped as above), or `cut off` (its last step leaves the model to answer: the user's message, tool results or a note after them; a killed process, a loop that failed mid-turn); not when it answered, the user stopped it or the model declined. Only this node's turns resume in place: one whose last step another node wrote is that node's (its records cannot tell a turn it stopped from one it runs now, and two loops on a thread fork it unsaid, the shorter branch left behind: on 2026-10-16 the phone, offered Resume on a thread at work on the Mac, ran three steps beside the Mac's, all failed, on a branch the Mac's longer one left), and so is a detached thread with no report (`running`, `waiting`: its loop resumes at its node's start); a fork nothing has continued yet is its forker's. `chat::unfinished` says whose (`elsewhere`), and `Request::Resume` refuses another's. Such a turn resumes in a fork instead, by choice: the chat says the turn is that node's and may still run there, and "Fork & Resume", once confirmed (what the original does next is not in the fork, and the fork's tool calls run here), forks the thread whole (§2.15, its trailing tool calls and results with it) and resumes the fork on this node, from the step the other loop was at. `Request::Resume` (`Stitch.resume`) runs the loop on such a thread from what is written (`chat::resume`, as a detached thread resumes after a restart), streaming as a chat. The app resumes by itself the chats it suspended when it is in front again; any other unfinished turn shows "stopped before it was answered" with Resume in its chat, not run unasked (it may be hours later, and costs a call).
     - **Why a confirmed Fork & Resume did not fork** (2026-10-17, `…isev`, from the records): the phone's node failed the fork with `1 attachment(s) not on this node`. A fork wrote the original's system prompt anew, reading it from its blob, and the phone had never fetched that blob for a thread gamer started (blobs come when a conversation needs them, §2.5): its copy in the app's container is dated 22:36Z, fetched by the chat the user typed next (m96, 22:36:40Z), whose turn fetches missing blobs and tries again, which the fork did not. Nothing was written, and the app showed the error as a bare line under the banner, cleared by that next message's turn, which also cleared the banner. Now: a fork names the system prompt by its hash, as the original does, without reading it (the fork's first turn fetches it like any blob); a fork that still lacks blobs (a summary reads attachments) fetches them from the peers and tries once more; a failure is logged on the node (`fork of thread …: failed`), and the phone's node keeps its log in `node.log` in its data directory (stderr, as a Mac's node writes it; `node.log.1` past 2 MB), readable from the app's container. The app says a failed Fork & Resume in the banner itself ("The fork failed: …"), kept until the next try, with Fork & Resume still there.
     - **The composer on another node's turn** (decided 2026-10-18, same case): while the banner says a turn is another node's, the composer is locked to that turn: what is typed goes to it as an interjection (`Stitch.interject`, `Request::Send` with `interject`, as an agent's `send`: woven into that loop between its steps on the node that runs it, or a turn there when none runs), said above the field ("Messages go to gamer's running turn.") and under the message ("Sent to gamer.": whether gamer wove it in or started a turn is not known here; until 2026-11-02 it said "running turn", then "Woven into the running turn." once the turn ended), until the transcript has it, matched by its text alone (`Entry.typed`, without the `[instructions updated]`/`[memories recalled]` lines a turn's first message gets, which kept the bubble for good, issue 2026-11-02T102522Z-mbp-2fkd) among your messages the transcript did not have when it was sent (no message ID comes back: the node that runs the turn writes it, a woven one only between steps), never a chat here; attachments wait for a fork. The default, which Settings → "A thread another machine runs" changes to Locked (nothing typed there; Fork & Resume only). Fork & Resume stays in the banner either way. The node refuses such a chat too (§2.7), for clients that do not lock.
     - **A message the process died with is given back.** The node writes the user's message with the first step, or with the failure of its model call (below); a process that dies first writes nothing. The app keeps each message whose turn runs in its defaults until the turn ends; at the next launch, one its thread does not hold goes back to that chat's composer (a new chat's to the next new chat), saying it was not sent. A failure that wrote nothing gives the message back to the composer, for a new chat and an existing thread.
     - **Out of usage: the message kept, the error shown** (decided 2026-10-22). On the phone, with a Claude Max subscription (Anthropic OAuth), the usage limit ran out: the chat just did not move, what the user had typed disappeared, no thread was created, and no error was shown. From the code: the 429 was retried by the HTTP layer like any rate limit (three more times, waiting its `retry-after`, capped at 60 s: minutes of a chat that looked frozen); the error then came before the first step, so nothing was written; the app's failure path put the message back in the composer and set the error on its `ChatModel`, then, the new chat's thread having been named at the start (`Reply::Thread`), opened that thread (`done`), whose chat was a fresh `ChatModel` (`NodeModel` drops a turn's chat when it ends) on a thread that did not exist: the composer and the error were left behind on the old one. Now:
       - an account out of usage is not retried, and is said (§2.8): "Anthropic (Claude subscription): usage limit reached (5-hour window); it resets at 18:00 (in 2 h 10 min). Wait until then, or switch to another model with /model and go on (send a message, or Resume). Stitch does not switch by itself."
       - **A model call that fails for good writes what its step had to write**, with a `failed` event (`t = "failed"`: the provider's label, its error, and for an account out of usage what ran out and when it resets; shown as a `failed` transcript entry, its reset time read in the reader's time zone): the user's message on the first step (a new thread's record with it), and the messages and notes woven in since the last step, which were acknowledged (`Woven`) or delivered. This reverses the earlier "a failure before the first step writes nothing": nothing is sent again, so no message is doubled; the turn is `cut off` (`chat::unfinished`), so Resume continues it, and the next message goes on from it (the model reads both). Not written: a request refused as it is, which writing would make every later turn repeat: too long for the model (the node compacts the thread and sends the message there, as before) or a 400, 413 or 422 on the first step (an attachment the model does not take); its message goes back to the client to be changed. The node ends such a chat with `Reply::Kept`, then the error (`Error::Kept`, the error as said): the TUI then keeps its input empty and says the message is kept in the thread (and starts a new thread again when a new one's first message was not written); `stitch chat` says so.
       - **A turn that fails before its model call is written the same way** (2026-11-10, after a chat typed on the phone into a thread that ran on mbp, whose Anthropic OAuth refresh was refused: mbp only logged it, wrote nothing, and the phone, following the turn, showed nothing, its message lost). A turn nobody typed here (a chat passed on from another node, a message or reports that woke it, `detached::turn_with`) whose provider does not resolve (no credentials, a refresh refused, a bad provider setting) writes the message that came for it and a `failed` event with why (`failed::before_call`: "The model call failed: …"), sealed (`chat::failed_before_call`), and ends as a failed model call does: a detached or reporting thread reports `failed`, a follower on another node hears the turn done (its assignment) and reads the entry once synced. A chat typed on this node's own client is not written: the client gets the error and the message back, as before.
     - **A chat opened again shows its turn** (rather than the node letting a client attach to a running turn's stream): `NodeModel` keeps the `ChatModel` of each turn running in the app, by thread, and a chat view opened on that thread takes it, with its streamed reply, steps, Stop and queued state. The streaming state already lives there, its task holds it, and the app is the only client of its node, so no protocol and no buffering of a turn's events in the node; a detached loop's turn still shows step by step as written. Stop reaches that chat only (`stop_chat`), not every chat running.
5. **Native Otter loop:** depends on Otter's Track A1 to A3 (H1, H3 to H6, H8) and their gates; Track B's text-tool subset for tools, Track C for shell, SSH and embeddings. Then the memory policy in Otter (§2.9), and Otter behind apps' data (§2.10). **Started 2026-10-16 with tools** (§2.17), before Tracks A2 and A3: skills' Otter scripts as tools on desktop nodes, the first being Stitch's `google-flights` skill; on every node, the phone and visionOS included, since 2026-10-17; and the language's own tools (`otter_search`, `otter_describe`, `otter_check`, `otter_run`), so a thread checks and runs code and writes a skill's tools, since 2026-10-17. The loop itself still waits on those tracks.
6. **Live channel,** then moving threads between nodes. Its first part is pulled forward for browser control (decided 2026-10-17, §2.18): screencast frames of the user's Chrome to the phone, and touches and keys back, so the user can watch and take over from it.
